The Harbor of Knives

The Harbor of Knives

A novel — first draft

Part One A share of the city

Chapter 1 The night cargo

The woman in the boat had a knife at her own throat and seemed annoyed about it.

Lyra saw the blade first, bright above a collar of wet blue silk. Then the hand holding it, which belonged to the man crouched behind her. He had braced one foot against the thwart. The boat slid sideways toward Harrow’s lowest step, carrying four covered crates and a second man who lay with his face in the bottom boards.

“Put out the lamp,” the man with the knife said.

Lyra kept it where it was.

The woman looked up at her. Her dark hair had come loose on one side, exposing a small gold ring in her ear. Blood ran beneath the silk collar. She was holding the wrist of the knife hand with both of hers, patiently keeping a little space between her throat and its edge.

“Your night rate,” she said. “Does it include conversation?”

“Only after the boat is made fast.”

“That’s encouraging.”

The man pulled her head back. She stopped speaking, but her eyes stayed on Lyra. Past the boat’s stern another vessel moved without a light. An oar touched stone. Lyra heard a low instruction and the scrape of wood as somebody drew the oar in.

She set the lamp on the upper step, where its light fell across the water and left her own hands in shadow.

“Throw me a line.”

“No lines,” the man said. “You send this boat away.”

“You’re coming onto my stones whether I send you or not.”

The bow struck the lowest tread. The woman moved with the impact, turning inside the arm around her neck. Lyra stepped down and caught the boat’s painter. The man saw the movement and thrust the knife toward her face.

She pulled the painter hard.

His foot slipped off the thwart. For an instant all three of them moved toward the same place. The woman’s elbow went backward into his mouth; Lyra saw two pale shapes fall against the blue shoulder. She caught the gunwale with her right hand, felt the old weakness bite, and shifted her weight to her legs. The knife passed beneath her chin and struck the stone.

The woman took it from him.

There was nothing elegant about the next movement. She drove the blade through the lower part of his cheek and hauled him sideways by the hilt. His scream filled the narrow space beneath the landing. Lyra pushed his shoulder down while the woman stepped over him onto the lowest tread.

“Keep him?” she asked.

“For the moment.”

The woman removed the knife. He tried to rise. Lyra hit his wrist against the step until his fingers opened, then turned him onto his stomach and held him with a boot between his shoulders.

From above, Belis said, “I thought you told us this one was lamp glass.”

“Fetch Orrel.”

“He’s gone for the handcart.”

“Fetch a rope, then.”

Belis came down with one already in his hands. He had been winding it while waiting for the shipment, and the neat coil remained over his elbow as he knelt to bind the man’s wrists. Lyra watched the unlit boat beyond the stern. It had stopped moving.

“Who is in that?” she asked the woman.

“People who thought I’d gone quiet.”

“Yours?”

“No.”

The woman wiped the knife on the captive’s coat and put two fingers in her mouth. The whistle was loud enough to bring a shout from a house beyond the footway. A second whistle answered farther out in the harbor. Lyra could see nothing there.

“How many boats have you brought?”

“One I intended. Two I didn’t.”

“You’ll pay for all of them if they touch the landing.”

The woman smiled then, fiercely and with blood on her teeth. She had bitten her own lip. “Vaska Rell.”

“Names later. Belis, behind the wall.”

A crossbow string snapped in the dark. The bolt struck the step where his knee had been.

Belis threw himself backward, taking the bound man with him. Vaska caught the lamp before it fell and set it on the lowest tread. Light spread outward across the attacking boat. Three men, one reloading, one rowing, the third holding a long hook for the landing.

Lyra went down two steps into the water.

The cold closed around her thighs with a welcome she had no time to enjoy. Her coat spread behind her. She reached beneath the gunwale of Vaska’s boat, found the loose end of its aft line, and pulled it through the ring Mavet had helped set months before. The iron held soundly against the stone. She drew the line across the narrow approach and passed it back to Belis.

“Low. Don’t lift until I say.”

He looked at the dark water, then at her. “I can do low.”

The hooked pole reached the steps. Lyra caught it below the iron and shoved the wooden shaft beneath the boat’s bow. The man holding it fought her for a moment, then understood that she was not trying to take it. She was pushing his own leverage beneath him.

“Now.”

Belis hauled. The taut line rose across the approaching bow. The rower shouted and tried to back water. His oar struck the stone cheek. Vaska stepped from the landing onto her own covered cargo, took the hooked pole from Lyra, and drove its blunt end into the crossbowman’s chest.

He fell over the side.

The others reached for him. Lyra held their boat against the line while Vaska crossed the small gap. She landed on the after seat, slipped, caught herself against the rower’s shoulder, and cut him across the back of his hand. He let go of the oar. The hook man came at her with a knife of his own.

Lyra could not reach them from the water. She could hold the boat. She locked her left arm around the line and leaned away, keeping the gunwale pulled low while Vaska put the rower between herself and the blade.

The crossbowman caught Lyra’s coat beneath the water. His weight dragged her toward him. She felt his mouth open against her sleeve. He wanted air; he had found cloth instead. For an instant she could have put a hand beneath his chin and lifted.

The boat jerked. Vaska fell to one knee.

Lyra tore the coat from his grasp and kept the line tight.

Above her, Orrel appeared with the handcart and an iron boat hook. He took in the scene, said something about the quality of imported glass, and brought the hook down on the knife man’s forearm. The blow sounded solid. The man dropped his weapon and reached for the side.

Vaska hit him across the ear with the knife hilt. He folded slowly, as though searching for a place to sit.

Another boat came out of the darkness, carrying two people who had answered the whistle. Vaska raised her bloody hand before they could strike anyone. One of them caught the abandoned oar. The other pulled the crossbowman out of the water by his collar.

He lay in their boat without coughing.

Lyra let the line slip. Her right arm trembled, though it had taken little of the final load. She climbed the steps using her left hand. Vaska was there when she reached the top, offering an arm she had not asked for. Lyra took it.

Below, the man pulled from the water began to cough. He made an unpleasant business of it. Lyra stood dripping onto her own platform and listened until she was certain he could go on without her attention.

“Lamp glass,” Belis said again.

Vaska looked at the crates. “There is some.”

They brought the injured man from Vaska’s first boat into the shed. He was alive, with a cut high in one thigh and a hand that kept straying toward it. Vaska put a folded coat beneath his head. When Lyra knelt, he tried to push himself away.

“She’ll look,” Vaska told him. “Let her.”

“Does he have a name?” Lyra asked.

“Everyone has one.”

“Then try using his.”

“Rusk. This is Lyra.”

Rusk stopped moving. He was a young man with one eyebrow shaved into two narrow lines. It gave him an expression of permanent surprise that his present condition did not improve.

Lyra cut the trouser leg. The wound was deeper than she wanted. She put a cloth against it and took his hand firmly away.

“Hold my wrist if you need something.”

He did. She felt his pulse through his fingers, quick and frightened. Her own blood had settled after the fight. The stillness that came with her changed body would once have frightened her more than the knives. Now she let it steady her while she found the source of the bleeding.

Vaska watched from the other side. The cut at her throat had opened again, staining the silk almost black.

“You’ll need that dressed,” Lyra said.

“After him.”

“He isn’t going to thank you for bleeding on the clean cloth.”

Vaska pressed her sleeve against her neck and leaned back.

Belis brought warm water. He had lost one shoe and found it again full of mud. Every step made a small sucking noise. Orrel had taken the prisoners into the lower store and set two men at the door. Neither guard belonged to Lyra. They were Vaska’s people, and she had told them plainly that anyone found touching the cargo would lose the hand involved.

“What have you brought me?” Lyra asked.

“Blue dye. A little silver. Good glass. Nothing that breathes.”

“Whose?”

“Mine.”

“Before it was yours.”

Vaska smiled without amusement. “A man who bought it from another man who had no better claim.”

Lyra pressed the folded cloth harder. Rusk gasped and clutched her wrist.

“Tell me before I find out from somebody with another crossbow.”

“The salt companies take a charge on goods that never touch their landings. These paid it once. Somebody wanted it paid twice. I persuaded the second collector to accept less.”

“With what?”

“An oar.”

Lyra looked at her. Vaska spread her free hand.

“He wanted the boat as security. I let him keep part of it.”

Rusk laughed, then regretted the movement. Vaska put a hand against his shoulder and stayed quiet while Lyra worked.

The torn vessel answered her touch slowly. She remembered Gyllan making her wait with her fingers beside a wound until she could feel the small living changes without imagining the ones she wanted. He had known how much she disliked waiting. He used to give her tasks that could only be spoiled by haste, then sit nearby eating something crisp.

The blood slowed. Lyra shifted her fingers, easing the damaged edges toward one another. The work would not make Rusk fit to run tonight. It would give the surgeon something he could close without losing the man first.

“Hold this,” she told Vaska.

Vaska held it exactly where she showed her. Belis went for the surgeon. Lyra stood too quickly and had to catch the table.

“You go cold when you do that,” Vaska said.

“I was standing in the harbor.”

“I noticed you seemed at home.”

Lyra washed her hands. “You can pay for noticing in the morning.”

The captive with the cut cheek was the first to speak. He sat on a stool beneath the loft, both wrists bound to the back, making a deliberate show of how difficult each word had become.

Lyra placed the recovered crossbow on the table. Vaska had given its owner no opportunity to recover it. He sat farther along the wall with a blanket around him and water dripping from his nose.

“Who sent you?” Lyra asked.

The man worked his mouth. “You know.”

“No. I know which answer you’re hoping I’ll fear enough to stop asking.”

He looked at Vaska. She was changing the bandage around her neck with a care that suggested she had done it before.

“The lower collection house.”

“Under whose mark?”

He named one of the Prince’s companies. Lyra had received three offers from it in the last month, each more courteously written than the last. The final one had included an estimate of the repairs her north cheek still needed. She had not invited anyone from the company to inspect it.

“Were you told to come onto my landing?”

“We were told to recover the cargo.”

“And the woman?”

He looked at Vaska again.

“Her too.”

Vaska drew the bandage tight enough to make her own eyes water. “That would have been an interesting unloading.”

Lyra went through the prisoner’s pockets. A little money, a key, a folded instruction bearing the company mark. She opened the paper. It described the crates and required them delivered unopened. Beneath that, in a different hand, somebody had written that no handling charge was to be paid at Harrow.

She read the line twice. Then she held it where the man could see.

“Was that worth the trouble?”

He turned away. She caught his chin, carefully keeping her fingers clear of the cut, and made him look at it again.

“Was this worth coming here with a loaded bow?”

“They don’t pay a charge to every woman who puts a rope across some steps.”

Vaska began to smile. Lyra felt the words settle in a pleasantly clear place inside her.

She took the recovered knife from the table and cut the prisoner’s sleeve above the elbow. He jerked, expecting the edge against his skin. She held him still and sliced away a broad strip of coat instead. On the inside was the company’s stitched mark.

“That goes back with you,” she said, setting it beside his feet. “Tell them what happened to the rest.”

“What happens to the rest?”

“Belis will cut it into cleaning cloths. He’s ruined a shoe.”

The man stared at her. Vaska laughed then, openly, and the sound filled the low room.

Lyra leaned closer to the captive. “You will pay the damage to the step. You will pay the surgeon for your men. You will pay my night crew. Then you can go home in the blanket. If your office won’t send the money, you can give me the address of someone who will.”

“They’ll remember this.”

“I’ve kept the instruction. It should help them.”

She left him with the crossbow laid beyond reach and went upstairs to change her wet clothes.

Morning made the damage look smaller and the work look larger.

There was a chip in the lower step, blood beneath the water jar, and a long scrape along Vaska’s boat. Rusk had gone with the surgeon, who expected him to live and refused to offer any opinion about whether he would learn. The prisoners’ payment arrived in a sealed bag. Lyra counted it in front of the messenger, found it short, and made him wait while she wrote out the missing sum.

Odel came while he was still there. She stood in the open gate, looking at the two unfamiliar boats.

“You had a busy night.”

“They arrived without booking.”

“Your agreement says you tell me when armed men pass the households.”

“They came by water.”

“They’ll leave on feet.”

Lyra glanced at the men waiting in blankets. One had bare calves beneath his borrowed covering and a face set against the morning’s indignities.

“They can leave by water too.”

Odel considered it. “That would be quieter.”

Vaska brought her own cup down from the loft. She had slept for less than an hour and looked irritatingly well in a clean shirt borrowed from one of her men. The blue silk hung over a rail in the sun.

“Do you charge for being looked at?” she asked Odel.

“Only when somebody makes it work.”

“I’ll stand somewhere easier.”

She moved to the table, where Belis was cutting bread. He had put his repaired shoe beneath a hanging lamp to dry and was telling Orrel that a workshop needed a floor above the wet line, a bench at the right height, and a partner willing to let tools remain where he put them.

“What does your partner say it needs?” Lyra asked.

“A customer.”

“That sounds promising.”

“Rima makes handles. She says I spend all day talking about the things that will go on them.”

Vaska took a piece of bread. “I’ve met worse uses for a mouth.”

Belis looked toward the men in blankets and grinned. He had been badly frightened in the dark; now he wanted to tell the story while everyone who might correct him was eating.

Lyra sat with them. The lower collection house sent the missing money. Odel arranged a passage for the prisoners back to their own company landing and stayed for breakfast at a rate she and Lyra disputed until the food arrived.

Vaska watched the cargo being brought into the shed. The silver lay beneath real lamp glass in the bottom of one crate. The dye was worth enough to make the night’s hiring worthwhile. Lyra had inspected it all and accepted a fee that did not depend on the collector’s opinion of her steps.

“You could use better men at night,” Vaska said after Odel left.

“I could use fewer guests with knives at their throats.”

“I was working on that.”

“You may leave the cargo until tomorrow. After that the charge doubles.”

Vaska nodded. “And if I bring more?”

“Tell me what it is before somebody tries to kill you over it.”

“That would spoil half the surprise.”

Lyra looked across the slip. The south ring held a working boat against the morning water. Above the shed, her own shutters stood open. No one had given her the place for behaving well. She had bought it, paid to make it stand, and been expected to surrender its use to a man with a short instruction and a knife.

Vaska followed her gaze.

“They’ll ask you to dinner now,” she said.

“Who?”

“People who can afford to pay other men for being rude.”

Lyra took the last piece of bread before Belis could reach it. He protested, and she split it with him. The sun had reached the lower platform. For the first time that morning she could feel it warming her hands.

Chapter 2 A table with a view

The invitation arrived with a basket of oranges and an apology for the hour at which it would require her to eat.

Daro Venn kept late hours, the note explained. He hoped Lyra would forgive the habits of a house whose boats disliked daylight. She read that sentence twice, amused despite herself, and looked for the request beneath it. There was none. A meal, a view of the harbor, and several people who wished to meet the owner of Harrow Slip.

Vaska took an orange without waiting to be offered one.

“He’ll feed you well.”

“You know him?”

“I owed him money once.”

“Only once?”

“He made it unpleasant enough to encourage originality.”

She peeled the fruit with a small knife, lifting the skin in one long strip. Lyra watched the clean motion and remembered the same hand in the boat.

“What does he want?”

“For you to stop making surprises in his harbor.”

“It isn’t his harbor.”

“Tell him over the duck. He likes an argument if the food’s hot.”

Four days had passed since the night cargo. Vaska’s goods had gone, her injured man had improved, and two of her people had hired ordinary work through Harrow without asking to be favored. Vaska herself remained in a room above a wine seller on the next street. She had paid for a week. Lyra had not asked whether she intended to renew it.

“You could come,” Lyra said.

“No. I couldn’t. Not if you want him to make the offer he means to make.”

“What offer?”

Vaska tasted the orange and made a pleased face at its sourness. “You’ll hear. Don’t agree before the sweet things. They bring them afterward so you think you’ve finished the business.”

Gannet House had blue tiles along its entrance passage and a man employed to make certain visitors noticed neither the chipped ones nor the knife beneath his coat.

He took Lyra’s cloak, asked whether she carried a weapon, and waited with a broad courtesy that made refusing to answer seem childish.

“A small knife.”

“For supper?”

“For things that need cutting.”

“Keep it, then. Master Venn dislikes making guests helpless before the soup.”

She smiled and followed him through the tiled passage. At its far end the house opened around an enclosed loading court. A hoist stood against one wall, with sacks beneath a dry awning and a wide watergate opposite the street entrance. A handcart could pass through that gate without taking off its load. Beyond, the quay ran along two bonded stores whose upper windows showed warm light.

It was a larger concern than Harrow by every measure she could see. More men. Better roofs. Room to leave an empty crate without having to move three others first.

The servant led her up a stair beside a cupboard full of folded linen. A woman coming down stopped to let them pass. She carried a little red thread case and had a pin between her lips.

“Your cuff,” she said around the pin.

Lyra looked at it. A loose thread hung from the place where the boat’s iron had caught her sleeve.

“It can wait.”

“It can tear. That’s different.”

The woman caught her wrist before Lyra had decided whether to permit it, examined the seam, and took the pin from her mouth. Her hands were dry and warm. There was a worn ring on the smallest finger, too loose for it until she turned it against the joint.

“Hold still. I won’t do you for more than the cloth.”

Lyra held still. The needle passed through the seam in two quick stitches. The woman tucked the loose end inside, drew it snug, and broke the thread with her teeth.

“There.”

“Your name?”

“Istre. You’ve come to eat, I hope. If you’ve come only to argue, he should have said so before I ordered the ducks.”

“Do people often come only to argue?”

“Some mistake it for an appetite.”

She continued down the stair, humming a few notes so clearly that Lyra turned after her. Istre had the easy, well-placed voice of somebody who could have filled a larger room and had chosen to use it on the stairs.

Above, Daro Venn came to meet her with his hands empty.

He was broader than the miniature portrait she had once seen on a customs surety, with dark hair beginning to gray at the ears and a beard trimmed close enough to show the shape of his mouth. He wore good black cloth, unadorned except for a gold fastening at the throat. When he smiled, the expression reached his eyes before she could decide whether to distrust it.

“You came without an escort.”

“I was invited to supper.”

“People have brought escorts for less.”

“Would you have fed them?”

“Of course. I would have resented it privately.”

He took her hand and released it before the greeting became a test of grip.

The dining gallery overlooked both the loading court and a strip of water beyond the roof of the opposite store. Lamps stood in niches high enough that no sleeve could overturn them. A low balcony rail carried a row of potted plants, most flourishing, one stubbornly dead. At the far end, a woman in dark red silk watched the harbor through a porcelain half-mask.

Serafine turned when they entered.

“Lyra. How well ownership suits you.”

“It has kept me occupied.”

“So I hear.”

She offered her cheek with a familiarity Lyra had not invited. Lyra let the greeting remain at a clasp of hands. Serafine accepted the change without making it an offense. Her exposed eye rested briefly on the repaired cuff.

“Istre has caught you.”

“Before I reached the table.”

“An efficient household,” Daro said. “Sit where you can see the boats. If I put you against the wall, you’ll spend the whole meal wondering what the rest of us are looking at.”

Odel arrived carrying nothing but her gloves. Lyra looked behind her.

“No chair?”

“I’ve eaten here before.”

Daro laughed and pulled out a place for her himself.

They began with a broth so clear Lyra suspected it of having cost more than the meat that followed. Daro spoke about a shipmaster who had brought a cargo of dyed silk through three inspections and then lost it to a leak from his own wine barrel. Odel knew the master and corrected the number of inspections. Serafine knew the buyer and corrected the number of barrels. Between them the misfortune became worse, until Lyra laughed into her cup and had to set it down.

No one mentioned the night at Harrow until the duck arrived.

“Your collector,” Daro said to Odel, “was telling me the men came away in blankets.”

“She has no collector at my landing,” Lyra said.

Odel lifted one finger. “I have a woman who notices things. She charges less than a collector because she does not carry money.”

“Then your woman who notices things,” Daro said, accepting the correction. “She was very precise about the blankets.”

“They were cold.”

Serafine’s mouth curved beneath the porcelain. “How considerate of you.”

“I included the blankets in the charge.”

Daro laughed again. He carved the duck, gave her a piece from the crispest side, and took the one nearest the joint for himself.

“He’ll forgive the injury,” he said. “Possibly even the money. The blankets will be difficult.”

“I don’t need his forgiveness.”

“No. You need boats to arrive without having their crews asked why they choose your steps.”

The duck smelled of pepper and bitter orange. Lyra cut a small piece before answering. She had been determined not to appear hungry. That determination had lasted until Istre put the plate down.

“Is this where you offer me boats?”

“This is where I ask whether the duck is to your liking.”

“It is.”

“Good. Istre dislikes being told afterward that somebody preferred fish. Now we can speak about boats.”

He described the work without a paper in front of him. Small cargoes delayed at the larger landings. Goods whose owners needed them moved quietly, without hiring the same crew that had previously sold their quantities to a rival. Repair space for lighters that could not wait through the main yards’ charges. A defined western run, with Harrow as its receiving point. He could supply regular cargo. The Court could bring customers who paid for discretion. Odel could negotiate a longer passage term and an answer from the Ragman before somebody made it necessary to negotiate in the middle of a loaded night.

Lyra ate while he spoke. Much of it was work she had tried to obtain one customer at a time. She asked about the hours, the boats’ draught, the price paid when a shipment failed to arrive. Daro answered readily and admitted what he did not know.

“You’d need to see the west store roof,” he said. “I’m told it is sound. The man who told me has never stood beneath it in rain.”

“Then why are you offering it?”

“Because a useful offer can survive a roof being worse than expected. I will pay to make it sound.”

Serafine set her cup down. “There would also be protection against certain inconveniences. Rival claims to your handling rights. Invitations from people who bring their own knives.”

“The people with knives seem able to find me without invitations.”

“Precisely.”

“Would the Prince sign?”

Daro’s smile thinned but remained. “He would be told where the arrangement stood. He understands which disagreements cost more than the goods.”

“He sent four men over a handling charge.”

“He sent them over a woman who struck his collector and took the goods through a different landing. You made the handling charge memorable.”

Lyra looked at Odel. She was eating the beans, unhurriedly, and gave no sign of disagreement.

“Do you think this would work?” Lyra asked her.

“I wouldn’t have come for the broth.”

“It was good broth.”

“It was small broth. Yes. I think it can work. The households get known traffic. You get known prices. The rest of us get fewer mornings devoted to somebody else’s wet men.”

Daro pushed the serving dish toward Lyra. She took more duck.

For a time they spoke of ordinary things. Serafine had dismissed a musician who would not stop teaching his pet bird to repeat the last word of a song. Daro regretted the dismissal and offered to buy the bird. Odel said he would regret that too. Istre came to replace a lamp wick and told him he already had enough creatures requiring more attention than they justified.

“How many am I allowed?” Daro asked.

“One fewer than you’ve got.”

He caught her hand briefly as she passed, affection unmistakable. She squeezed his fingers and continued with the lamp. Lyra watched them. She had not expected the touch to trouble her.

A sound rose from the court below: a man protesting, then a hard slap. Daro’s hand rested against his cup. He listened until a second voice answered.

“Excuse me.”

He went to the balcony rail. Lyra could see the court through the open arches. Two carriers stood beside a cask. A third man, better dressed, had one hand against his mouth.

Daro spoke down to them without raising his voice.

“What has he done?”

“Changed the measure,” one carrier said. “Then called me a thief.”

The better-dressed man began to protest. Daro waited until he stopped for breath.

“Did you alter the measure?”

“I was making allowance for—”

“Did you?”

“Yes, but—”

“Then pay the load at the original measure. Apologize for the name. We can discuss the slap in the morning, when neither of you has an audience.”

The man looked up at the gallery. He saw the guests and lowered his hand from his mouth.

Daro returned to the table.

“Your officer?” Lyra asked.

“My wife’s cousin. He finds new ways to make the relationship expensive.”

“You keep him.”

“He is good with the eastbound schedules. I have tried telling him that is not a qualification for every other task.”

There was no performance of regret. Daro served himself beans and asked Odel whether her woman had found a dry room for the widow from Rope Court. Odel said she had, but the stairs were wrong for the woman’s knee. They discussed two alternatives while Lyra finished eating.

The papers came with little cakes soaked in honey.

Vaska had been right about the sweet things. Lyra took one anyway. Daro cleared a space beside her plate and set down a draft with the partners’ names already written at the head. Harrow’s title would remain hers. The agreement concerned traffic, protection, and the additional western receiving rights. It promised a minimum flow of goods and a contribution toward the north cheek repairs. At the proposed rates she could build the covered workroom she wanted before another wet season.

Then she reached the passage about replacement.

“The receiving appointment can be reassigned.”

“Under the stated conditions,” Daro said.

“By two of the partners.”

“If the work stops or the place cannot safely receive.”

“Or if my successor is not approved.”

Serafine leaned forward. “Someone must be able to keep trade moving if you are absent.”

“How long absent?”

“That can be specified.”

“And if I sell?”

“Your ground is yours to sell,” Daro said. “Our cargo arrangements don’t pass with it unless the other parties accept the buyer.”

That was reasonable. Lyra disliked how reasonable it was. She turned the sheet and read the clause about work beyond the agreed district. A vote before new armed ventures. A limit on independent claims to routes used by the partners. She could make money, maintain her own house, employ willing crews, and be required to ask before making those things larger.

“What happens if I decline a vote?”

“The work continues under the terms you accepted,” Serafine said. “That is the attraction of terms.”

“Until two of you decide another person could carry it better.”

Daro set down his fork. “You are hearing a threat where I have offered an arrangement against threats.”

“I am reading the arrangement.”

“Yes. And you should. Change the conditions you dislike. Let us argue over them. You have built something worth including, Lyra. I would rather include it than spend the next five years discovering which small offense has turned into a broken boat.”

He spoke warmly. She believed him. He wanted her productive, secure enough to risk her own money, prosperous enough to keep others from taking what passed through her hands. He wanted to know how far she would go before she required his answer.

“I’ll take a copy,” she said.

“Of course.”

“And inspect the receiving rights.”

“I’ll send someone who knows more than the roof’s good intentions.”

Serafine reached across the table and turned the draft toward herself.

“There is no need to answer tonight.”

“I hadn’t intended to.”

“No,” Serafine said. “I don’t suppose you had.”

They returned to the cakes. Daro spoke about buying a boat whose previous owner had named it after a woman and then tried to charge extra to have the name removed. Odel asked whether he had bought the woman too. Daro said he had met her and decided the boat would be cheaper.

Lyra laughed with them. She could feel how easily this might become an evening she attended every month: good food, familiar insults, people who knew what her cargo cost and could make the difficult man at a gate become courteous. There would be a place for her coat, a cup brought before she asked, a silence when she entered that need not mean danger.

She took the copy home beneath her cloak.

Istre met her at the servants’ stair with a parcel of cakes.

“They’re better tomorrow if you keep them covered.”

“You needn’t pack food for me.”

“I packed it before you said that.”

Lyra accepted the parcel. The red thread case lay open on the linen cupboard. Beside it stood a stack of printed sheets bound in blue wrappers.

“Invitations?”

“Household books. Men here can remember who insulted them fifteen years ago and forget what they took out of the linen chest this morning.”

“Who makes them?”

“A little bindery off the dyers’ street. There’s a woman there who sews the folds properly now. Leda. Do you know her?”

“Yes.”

“She’ll be good if they let her do the work instead of fixing everybody else’s. I told her so. She looked at me as though I’d asked for a free copy.”

Lyra smiled. “She may have thought you had.”

“I paid before I complimented her. People are impossible to reassure.”

From below came the murmur of the departing carriers. Istre leaned over the rail and called to one of them to come back for the scarf he had forgotten. Her voice carried easily down the whole stair. The man returned, sheepish, and she looped the scarf around his neck herself before sending him away.

“Did the dinner go well?” she asked Lyra.

“The dinner did.”

“That is my part. Good night.”

Outside, the harbor air felt cold after the gallery. Odel walked with her as far as the turning.

“He means the offer,” she said.

“I know.”

“You could do worse than let it make you comfortable.”

“Comfort seems to occupy a great many clauses.”

Odel stopped beneath a lamp. “You own a place. That’s more than you had. An agreement about traffic doesn’t put you back in somebody else’s bedroom.”

Lyra looked at her sharply. Odel met the look without apology, then softened a little.

“I am speaking of the rooms you rented. Nothing else.”

“Then leave rooms out of it.”

“As you please.”

They parted without embracing. Lyra carried the cakes through streets that were quieter now, though every third doorway seemed to contain somebody who had not finished the night. A woman sang above a tavern. Two men argued over a borrowed belt. At Harrow, Belis sat on the platform with a lamp beside him, dressing a length of rope.

“Good meal?”

She gave him a cake. He bit it, closed his eyes, and said Rima would be furious if he did not bring one home.

“Take two.”

He took two and put them carefully aside.

Lyra went upstairs. She laid the proposed agreement on her table, opened the tool chest beneath the bed, and took out the oiled rubbing from the old chamber. The pale stone’s recessed pattern crossed the cloth in dark lines. She had followed those lines with her fingers often enough to remember where the impression broke.

There was a door down there that nobody at Daro’s table could explain. The Prince had paid for men and pumps to reach it. He had paid men to come onto her landing over a handful of crates. She set the rubbing beside the agreement and held down both corners against the night wind.

In the morning she would ask Mavet whether the old boarding could be removed without bringing the loose lintel down on them. Tonight she washed the honey from her fingers and ate another cake before putting the lamp out.

Chapter 3 The lower door

Corven would not take the job until she stopped calling it a look.

“A look is what you do from a doorway,” he said. “You’ve bought rope.”

“People fall while looking.”

“Then tell me how far you expect to fall.”

They sat in his workroom behind the cooper’s yard. The eel’s jar stood on the shelf with fresh water darkened by salt. Corven had fed it before she arrived. A fragment of fish remained near the glass, too large for the little creature to swallow and apparently too valuable to abandon.

Lyra laid Mavet’s drawing between them. She had brought the version with the doubtful places left blank. Corven put a finger on one of them.

“What’s that?”

“We don’t know.”

“You have a whole blank side.”

“It’s a very honest drawing.”

He smiled. She had missed the expression and was irritated with herself for having prepared for the visit as if it would be only difficult.

They agreed the work. One descent from the boarded outer inspection mouth beyond the western Sluice House. A survey of the existing chamber and the older face beyond the broken partition. Corven would watch the barred stair and help bring the crew out if the Prince’s people approached from the inner works. Nobody would enter a new flooded reach without setting another line and naming a return. He could stop the descent if an exit ceased to be usable.

“And the money?” he asked.

She named it. He shook his head, named another figure, and looked surprised when she accepted.

“I expected more argument.”

“I’ve been fed well. It has made me agreeable.”

“That sounds expensive.”

“It may become so.”

Lyra moved Mavet’s drawing clear of the shallow dish on Corven’s table and lowered her slate into the salt water. He covered the cuts with a strip of cloth. She traced the sequence beside them, stopped, and went back a place. He left the cloth where it was until she had finished.

When she lifted the slate, a pale crab clung to its underside, scarcely wider than her thumbnail.

“I put that back in the harbor.”

Corven fetched a little earthen pot. “Put it in here.”

He called it a thiasos as he pierced the lid. Lyra tipped the dish’s salt water into the pot and let the crab go. He showed her how to tie the cover down.

She told him about Daro’s offer while he checked the chain. He did not interrupt. The marked links lay across his knees, each one inspected before he let it pass through his hand.

“You could take it,” he said when she finished.

“I could.”

“Will this look decide?”

She glanced at the blank part of Mavet’s drawing.

“It may tell me how much he hasn’t offered.”

Corven put the chain away. “Bring ordinary wedges. Your little door won’t hold a roof up.”

“I know.”

“Bring them anyway.”

Belis brought the wedges in a bag with a new handle.

Rima had made it from a length of ash and shaped the underside to his fingers. He showed it to everyone before allowing it to carry anything dirty. Mavet tested the curve and pressed a thumbnail against the grain at its narrowest point.

“Good grip. Don’t pry against the stone with it; it’ll open here.”

“It isn’t a lever.”

“Pack the lever where you can reach it, then.”

Belis grinned and took the bag down to the water.

They left Harrow while the morning traffic still occupied the other end of the harbor. Orrel brought them in a narrow workboat that could nose close to the inspection mouth without scraping its gunwales. The boarded opening lay beneath a newer wall, low enough to escape the attention of anyone who looked only where a person could stand upright. Lyra had seen it in rain, in floodwater, and under the strange quiet after men had died nearby. In morning light it looked like a place where somebody had put unwanted timber.

Mavet went first. She tested each board before taking out its fastenings. The outer lintel had shed a small piece since their last visit. She put a timber support beneath it and made Orrel hold the far end while she drove the wedge.

“We bring that out last,” she said.

Orrel tied the boat where it could rise without pulling the line tight. He would remain at the mouth with a second lamp and the hauling rope. Belis checked the signal knots, then made Lyra pull each one while his eyes were shut.

“One pull is stop,” he said. “Two, take strain. Three, bring us out. Anything foolish and frantic means the same as three.”

“Not an invitation to pull us into the lintel,” Mavet added.

“I’ll put that in the foolish part.”

They crawled beneath the first lintel. Lyra felt the stone close above her back. Damp touched the lower part of her coat. She had to turn her head sideways beneath the second, and for a moment the lamp ahead showed nothing but Belis’s muddy heel.

Then the passage opened enough to stand.

The inspection chamber remained as they had left it. The barred stair rose toward the newer works. Water lay below the broken pipe. The partition’s breach showed the excavated platform beyond, pale spoil scattered beneath the three broad steps leading to the ancient face.

Corven went to the stair. He tried the bar gently, listened, and laid a folded cloth beneath the chain so the links would not sound against the stone. Lyra could hear distant machinery through the wall, ordinary pumps working under a different employer. No voices came near them.

Mavet crossed to the breach. Belis set the first line and passed the lamp through. The stone on the far side was colder than the chamber floor. Lyra felt it through her boot before she had put her full weight down.

The pale face waited above the steps. Bronze seats ran along its lower edge. Between them, narrow toothed rods vanished into the stone; one retained a little wheel whose teeth had worn bright on the same side. The dark inspection crystal sat deep in a threaded bronze bezel. It gave back no reflection of the lamp held toward it. When Belis moved, his shadow crossed the stone without crossing that surface at all.

He stopped.

“That’s unpleasant.”

“Which part?” Lyra asked.

“The part where I ought to be.”

Mavet knelt beside the nearest socket. She used a soft brush and a wooden scraper before putting metal against the ancient work. Lyra waited through the patient scratching, relieved and disappointed that nothing answered it.

The first useful discovery was a line that had looked like a crack.

Mavet cleaned it from one end to the other and found a fitted edge. Below the main sealed face, set aside from the broad steps, a smaller bronze cover lay almost flush with the stone. Its hinge was on the far side. A narrow stem passed through its lower corner into a seat somebody had damaged while trying to fit a replacement.

Lyra recognized the attempt before Mavet named it. The newer tool marks were shallow, crude beside the older work. The Prince’s men had enlarged the first part of the seat and stopped when the shoulder behind it refused the piece they wanted to insert.

“The collar,” Lyra said.

“The one from Zakov might pass the opening,” Mavet answered. “It wouldn’t sit behind it. I told you that.”

“I remember.”

“People remember measurements until something looks close.”

Belis held the lamp lower. Beneath the cover, through the narrow opening left by the damaged seat, Lyra could see the edge of a shutter and another bronze surface beyond it. There was a passage of some kind inside the wall. No water showed there.

“Can we see behind it?” she asked.

“After we know what’s pressing on it.”

Mavet touched the little stem with the wooden scraper. It moved a fraction. Somewhere inside, something answered with a sound like a cup touched beneath deep water.

Lyra felt it behind her breastbone.

Belis looked up sharply. Corven came to the breach.

“What was that?” he asked.

“We moved the stem.”

“Move it back.”

Mavet did. The sound stopped. Lyra waited for the sensation in her chest to fade. It remained longer than the sound had, drawing her attention inward toward the small places where breath waited before becoming movement.

She knew that feeling. She had practiced toward it alone, in a little frame above the ordinary harbor. This was not her working. It had no reason to stop where her hands stopped.

Mavet sat back on her heels.

“There is a bleed passage. Or what somebody used as one. A small opening should tell us whether the cover carries a load.”

Corven laid the retrieval rope beside her knee. “And if it goes somewhere else?”

“I close it. Nobody stands in front.”

Lyra looked at the cover’s sound edge. Contact with frame and face. A known direction, if the load came outward. She could hold a moving cover for the time it took Mavet to restore the stem. That was work she understood.

“We’ll keep clear of the face,” she said. “Belis at the stem from the side. Mavet watching the shutter. I take the cover. Corven stays where he can pull us back.”

Mavet frowned. “We don’t have to do it today.”

“No. We have the people and the exit today.”

“We also have a paid survey.”

“And a survey that ends at a dirty cover tells us less than we came for.”

Corven watched her across the lamp. Belis looked from one to the other, waiting. He had stopped making small jokes.

Lyra poured brine into her palm and touched the cover’s edge.

“One small opening,” she said. “If it moves, we close it. No further work.”

Mavet considered the seat, then took the smallest wedge from Belis’s bag.

“I set the limit. Nobody takes another turn because the first one looked promising.”

Lyra nodded.

They moved into place. Belis stood to the left of the cover, with his head close enough to hear the stem while turning it. Mavet told him where to stop and made him show the movement before he made it. Corven passed the safety rope behind Lyra’s waist and kept its end in his hand.

The first movement brought the deep cup sound again.

A line of water appeared beneath the bronze edge. It rose rather than fell, collecting along the top of the cover in a bright bead. Lyra watched it with a fascination that delayed her understanding by one breath.

“Back,” she said.

Belis began to turn the stem.

The bead vanished. Something struck the room without crossing the space between.

Lyra’s teeth met. Her sight went narrow and bright. Belis dropped the tool and fell against the wall, one hand clamped over his left ear. Blood ran between his fingers. The lamp hit the step and went out.

For an instant there was only Corven’s light at the breach and the enormous pressure of a place too deep to exist beneath this much stone.

The cover lifted.

Lyra caught it with both hands. Brine slicked her palms. She felt the hinge take the load, the fixed edge against her left fingers, the moving bronze beneath her right. Outward. The force was outward. She held that knowledge long enough for the words to find their place in her throat.

The cover stopped moving.

“Shutter,” she said.

Mavet was already reaching for the stem. She could not see it from where she stood. Corven lifted his lamp, exposing himself in the breach, and light fell across the torn seat.

Belis had slid to the floor. He was trying to rise, and each attempt turned him toward the stone. His open mouth made no sound Lyra could hear. She did not know whether the room had gone silent or her own hearing had failed.

Mavet turned the stem. Nothing changed.

“Farther,” Lyra said.

Mavet shook her head violently. The stem had reached the stop. She took the wooden scraper, drove it into the gap beside the inner shutter, and struck its end with the heel of her hand.

The pressure shifted. Lyra’s right arm began to open under the strain.

Corven caught the back of her coat and held her upright. He was speaking. She saw his mouth move and heard only a thin ringing sound, high enough to hurt.

Mavet struck again. The scraper broke. The shutter moved sideways by the width of its broken end.

The cover fell against its seat.

Lyra released the working before the change of force tore her hands from it. She went to her knees. The ordinary room returned in pieces: Corven swearing, water dripping from the pipe, Belis making a choking sound as he tried to breathe and vomit at once.

Mavet thrust a wedge into the stem’s stop and tied it there with cord. Then she went to Belis.

He pushed her away.

“Hold his shoulder,” she told Lyra. “He can’t tell where the floor is.”

They could not carry him through the breach upright.

Belis fought when they tried to lift him. His good hand gripped the edge of the stone until a nail tore. He kept saying something Lyra could not understand, the words flattened by panic and the noise inside his head.

“Look at me,” she said.

His eyes passed her face and went on moving.

“Belis. Look at my hand.”

She held it on his right side, where he seemed able to follow the sound. His gaze caught briefly. She made herself speak slowly.

“We’re going through the wall. Corven has your shoulders. I’ll hold the legs. Mavet goes ahead. You need to let go.”

He shook his head. Blood ran down his neck.

Lyra put her hand over his and began opening his fingers one at a time. He screamed when the last one came free. She had no way to make the next part kind. They passed him through the breach while Mavet steadied his head. His heel struck the stone; he tried to twist away, and Corven took the whole of his weight against the healed scar on his side.

“Keep going,” Corven said through his teeth.

They laid Belis on a board in the inspection chamber. The upper stair remained barred. From beyond it came a voice.

“What broke?”

Corven raised his head.

Another voice answered, farther away. They had heard the pulse, or the falling lamp, or Belis. It no longer mattered which.

Lyra took the three-pull rope and hauled. Orrel answered from the mouth. Mavet crawled into the low passage first, carrying the forward end of the board. Lyra followed beside Belis’s shoulder. Corven stayed at the rear with the chain unwrapped.

The first lintel caught the board’s corner. Mavet backed it out, changed the angle, and tried again. Belis began to vomit. Lyra turned his head before he could choke, keeping her own body low enough to avoid the stone. His hair dragged through the water beside the board.

There was no room to wash him. No room to put her hands where the old healing might ease something. She could keep his mouth clear and make certain his head did not strike the lintel again.

Behind them the bar on the upper stair moved in its brackets.

Corven set the cracked shell against the stone. Lyra heard one man’s voice stop in the middle of a word. The silence did nothing to stop the second man shouting for a hammer.

“Go,” Corven said.

Orrel took the board at the outer mouth. Daylight struck Belis’s face. He closed his eyes and cried out again, a small animal sound that made Lyra want to go back into the stone and break whatever had done it.

Instead she climbed into the boat and helped lower him between the seats.

Mavet came out carrying the tool bag. Corven followed last, his chain wet and one sleeve torn where the passage had caught it. They left the lost lamp beyond the breach. They left the wooden scraper broken in the shutter. Mavet pushed two boards across the outer mouth without pretending they would stop determined men.

Orrel shoved away. The boat moved into the lower harbor. The sun was bright on everything.

The healer examined Belis in a room above a sailmaker’s yard because it had a bed and someone willing to keep people from crowding it.

Rima arrived before the examination was finished. She was small and solidly built, with a shaving of wood caught in the fold of her collar. She had been working when the messenger found her. She stood beside the bed, watching Belis stare at a point that would not remain still.

“Left side?” she asked.

The healer nodded.

“He says I’m moving.”

“He feels the room moving.”

“I know where I’m standing.”

“I know you do.”

Rima looked at Lyra. The question was in her face before she spoke it.

“I told them to test the little cover,” Lyra said. “There was more behind it than we understood.”

“Did he turn it wrong?”

“No.”

“Did he fail to come when you called?”

“No.”

Rima nodded once. She sat on the right side of the bed and took Belis’s hand. He recognized her touch, or her position, or the sound that reached his good ear. His fingers closed around hers.

Lyra stayed until the healer had done what could be done that afternoon. She fetched water, paid for the room, and held a basin without making an instruction out of every movement. The hearing on the left did not return when the ringing eased. Belis could hear Rima from one side and not the other. When he tried to sit, the room threw him sideways.

“He won’t dive again soon,” the healer said outside.

“How soon?”

“I don’t know that he will.”

Lyra looked at the closed door. From within she could hear Belis asking Rima to stop moving. Rima said she would stay where she was.

Mavet waited on the stair with something wrapped in cloth.

“It was loose beside the lower seat,” she said. “I picked it up before we moved the stem.”

Inside lay a broken bronze shim, curved like half a small moon. Its edge carried the same paired cuts as one mark on Lyra’s rubbing. It had once seated a component closely enough to leave a bright inner band that the years had not entirely dulled.

“Is it useful?” Lyra asked.

“For understanding what fits. Perhaps.”

She wrapped it again and put it in Lyra’s hand.

“The outer shutter is closed. I tied the stem against its stop. We don’t touch that seat again until we can isolate what feeds it from farther in.”

“You know there is a way farther in?”

“I know the shutter moved across the line. I saw another seat behind it. Somebody built it to come apart in a sequence. We found the wrong beginning.”

Lyra closed her fingers around the cloth. Below them, Corven was paying the boatman from his own fee. She went down to stop him, then remembered that she had already agreed his expenses were his to settle and hers to reimburse.

He looked up as she reached him.

“Tomorrow?” he asked.

She looked at the blood drying on his torn sleeve.

“No.”

He nodded and put the last coin into Orrel’s hand.

Lyra climbed the stairs again. Rima had moved the lamp behind a cloth so Belis did not have to look at it. He lay with his eyes open, listening from the side of the room where his partner sat. Lyra took the empty basin away and brought back clean water.

Chapter 4 Those who stayed

The knife on Tesset’s table belonged to a woman who wanted a parcel opened.

Tesset had spent a considerable moment believing otherwise. She stood in the doorway of the little counting room, looking at the blade beside her name, while the woman waited beneath the window with an expression that suggested the delay was becoming personal.

“The string’s too tight,” the woman said. “And your man outside told me not to put it on the floor.”

Tesset set down her bag. “It’s a kitchen knife.”

“It cuts string as well.”

“I meant I thought—”

She stopped. The woman had already begun explaining the parcel, which contained printed wrappers for lamp oil and had been delivered to the wrong landing twice. The knife was clean. The woman was annoyed. No one had come to announce anything about Lyra.

Tesset cut the string, checked the wrappers, and gave them back. After the woman left, she sat with the knife still on the table. She was angry at herself for the fright and more angry at the way she had begun carrying other people’s danger into a room she had chosen precisely because it was her work.

Sera arrived with the boat returns and noticed the blade.

“Yours?”

“Apparently now.”

“Keep it off the returns. One came back with fish scales on it last week.”

Tesset laughed, a little too hard. Sera waited until she had finished before asking whether the extra receiving charges had been posted.

They worked through the morning. Three merchants wanted a special rate that would become ordinary only for them. A master refused to pay for waiting time because he said his crew had enjoyed sitting down. Tesset sent him to ask the crew whether enjoyment constituted their wages. He came back without the argument and with the wrong sum.

By noon she was herself again. Hungry, impatient, good at the part of the day she knew how to do. Sera had agreed to take the afternoon inquiries so she could go to Belis.

“Do you know what to bring?” Sera asked.

“Rima said nothing that needs eating before tomorrow.”

“That’s an answer from somebody who has been given six suppers.”

“Seven. One of them was a duck.”

“From Lyra?”

“From someone who thinks Lyra’s injured people eat more expensively.”

Sera’s expression changed. “How is he?”

“I’ll tell you when I’ve seen him.”

Tesset wrapped the kitchen knife in the unwanted string and put it on the shelf where the parcel woman could find it if she remembered to come back.

Belis disliked the bed because it moved even when Rima proved it did not.

She showed Tesset the blocks beneath its feet, the brace against the wall, and the little wedge a visiting carpenter had added before admitting that he had not understood the problem. Belis watched from the pillow with one eye closed.

“Ask her to move it to a different sea,” he said.

Tesset went to his right side. Rima had written that instruction on a paper beside the door. There were several others: speak before touching, no sudden lifting, no lamp directly in his eyes. At the bottom Belis had added, in an uncertain hand, NO MORE DUCK.

“I’ve brought work,” Tesset said.

His open eye moved toward her.

“For Rima. If she wants it.”

Rima looked surprised. Tesset took out the bundle of plain wooden tags ordered for the landing stores. Their edges needed smoothing and small grips bored through the ends so wet fingers could lift them. Rima had said once that people remembered what she made only when they needed the handle of a tool they had broken.

“Can you do these?”

“Here?”

“If you can. If you can’t, I’ll take them away and find a less foolish thing to ask.”

Rima inspected one. Belis reached for it, missed, and knocked two onto the floor.

For a moment all three were still.

“Pick them up,” he said. “They’re wood. They don’t bruise.”

Tesset did. She put one into his open hand. He felt the edge with his thumb, then turned it over to examine the grain.

“That one will split if you bore there.”

Rima bent over it. “Yes. We can use the other end.”

They began discussing the size of the grip. Tesset sat on a stool and let them. Belis grew animated enough to raise his head from the pillow. The movement stopped him. He closed both eyes, took a breath through his nose, and waited.

Rima put the tag on the cover beside his hand without trying to make him look again.

“How long?” Tesset asked quietly.

“The healer says the left hearing won’t come back.”

Belis opened his eyes. “I heard that. You don’t have to make your voice kind around it.”

Tesset felt her face warm. “I wasn’t sure whether you’d want to talk about it.”

“I don’t. That doesn’t improve if other people whisper.”

Rima stood and took the basin to the door. Tesset watched her leave, wishing she would stay and tell her which ordinary words were permitted.

Belis looked toward the window. A sail hung outside, cutting the sunlight into moving strips. The yard below smelled of hemp and hot pitch.

“They told me to keep still,” he said. “I can manage that. Then they say I ought to try sitting. I can manage a little of that too. Then somebody says it looks better. Usually when they’re about to leave.”

Tesset leaned forward. “I don’t know whether it looks better. I haven’t seen you before today.”

He turned his face toward her. “You used to work for her.”

“Yes.”

“Was she always in a hurry?”

“Not always.”

“That’s a carefully useless answer.”

Tesset smiled despite herself. “She can wait a long time when she wants the thing on the other side of it.”

Belis considered this. From the yard came the thump of a sail rolled onto a table. He flinched, then looked angry at having done so.

“She told Rima it was her instruction,” he said.

“Yes.”

“She told everyone. The healer, the boatman, a woman who came with broth and didn’t know which room was ours. As though that could make it stay in her body instead.”

Tesset had no answer for him. He seemed grateful that she did not find one immediately.

“Will you take me to the window?” he asked.

“Can I?”

“Help me sit first. Slowly. If I say down, put me down. Don’t ask whether I’m certain.”

She followed his directions. One hand behind the shoulders, the other available for him to grip. The weight was awkward. He was larger than she had remembered from the landing. When his feet reached the floor, he bent forward and held both sides of the bed.

They waited.

Rima returned and found them there. She put down the basin without speaking. Together they helped him stand. He could take a step if he looked at the fixed line between two floorboards. A second step brought him close enough to the window to feel air against his face.

He sat in the chair beneath it with a force that frightened Tesset, then leaned back and smiled.

“That’s better.”

Rima laughed, sudden and relieved. She crouched beside him and put her forehead against his hand. He laid his fingers on her hair and left them there.

Tesset went to fetch the tags from the bed. They could do nothing about the left ear. They could put the work on a table he could reach from the chair. Rima found a board, Tesset fetched two boxes, and Belis told them exactly why both boxes were the wrong height until they had made something he could use.

At Ada’s washing yard, Jorin had stolen a pear.

Ada used the word with a pleasure Tesset could hear before she understood why. The pear had been set on the shelf beside the clean cups, out of the way of a bucket. When she turned, it was gone. She found it in Jorin’s basket, beneath a cloth he had pushed over it with the same painstaking care he gave to things nobody else would think to move.

“He didn’t eat it?” Tesset asked.

“He bit it. Then put it away.”

Jorin sat in the shade beneath the wall. His salt-pale fingers rested on the basket’s rim. The cloth concealed the pear badly enough that one curved yellow side remained visible.

Tesset glanced at the yellow curve beneath the cloth. “Can you take it back?”

“I haven’t tried. I wanted you to see.”

Tesset looked at Ada. She was smiling. Nothing in her face asked Tesset to turn the small theft into a promise about what might return next.

“It’s a good pear,” Tesset said.

“It was mine.”

“Then you should guard the next one.”

Ada laughed. Jorin turned his head toward the sound. He watched her for a moment, then drew the basket closer with his foot.

They ate the remaining fruit in the yard while a washing line creaked above them. Ada had put two cups where Jorin could reach them and learned that he preferred the one with the broader handle on some days and neither on others. The woman who sat with him had taken him as far as the shaded lane. He had stopped at the sound of a cart and remained there until Ada came, but on the return he had chosen the correct doorway himself.

“Or followed the bucket,” Ada said. “I was carrying the blue one.”

“You don’t have to undo every good thing before telling it.”

Ada looked down at her cup. “I don’t want people telling me he’s ready to work.”

Tesset remembered the oil merchant at the rope loft, deciding that Jast’s attempt to stand ought to lower the cost of his care. She set her cup down.

“I won’t.”

“I know.”

A man called from the next yard for help with a line. Ada told him to wait until she had finished eating. She had once got up at every request because it was easier than explaining why Jorin needed her near. Now the neighbor knew which gate to knock on and how long she meant by wait.

When she went, Tesset remained with Jorin. He watched her hand as she moved a cup away from the edge. She put it down and let him see where it had gone. After a while he lifted the cloth from his basket, inspected the pear, and covered it again.

Tesset found herself smiling alone in the yard.

On her way home she stopped at the rope loft to leave the new care shifts. Hessa was seated beside the window with her shallow bowl. The attendant had wrapped a loose strand around the basket handle instead of cutting it away, and Hessa kept touching the new thickness, deciding something nobody else could hear. Jast stood between two supports while the healer watched his injured leg. He was on his feet for less than a minute. When he sat, the attendant gave him water and did not ask him to try again for the visitor.

Tesset left the papers where the woman could read them after the work. She went out before making the room wait on her interest.

Leda had borrowed Tesset’s table for the afternoon and filled it with a book that could not yet be closed.

Two folded gatherings stood beneath small weights. A length of thread ran from the nearer one to a needle stuck carefully in a scrap of cloth. Beside them, Istre sat with her sleeves rolled back, holding a strip of linen against the light.

Tesset stopped in the doorway.

“I said I’d bring somebody to see the sewing,” Leda said.

“You said somebody might come by.”

“She has.”

Istre lowered the linen. “I can go if I’m in the way.”

“No,” Tesset said. “You aren’t.”

She put the supper parcel on the mantel, which was the only place still available. Leda rose to kiss her. There was paste on one cheek and a line of thread caught around her wrist. Tesset wanted to pull her into the room’s small privacy, then remembered that Istre was sitting three feet away and settled for touching her waist.

“I’ve brought fish.”

“We’ll finish this edge.”

“Will the fish agree to wait?”

“It can do as it likes. We are finishing the edge.”

Istre laughed. Her voice was low when she laughed, warmer than Tesset had expected from the severity with which she examined the linen.

They cleared half the table and ate while the loose book remained on the other half. Istre had brought a little pot of pickled roots from Gannet House. Daro’s cook, she said, put too much pepper into them unless watched. Leda liked the pepper. Tesset liked watching the argument become an excuse for both women to take more.

“I’ve met your former employer,” Istre said.

“Many people have.”

“She came to dinner. Good manners when she remembered them. I mended her cuff.”

“Did she let you?”

“I didn’t ask in time for it to become a matter of government.”

Tesset smiled, then looked at Leda. She knew the expression that came over her own face whenever Lyra entered a conversation. Leda saw it too and did not rescue her from it.

“Was it a good dinner?” Tesset asked.

“The food was. They carried on afterward with papers. I left them to it.”

“Gannet has been ordering more books,” Leda said.

“Because the last lot were good.” Istre took the unfinished gathering and showed Tesset the thread. “See how that lies? The first ones stood up so you could feel every stitch. Now you can turn a page without being reminded that somebody made it.”

Leda looked down at her plate. Tesset knew she was pleased. She reached beneath the table and put her hand on Leda’s knee.

Istre pretended not to notice with such completeness that Tesset almost laughed.

After supper they finished the edge. Istre’s own sewing was quicker, but she did not take the work from Leda when she hesitated. She held the linen, waited for the needle, and once moved the lamp so Leda could see without bending her neck.

Tesset washed the dishes in the little basin. The window was open to the courtyard. The dog upstairs gave one indignant bark, and a man’s voice answered it with the familiar weariness of an old disagreement.

Istre hummed while Leda worked. The sound filled the room without demanding attention. Tesset dried a plate slowly, listening.

“You should sing more loudly,” Leda said.

“Then you’d stop to listen, and we’d never finish.”

“I can sew and listen.”

“Not to that song. It’s a filthy one.”

Leda looked up, delighted. Istre sang the next line quietly enough that Tesset had to lean nearer to hear it. The rhyme was worse than she expected. She laughed with a wet plate in her hands and nearly dropped it.

They finished the book’s first section before Istre left. She packed the red thread case and took two spoiled sheets for the household drawers. Leda walked her to the stair. Tesset heard them making an arrangement about the next order, the date of delivery, and which of Daro’s young men could be trusted to carry anything without bending it beneath his elbow.

When Leda returned, she stood with her back against the closed door.

“She’s good to me.”

“I can see.”

“Some people come to inspect until you’ve made a mistake they can be useful about. She comes to see what I can do.”

Tesset crossed the room and kissed the place where paste had dried on her cheek.

“I’d like to see more of that too.”

“You’ve been away all day.”

“I know.”

She told Leda about Belis, about the chair by the window, about Jorin’s pear. Leda listened while undoing her hair. When Tesset came to the part where Belis had asked whether Lyra was always in a hurry, Leda stopped moving.

“What did you tell him?”

“Something true that didn’t help.”

“Those are plentiful.”

Tesset leaned against the table. “I wanted to make him feel better about having gone with her. I heard myself preparing to do it.”

“Did you?”

“No.”

“Then sit down.”

They sat on the bed with the book between them. Leda showed her where the stitching had begun to improve. Tesset could see the difference now. Her finger followed the fold without touching it, and Leda moved the candle closer so she could see the small places she was proud of.

Later they put the book beneath its weights and made room for the two cups on the table. Leda stayed. Tesset had not needed to ask twice, and in the dark she let herself be pleased about that without trying to arrange the following week.

Chapter 5 A man from Zakov

Rima put the money on Lyra’s table in the little paper it had come in.

“He said it was for the trouble.”

“Belis’s?”

“Mine. Belis wouldn’t speak to him.”

Lyra counted without touching the coins. Enough for the room and treatment through another interval, with something left for food. An amount chosen by a person who had asked what those things cost.

“What did he want?”

“To know where Belis stood. Which side he turned toward. Whether the sound came before the water.”

“Did Belis tell him?”

“He kept speaking into the wrong ear. Belis said if he wanted an answer he could come round where people lived. The man didn’t like that.”

“His name?”

“He didn’t give one. Good boots. Very clean. He had somebody waiting in the yard.”

Rima folded the paper over the money again. Her hands were steady, but she had wrapped one finger in cloth where a carving blade had slipped.

“Keep it,” Lyra said.

“No.”

“He came to disturb you. You can charge for that.”

“He came to buy something I don’t want him to have.”

Lyra looked up. Rima met her eyes without waiting to be praised for the distinction.

“I’ll find him,” Lyra said.

“Then tell him not to come back. That is the part I asked for.”

After she left, Lyra took out the wrapped bronze shim. She set it beside the little parcel of money and thought about the questions. Where Belis stood. Which side of the face had answered. Somebody knew enough to understand that a man could be injured without the room flooding.

Revet had an order for a collar of exactly the wrong shape.

He did not show Lyra when she first asked. He stood behind his bench with the familiar resentment in his face and told her that customers’ drawings were their own concern.

“You’ve changed your mind about that before,” he added.

Lyra looked toward the back room. Ansel was out. She was glad not to have him there while his brother made her hear the sentence.

“I’ve come to buy a comparison.”

“Then bring your own drawing.”

She laid Mavet’s copy on the bench. The dimensions were plain. The shape beneath them had made less sense to Lyra until she saw Revet recognize it.

“You’ve been asked for something like this.”

He did not answer.

“Mine wouldn’t fit the seat. The outer diameter is right. The shoulder isn’t.”

Revet’s eyes went to the shaded part of the drawing. His hand moved, almost involuntarily, toward a rolled sheet beside the tools.

“Whoever brought you the other shape is going to tell you it’s your casting when it won’t sit,” Lyra said. “Charge him for the work. Charge me to know whether you’ve been given the same mistake.”

“I don’t need advice about my customers.”

“No. You need a fee from this one.”

She put money beside the drawing, less than Rima had returned and enough to make the distinction visible. Revet looked at it for a long time. Then he opened the roll.

The shoulder was wrong in the same place. Two narrow rings had been added to the original profile in a newer hand. At the bottom stood a name, written without ornament: Iven Sarr. Beneath it was a delivery instruction to a warehouse leased by one of the Prince’s companies.

“Where is he from?” Lyra asked.

“Zakov. He wanted local bronze because the salt on the thing he brought had eaten through its sleeve.”

“Did he bring the thing?”

“A broken example. He took it away.”

“Can you send him a message?”

Revet rolled the sheet closed. “I haven’t agreed to become one of your men.”

“Tell him somebody has a loose seating piece from the old works. The marks match his drawing. They’ll sell, but they won’t come to the company’s warehouse.”

“Who is somebody?”

“Put my name on it.”

Revet stared. She could almost hear him rearranging the encounter, deciding whether it had become safer or worse.

“And when he asks why you came here?”

“Say I paid for a comparison. You can keep the money.”

He took it after she had turned to leave.

Vaska inspected the old candle-packing house and asked who had chosen it.

“I did,” Lyra said.

“Of course.”

The room opened onto a narrow landing used by boats that supplied wax and lamp oil. A street door stood opposite, with a smaller side window above the water. Empty racks covered one wall. The floor smelled of tallow even after years of other cargo. Lyra had hired it for an afternoon from a woman who cared only that nothing capable of staining the boards should be unloaded without a separate price.

Vaska opened the street door, shut it, and checked the hinges.

“Sound. That’s useful.”

“For what?”

“For being a door when somebody hits it.”

She moved one rack away from the side window and ordered two of her people to wait in the empty boat shed beyond it. A third went to the street corner. They had been paid for the day before she gave the instructions. Lyra noticed the sequence.

“How many will he bring?” she asked.

“More than you want and fewer than he trusts.”

“You’re being very reassuring.”

“You could ask him nicely and believe the answer. It would save the hire.”

Lyra set the wrapped shim on the table. Beside it she put a copy of the relevant section of the rubbing, weighted with the wrong-shouldered collar recovered from the previous year’s salvage. The collar had never been fitted. The metal looked ordinary in daylight, and that made the depth of trouble surrounding it more irritating.

Vaska touched the broken shoulder.

“Is that worth killing for?”

“Somebody thinks it may be.”

“Then don’t sit with your back to the water.”

Lyra moved her chair.

The buyer came before the agreed hour. A low boat brought him to the landing with two men aboard and a third standing at the bow. The standing man was Worn. Fine cracks ran across his neck and disappeared beneath the collar. When he stepped onto the stone, his joints made a dry series of clicks.

“Iven Sarr?” Lyra asked the man in the good boots.

He nodded. He was clean-shaven and younger than she had expected, with pale lines around his eyes where the weather had failed to reach. His coat had been brushed recently. A narrow case hung from his hand.

“You said you had a seating piece.”

“I said somebody did.”

“You put your name beneath it.”

“I find people arrive more promptly.”

The Worn came in behind him. Iven looked back.

“Wait outside, Heth.”

“The case first,” Heth said.

His voice was dry and ordinary enough to unsettle Lyra more than a groan would have. He held out his hand. Iven gave him the narrow case. Heth set it on the table, turned it so its clasp faced the buyer, then stepped back toward the landing.

“Your return is paid,” Iven told him.

“The boat’s return. Mine was another amount.”

“I remember.”

“Good.”

Heth went outside. Iven opened the case. Inside, on fitted cloth, lay a broken bronze throat sleeve and two sheets protected between thin boards. The sleeve bore old salt in a groove too narrow to scrape clean. Its surviving shoulder matched the shape on Revet’s drawing.

Lyra unwrapped the half-moon shim.

Iven forgot her for a moment. He bent toward it, turning his head to put the light along the inner curve. She watched his attention find the paired cuts.

“May I?”

“Put your example beside it.”

He did. The two pieces did not join. They had never been parts of one object. But the shim’s inner curve corresponded closely to the band on his sleeve, and a small squared recess appeared in both at the same distance from the edge. Mavet had measured that recess twice.

Iven took out the first protected sheet. A drawing showed a second arrangement of seats, larger in one dimension, almost identical in another. Along its side somebody had marked three failed attempts to fit a replacement. The fourth space remained blank.

“Where was this made?” Lyra asked.

“Near the northern coast.”

“Of Zakov.”

“Yes.”

“And you believe something here will fit it.”

“Something here may tell us what should fit.”

He had recovered his caution. He put one finger on the drawing, covering a note near its lower edge. Lyra could still read two words: return member.

“What does it return?”

Iven looked at her.

“The thing you opened,” he said. “Did it sound before it moved?”

“You’ve asked that already. You chose the wrong man to pay.”

“Your worker needs treatment.”

“He needs you to stay away from his room.”

“I wanted his description.”

“Then you should have asked him where he could hear you.”

Outside, a boat knocked against the landing. Iven’s gaze moved toward the door and returned too quickly. Lyra heard a shoe drag beyond the street entrance.

Vaska had gone out through the side window ten minutes before he arrived. Lyra could not see her now.

“You have the piece,” Iven said. “I can give you a price.”

“And I can give you a question.”

“I haven’t been sent to answer your questions.”

“No. You were sent to take the piece and discover whether I kept a copy of the marks.”

She had guessed the second part. His expression did not confirm it. The man entering through the street door did.

He came in with a knife low against his leg, the blade hidden until his hand rose. Lyra pushed the table toward him and stood. Iven snatched for the shim. She caught the protected drawing instead, leaving him the piece he had come for, and stepped behind the empty rack.

The knife man vaulted the table. He was good. He landed without stumbling and cut toward her left hand, where the drawing would make her reluctant to let go. She threw the drawing into the gap between two racks and caught his wrist with both hands.

His strength surprised her. He drove her backward until the rack struck the wall. The knife edge touched the skin beneath her jaw. She could smell onions on his breath, and a sweetness from the oil he used on his hair.

Lyra brought her knee up between them and missed his groin. He laughed against her face.

She put her thumb into his eye.

He stopped laughing. She pressed until his weight changed, then turned his wrist over the rack’s iron corner. The knife fell. She caught his coat and pulled him face-first into the timber.

At the landing door, Vaska struck the second man as he came in. The blow took him in the throat. He fell against Iven, and the narrow case hit the floor, scattering its carefully packed pieces.

The street door began opening again.

Lyra seized the brine bottle from her belt, dashed it over the jamb, and kicked the door shut. She put her left palm against the frame and her right against its moving face. The man outside hit it with his shoulder.

Inward. Sound timber. Contact.
