The pressure settled through her hands. She could hear Vaska fighting behind her and Iven telling someone to stop. The man with the injured eye had found his knife again. Lyra saw him turn in the edge of her vision.

“Vaska.”

“I see him.”

The next sound was brief and wet. A weight struck the floor. Lyra kept holding the door.

“Five breaths,” she said.

“You’ve had four.”

Vaska swept the papers and bronze into the case. She caught Iven by the collar and drove him toward the side window. He resisted until she put the knife against his lower back.

“Your boots can be repaired,” she told him. “Climb.”

Lyra released the working and stepped aside. The door burst inward. She swung the empty rack across it, catching the first man at the knees, and followed Vaska through the window before he could rise.

The boat below rocked when she landed. Pain ran through her shoulder. Vaska’s people shoved away. Somebody on the landing raised a bow, then lowered it when Vaska pulled Iven upright in front of her.

Heth stood beside the first boat, looking at the packing house. He had not joined the fight.

“The return,” he called to Iven.

Iven stared at him.

“Still owed,” Heth said.

Vaska laughed once and pushed the captive down between the seats. The boats separated. Lyra kept her eyes on the bowman until the curve of the wall took him out of sight.

Iven’s good boots had not survived the escape unmarked. He looked at the scrape across one toe while Lyra emptied his case onto the table at Harrow.

Vaska had bound his wrists in front of him, leaving him enough movement to drink. One of her men stood at the door. She had lost nobody. The knife man with the injured eye had died in the packing house; the man struck in the throat had been breathing when they left him.

Lyra set the bronze sleeve beside the shim and the old collar. Mavet came when the runner fetched her and looked at the three objects without touching them.

“Where did that come from?”

“Ask him.”

Iven lifted his head.

“The northern work,” he said. “In Zakov.”

“Which work?”

“The one that has been trying to destroy every replacement fitted to its receiving seat.”

Mavet looked at the drawings. The first sheet showed the northern apparatus. The second contained correspondence, folded twice and annotated in the margins. Lyra read the request aloud: measurements from the southern survivor, comparison of the return throat, no commitment to remove until the isolation sequence could be established.

There was an older letter beneath it. The date preceded Lyra’s first work at the Opera House. It spoke of reports from Silkstrand and the cost of keeping an excavation open through ordinary harbor repairs. A familiar company mark closed the page. The same mark had accompanied the expensive pumps and the collection order at Harrow.

“You aren’t buying salvage to sell here,” Lyra said.

“No.”

“You’re comparing this chamber to a broken one at home.”

“Yes.”

“What does the Prince expect to gain?”

Iven looked at Vaska. She was washing blood from her knife into a bowl, taking care not to splash the table.

“The ability to keep the northern work from consuming what is put into it.”

“Consuming?” Mavet asked.

“Crushing. Breaking. Taking the joints apart while the outer vessel appears empty. Choose the word that makes you most cautious.”

Lyra remembered the water bead climbing the cover. Belis’s hand against his ear. She put the letter down.

“Can it be repaired?”

“We don’t know.”

“Does he?”

“No. He believes the return member here may be intact. He has paid to continue believing it.”

Mavet turned the northern drawing beside her own. The paired recesses matched. The shoulder did not. She put a finger at the narrow line between the two sections.

“This is a separate isolation shutter.”

Iven nodded.

“You knew it fed the small cover.”

“I knew that one did. I haven’t seen yours.”

“Would you have opened the bleed first?”

“No.”

He said it quietly. Lyra felt her hands close against the edge of the table.

Vaska stopped washing the knife.

“Mind the next question,” she said to Lyra. “You want him able to answer it.”

Lyra looked at her, angry enough to welcome a quarrel. Vaska held her gaze. At last Lyra released the table.

“You tried to take my papers.”

“I was told to recover any copies offered with the piece.”

“By killing me?”

“I was told you might not be willing to sell.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“No.” He looked at the knife now lying clean beside Vaska’s hand. “It isn’t.”

Mavet rolled the two drawings together, then separated them again so their edges would not be mistaken later. She asked Iven about the order of the shutters. He answered only where the drawing showed enough to support it. In three places he said he did not know. She marked all three.

Lyra listened. She had expected a secret she could take from a man’s mouth. Instead she had parts that failed to fit, observations paid for over years, and a powerful man spending more than the public harbor works could ever earn him.

When the questioning ended, she put the northern papers, the half-moon shim, and the broken sleeve into her tool chest. The original apparatus remained under the harbor. Nothing on her table could make the old cover safe by being carried back to it.

Iven watched her turn the key.

“What happens to me?”

“You stay tonight.”

“And tomorrow?”

“I’ll decide what work I can offer you.”

He glanced at the bound wrists. “Offer.”

Lyra met his eyes. “The rope is part of what we will discuss.”

Outside, the evening boat called for someone to take its line. She left Vaska with the captive and went down to her landing, where ordinary cargo had arrived in need of ordinary hands.

Chapter 6 No renewal

Iven had taken off his boots in the night.

They stood beneath the chair in the little store beside the workroom, polished toes pointing toward the door. Someone had set a basin of water beside them. He slept sitting with his hands in his lap and the rope still on his wrists. Vaska’s woman at the door had one foot against the chair leg. She was eating an apple with a small knife.

“Did he try anything?” Lyra asked.

“He asked me to turn the lamp down.”

“Did you?”

“No.”

“Turn it down if he asks again.”

The woman looked at the wick, then at the daylight in the high window. “I will remember for next time.”

Iven opened his eyes.

Lyra had brought bread, a jug, and the terms she had written before sunrise. She put the food on the crate serving him as a table. He watched her hands until she drew her knife. Then he sat very still.

“Hold them out.”

He did. She cut the cord on the outside of his wrists. The skin beneath was rubbed but unbroken. He flexed his fingers and put his right hand round his left wrist, warming it.

“The door stays guarded,” she said. “You can use the yard with someone beside you. The upper room over the empty sail bay is yours to sleep in. It locks from inside. Someone will be outside it.”

“An improvement with great care taken to preserve its character.”

“Read this.”

He accepted the sheet. His eyes moved down the first paragraph and stopped. Lyra poured water into his cup. The woman with the apple remained at the door.

“Three weeks,” he said.

“From today. You work with Mavet on the old fittings and the notes you brought. You tell us what you know about access and isolation. You can say you don’t know. If we catch you inventing an answer, we stop using it.”

“That is all?”

“You won’t be required to fight. You won’t go into a chamber Mavet hasn’t agreed we can enter. You will go under guard where the work needs you. After three weeks, you can leave the landing with your wages and your boots.”

He glanced down at them. “How generous to include the boots.”

“I considered selling them.”

“They wouldn’t fetch what you imagine.”

“Then you paid badly.”

For the first time, his mouth moved as though he might have smiled under different circumstances. He continued reading. Lyra waited. The wages were those Mavet had demanded for a skilled man working under danger, with no reduction for his inconvenient method of introduction. Half would be put into a purse for him that morning. The balance became due each week. If he died in the work, the money went where he named. If he refused the undertaking, he would remain under guard while Odel negotiated his return. Lyra could not promise how long that would take, or which employer would thank Iven afterward.

“This is not a choice I would have made yesterday,” he said.

“No.”

“You are planning to attack his works.”

“Yes.”

“Then three weeks may be longer than you have.”

“Will that affect your price?”

“It affects where I would ask you to send the money.”

He took the bread. At first he broke it into very small pieces. Then hunger won. Lyra had made herself eat before coming down, but the smell of the crust brought back the empty feeling below her breastbone. Yesterday’s fight had left a sore place along her ribs, and she had found a bruise at her right elbow whose color was already spreading. She resisted the wish to press it.

Iven asked for a pen. He amended the sentence about danger: he could refuse a descent if he believed the agreed conditions had changed. Mavet would hear the objection before he was taken below. Lyra thought about the time this could cost and accepted it. He added an address outside the city for the money, then folded that portion of the sheet so the guard would not read it from the doorway.

“I have written to a sister,” he said. “She did not purchase this employment for me.”

“I won’t send people to her.”

He looked up. He had not asked. She disliked the recognition in his face and could not tell which of them it embarrassed more.

When he had signed, she gave him his own copy. His first wage purse went into his coat. She kept the drawings and the broken fittings; they were not part of the offer. He could inspect them beside Mavet and make his own working notes. Nothing they had taken from the candle house was being returned to the Prince.

At the door, Iven spoke again.

“He will not understand this as a disagreement over repairs.”

“Neither do I.”

“Then tell the people you hire.”

She left him with the bread and the lamp burning in broad daylight. Behind her, he asked the guard to put it out. This time she did.

The west store roof leaked in six places.

Lyra counted them herself in the rain. Daro’s man had set tubs under five and missed the smallest, which ran down a beam and dripped on a shelf of empty jars. The floor was sound. So were the steps, the crane bed, and the stone throat of the landward passage. Two lighters could unload together without borrowing the adjoining frontage. With the promised roof work and a better door on the rear room, it would earn money from the first week.

She had asked to see it before the next meeting. Daro had sent the keys before breakfast.

The man showing her round was prepared to be contradicted. By the time she had measured the rear door, he had begun pointing out faults himself. She liked him better for it. He showed her the water mark on the lowest shelf and the place where a previous tenant had hidden a broken pulley instead of paying for it. He was still complaining about the pulley when Serafine arrived beneath a waxed cloak.

“Am I early?”

“You’re wet,” Lyra said.

“An accomplishment for which I have paid a great deal.”

Her attendant took the cloak. Beneath it, her dress was dry, the color of a cut plum. Rain glittered on the white cheek of the mask. She wiped it away with one finger and looked round the store.

“Would it do?”

“Yes.”

“Good. We can leave before the roof persuades you otherwise.”

They walked together to the counting room above Daro’s smaller landing. Lyra could see Gannet House across the turn of the quay, its gallery lamps already lit against the dull afternoon. This room was plainer. A stove warmed one wall; a basket of wet gloves steamed beside it. Daro stood to greet her, and Odel moved her own cup out of the way to make room for Lyra’s coat.

“No duck today?” Lyra asked.

“I was told you distrusted offers served with food,” Daro said.

“I distrust offers served without it more.”

“Then we may still reach an understanding.”

There was bread, cheese, hot onions, and a pot of fish with the bones already taken out. Daro ate standing while he searched among the papers for the right copy. Odel found it beneath his elbow and put it in front of Lyra.

Her requested changes had been made in a different hand.

The receiving appointment could not be taken away while Harrow kept the stated capacity and Lyra performed the work. Illness gave her a season to appoint a deputy; disagreement over the deputy went before a named neutral shipmaster. There was no annual renewal at another partner’s whim. If Daro’s promised cargo failed to arrive, the minimum payment still fell due. The repair contribution was now a definite sum, payable before the first boat. Harrow’s existing customers and ownership were expressly outside the arrangement.

She read those passages twice.

“And the Prince?”

“He has accepted that the western run is ours to settle,” Daro said. “He wants compensation for the cargo diverted through your landing. We will absorb half the disputed charge. Vaska can either pay the other half or work it through her first season’s share.”

“Did you ask her?”

“Not yet. I asked you first.”

“The men yesterday were not collecting a charge.”

“No.” Daro set down his plate. “I have heard what happened. I have also heard that you offered to sell a piece recovered from his excavation.”

“Recovered from work he doesn’t own.”

“Very well. An old piece from a disputed hole. I am willing to help settle that too. You keep the comparison notes your stoneworker made. He gets the two pieces brought from Zakov. The question of who put a knife into whom at the candle house is finished. Iven can return or take other employment when you release him.”

“He agreed to three weeks with me this morning.”

“Then in three weeks. I am not proposing that you deliver a man to be skinned before supper.”

Lyra looked sharply at him. Daro’s expression did not change. He pulled out his chair and sat.

“You know what he is,” she said.

“I know what agreements he has kept, and what it costs him to break one in front of enough people. Serafine knows more than I do about some of those people. Odel knows where certain goods have to travel. Between us, we can make keeping this arrangement cheaper than breaking it.”

Serafine took the revised sheet from Lyra and touched the passage about interference with the western run.

“Our names would be on it. Our customers would see whether those names were worth anything. That is a substantial thing to put into a room, Lyra.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

Lyra met the uncovered eye. She thought of the men who had begun treating Harrow differently after the affair at the Opera, and of how quickly such courtesy could turn back into amusement if the boats stopped coming. The Court’s name was something customers brought with them before they had seen a single rope.

“Yes,” she said. “I do.”

Odel pushed the fish toward her. “Then eat before you tell us which part is still wrong.”

Lyra ate. The onions were soft and very hot. Daro poured wine for the others and water for himself, saying the last man he had met had mistaken a long lunch for an advantage and left him with the beginning of a headache. For a few minutes they argued about whether headaches excused bad arithmetic. Lyra could have remained there. The room was warm; rain struck the shutters instead of her face. They had taken her objections seriously. Beneath her hand lay enough assured work to finish the north side and replace the rotten shelter over the winch.

She turned to the last page.

“Expansion still needs agreement.”

“Armed expansion into our routes does,” Daro said. “You may buy another shed without inviting us to admire it.”

“And the appointment doesn’t pass to someone I choose.”

“It can, with approval. We have promised you our business. We haven’t promised it to anyone who might purchase your good opinion.”

“If I die?”

“Your property goes where you direct it. Your people receive the work already contracted. We consider your successor. I will not swear now to trust a person neither of us has met.”

She could not find dishonesty in it. The terms gave each of them a way to protect what they brought. Daro had not demanded the keys to Harrow. Serafine had not asked to choose who slept upstairs. Odel wanted an enforceable limit on the passage loads and a price that rose when her people had to carry more. Lyra could sign and be richer by the end of the month.

She looked again at the line requiring agreement before an armed claim beyond the western receiving district.

The old chambers lay beyond that district.

“So I leave the lower works to him.”

“You leave the inner excavation alone,” Daro said. “The harbor authorities can inspect the outer passages through people agreed by all concerned. Your stoneworker needn’t lose what she has learned. There is useful employment there that doesn’t require taking possession.”

“And if I think possession is useful?”

“Then you ask us to risk what we have, and we decide whether the gain is worth it.”

Lyra laid down the sheet. She could see how the next ten years might go. There would be disputes she won. More cargo. A proper kitchen above the shed. People would bring offers to her, and some would stay for supper. When the matter grew large enough, she would come back to a room like this one and explain why she ought to be allowed to proceed.

She wanted the kitchen. She wanted the boats, the repaired stone, the people at her table. She also wanted a day when Daro Venn would bring the disputed paper to her.

Serafine had been watching her without touching her cup.

“What would satisfy you?”

“Leaving that decision mine.”

“It would remain yours,” Serafine said. “You could withdraw from the arrangement. There are notice terms.”

“And start again with everyone knowing which work to deny me.”

“Some work would no longer be ours to give you. That is what leaving means.”

Odel leaned back. “You asked for a lasting share. This is one. You can make it larger by doing work the rest of us want done. If you mean something else, name it before Daro finds another pot of fish.”

Lyra smiled despite herself. Then she pushed the paper into the middle of the table.

“I won’t sign.”

Daro looked at the pages, not at her. “Take another night.”

“I don’t need it.”

“Your man has lost his hearing in that hole. You have had knives put against you over a handful of old metal. You are in a room with three people offering to make those losses the end of the matter. I am trying very hard not to mistake pride for judgment.”

“Then don’t.”

His eyes rose to hers. For a moment the room had no easy sound in it. The rain had slackened. Someone below was dragging a crate across the floor in short, laborious pulls.

Lyra rested her bad arm on the table, where its ache would not make her shift.

“You have made a good offer. I can afford to refuse it. Those are both things I needed to know.”

Serafine’s fingers closed on the stem of her cup. Odel looked at Lyra for a long time and then took another piece of bread.

Daro gathered the papers. He did it carefully, aligning the altered pages with the originals.

“Your ordinary traffic stays ordinary,” he said. “I am not sending men to punish you for declining a contract. But when you ask a boat to cross my route armed, do not tell its master I have agreed.”

“I won’t.”

“And if you come to my landing with those men?”

“Then you’ll know why.”

She heard how it sounded only after she had said it. Daro’s face did not harden into a stranger’s. That would have been easier. He looked disappointed and increasingly interested, as though she had put an unexpected sum in front of him and he was deciding which part had been omitted.

“I expect I shall,” he said.

When she stood, Serafine gave her the copy with the revisions.

“Keep it. Offers are occasionally better remembered when the words survive.”

Lyra folded it and put it inside her coat.

Downstairs, a carrier was still struggling with the crate. Its corner had caught against a raised board. Lyra put her shoulder to the other end while he pulled. Together they got it over. He thanked her without recognizing her, and she went out into the rain.

Vaska was trying on a coat that had not been made for her.

It belonged to the man who rented the back room above the wine seller. He was away, and she said he had left it in exchange for money he had not yet returned. The shoulders were too broad, but the cloth was excellent. She stood before a small polished mirror, turned one sleeve back, and bared her teeth at the effect.

“Would you fear me?”

“I’d fear for the seams.”

“That can be repaired. A good coat at a bad price cannot.”

Lyra shut the door. The room smelled of wine, damp wool, and the sour fruit cut open on the windowsill. Two knives lay on the bed within reach of the mirror. Vaska saw her notice them.

“Sometimes one is underneath me when I wake. It is unpleasant.”

“I need a captain.”

“For how many boats?”

“For the people I hire. Boats as we get them.”

Vaska took off the coat. Under it she wore a plain shirt open at the throat, a strip of clean linen round the forearm struck at the candle house. She sat on the edge of the bed and took a piece of fruit.

“What happened to the wonderful dinner?”

“Lunch. I refused it.”

“Was there something wrong with the food?”

“No.”

“The offer?”

“Not enough.”

Vaska chewed. Juice brightened a small split in her lower lip. “Good. I was afraid you were going to tell me it had all been a trick. Daro gives people quite comfortable cages. Some of them build upstairs.”

“It wasn’t a cage.”

“Then you are even harder to feed than I thought.”

Lyra told her what she intended to take first. The outer works beyond the western Sluice House, the inspection chamber, the stair above it, the maintenance approach that fed the Prince’s excavators. A place to work without a hammer coming down on the barred door while they carried an injured man. Nothing deeper until Mavet could make the isolation sound. After that, they would decide which stores paid for holding it.

Vaska listened without interrupting. When Lyra finished, she leaned across the bed and took one knife out from under a folded blanket.

“You know there will be men sleeping there.”

“Yes.”

“Some will be easy to frighten. Some will have nowhere better to go. We may kill them.”

“Yes.”

“And after that, I can’t finish a month’s work and go back to carrying the same little loads past the same officers. He’ll have my name whether yours is on the purse or not.”

“I am offering standing work.”

“You are offering standing danger. The work can be priced afterward.”

They priced both.

Vaska wanted a regular wage high enough to pay for living somewhere she could leave her coat, a share of the net gains from armed work she captained, and the right to refuse a plan before men were committed to it. Lyra would choose the objective. Vaska would place the people in a fight and could pull them out when the route failed. Neither could quietly change the other’s agreed task after departure and call it obedience. The share would come after crew wages, treatment, and repairs, before Lyra took her own profit.

“Not every broken oar in the harbor,” Vaska said. “Repairs to the work I was part of.”

“Agreed.”

“And when you decide someone must die?”

Lyra looked at the knife in Vaska’s hands.

“I tell you who and why.”

“You can leave out why if you like. But don’t tell me to collect a debt and act surprised when I bring a debtor back breathing.”

“I won’t.”

They argued longest about the share. Vaska asked more than Lyra wanted to pay and did not seem troubled by the silence after her number. Below them, someone began singing badly. Another voice called the singer by a different name and told him his wife had come. The song stopped.

Lyra laughed. Vaska smiled without lowering the price.

At last they agreed. Vaska set her knife down and offered her uninjured hand.

“Now tell me when.”

“Tomorrow night, if the tide and Mavet agree.”

“I can persuade a stoneworker. I have had less success with tides.”

“Don’t persuade her.”

Vaska’s smile faded just enough to show she had heard. “All right.”

Lyra shook her hand. It felt good to settle something with a person who wanted the same difficult thing and had named what it would cost.

The money went out before the men did.

Lyra sat at the workroom table with a lamp, two purses, and the list Vaska had brought. The original small gang came one at a time. Vaska did not answer for them. Each heard the place, the work, the pay, and the fact that the Prince would consider it an attack on his interests. One woman wanted only boat duty, with no promise to go below. One man refused and asked whether he still owed for the blanket Lyra had given him after the night cargo. She told him to keep it. He looked pleased until he realized she was serious about his leaving, and then he wished the others luck and went.

The woman at Iven’s door accepted two weeks of guard work. She asked for a stool with a back. Berris found one with a loose rail and repaired it while they talked.

Rusk came on a stick, the bandage at his thigh broad beneath his trousers. Vaska told him to go upstairs and stop making an entrance.

“I can watch a door.”

“You can watch the door of the room where you are recovering.”

“I get bored.”

“Good. You were becoming too interesting.”

He stayed to eat, but his name did not go among the people leaving. Lyra paid the rest of his treatment and asked where his ordinary wage should be taken while he could not work. He gave an address and made her repeat it because there were two doors with blue paint on that lane and the wrong woman would spend it more quickly.

Orrel agreed to handle the retrieval winch from above. He would not go under the lower lintels. Lyra wrote that beside his name. Daven stood over the table long enough to hear the proposal and said Brindle was not being taken into a landing fight.

“I have paid to mend that bow once,” he said. “You may remember the occasion.”

“I do.”

“Then hire something you love less.”

Vaska supplied a shallow workboat through one of her own people. Its owner inspected the promised route and charged extra for returning without both gunwales. Daven agreed to carry stores in daylight after the approach was held, with another price to be settled then. He took no advance for work he had not accepted.

Sova’s supper arrived in covered pots. She had sent enough onions to explain the smell from the lane and a small written complaint about people who returned a pot with a different lid. Lyra read it aloud. Berris said the wrong lid was better fitted than the right one and ought to be considered an improvement. They ate from work bowls at the cleared end of the table. Vaska listened to Orrel describing the arguments of winches with the solemn attention she might have given a particularly slow threat.

Mavet arrived last, rain on her shoulders, carrying a narrow board on which she had drawn the approach in charcoal. She let the supper wait while she showed Vaska the barred stair.

“This door opens toward the upper landing. If you put three people below it with shields, they will stop the rest of us reaching them.”

“Two above, then,” Vaska said.

“If you can reach the upper yard.”

“We can reach the yard. Whether we can leave it is what I am asking you.”

Mavet turned the board and began again. Lyra watched their fingers move across the marks. The plan lost one attractive turn and gained a longer, uglier way round a maintenance wall. She liked it better when Vaska stopped smiling.

Corven came in with the saltwater vessel wrapped against the cold. He set it where no one could kick it and accepted a bowl. His chain was wound beneath his coat; a short length showed when he reached for the bread.

“One entry,” he said to Lyra. “Holding the lower route while your people take the stair and the yard. Getting those people out if the door remains closed. No promise to enter beyond the old face.”

“That is the job.”

“No promise about the next one.”

“I remember how you charge.”

“You remember and try anyway.”

She paid him the agreed advance. He counted it without apology and put it away. Then he asked who had salted the onions, and half the room blamed Sova while the other half asked for more.

Later, Lyra carried a separate purse to the sailmaker’s yard.

Belis was awake at the window with Rima’s work across his knees. He held a half-shaped handle and was drawing a line for her to shave. Someone had fixed a board between two crates to make a table that would not move when he steadied himself on it. Rima sat on the floor sweeping curls of wood into a pan with her hand.

“Come round this side,” Belis said when he saw Lyra. “Unless you’ve brought bad news. I prefer that on the left.”

She came to his right and showed him the purse. Treatment, room, and their ordinary earnings for the next month. After that, they would settle the actual costs again. The money did not buy his attendance at the works or a promise to resume diving. Rima took it to the bed, counted it, and returned one coin.

“Too much?” Lyra asked.

“False.”

Lyra stared at it. Belis began laughing and had to shut his eyes when the movement made him dizzy.

“Your new empire is already full of thieves.”

“I bought onions with that money.”

“Then Sova has the rest.”

Lyra replaced the coin. She stayed while Rima finished shaving the handle. Belis closed one eye, inspected the curve, and told her it was right. Rima asked whether it was right or whether he was hungry. He said a person could possess both judgment and appetite, and Lyra passed him the bread she had brought.

He heard her plans only because he asked. She told him the first part, the people taking the upper yard, Mavet’s authority to stop the work below. When she finished he rubbed his thumb along the new handle.

“I won’t tell you it was worth it,” he said.

“I didn’t come for that.”

“All right.” He put the handle beside its mate. “Then hold this board while she fixes the clamp. It slips.”

She held it. Rima tightened the screw, bent over her work, and began again. For a while, the only sound was the little blade passing through the grain.

Before dawn Lyra went down to the landing alone.

The hired boat lay in the outer berth with its oars under a canvas cover. Vaska had moved it after midnight, testing the water at the hour they would leave. A coil of new rope waited under the shed, paid for and not yet cut into useful lengths. In the workroom, the charcoal board was propped against the lamp so no one could put a bowl on the route.

Lyra walked the south anchorage and pressed her foot against the stone. It held. Above her, Iven’s new window showed a thin line of light. He was awake already, or had not slept. She could see the guard’s shadow move outside his door.

The revised offer was upstairs beside her bed. She had read it once more after coming home. The sums had not changed. Neither had the neat line setting out what she could build without asking anyone. She had folded it with the others and put it away.

A gull landed on the far post and complained at the empty water.

“Nothing for you,” Lyra told it.

It looked at her with one bright eye, unimpressed by private ownership.

She laughed softly and went to wake the house.

Part Two The water she takes

Chapter 7 Night work

The man at the grating wanted his supper hot. That was why they had a way in.

For three evenings Lyra had watched the delivery boy carry a covered bowl to the upper gallery. Each evening the guard opened the little gate before the boy had finished knocking, took the bowl, and left the boy waiting outside while he tested it. The guard had burned his mouth on the second evening. On the third he made the boy taste it first.

Tonight Vaska carried the bowl.

She had put on a plain skirt and a good dark coat, making no attempt to look like a boy. Her hair was pinned close to her head. The narrow piece of steel inside the pin had no ornament on it, and Lyra found herself looking at it instead of the other knife. Vaska noticed.

“I bought the coat for something more amusing,” she said.

“You can keep it clean.”

“Can I?”

Lyra took the last step down into the waiting boat. Water touched the soles of her boots. The little vessel belonged to Vaska’s company; its master sat low in the stern, chewing something that smelled of cloves. He had already been paid. His second payment depended on bringing the boat back, not on pretending that a bad landing was a good one.

Corven passed him a bag and then came aboard with the eel’s covered jar held against his chest. The marked chain lay wrapped beneath his coat. He had shaved, and the pale line of his old side wound showed for a moment when he bent to put the jar in its padded box. Lyra looked away before he looked up.

“One watch,” he said.

“I know.”

“Until the upper guardroom and the outer passage are secured, or until we abandon it. I am saying it in front of her.”

Vaska lifted the bowl in acknowledgment. “I heard. If I die before then, you may leave early.”

He gave her a flat look. She smiled without expecting a reply.

Lyra checked the chalk in her pocket, the small brine vessel, and the piece of clean cord she would use to tie back her sleeve. No charged vessel accompanied her. The old chamber had injured Belis and given them measurements. It had not yet given her anything she could carry into a fight.

Iven had marked the guardroom on the drawing under her hand. His information was two weeks old. He had refused to call it a promise, and she had not let him call it useless. The upper men changed after the second night bell. The supply door opened onto a gallery that ran above the excavated platform. The Prince’s inner people used another route. Taking the first would let her approach the old apparatus without finding an armed overseer at her back. It would not close every way the Prince could come.

They pushed off while the lamps were being lit along the quay. Vaska went ashore at the first stair with the bowl under its cover. Two of her people followed along the longer footway around the maintenance wall, far enough apart that a watcher would have to decide which one mattered. Orrel waited at the upper retrieval winch, with orders to remain above if the lower way failed. Lyra remained in the boat with Corven and the master, moving toward the boarded inspection mouth beyond the western Sluice House.

The neighborhood watch’s lamp shone above the working outflow. Lyra had paid for an ordinary passage through the appointed way. She had not asked the local keeper to assist her war. He would be able to tell Sera exactly that when the noise brought questions.

The master brought them beneath the low lip of stone. Lyra could smell the old wet wood before she could see the boards. One panel was already loose from their last inspection, fixed so it could be opened from the outside without tearing the whole barrier down. She took it away and passed it to Corven. He laid it in the boat.

“After you,” he said.

She lowered herself into the mouth. Water filled one boot at once. The cold was familiar enough to feel like privacy. She pulled the hood of the lamp down until only a line of light remained and went beneath the first lintel.

At the second, somebody coughed.

Lyra stopped. Corven’s hand touched her heel, asking whether she had reached the obstruction. She moved the foot once to tell him to wait.

The cough came again, dry and shallow. A small scraping followed it. She could see the inspection chamber beyond the lintel, the foot of the barred stair, and a shape seated in the shadow beneath it.

“You’ll scrape your left shoulder if you go forward like that,” the shape said. “The stone has come down.”

Lyra knew the voice. Iven had spoken to the man at the component landing, calling him Heth as though the name were an inconvenient part of the transaction.

She lifted the lamp a little. Heth sat with his knees wide apart and a length of broken carrying pole beside him. One wrist clicked when he raised a hand against the light. The sound came from inside the joint. Salt lay in the creases at his mouth.

“Where are the others?” she asked.

“Above.”

“How many?”

“Which others?”

“The men with weapons.”

“More than you would like. Fewer than they would like.”

“Make that useful.”

Heth looked at Corven coming through behind her. “Four on the upper reach. Two near the door. Another asleep unless somebody has woken him. Three of my company moving stone. I want them out before the shouting begins.”

“You knew we were coming?”

“I knew someone was watching the supper.”

She felt a brief, cold anger at the boy who had seemed too occupied with his bowl to notice them. Then she realized Heth might have watched the watching himself. His stillness was no evidence of inattention.

“Can you bring your people down?”

“Not if the grating stays barred.”

“We intend to open it.”

“I’d prefer an intention about what happens afterward.”

Corven stood with his hand on the chain bag. Heth did not look frightened of it. He looked tired, with a particular interest in the distance to the stair.

“Once they’re below,” Lyra said, “keep them clear of the platform. The boat will take you to the outer steps.”

“Your boat?”

“Yes.”

“And then?”

“You can leave.”

Heth worked his jaw. A little salt fell from it. “I want that said to the person in the boat.”

She turned to Corven. He went back to the second lintel and called softly to the master. The man answered with irritation, then repeated the terms. Heth listened until he had heard them correctly.

“There’s a hook beside the top bar,” he said. “The man who opens it thinks the bar is enough. Pull the hook before you lift.”

Lyra went up the stair.

Vaska’s voice reached her through the iron grating.

“The boy’s ill.”

“What’s wrong with him?”

“His employer sent him three nights running to a man who makes him taste the food.”

A pause. Lyra heard the little outside gate opening.

“Give it here.”

“He wants the bowl back.”

“He’ll get it.”

“You can tell me that after you eat. I’m the person who has to take it away.”

The guard was laughing now. He had not heard the hook lift at the barred stair. Lyra eased the lower bar out of its seat. Corven took its weight before it could strike stone. She could see the guard’s back through the gap, a broad expanse of worn brown cloth, his knife hanging where a seated man could reach it. Vaska stood in the light of the small door with the bowl held between them.

The man dipped a finger into the sauce.

Vaska’s other hand closed over his wrist. She drew him toward her, turned him enough to conceal the motion from the room beyond, and put the narrow blade beneath his jaw.

“Quietly,” she said.

The bowl fell. It broke against the threshold. The guard tried to shout.

Corven had prepared the shell while she climbed. He held it now with the dark inch of water trembling against its cracked lip. The guard’s mouth opened. Nothing came out except breath.

Lyra stepped through the grating and caught his knife hand. He was strong. He drove her shoulder against the iron and brought a knee up between them. The old arm flared with pain. Vaska drove him lower, but he kept fighting, his free hand reaching for a bell cord beside the door.

Lyra put her own knife into him beneath the arm.

He stopped reaching. For an instant all his weight hung from the wrist she held. Then Vaska lowered him onto the broken bowl, and the hot sauce soaked his coat.

From the room beyond, someone called his name.

Vaska looked at Lyra. Her face had changed without becoming expressionless. She had stopped offering the world a chance to amuse her.

Lyra pointed to the inner door. Corven moved toward it while she pushed the grating wide enough for Heth. Below, the Worn man’s joints sounded softly as he climbed.

“Where’s my bowl?” Vaska called, raising her voice. “If he’s finished spilling it, somebody can pay me.”

A man came through the inner door with a lamp in one hand. Corven struck the lamp aside, caught the man by the coat, and drove him against the wall. The flame went out in spilled oil. The sound was much louder than the struggle at the grating.

Beyond, a chair fell.

Lyra ran into the guardroom. Two men stood by the far arch. One had a short crossbow, already winding it. The other seized a spear from the wall. She threw the fallen chair at the crossbowman. It struck his shoulder, and the weapon went off into the ceiling. Stone dust came down over her hair.

The spearman jabbed. She turned the point with the chair leg left in her hand, felt the shock reach her sore shoulder, and let the wood go rather than hold against the whole force. Vaska came through behind her and cut across his forearm. He dropped the spear. She kicked it out of reach and followed him backward into the far wall.

The crossbowman had drawn a knife. He was quicker than Lyra expected, stepping around the broken chair and driving low. She caught his sleeve with her left hand and pulled him toward the table. The knife grazed her ribs through the coat. She put her hip against him and sent him over the edge, taking the table with him.

Neither of them fell cleanly. Her knee struck the floor. His knife went beneath the table, and his hand followed it. She brought the chair leg down on his wrist.

He screamed. Corven’s silence had belonged to one man at the outside door; this man’s voice filled the room.

“Leave it,” she said.

He looked at her, looked at the hand, and left it.

Across the room, Vaska’s opponent slid down the wall with blood running between his fingers. He was alive. He put his other hand flat against the stone when she told him to, his eyes fixed on the blade she held beside his throat.

A third man emerged from the sleeping alcove in his shirt. He saw the overturned table, turned, and ran through the arch toward the pump gallery.

Lyra went after him. Behind her Corven said her name, but she had already seen the door he meant. The runner reached it before she did and began pulling a locking bar free.

Men waited on the far side. She could hear them calling to him.

She threw the short iron hook from her belt. It struck his shoulder hard enough to make him turn. He left the bar half lifted. Lyra hit him with her body and sent him against the jamb. He caught her throat, pushing with both hands. His fingers were hot.

She drove her forehead into his face. The pain was immediate and bright. He let go, staggering. Vaska arrived and pulled him away from the door by the back of his shirt.

The far men hit the moving face. It came inward an inch.

Lyra caught the inner edge and shoved it back until the bar dropped into its seat. The wood was sound; she could feel that in the first impact. She took brine from her pocket, wet the frame, and put one palm against it, the other against the door. The force came toward her from the gallery. She knew its direction. She had both surfaces.

“Hold the room,” she said.

Pressure settled into the lines beneath her hands. The next blow did not move the door. Her right arm trembled. She bent her elbow, bringing her body close enough that the injured shoulder did not take the whole strain.

Vaska went back through the guardroom. The corridor beyond the door shook with running feet. Lyra could not hear how many. The working would give her a little time. She had to spend it on something more useful than being impressed that it worked.

“Heth!” she called.

His answering voice came from the stair. “Two here.”

“You said three.”

“The third won’t leave the load.”

“Show him an empty route.”

“He isn’t following routes. He is holding a stone.”

Lyra closed her eyes for one breath. Corven was taking prisoners. Vaska’s people were coming through the outer door. The boat lay beyond two lintels and a passage filling slowly as the tide changed. The man with the stone had not agreed to fit into that interval.

“Corven. The lower platform. Help Heth.”

He looked toward her hands. “How long?”

“Long enough if you go.”

He went.

The men beyond the door tried a new tool. A heavy metal edge struck close to Lyra’s right hand. The wood did not split, but she felt the blow through her skin into the old injury.

Vaska came back carrying a crosspiece from the sleeping alcove. “Where do you want it?”

“Floor socket. Under the bar.”

“It isn’t long enough.”

“There’s another piece under the table.”

Vaska fetched it. Together they would reach from the lower floor recess to the bracket beside the door. She fitted them, discovered the joint would slide, and asked for a wedge. Nobody had brought a wedge into the guardroom. There had been several in Lyra’s equipment bag, which remained below.

Vaska looked around, took the fallen table’s detached leg, and drove it between the pieces with the butt of the captured spear. The brace tightened. She struck again. Lyra felt a fraction of the load leave her hands.

“Try it,” Vaska said.

Lyra released the working slowly, keeping her palms against the wood while the ordinary brace accepted what they could no longer hold. The door stayed shut. She stepped away. Her right hand would not uncurl at once. She used the left to straighten its fingers.

The man in his shirt sat against the wall with blood from his nose spread down his chest. Vaska had tied his hands. He watched Lyra with a hatred so exact that she preferred it to the stunned expression of the man whose wrist she had broken.

“Who’s in command on the other side?” she asked him.

He spat onto the floor.

“Then we’ll ask somebody who can speak.”

She went to the gallery’s narrow viewing opening. Through it she could see a short stretch of raised walkway, two pump bodies, and the rail along the platform below. Men stood at the far bend, beyond her reach. The inner route ran past them into the Prince’s retained works. She could not take it with the people she had tonight.

Vaska stood beside her. “We can hold this door.”

“For tonight.”

“Longer if we make it expensive to visit.”

Lyra looked back at the room: the broken table, the window over the street, the barred stair now open behind them. It was a mean little place. Men had been paid to keep it because holding it let them decide who went below. She had paid more, killed one, wounded others, and taken the decision from them.

“Set your two people at the street gate. I want a lamp shielded from the gallery. Nobody walks in because they used to belong here.”

“And the prisoners?”

“Bandage them. Separate them. The man with the wrist can go out under escort when he names someone who’ll take him. We keep the other two until the Prince has heard we hold this end.”

Vaska nodded. “If they try to retake it?”

“We stop them.”

She said it more easily than she had expected. Vaska’s smile returned, smaller and warmer than the one she had worn at the supper door.

Below, Corven and Heth had got the third Worn moving. The man came up the stair carrying a stone no larger than a loaf, held against his stomach with both arms. Corven had taken the rest of the load apart rather than trying to make him understand that he should put it down. Heth kept one hand on the stair rail, calling the man at each turn.

“The boat?” Heth asked.

“Waiting.”

He looked at Lyra’s bloodstained sleeve. “You said we could leave.”

“You can.”

“Not through that door.” He pointed toward the Prince’s gallery.

“No.”

“Then tell your new watch we may come back for the tools we were using. They aren’t all his.”

“Tomorrow, after the first light. Ask for Vaska.”

Heth repeated the name. His shoulder clicked when he turned. The Worn carrying the stone followed him down, moving slowly but under his own weight.

Lyra went with them to the lower landing. The boat master complained about the additional load, accepted the additional payment, and shifted his own bag to make a space. Heth watched until every person he had named was aboard. Then he sat in the bow, with his broken pole across his knees.

As they left, Lyra saw him look away from the city, toward the dark beyond the harbor lights. The glance lasted longer than the boat’s turn required. Then he looked back and asked the master to mind a hanging chain.

Corven washed the blood from his hands in the little channel beside the inspection mouth. He used clean water from his bottle afterward, checking the scrape across his knuckles. Lyra crouched beside him and offered a cloth.

“The room is held,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Your people have the watch.”

“Yes.”

“Then I am finished.”

She gave him the remaining pay. He put it away without counting it until his hands were dry.

“I’ll need help with the next descent,” she said.

“Ask me about the next descent.”

She nodded. He carried the eel’s jar out through the low mouth into the waiting second boat, leaving her the lamp.

By dawn Vaska had found a kettle, repaired the least broken chair, and put a watch list on the guardroom wall. She sat in the chair with her boots off, showing a torn stocking and a small cut at the heel. The good coat lay over the back. Blood had dried along its lower edge.

“Your supper friend owes me a bowl,” she said.

“He’s dead.”

“I saw.”

Lyra sat on the floor beside the table’s wreckage. The place beneath the window was warm enough to dry the damp in her clothes. She accepted the cup Vaska offered and drank. It was bitter, overboiled tea. Through the blocked gallery door came one final, testing blow. Neither woman moved toward it.

Chapter 8 Fit for purpose

The first vessel broke while it was still empty.

Mavet had asked Lyra to pass it across the bench. Lyra lifted it by the bronze collar, felt the ceramic body shift beneath her fingers, and caught it with the other hand before it struck the floor. A pale line had opened below the rim. The collar stayed in her right hand. Most of the vessel stayed in her left.

Iven laughed once, sharply, then stopped when she looked at him.

“I paid for that,” she said.

“Then you should be grateful it broke before you filled it.”

“I can be grateful and want my money back.”

“From the potter or the founder?” Mavet asked.

She took both pieces. The lining had drawn unevenly as it cooled, leaving a hard bronze edge pressed against clay too thin to receive it. Whoever had made the collar had polished the visible part beautifully. Mavet ran her thumb along the buried edge, held up the cut it made, and said they would begin again.

They were working in Harrow’s lower shed, with the doors open to the morning and a guard outside. Iven sat at the end of the bench where he could see the entrance. He had chosen that seat himself. Vaska had agreed to it because the wall behind it had no window and the guard could reach him before he reached the door.

His fine boots had suffered during the ambush. He had repaired one heel with a piece of dark leather and kept the other turned away. Lyra had offered him suitable working boots. He preferred to be uncomfortable in his own.

“You can leave the binding off,” he told the guard when Mavet asked him to hold a drawing.

“The binding is off,” Lyra said.

“He watches my hands as though he’s still deciding.”

“He is.”

Iven unfolded the drawing with care. His paid three weeks of comparison work forbade private messages to the Prince’s people. It did not permit Lyra to add days for a failed experiment. He had made her confirm that before touching the broken collar.

Now he put the old Zakov collar beside the broken one.

“This shoulder,” he said. “You keep measuring the width because the width is easy to measure. The old piece sits deeper before the flange takes the load. Your modern part was made to stop here.”

He marked the difference with the back of a knife. Mavet looked at it, then at Lyra’s first drawing of the receiving face.

“He’s right.”

“I know,” Lyra said. “I dislike the pleasure he takes in it.”

Iven smiled. “You could pay extra for humility.”

“I have paid enough for the other thing.”

The next vessel had a thicker ceramic body and a bronze lining turned over a broad lip before it met the collar. They made three before Mavet accepted one. The failures remained on the shelf. Lyra wanted them kept until the maker had seen exactly where each had parted.

Belis came to see the third. Rima walked with him, one hand near his elbow without quite touching it. He turned his head too quickly at the shed door and caught the post to steady himself. His mouth tightened. When he spoke, he spoke louder than he meant to.

“Looks like a cooking pot for somebody who hates food.”

Rima laughed. Lyra did too, and made sure she stood on his right when she answered.

“It has been expensive enough to serve at a wedding.”

“Then don’t invite anyone.”

He examined the carrying cage Mavet had devised: a wooden cradle with leather pads and a handle that took the weight beneath the vessel instead of pulling on its neck. Rima worked wooden handles, and immediately disliked the first curve of this one. She said a wet hand would slide toward the joint. Belis agreed, then said a diver’s hand would not.

“Your hand did,” she told him.

The silence after that belonged to all four of them. Rima looked down at the handle. Belis took it from her, turned it, and said she was right about the curve.

Lyra asked him to make the next handle on the bench, with Rima shaping the grip. He looked toward the water beyond the doors.

“Will it go down?”

“Yes.”

“Will I?”

“No.”

His eyes came back to her. For a moment she expected an argument. He had given her so many during the preparations, most of them useful and all of them long enough to include what he had eaten or whom he had met on the way to work. Now he touched the handle’s bad curve with one finger.

“I can make this better,” he said.

She paid them both for the work. Rima made the arrangement herself, refusing the first sum because Lyra had counted only the cutting and not the fitting. Belis listened, smiling a little on the side of his mouth that faced her.

Below the outer guardroom, the old stone held its cold through the warmest part of the day.

Vaska’s watch had made the upper room habitable without making it pleasant. A fresh brace held the gallery door. The Prince’s men had tested it twice, then settled into watching the far bend. A covered lamp showed where they stood. Vaska kept her own light lower and moved her people often enough that a crossbowman could not count on finding the same shoulder in the same place.

The workers used the inspection mouth. They went beneath the two lintels, into the chamber, and through the broken partition onto the excavated platform. Three broad steps led toward the main sealed face. Its dark inset crystal caught light from below the angle at which a person naturally looked. Lyra no longer approached it without crouching first. Standing made her feel that she had missed something directly in front of her.

They left the main face alone.

The smaller service face beside it had a distinct bronze seat and two controls set at unequal heights. One had been opened too soon during the first descent. Belis had been standing nearest it. Lyra could see his hand on the tool whenever she looked at the polished place where it had slipped.

Esten had made a stop for that control. Nobody could move it through the forbidden interval without first removing a pin. Mavet approved the stop and refused to call it protection against stupidity. Somebody would eventually remove the pin because a job seemed easier without it. She scratched the necessary order into a plate fixed beside the controls, where it could not be carried away in a pocket.

They tested the outer shutter with ordinary water first. A channel cut in the stone led to a shallow basin at their feet. Opening the shutter let the basin fill; closing it stopped the flow. The deeper source remained isolated. There was no cold beyond the stone’s ordinary chill, no pressure in Lyra’s teeth, no strange delay between moving a lever and feeling that she had moved it.

“Again,” she said.

Esten’s assistant timed the flow with a small cup marked inside. Behind the removed guard, a worm shaft turned a broad toothed quadrant, reducing a full turn of the handle to a grudging movement of the shutter. Iven crouched to read the worn index marks.

“Isolation,” he said. “The northern drawing calls this the isolation.”

Mavet chalked an arrow beside it. “Put that on the drawing too. Mark which way closes it.”

On the sixth trial, the outer shutter failed to close fully. A grain of old stone held it from its seat. They drained the basin, took the seat apart as far as they safely could, and cleaned it. The delay consumed the remaining useful hours. Lyra went back to the surface angry with the little grain and grateful that it had shown itself in water she understood.

The real source was opened four days later.

Corven had taken a new, separate contract for the trial and the retreat. He stood near the inspection passage, with the retrieval line in one hand and the chain laid where it would not catch the ordinary ropes. His eel moved in its vessel on a dry shelf. Lyra could hear its small body brush the glass when the room was quiet.

Mavet stood beside the upper shutter. Esten’s assistant held the lamp. Iven remained behind the marked line, watching the receiving seat. They had fitted a plain bronze blank there rather than risk a vessel on the first opening. A strip of wax lay across the blank, marked with a straight line.

Lyra wet the seat with worked brine, taking time over the join. The words she used belonged to her practice. She had spoken them at doors and small test frames, learning where a phrase lost its shape when she hurried. Here the old bronze acquired the taste of the words before she finished saying them. She could feel it in the roots of her teeth.

She placed her palm against the fixed rim and told Mavet to begin.

The source control moved no farther than they had agreed. Nothing poured into the visible basin. The ordinary water there remained flat.

The wax line bent inward.

Lyra felt a depth beneath her hand that did not correspond to the space beyond the bronze. It was not a room she could measure by reaching farther. Her shoulder drew toward it. She braced both feet, keeping the fixed rim separate in her attention from the face that could move.

“Close,” she said.

Mavet closed the source. The assistant called the order back, then watched while the outer isolation was put in place. Only after both were confirmed did Lyra take her hand away.

The wax stayed bent.

Iven approached and looked at it for a long time. “They weren’t carrying water,” he said.

“No,” Lyra answered.

He looked at her, waiting for more. She had none to give him. The basin held a finger’s depth of brine. The wax showed a weight the shallow room could not contain in any ordinary way.

Corven put the retrieval line down. He had been holding it so tightly that its twist remained marked across his palm.

The crosswise isolation shutter lay beyond the service cover, reached from the lower side of the gallery they had taken. The Prince’s workers had cleared most of that access and left the last stone in place. Mavet had it shored before anyone touched it. Iven’s half-moon tracing matched the worn band on the loose shim Lyra brought down; it told them where a bronze stop had once seated, not what waited when the stop was removed.

With the source closed and tested, they cleared the remaining stone. The side passage led into a wider stripped receiving bay, separate from the main pale face, which remained sealed above its three broad steps. Bronze rails ran along two walls of the bay; dark crystal plates lined an inspection hollow in its floor. An old iron security drop closed the deeper gallery beyond. They left that route alone. Off one side, three narrower steps descended into a receiving cell no larger than a small sleeping room. A low bronze face stood opposite its entrance. The fixed manifold continued through the back wall toward the still-inaccessible inner works. The cell was a place to receive a little of what it held, then shut the connection again.

Lyra entered only after Mavet had tested the floor. The air tasted of an extinguished lamp, although no lamp had burned there in their time. She put a hand against the wall and felt the wrong depth beyond it. A person could stand here, set a vessel at the low face, and reach the isolation control without crossing the source mouth. The old builders had made room for those acts. They had not left an explanation of what a person would feel while performing them.

They learned the small receiving chamber in pieces.

The outer source shutter had to be closed before the isolation face could be moved. The correct seat mattered down to a lip Mavet could barely catch with her nail. A vessel lifted before the charge was settled shed its brine in a fine cold mist and cracked along a line none of them had seen. One placed too deeply would not release from the seat until they bled the whole branch back into the fixed apparatus, keeping everyone above the marked line while the bronze sang against their teeth.

Lyra tried to move a control during that song. Corven caught her wrist.

“Wait.”

“It is closed.”

“Then waiting won’t hurt it.”

The song lowered, thinned, and ceased. The vessel came free when Mavet tried it again. Its ceramic body was intact. The bronze liner had folded inward, taking the shape of a clenched mouth. They carried it out and put it beside the first failures.

Their first successful vessel held less than she intended. It was squat enough to fit in a carrying cage against a person’s thigh, with the bronze closure pinned across its broad mouth. The outside stayed cold even when they took it upstairs and stood it beside the guardroom kettle. A drop of ordinary water placed on its side gathered into a tight bead and remained there instead of running down.

They tested it in a disused stone recess off the outer gallery. Mavet had put up a sound wooden frame, with a sacrificial moving face and a brace whose grain they could inspect. Nothing lived on the other side. The adjacent passage was closed for the trial. Vaska stood at the entrance to stop her own curious people from improving their view.

Lyra marked the known brace with chalk and brine. She set the vessel’s mouth against the prepared contact, keeping one hand on the fixed frame. The closure came away under her thumb.

For an instant the room seemed to narrow around the place she touched.

The brace bowed. The grain whitened. A split ran through it with a noise so small that she leaned closer to make sure she had heard. Then the wood folded into itself and the moving face slammed against the floor.

Lyra was thrown onto one knee. Her hand stayed against the rim too long. Cold ran through the wrist, elbow, and shoulder, deep enough that the old injury seemed to have been made that moment. She could not find the edge of the force quickly enough to turn it away from her own bones.

Corven pulled her back by the belt. The vessel rolled in its cage and struck the wall. Its lining had gone dull. Water ran from it, ordinary water now, making a narrow dark path across the dust.

Vaska came into the recess. “How many times can it do that?”

Lyra was trying to get her breath. Mavet answered.

“Once.”

“You’ll need more than one.”

“We’ll need better contact,” Lyra said.

Her voice sounded wrong even to her, flat and far away. She sat until she could feel the fingers of her right hand. Beyond the intact frame, the brace lay broken in two places.

Corven crouched beside her. “You had time to let go.”

“I had time to notice I should.”

“That will have to become the same thing.”

She looked at the spent vessel. Its mouth was no larger than a cup. The room around it had made space for a darkness much greater than the room. She wanted to touch the lining again and see whether anything remained. She kept her hands on her knees until the desire passed enough to be examined.

They made the next brace thicker. They did not fill another vessel that day.

On the last evening of Iven’s undertaking, Lyra took him to supper at Harrow instead of having the food carried to the guarded room.

He inspected the chair before sitting. Sova, who had delivered the meal, told him it had outlasted better men. He asked whether that was a claim about the chair or the food. She told him to eat while he could still tell the difference.

Vaska wore her repaired coat. A dark strip concealed the bloodstain along the hem. She had chosen the strip so that it looked intentional, and Lyra admired the result enough to say so. Vaska turned a little in her chair, showing the line of the new trim.

“I thought you might prefer me poorly dressed. Easier to explain your employees.”

“You aren’t an ornament.”

“Pity. It sounds restful.”

They ate fish in a thick sauce, with bread to clean the dish. Belis and Rima had come to deliver the finished handles. Belis sat with his good ear toward the table. When several people spoke at once he smiled as if he had heard, and Rima touched the edge of his cup to draw his attention back before answering the question he had missed. Lyra watched her do it once, then moved her own chair so he would not have to keep turning.

He told a long story about a man who wanted handles that could be used without getting dirty. The story had no point beyond the man’s unreasonable expectations and Belis’s pleasure in recalling his own replies. Nobody hurried him to find one.

After the meal, Lyra took Iven’s written undertaking from the drawer and marked it complete. She paid the remainder in front of Vaska, then gave him the travel paper and the small knife kept from him during the watch.

“Where will you go?” she asked.

“You’re asking after giving me the knife?”

“It is a small knife.”

He turned it in his hands. “I have a room near the glass market. Whether I still have it depends on who has spoken to the keeper.”

“You can send someone to ask.”

“I can go.”

“Yes.”

He waited for a condition. She did not add one. Vaska watched him with interest, as if discovering what a person did with an open door was another kind of gambling.

“I have more comparison work,” Lyra said. “Paid by the day. You would come here when required. No sleeping under watch.”

“What about messages?”

“If you work for the Prince again, tell me before you bring his people to my room.”

“He may not consider me qualified to work for him now.”

“Then you have a reason to charge me more.”

Iven laughed. It was the first unguarded sound she had heard from him. He put the knife away, named a rate she refused, and accepted the lower one after a discussion long enough for Sova to take the last plates.

He left that evening. The guard did not follow.

When Lyra went upstairs, the three usable empty vessels stood against the wall, padded in their cages. She had ordered their closures marked so a spent vessel could not be mistaken for a charged one in bad light. The broken trial brace lay beside them. She picked up one half, ran her thumb along the compressed grain, and laid it down again.

The little pot stood on the table beside her slate. She changed its water and put a shred of fish beside a fragment of shell. A light scraping beneath her thumb made her look at her hand. It rested on the bare table. In the pot, the crab emerged and dragged the fish beneath its shelter. She watched until it had gone, then tied the lid down.

From the slip came Vaska’s laughter, joined by Belis’s louder voice. The sound pleased Lyra. She stood at the window listening until the little room felt too open behind her. Then she closed one shutter, moved the chair close to the wall, and began writing the changes for the next trial.

Chapter 9 The Prince’s share

The invitation came wrapped around a piece of the first vessel.

Lyra recognized the glaze before she recognized the shape. The broken ceramic had been swept from the maker’s floor, sold with other spoil, or given to someone who asked a useful question in the right voice. Its inner edge carried the mark Mavet had made to show where the bronze bit too deeply.

Vaska turned it in her fingers. “Somebody should have broken it smaller.”

“It would still be clay.”

“It would have made him work harder to send a message.”

Lyra read the invitation. The Prince offered an afternoon at a house above the eastern navigation canal. She could bring one companion, armed as a courtesy between people who understood the value of being armed. He would bring the same number. The house’s servants would remain. Lyra could inspect the room before he entered.

“He expects you to come,” Vaska said.

“Yes.”

“Shall I make him unhappy?”

“Come with me.”

Vaska looked down at her coat. “I’ll need something else.”

“So will I.”

They went to the market before noon. Lyra had intended to buy a plain dark coat with a sleeve that allowed the right arm room to move. The seamstress found one in a good blue cloth and insisted she try it near the window. The color changed where the light touched it. Lyra turned her wrist and watched the silk lining slide past her skin.

“That one costs more,” Vaska said.

“I can read the tag.”

“The tag doesn’t say what the lining feels like.”

“It should.”

Vaska smiled and took a coat of her own from the rail, a deep green with fastenings that lay flat enough not to catch a drawn knife. The seamstress watched them inspect the seams and asked whether either intended to use a chair. Lyra said occasionally. The woman altered the hem while they stood, refusing to be impressed by either their weapons or their urgency.

Lyra paid from her own purse. The coat had nothing to do with a repair fund or an employee’s wages. She liked the movement of it when she walked, the way the blue made her cold hands look deliberate rather than ill. At the canal, Vaska stopped her and straightened one fastening.

“There. Now he can regret the occasion.”

Lyra laughed. They crossed the bridge together, with the city warm around them and the new cloth touching her at every step.

The house smelled of orange peel burned in a shallow dish. Its receiving room overlooked the canal through three tall windows, one open to the sound of water against the quay. A servant offered them wine and let Vaska inspect the passage behind the screen without protest. The wine was good enough that refusing it would require a reason beyond suspicion.

Lyra drank a little. Vaska kept hers untouched until she had looked at both doors, the window drop, and the broad cupboard near the wall. She opened the cupboard. It contained cushions.

“Disappointing,” the Prince said from the doorway.

His companion was a lean man in an ordinary coat, with the quiet hands of someone paid to put them in sudden places. He remained by the inner door. The Prince came into the room alone.

The father’s skin lay across his shoulders beneath a thin outer cape, its edge showing at the wrist where a cloak would usually be lined. Lyra had seen the material wet and streaked with salt, used in the pressure of a fight. Here it had been brushed and treated until it looked soft. A fine seam ran beneath the Prince’s ear. The skin there fitted his face imperfectly, as though the face had changed its intention since the seam was made.

“My lady.”

“Your people have been buying rubbish.”

“Occasionally it proves useful.”

He glanced toward Vaska. “Mistress Rell. You have improved your employment.”

“I improved my coat.”

“A clearer achievement.”

He offered his hand toward the chairs. Lyra chose one from which she could see the window and the door. Vaska sat near the screen, with her cup on the floor beside her boot. The Prince noticed the position, then sat opposite Lyra.

“I will pay the damage to the outer watchroom,” he said.

“You haven’t seen it.”

“I employed the people living in it. I have a good idea.”

“You also employed the people trying to break the new brace.”

“They have stopped.”

“For the afternoon?”

“For as long as we find something worth discussing.”

He took a folded drawing from the table beside him. It had been made on thin cloth rather than paper, so the folds opened without splitting. Lyra saw a bronze seat in section, a ring of marks around a lower face, and an area left blank where the person drawing it had been unable to see behind the structure.

“Zakov,” she said.

“A cellar there. A large cellar, inconveniently built before the house above it.”

“The collar came from it.”

“A replacement was attempted there. The collar came from the failed attempt.”

“Then your people knew the shoulder was wrong.”

“They knew it failed. They were less interested in agreeing why.”

He let her look. He did not offer her the drawing to keep. The similarities to the Silkstrand rubbing were close enough to make her lean forward. One set of seat marks had the same intervals. Another differed. The northern apparatus had a fracture across a part that remained whole beneath the harbor here.

“What is it holding?” she asked.

“Something that has remained where it was put longer than the people who put it there.”

“That could describe a poor chest of drawers.”

“This has been more expensive to move.”

He smiled at her answer as though the conversation pleased him. She could believe it did. He liked an appetite that could recognize the meal before it was served.

“One surviving seat here may steady the northern working,” he said. “I had hoped to take it out whole. Your recent attention has made the approaches less convenient.”

“You bought pumps fit for a busy harbor to drain a passage scarcely anyone used.”

“Yes.”

“And labor that could stay beneath it.”

“Some jobs are not improved by changing the workers every season.”

Vaska shifted her boot. The Prince’s companion looked at the movement. Nothing followed it.

Lyra put the drawing down. “You don’t know whether removing the seat will break what stands around it.”

“No.”

“Or whether the northern apparatus will take it.”

“No.”

The admissions were more persuasive than assurance would have been. She had seen the cost of believing an ordinary bleed was ordinary because it was shaped like one. Belis had to turn his good ear toward a room to hear it now.

“What are you offering?”

The Prince brought out a second sheet. This one had words on it and no empty spaces.

She would retain Harrow and the outer works already taken. He would recognize her use of the receiving branch and provide components at an agreed cost. The two companies would divide the sale of carried charges or prepared services along terms written plainly enough for her to calculate. The inner apparatus and its removal would remain his undertaking. She would have secure passage through specified Salt Ward routes, including the freight channel east of Cinder Basin. Her property could pass to a successor she named. No other house would have a right to reassign it merely because they disliked that successor.

She read the succession clause again. He had learned exactly which part of the earlier settlement she refused.

“Your share depends on the main works,” she said.

“Our shares do.”

“And you control the main works.”

“I have spent a good deal acquiring them.”

“So have I.”

“You have spent enough to discover that you might spend more.”

He leaned back. A faint rasp continued beneath the sound of his breath. The room’s curtains moved in the open window. Lyra could smell the orange peel again, sweet enough to conceal the salt until she knew to look for it.

“There is a house adjoining this one,” he said. “A good room above the water, a workroom below. It can be included if Harrow has become small.”

“Harrow is mine.”

“This would be too.”

Vaska looked at Lyra, then away. The offer was not a bedroom to which somebody else held the key. Lyra could sell the house, use it, refuse to live in it. The Prince had put that much on the page. It would make the next year easier. It would let her bring vessels up through a safe passage, pay Belis for work without wondering which cargo had to arrive first, and sleep in a room whose roof did not meet her forehead.

She wanted it for a moment. She imagined the long table she would put beneath the window, the lock on the tool room, the space around her bed. The wanting did not shame her. It made the next part exact.

“I want the source,” she said.

“You would have access.”

“I want to decide when that access ends.”

“Then buy a source elsewhere.”

“There isn’t one elsewhere that you have shown me.”

His mouth changed at the corner, not quite a smile. “You have a generous understanding of what I owe you.”

“I haven’t asked you to give it away.”

“No. You have asked me to sign an account of how you intend to take it.”

She placed the sheet on top of the drawing. “Keep your inner share, and I will always be buying permission to do the part of the work that matters.”

“Keep the arrangement, and you will be rich enough to discover other things that matter.”

“I have discovered them. I still want this.”

The Prince looked at her for a while. Then he laughed, a low sound that never fully separated from the rasp.

“Maeron did recognize a useful hunger.”

Lyra’s fingers tightened on the chair arm. She had expected threats against a boat or a worker. The dead man’s name came through a different door.

“He’s not here to make this agreement,” she said.

“No. You were thorough about that.”

Vaska rose a little in her chair. Lyra lifted one finger without looking at her. The movement was small enough that the Prince’s companion might mistake it for an adjustment of the cuff. Vaska sat back.

The Prince watched the exchange with pleasure. “You are learning.”

Lyra stood. “Keep the house.”

“The offer will remain through tomorrow.”

“It needn’t.”

“Offers have uses even when people refuse them.”

He rose to walk her toward the door. As he moved, the preserved skin slipped at one wrist, exposing a line of his own flesh. The line opened while she watched. Beneath it was a dark depth that should have required more than the width of an arm. He drew the old skin across it and pressed the fold flat. Salt gathered under his fingernails.

Vaska saw it. Her face did not change, but she put her cup on the table rather than attempt to drink what remained.

At the door the Prince said, “Blackglass Cut is an expensive passage to enter without an agreement.”

Lyra turned. “Then I will examine the charges.”

“Do. They are seldom fully appreciated beforehand.”

A man was waiting on the lower stair when they came out. He had an account case beneath one arm and a small line of blood on his lip. The Prince’s servant tried to send him away before he could speak to Lyra.

“I have delivered six times,” the man said. “Six. Your master has the goods. If he wants to dispute the seals, he should do it before he opens the barrels.”

The servant kept his hand against the man’s chest. It looked gentle until the man tried to move past it. Then the servant twisted the coat and struck him once below the ribs, hard enough to fold him over the account case.

Vaska caught the servant’s wrist before he could strike again. She did it with no haste at all.

“Let the stairs clear first,” she said.

The servant looked at her green coat, the knife hanging plain at her side, and the Prince above them. He let go. Vaska released his wrist. The man with the case slid down onto the step and sat with his forehead against the leather.

Lyra could hear the Prince coming nearer behind her.

“He is persistent,” he said. “It is a quality I used to admire in suppliers.”
