“Pay him, then.”

“He has confused payment with prompt payment.”

“Perhaps give him the distinction in writing.”

The Prince smiled. “You are leaving before the best part of the afternoon.”

Lyra went down the steps. She did not take the man with her or promise that Harrow could absorb every trade made difficult by the Prince. At the bottom she asked his name and the kind of barrels he supplied. He gave both between breaths. She told him where to send an ordinary offer.

Vaska waited until they had crossed the bridge before speaking.

“He would keep the terms.”

“For as long as keeping them was useful.”

“So would most people you buy from.”

“Yes.”

They walked through a narrow market where sellers had hung colored cloth above the stalls. The light turned Vaska’s coat almost black. She stopped to buy sour plums and ate one while Lyra looked at a stall of knives she did not need.

“You could take the house,” Vaska said. “Make money while you learn the source. Fight later.”

“He would move the part he wanted.”

“Perhaps.”

“He offered the receiving branch because he wants the center before I understand what he is taking out.”

“That doesn’t make the house a bad house.”

“No.”

Vaska held out a plum. Lyra took it. The sourness made her mouth fill with water.

“Do you want me to tell you it was all a trick?” Vaska asked.

“No.”

“Good. It was an expensive offer. I’d rather follow someone who knew what she had refused.”

Lyra looked at her. Vaska ate another plum and watched a woman at the next stall haggle over the price of a scarf. Her expression showed no wish to turn the moment into an oath.

At Harrow, the regular work was nearly finished. Orrel had the last load under cover. Belis and Rima were collecting their tools. Iven had sent a note naming a different pattern of locking pin in the northern drawing and suggesting that Lyra stop asking him questions through runners who did not know which part they were carrying.

She went upstairs, took off the blue coat, and hung it where the damp of her other clothes could not reach it. Then she took the brine vessel and went to find Corven.

He listened while she described the next working she intended. She did not mention the offered house until the end.

“You could have taken it,” he said.

“I know.”

“You could still take the district agreement.”

“I know that too.”

He looked at the practice lines on the cloth she had brought. Her marks were not his chain’s syllables. He touched the place where she intended to hold contact inside the receiving cell and asked whether she could release it from there.

“Yes.”

“If you change your mind?”

“Yes.”

“If you can’t speak?”

She showed him the retrieval cord and the separate stop signal. He asked her to explain both again. She did it, finding a mistake in her own arrangement before he had to point it out. They changed the line’s position so it would remain reachable if she was driven against the side of the cell.

He set her slate beside the practice cloth. She read the cuts beneath salt water, covered them, and traced the sequence without his correction. Beside it, the crab waited beneath its shell. She checked the cord on the lid.

“The slate goes with you,” he said. “The living witness stays by the cell in clean salt water. You will have to tend both after tomorrow.”

“And swear.”

“Yes. The work you’ve learned will still be work. These won’t make it safe.”

Only then did he ask what she meant to give.

Lyra looked down at her hands. “What comes back with me.”

“That isn’t a measure.”

“No.”

He sat for a long time before saying what work he would agree to do outside the cell. She wrote it down as he spoke. The private part of the descent did not appear among his tasks.

Chapter 10 The descent

She woke before the people paid to wake her.

The room above Harrow was dark except for the narrow gap beneath the shutter. Lyra lay still, listening to the ordinary occupation of the building: a foot on the stair, a cup put down too hard, somebody coughing in the shed. She knew which boards would complain when the night watch came up. She knew the smell of the harbor at this water, with the weed exposed beneath the south anchorage. She had bought the room, repaired it, slept in it through enough nights that waking there no longer required a moment to remember whose door stood between her and the world.

She wanted to come back to it.

She sat up and lit the lamp. The blue coat hung on its hook, useless for what she intended. She put on the old dark one, the sleeve altered to leave the injured arm free. On the table lay the cloth carrying her pressure lines, the prepared brine, and the closure for the trial vessel. She had cut a second contact cloth in case the first tore. She packed both, then the slate in its wet wrapping. The crab caught her fingertip while she checked its water. She waited for it to let go before tying the lid down.

The small repair knife lay beside them. Maeron had given it to her before she knew what he was willing to do with other people’s returns from the sea. She touched the worn handle and remembered his fingers pressing hers into a better grip. She missed the pleasure he took in her learning. She also remembered the moment his voice took her body away from her. The memories would not take turns merely because she wanted a clear morning.

She put the knife in her pocket.

On a separate sheet she had written the payments that would continue if she did not come back. Belis and Rima’s work, the injured man’s treatment, wages through the current agreements, the price of the boat waiting at the inspection mouth. She had named the people authorized to complete them. She had not given the property away. If she died, the ordinary claim would have to be settled without a last command from her trying to govern it through every future day.

A knock came. Vaska entered carrying bread, a pot of butter, and a bowl of stewed fruit.

“You can still eat,” she said.

“I intended to.”

“People intend all sorts of things before going below. Then they leave the food and tell the person who brought it that eating would be a distraction.”

Lyra cleared a space. Vaska sat on the bed because the second chair had gone downstairs for a man with an injured knee. She watched Lyra butter the bread.

“Do you want to come?” Lyra asked.

“I am coming to the outer room.”

“I meant farther.”

“No.”

The answer came so quickly that Lyra laughed. Vaska took a piece of bread from her plate and dipped it into the fruit.

“I like what I can do now,” Vaska said. “Perhaps I’ll want something else later. Today I’m keeping the door.”

“It’s a useful job.”

“I know. That is why I want to be paid for it.”

Lyra passed her the completed arrangement. Vaska read it carefully, moving her lips over one long sentence. She asked for a clarification about the people who could call the retreat. Lyra added Mavet’s name beside Corven’s. Vaska signed, then pushed the bowl toward her again.

They ate until it was empty. The fruit was tart, with a bitter place where the cook had left a strip of peel too long in the pot. Lyra liked it. Vaska liked it enough to scrape the last spoonful from the side and lick the spoon without pretending the morning made her more dignified.

When they rose, the first light had reached the floor beneath the shutter. Lyra left the room tidy enough that nobody would have to decide which scattered object she had thought important. She took her keys with her.

Corven read the retrieval signals back at the mouth of the receiving passage.

One sustained pull meant hold the controls where they were. Two sharp pulls meant stop the source and bring her back. A slack line accompanied by no answer meant the same. Before the inner isolation, either he or Mavet could call the end of the trial. After Lyra chose to close herself into the receiving interval, the line would be drawn clear of the seal. The outside release would remain in Corven’s hand. Three blows on the bronze return plate, or the agreed time without a reply, would make him operate it whether she liked the result or not.

The release would end the exposure. It could not make her unchoose what she had already done with it.

She repeated the signals. Mavet asked her to point to the release from inside the cell. She did. Then they tested the mechanism with no source open, sending the weight onto its stop and recovering it by the outside lever. The lever worked. Corven made them test it again after the leather on the line grew wet.

Esten’s assistant had brought spare pins, dry cloth, and a little charcoal brazier for the upper room. There would be warmth after the descent if Lyra could still use it. Orrel waited above the narrow steps, where the equipment could be hauled out without crowding the cell. Iven had given them the last comparison drawing and remained on the surface. He had said he would not stand near the receiving face while somebody deliberately became more willing to bear it.

Lyra had paid for his drawing and accepted his absence.

The cell lay beneath them in the lamplight, its bronze face dull and wet. The original manifold remained in the wall. They had not moved its central restraint or opened the main pale face. The receiving branch was all they had made usable. She could reach the prepared vessel at its seat, the local isolation, and the fixed edge against which her own body would work. The space was narrow enough that she could touch both sides without stretching.

Corven came down to the second step. “There is time to stop.”

“Yes.”

“I am saying it now because I won’t say it while you’re trying to listen to the pressure.”

She looked at him. He seemed older in this light, the hollows beside his mouth deeper than she remembered. His chain had been wrapped so no mark could catch against the source fittings. His eel rested above in a vessel of clean salt water. Beside the cell, Lyra’s little pot stood secured behind a bronze lip. She set her wrapped slate on the step, ready to take down with her. Corven watched her check the pot’s fastening, then looked at her face.

“What changed for you?” she asked.

“When?”

“When you decided you would keep going.”

He looked into the cell. “I kept deciding. It didn’t happen once.”

She nodded. That was a less useful answer for a person who wanted one morning to settle the rest of her life. She could use it anyway.

He took her hand briefly. His fingers were cool. Hers were colder.

“Come back when the work is done,” he said.

“I intend to.”

“Good.”

He went up. Lyra descended the last step and stood before the low face.

She began with ordinary care.

She laid the slate in the shallow water at her feet, where she could read its cuts without taking either hand from the working. The vessel sat in its matched bronze seat. Its outer cage had been removed so no wood would swell against the join. Lyra checked the lining, the closure, the pins that held it, and the brine along the fixed rim. She ran a strip of clean cloth around the seat and looked at what it brought away. Nothing. She did it again because the first inspection had been too quick.

At the cell’s side she laid the repair knife, its blade closed, where she could reach it if a cord caught. She did not need blood to persuade the apparatus to work. She had learned that much. What she intended to give was not a drop that could be wiped from the stone afterward.

The contact cloth went beneath her left palm. Her right hand settled on the prepared moving face. Her feet stood where she had marked them, one slightly behind the other so a change of force would not take both at once. She called to Mavet.

The first shutter moved.

Ordinary brine reached the shallow lip. Lyra tasted salt without putting it to her mouth. She breathed slowly, letting her body find the enclosure it already preferred. The stone at her back was neither friendly nor hostile. It held the weight above it. She had trusted such things when they were sound and suffered when they were not.

She spoke the opening words of the pressure practice, the ones she had used at doors, on frames, beside the hatch while men begged from the other side. The familiarity did not soften that last memory. Her hand knew exactly where the iron had struck her palm. She continued.

Mavet opened the source to the first mark.

A depth entered the cell without filling it.

The lamp still burned outside. Lyra could see its reflection in the bronze, a little wavering shape that appeared farther away the longer she watched. The visible water scarcely moved. Her ribs tightened as though a heavy hand had closed around them from every side at once.

She wanted to draw a larger breath. She took a smaller one instead.

The source was within the interval they had tested. She could stop here, fill the vessel, close the branch, and go home with the limited result already known. The pulse would break a small brace. Corven could help her release it. She could build a business around those careful uses and let the Prince own the main works behind the wall. She could keep the blue coat, the room, the mornings when somebody brought her fruit.

Lyra looked at the return plate. She could touch it. She could call. Nobody would have to defeat her to get her out.

“Khemesh,” she said.

The name seemed to descend farther than her voice should have carried.

She named the depth she had practiced carrying, the cold that had already altered her rooms. Then she spoke her own name, clearly enough to hear it in the narrow space.

“I come as myself.”

Her voice shook. She let it shake.

“I will keep your rites. I will carry the deep.”

The cuts in the slate darkened. Behind the bronze lip, the crab pressed against the side of its pot nearest her. She could feel each small point of contact along her own ribs.

“Never helpless,” she said, so quietly that Corven could not have heard.

No answer came. The pressure continued through the stone.

Outside, Corven called the first check. She answered with the agreed pull. Hold.

The second source mark brought pain behind her eyes. The bronze seemed to lean away from its fixed seat, though her fingers remained across both surfaces. In the thin gap, something pale uncurled past another length of itself. Round hollows travelled along its underside, each opening onto further darkness. She could find neither an end nor a place where the lengths joined. The gap was too narrow even for her smallest finger.

Her stomach turned. She swallowed the rising sickness and felt brine at the back of her throat, although the water had never reached her mouth.

One of the pale lengths passed the opening again. The nearer hollows were large enough to take her body; those beyond them diminished through a distance the cell could not contain. She could not tell whether anything had noticed her. Her hands, braced against the rim, had stopped shaking. She wanted to remain where a force this vast could reach her. She wanted to learn how to bring it close when someone else’s hand closed on hers.

The words of the practice became difficult. She said them anyway, slowly enough to keep each connected to the place she touched. The moving face was this. The fixed rim was this. The force came through the source, bore against the receiving space, sought every weak agreement between one surface and the next. She did not have to stop its existence. She had to know where it ended when she chose to give it an edge.

Her right arm began to tremble. The old injury supplied a precise, ordinary pain beneath the impossible pressure. She was grateful for its precision. She moved her elbow closer to her side, preserving contact.

“Answer,” Corven called.

She pulled once. Hold.

Then she took the line from her wrist.

“I am closing the isolation,” she said.

Mavet repeated it. Corven repeated it after her. They had not mistaken the moment for a failed signal or a word swallowed by the cell.

Lyra drew the line clear, placed it on the dry step, and moved the inner isolation into its seat.

The sound of the outside room changed. She could still hear it, but it arrived through distances that had no relation to the stone. A boot scraped above her. The scrape went on too long. Somewhere much farther away, a chain struck a boat’s side. She could hear the individual links meet.

The source shutter closed behind the received interval, separating the cell’s held depth from the great fixed apparatus. The charge was contained here now, in a space she could leave only by ending what she had opened. The vessel stood at its seat. Her hands joined the fixed edge and the face. She had made herself part of the working because she wanted to learn the part no tool could hold for her.

Her knees bent under the pressure.

For a moment she was certain the room was narrowing physically. She felt the wall touch her back where it had not touched before. She tried to turn and found the movement too large. Panic rose, hot and startling in a body gone cold. There was no direction in which she could stretch without meeting more of the same enclosing weight.

She wanted her mother.

The want had no face attached to it. A bell swung beside a knee. Someone’s fist held her dress tight under her arms; her feet could not reach the doorstep. She had cried into the cloth while the people who burned the houses carried her away.

Her left foot slipped against the cell’s wet stone. She placed it again. The return plate was within reach. At her feet, the cuts she had made in the slate remained legible beneath the water. She read the next one and stayed.

She leaned into the fixed stone and found the shape of her own hand.

The depth had an edge. It was not where her eyes put it.

She closed them.

Without sight, the pressure separated into directions. The weight that had seemed everywhere met the prepared rim and turned. The vessel’s mouth held a smaller darkness within the greater one. Her body lay against one boundary and had been trying to pull away from it, making the force cross the wrong part of her chest. She let her shoulder settle. She loosened her jaw. The need to gulp air receded without becoming easy breathing.

She was still afraid. She could also feel what the next movement would do.

Lyra shifted the contact of her right palm by the width of a finger. The force turned toward the receiving seat. The vessel’s lining darkened. A bead of water rose from the lip and held against the underside of the bronze closure, refusing the direction in which water ought to fall.

She laughed once, without intending to. The sound frightened her more than the pain. It came from a depth in her chest she had not known how to open, rich and hollow and terribly pleased.

She kept her hands where they were.

The closing words came slowly. She spoke each with the place she meant it to hold. The force gathered against the bounded face instead of crossing through her ribs. She could distinguish what belonged to the source from what she was carrying in herself. She could not make the second part leave merely by completing the first.

She had known there would be a return. She had not known how close the return would feel to bringing the whole cell home inside her.

The outside plate sounded twice. Corven checking the time.

Lyra answered with three deliberate blows from the heel of her hand.

The release moved. For an instant all the pressure seemed to seek the opening at once. She kept her left palm on the fixed edge and turned the moving face under her right, guiding the last part into the prepared seat. Her shoulder cried out. The vessel’s closure went home beneath her thumb. She drove its pin with the butt of the repair knife and held it until the old apparatus’s isolation took the remainder.

Then the cell was a cell again.

She could not stand.

Corven reached her before the lamp did. His hand came around her upper arm, carefully above the old injury, and she recoiled from the sensation of another body inside the space she had just learned to bear.

He let go immediately.

“Lyra.”

“I know.”

“Can you stand?”

She tried. The floor seemed farther away than her feet, then too close. She put one palm against the wall and found that this helped. Mavet remained by the controls, watching the pins and the vessel instead of asking her to decide whether she was well.

“Both isolations closed,” Mavet said. “The charge is seated. Don’t touch the mouth.”

Corven lifted the slate from the water and laid it where Lyra could reach it. “Your codex.”

She wrapped it herself and slid it into its case. He untied the little pot from its fastening; the crab was moving beneath the lid. She let him secure both at her waist.

Her teeth struck each other once, hard enough to hurt.

Corven offered his arm again without taking hers. She put her left hand on it and got to her feet. His sleeve felt warm. The warmth was unpleasant for the first few breaths, then merely strange.

On the steps she heard Vaska say something in the upper room. The words were indistinct, but the sound came through the stone as though Vaska stood just behind her. Lyra turned toward it. Corven steadied himself against the wall.

“What?” he asked.

“Nothing.”

His free hand had gone to the chain. She faced the steps again before he could ask her to explain.

They took the charged vessel out only after Mavet had finished checking it. It went into the carrying cage made by Belis and Rima, the handle shaped so a wet hand could keep its hold. Lyra watched the wood receive the strange cold of the jar and thought of the supper when they had argued about its curve.

In the outer guardroom, Vaska had a blanket ready. She put it around Lyra’s shoulders, then paused when her own fingers touched the skin at Lyra’s neck.

“That’s new,” she said.

Lyra looked down. The shadow beneath her hand lay too thick on the blanket, dark enough to seem wet. When she moved her fingers, it followed a little slowly. The lamp itself had not dimmed. The room seemed to have acquired a deeper place beside her chair.

“Water,” Lyra said.

Orrel brought a cup. She thanked him. He looked at her mouth before putting it down, then stepped back farther than he needed to. She heard the cup settle on the table through her own bones.

She drank. The water tasted intensely of the clay that held it. Bread tasted of grain, ash, a trace of the baker’s oven smoke. She could swallow only a little. The room’s far wall kept drawing her attention. She moved her chair closer to it and felt the effort of remaining upright lessen at once.

Vaska watched. “Was it worth it?”

Lyra put down the cup. She could have answered before she knew what the next hour would cost. She could have frightened the room by pretending to know everything that had happened to her.

“I held it,” she said.

Vaska’s eyes went to the caged vessel. “I can see that.”

Lyra smiled. The expression felt unfamiliar on her own face. Vaska smiled back, though the skin along her forearms had tightened with cold.

Two of the outer watch asked to finish their work at the end of the agreed term. Neither wished to sleep in the guardroom after seeing Lyra return through the stair. One apologized so earnestly that she wanted to shake him.

“Take the work you’ve been paid for,” she said. “Leave when it ends.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“I heard what you meant.”

They went. She paid the remaining days without deducting the night they had lost to fear. It was a practical expense. She also disliked being feared by men whose names she knew. The dislike did not make her want to return to the cell and undo the morning.

Orrel asked to keep to the surface winch on later jobs. She agreed, after asking him twice whether he meant no more night work or no more work below stone. He meant the latter. She wrote it where she would see it before hiring him again.

Belis came that evening to inspect the cage after its first use. He stood on her left and did not hear when she greeted him. Rima touched his elbow. He turned, smiled, and asked her to say it again.

Lyra did. The deeper note in her voice made his smile falter. Then he looked at the handle and complained that Mavet had put one of the straps back twisted. Rima made him sit while she fixed it. The old damage in his ear had not altered because Lyra could hear a rope turning against a post outside the closed window.

Later, alone, she took off her clothes slowly. Bruises had risen along her ribs and the inside of the right arm. There was dried blood beneath one nostril. The mirror showed the same eyes until she looked away from them; then the reflected space around her face seemed to deepen. She turned the mirror toward the wall.

The bed stood too far from the corner. She moved it close enough that the plaster would be within reach when she slept. The effort hurt her arm. She stopped before finishing the last few inches and accepted the gap.

From the shed came Vaska’s voice giving the next watch its orders. Lyra could hear more than the voice: a man shifting his weight on a sore foot, leather creaking as somebody bent to lift a lantern. A quick dry click came from behind her. She turned, certain someone had entered with a knife. Her door was locked. Through the window she saw the watchman below release the lantern catch a second time; the same click reached her from the wall beside the bed.

She shut the window. The voices remained, quieter but distinct.

She put one palm against the cool wall and waited for her breathing to settle. The charged vessel had been locked in the dry lower room, marked and watched. There would be work for it soon. For now she wanted to sleep in the room she had bought.

When she lay down, the enclosure felt good enough to make her close her eyes with relief.

Chapter 11 Three black windows

Blackglass Cut received its tar on the evening tide, when the people who would have objected to the smell were indoors eating.

The first cart brought empty casks. The second brought filled ones from the inland burners. A shallow lighter took them through the cut to the Prince’s stores, beneath three windows made of glass so dark that a face behind it appeared only when a lamp was brought near. Men watched from those windows. Below them, a salt-worked gate closed the lower entrance, its two leaves meeting against a heavy brace set between the walls.

Lyra knew the brace because she had paid a cooper to complain about it.

The man had delivered a cask too broad for the opening while the gate stood at its ordinary width. The keeper had cursed him, moved the gate, and made two workers lift the crosspiece from its lower sockets. The cooper had watched how it was held. He had also measured the distance between the sockets with a length of cord concealed among his own hoops. He charged Lyra for the spoiled cask, the measurement, and the risk of being remembered.

She paid all three.

Vaska had worked the cut before, carrying goods whose owners preferred them counted somewhere else. She knew the side landing and the narrow passage behind the tar store. She did not know how many men the Prince had added since the outer works were taken. They spent four evenings finding out.

On the fifth, Lyra stood with her beneath the arch of a shuttered shop and watched the last cart arrive. Her codex lay flat against her hip in its wet case; the crab’s pot was secured beside it. The charged vessel rested in its cage at her feet, covered by a cloth that looked too thin to conceal anything worth stealing. She could feel its cold through the boards beneath her boots.

“Seven above,” Vaska said. “Four below when the lighter comes. Two at the farther boom.”

“More than yesterday.”

“He heard something.”

“He always hears something.”

Vaska looked down the cut. “The question is whether he believed the useful part.”

They had allowed the Prince’s watchers to see two of Lyra’s people buying rope near the southern road. A cart had left Harrow in that direction with timber large enough to suggest work at a different landing. The cart carried real timber for a real customer. Lyra had paid extra to have it delivered at an inconvenient hour. She did not know whether the watchers had followed. She would know when the first alarm traveled.

Corven came under the arch carrying his chain wrapped around his shoulder. His task was the side passage and the retreat. He would hold that opening long enough for the crew to enter or come out. He had not agreed to pursue men into the Salt Ward after the store fell. Lyra had written the end of the job as carefully as its beginning.

Orrel waited aboard the boat below the public steps, with the portable winch secured on deck. He would remain in the open. Rusk, whose thigh still stiffened if he sat too long, had the lower line work. Vaska had put him there because it required a steady hand and did not require him to run the stairs. He disliked the arrangement, which had not persuaded her to change it.

Lyra looked at each place before giving the first signal. No one else would arrange them for her if the work went badly. The thought sharpened rather than frightened her. She had spent days making the positions fit what the people could do. Now she wanted to see them move.

The second cart rolled toward the gate.

She lifted the vessel.

The cooper had put the narrowest filled cask at the rear. Lyra climbed onto the cart behind it while the driver argued with the keeper about a missing mark on the lid. The argument had been rehearsed only in the sense that both men had argued over the same mark before. This time the driver was being paid to continue until he heard the side latch open.

Vaska went along the wall with two of her people. Corven entered the rear passage from the water stair. Lyra could see the edge of his coat vanish into the dark. She listened for him and heard, beneath the driver’s complaint, the scrape of metal against stone and a breath taken through clenched teeth.

The new hearing did not tell her whose breath it was. She waited for the agreed sound.

Two taps on the back of the cart.

Lyra slid down beside the cask. The gate leaf stood open enough for a worker to pass. Inside, the brace occupied its lower sockets, leaving the gap above it clear for goods lifted over. Along the left socket, salt had hardened over the bronze straps in a pattern too regular to be ordinary damp. The Prince’s ward held the brace in a kind of confidence the wood itself had never earned.

She stepped through the gap.

The keeper turned from the driver. “Who are you?”

“The new charge for passage,” she said, and drove her elbow into his throat.

He staggered, catching the cask. Her right arm hurt at once. She used the left hand to push him farther, into the path of the man behind him. Vaska came through the side latch and struck the second man with the hilt of her knife. The first tried to recover his breath and reach for the bell.

A bolt struck the cart’s side above Lyra’s head.

The upper windows were open.

“Down,” Vaska called.

Lyra dropped behind the brace. The charged vessel hit its cage against the paving, hard enough to frighten her. She caught it, checked the closure by touch, and set its mouth against the left socket where the cooper’s cord had told her the brace took its load.

Chalk first. Brine across the known face. Her left palm on fixed stone; the right on the vessel’s treated collar. She had practiced the movement until the hands knew which one had to remain still.

Another bolt struck the cask. Tar began to seep through the hole, thick and black. The driver had already jumped clear. The horses pulled against their traces, making the cart move at the worst possible moment.

Lyra opened the vessel.

The space beneath the brace went deep.

She felt the force seek the far wall, the cask, the weak joins in the gate, her own shoulder. This time she knew its edge before it crossed her. She turned the contact against the marked socket and held it there.

The salt pattern whitened, then folded in on itself.

The brace made a soft, appalling sound. Its grain compressed until the surface looked wet. The bronze strap tore free at one end. Both gate leaves shuddered inward, and the left socket broke apart beneath Lyra’s hand without taking the sound wall above it.

She released the spent mouth before the recoil could find her wrist.

The gate fell against its own brace. The impact lifted dust and broken salt into the air. For one bright instant the upper men had no clear mark to shoot at. Vaska used it. She went through the opening with a short sword in her hand and her people behind her, striking the man who tried to draw a second bolt before his crossbow was ready.

Lyra followed. The empty vessel knocked against her knee. She set it behind a stone pier, where it could not be mistaken for a second weapon, and took her knife.

Inside, the tar store smelled of resin, damp wool, and men afraid enough to breathe through their mouths. The lower floor had two rows of casks and a loading passage between them. A narrow stair climbed toward the black windows. The keeper had reached it first. He struck at Vaska from above, forcing her to stop beneath a turning step where her sword could not swing freely.

Lyra went under the stair.

The passage there was low and crowded with broken hoops. She heard the keeper’s feet above her before she saw the gap between the treads. She drove the short iron hook through the opening and caught his boot. He fell one step, recovered, and looked down just as Vaska took his wrist against the rail.

He let go of his sword. It struck the lower floor close to Lyra’s cheek. She pulled the hook free and came out the other side while Vaska dragged the keeper down to the landing.

A man at the nearest black window turned his crossbow toward them. Corven’s cracked shell was in his hand at the side passage. The man opened his mouth to call, found no voice, and fired without the warning his companion expected. The companion stepped into his line. The bolt struck his upper arm and drove him against the window frame.

Corven’s silence belonged only to the shooter. The wounded man’s cry carried across the cut.

Lyra reached the stair. The confined space favored her in ways she had known before and could use more completely now. The dark beneath the rail did not conceal the next step. The pressure of the wall against her shoulder steadied her instead of limiting her turn. She could hear the upper man shifting the crossbow to reload, the little catch failing once under his thumb. She reached him before it caught a second time.

He struck her with the stock. It glanced off her left temple, filling that side of her sight with light. She put her hand around the weapon and pushed it against his throat. He could not shout because Corven had taken his voice; he could still fight. His knee caught her hip. She held the stock, drove him backward into the window opening, and stopped when his feet left the floor.

Below them lay the water and the broken gate.

“Drop the knife,” she said.

He had drawn it with the hand she could not see. She felt the decision in the hardening of his wrist against her side. Then the knife struck the stair beneath them.

She pulled him back into the room and let him fall.

The men at the next window were leaving by the upper passage. Lyra could hear their boots. She called to Vaska, and Vaska sent one of her people to close the lower route instead of chasing them into rooms they had not seen. The store was almost theirs. The cut was not yet.

Across the water, the farther boom began to move.

The Prince’s outer men had understood the purpose of the attack. They were drawing the channel chain tight, trapping Lyra’s boat between the store and the far pier. A second boat came from the Salt Ward side carrying armed men behind raised shields.

Orrel saw it from the winch. He called for Rusk to clear the lower line. Rusk was beneath the loading platform, freeing a snag where the rope had caught the side of a broken step. His companion stood on the narrow service ledge beside him. Both looked up when the warning came.

A defender in the upper passage threw down a clay firepot.

It broke across the spilled tar near the cask. Flame ran along the black trail faster than a person could stamp it out. The cask itself did not explode. It burned slowly and with a thick hungry smoke that filled the lower passage. Somebody kicked it in an attempt to move it away from the stair. The barrel struck the retaining hook of the separate service hatch. Its heavy leaf swung down across the entrance to the lower ledge, with the cask bearing against it.

Rusk and the other man were behind it.

Lyra saw their hands through the narrow gap. She could reach the door from the upper floor only by crossing the burning tar. Vaska’s first attempt ended with her sleeve catching fire. She beat it against the wall, swore, and went for the cask with a hook.

“Leave that,” Lyra said.

“They’re underneath.”

“I see them.”

The boat from the Salt Ward kept coming. Corven stood at the side opening, chain looped around one forearm, keeping the first boarder from getting a foot onto their platform. His ordinary knife did the work his rite could not do twice at once. Orrel needed the lower line freed to turn their own boat against the chain. Rusk’s hand appeared again beneath the fallen leaf, opening and closing in the smoke.

Lyra went to the water.

Vaska caught her coat. “Where?”

“Under the platform.”

“The tide’s running.”

“Keep the upper men away from my head when it comes up.”

She gave Vaska the coat because it was easier than tearing it out of her hands. Then she stepped from the broken loading edge and went beneath the surface.

The water closed over her with a force she recognized and a coolness so complete it was almost pleasure. For a few strokes the noise of the battle changed into pressure against her skin. She went below the burning slick, keeping close to the stone where the current was slower. Her left hand found the submerged edge of the old platform. Her right drew her forward, carefully, without asking the shoulder for a long pull.

She would have needed air by the second piling on an ordinary day before Maeron. Before this descent she could have reached the third. Now the need remained somewhere farther inside, urgent enough to know about and distant enough to work around.

She did not spend the whole gift on admiring it.

At the service ledge, the lower half of the hatch hung in the water. Its upper hinges remained sound in the loading beam, and the fixed frame was unbroken. The leaf wanted to swing inward under its own weight and the load piled against it. Rusk’s boot protruded from the narrowing space.

Lyra surfaced beneath the ledge, where a hand’s breadth of air lay between the water and the underside of the boards. Smoke had begun to reach even here. She spat water, took one careful breath, and put her left palm against the fixed stone frame.

Her right hand found the moving leaf. The brine was all around her, but the prepared words still mattered. She spoke them with her mouth almost against the wet wood, felt the known direction of force, and held.

The hatch stopped moving.

She could not lift the cask and the fallen timber. The working held the sound face where it was; it had not shifted the load above. It gave her a brief space in which Rusk’s foot could come free and the other man could turn his body.

“Into the water,” she told them.

Rusk stared at her through the gap. His eyes were red. He seemed unable to make her voice belong to the face he could see.

“Rusk. Now.”

He moved. His companion caught him beneath the arms and lowered him past the submerged edge. Lyra kept her hands in contact while the second man followed, shoulders scraping wood. The current tried to take them beneath the platform. She caught Rusk’s collar with two fingers of the hand holding the frame, then had to let go before she lost the working.

A boat hook came down from above.

Vaska had reached the side opening. She caught Rusk’s coat and dragged him toward the light. The other man took the hook after him. Orrel hauled from the boat, pulling them clear of the burning edge.

Lyra released the hatch.

It struck the stone with a sound she felt through the water. The narrow air space vanished under falling wood. She went down before it closed, turned along the piling, and swam toward the gap where Vaska’s hook had appeared.

Her chest began to burn.

She could hold the breath longer. She still needed it. The new cold did not make the fire overhead harmless, and the falling timber had struck her shoulder. She followed the known stone with her left hand until the surface changed from smoke-dark to the pale movement of open water.

Vaska’s hand closed around her wrist.

“Come on.”

Lyra let herself be pulled high enough to catch the boat’s edge. Orrel took her other arm. She came over the side on her stomach and lay coughing beside Rusk while the boat rocked under all three of them.

Vaska crouched over her. “Tell me before you do that again.”

“I did.”

“Earlier.”

Lyra laughed, and the laugh turned into a cough. Vaska’s face was wet. One side of her green sleeve had burned away.

Across the cut, Corven had forced the second boat to sheer off. The boom crew could not hold both the chain and a fight at the far pier. Two of Vaska’s people had reached them along the shore passage while everyone watched the burning store. The chain slackened. Orrel took the strain on the winch, and their boat turned clear.

Lyra sat up. The salt air seemed enormous after the space beneath the boards. She could see the three black windows above her, two empty now, one with a man leaning out and waving a strip of cloth.

He was asking to stop.

She raised her hand where he could see it.

By the time the local bucket crew arrived, the burning cask had been rolled into a stone sump and covered with sand. One storage bay was lost. The roof above it smoked. Vaska’s people stood at the captured gate with weapons lowered but ready, keeping curious neighbors away from the damaged beam.

Lyra had eight prisoners and one dead defender. Two of the prisoners needed carrying. Her own losses were burns, a broken wrist, Rusk’s reopened thigh wound, and the tools abandoned beneath the fallen timber. The spent vessel had cracked when the timber struck the pier beside it. Mavet would have wanted to examine the lining whole. Lyra collected the largest pieces anyway.

The keeper sat with his hands tied in front of him. He watched her put the fragments into a cloth.

“You could have asked for the passage,” he said.

“I did.”

“You could have paid.”

“I will pay for keeping it.”

He looked at the broken gate. She followed his gaze. There was work everywhere she looked, expensive work, and men would have to remain when the exhilaration of taking it had gone. She could feel that cost without wanting the victory less.

“Who knows the boom mechanism?” she asked.

He named two men. Both were alive. She sent for them and put the employment terms on the table before asking which would stay. One agreed to work through the emergency night if his wounded brother could leave with the first cart. The other refused. She had him disarmed and kept with the prisoners until a place for their departure was arranged.

Corven came to her after the approaches were secure. He had a cut on the back of one hand and blood that was not his along the chain’s wrapping.

“The passage is held,” he said.

She nodded. “Your work is finished.”

He looked at her for a moment, then at the burned opening beneath which she had gone. “Did you know you could return?”

“I knew the way.”

“That wasn’t my question.”

“No,” she said. “I didn’t know.”

He put the chain away without answering. She paid him before he left, including the damage to his coat. He had earned it. He had also seen her laughing in the boat after nearly dying, and she could not decide whether she wanted to explain that to him or keep it wholly hers.

Vaska stood in the middle black window as dawn reached the cut. Lyra joined her. Below, the first ordinary boat waited for someone to tell it who now collected the charge. Its master looked up, recognized Lyra, and took off his cap.

Vaska leaned her unburned shoulder against the frame. “They’re waiting for your price.”

Lyra looked along the captured water, past the broken gate toward the Salt Ward still beyond it. The morning was warm. She could not feel the warmth on her skin, but she could feel the width of the passage and the work a boat could do inside it.

“Bring him in,” she said.

Chapter 12 Good customers

The new customer brought his own pen and three men who did not intend to write anything.

Tesset watched them come along the quay toward her table. The customer wore a coat lined with pale fur, too warm for the weather, and carried a parcel of sugared nuts as though he had mistaken the landing for somebody’s sickroom. One of the men behind him had a finger missing at the first joint. The other two looked alike enough to be brothers and behaved as though they disliked the comparison.

“Mistress Tesset?” the customer asked.

“Yes.”

“I’m told you keep the receiving account.”

“For the subscriber cargo passing here.”

“And Mistress Lyra’s new undertaking?”

“Her freight has separate sheets.”

He put the nuts on her table. “We shall be good customers.”

“That will depend on what arrives.”

He laughed as though she had offered an amusing compliment. She moved the parcel away from the ink and asked which boat he meant to enter. He named two. Neither was on her expected list.

“They are at Blackglass,” he said. “The old charges have been settled.”

“By whom?”

“By the people who no longer collect them.”

One of the men behind him smiled. The customer did not. He had brought a paper with Vaska’s mark on it and a plain description of goods to move through Harrow after being stored at the captured cut. The cargo itself was ordinary enough: lamp oil, cloth, bundles of dried fish. Tesset copied the quantities, checked the proposed receiving day, and left the charge blank until she could see the applicable rate.

“We have agreed with Mistress Lyra,” he said.

“Then she can provide the figure.”

“She said the usual.”

“Which usual?”

His smile thinned. Tesset had learned that the word usually concealed at least three arrangements, all better understood by the person who wanted to pay last. She fetched the rate sheet and asked him to choose the entry matching the work. He read it with care. Whatever his men were meant to accomplish, he could still count.

When he had gone to find Lyra, the nuts remained. Tesset opened the parcel, ate one, and put the rest where the carriers could take them. They disappeared before the first boat arrived.

There was more work than there had been before Blackglass fell. She could see the difference in paid hours and in the quality of food men brought wrapped for their midday rest. One woman had replaced a boot held together with cord. A porter paid a debt to another porter in front of her table, perhaps because he wanted a witness, perhaps because he was pleased to be able to do it. The regular wages did not depend on guessing whether Lyra was in a good temper when the pay was requested.

At the far end of the shed, Vaska was making a man return a knife he had taken from a prisoner.

“He gave it,” he protested.

“Then you can give it back.”

“He doesn’t need it.”

“You don’t get to decide what belongs to the company by putting it in your pocket.”

The man handed over the knife. Vaska tested the hinge, closed it, and put it with the other captured property. Tesset looked away before the inspection became an invitation to put the knife on her own list. She counted freight. A different hand would account for weapons taken in a private war.

Lyra came down the repaired stair carrying two cups.

“Tea,” she said.

“I can see that.”

“You’re meant to be pleased.”

“I will be when I know which one has less leaf in it.”

Lyra gave her the one with more. Tesset laughed, and for a moment the deeper resonance in Lyra’s voice became part of the shared joke rather than a sound she had to get used to. Then Lyra set her own cup on the table. The air near her hand cooled enough that Tesset drew her fingers inward before she could help it.

Lyra saw. She moved the cup a little farther away.

They ate upstairs when the rush ended.

The room had changed since Tesset last came to see the stairs. The bed stood close to the wall. The mirror faced backward. A dark cloth covered one side of the window, leaving a narrow view of the working water. Lyra moved a chair for Tesset and sat where the plaster was close to her right shoulder.

“How is it?” Tesset asked.

“The room?”

“The arm.”

“Still attached.”

“That is not the whole condition of a useful arm.”

Lyra held out her hand, closed it, and opened it again. There was stiffness, but none of the violent tremor Tesset had seen after the basin fight. A new bruise lay along the wrist. Tesset did not ask to inspect it.

Sova had sent soup in a covered pot and charged for the carrying. Lyra complained about the charge while giving Tesset the larger bowl. The soup smelled of fish, onion, and a herb Tesset could never distinguish from another herb she disliked. She ate it anyway. It was good.

“The man with the fur,” she said. “What did you promise him?”

“A store at Blackglass, passage at the written rate, and people who will prevent the Prince’s collectors taking his goods twice.”

“And he promised?”

“A share of the cargo through the store. Information about the boats he sends. No carrying for a house working against us while the agreement lasts.”

“Who decides which house that is?”

“I do.”

Tesset put down her spoon. Lyra had said it as easily as she might name the person responsible for a broken hinge. Perhaps that was how it felt to her now.

“He has people working for him,” Tesset said.

“He can explain the agreement to them.”

“Have they agreed?”

“He is agreeing for his boats.”

“People have to move the boats.”

Lyra reached for the bread. “The people can ask about their employment. I’m not taking over his whole company.”

“Yet?”

The word escaped before Tesset had decided to say it. Lyra looked at her for a moment, then smiled.

“You could let me finish my soup before improving my ambitions.”

Tesset almost smiled back. She wanted to. The familiarity was real enough that refusing it altogether would require an effort she did not always wish to make.

Lyra told her about the first customer’s missing wick at Harrow months before, a small disagreement Tesset had heard at the time and forgotten. She remembered every part now, including the merchant’s expression when the wick was found exactly where his own man had packed it. They laughed. Lyra asked about Leda’s binding work and listened while Tesset described a book that opened flat enough to make its maker unreasonably proud.

“I’d buy one,” Lyra said.

“She’ll want to make it properly.”

“She should.”

“And charge you for that.”

“I’ve heard that principle mentioned.”

Tesset finished her bowl. Lyra took it downstairs herself, giving her a few minutes alone in the room. Through the gap at the window, Tesset watched a boat pass with no guard aboard. Farther along, another waited while Vaska’s people inspected the covered load. The new customer stood beside it, explaining something to a man who had not been entitled to ask him questions before this week.

When Lyra came back, Tesset had her work papers ready.

“The ordinary freight is entered,” she said. “Your agreements about enemies go in your own book.”

“They already do.”

“Then don’t send someone to my table expecting me to enforce them.”

Lyra’s expression tightened. “I’ll tell Vaska.”

“Tell the customers.”

There was a silence. The cooler air beside the wall reached Tesset’s ankles.

“I will,” Lyra said.

Tesset took the cups down with her. She had meant the boundary, and Lyra had heard it. She steadied the cups against each other and continued down the stair.

Leda’s hands were blue when Tesset reached the bindery.

“The good blue,” she said before Tesset asked. “Not your brother-in-law’s blue. Mine will come off.”

“It might. He says that too.”

Leda held up one hand. The color lay in the folds beside the nails and across the base of the thumb. She had been helping lay a colored border on a set of covers, work she had wanted for months and now described as a way of discovering how many different surfaces one could touch by accident.

Tesset put down the bread and onions she had bought for their supper. The bindery smelled of paste, warm leather, and the faint sweet dust of cut paper. Leda’s employer was in the next room complaining to a supplier about thread. In front, Istre sat mending the edge of a long household cloth, her needle traveling in and out with a speed that made Tesset want to hide every seam she had ever repaired.

“Istre has been inspecting my work again,” Leda said. “She tells me when it is badly folded before anyone else has had time to be diplomatic.”

“Because I have to put the cloth over it,” Istre said. “A crooked thing beneath remains crooked no matter how neatly I hide it.”

Tesset remembered that low, full voice singing over the fish at their earlier supper, and smiled. Istre looked at Leda’s hands and told her to wash before touching the finished seam. Leda obeyed with a complaint about being treated like an apprentice. Istre said an apprentice would have washed without complaint because she wanted to be paid.

Tesset sat beside her while Leda went to the yard. The cloth belonged to Gannet House. It had been caught on a nail during a move and torn down one edge. Istre did not intend to let the household order a new one merely because a person had been careless with the old.

“Do they listen?” Tesset asked.

“When they are hungry. The table is bare until this is finished.”

“An effective position.”

“Only if you don’t mind hearing about it while you work.”

She smiled. There was a little red mark on one finger where she had pricked herself. She put the finger to her mouth without losing the place in the seam.

Leda returned with wet hands and a smudge of blue still below one nail. She brought out the cover she wanted to show Tesset. The border was uneven at one corner. Tesset saw the flaw and also saw how much better the work was than the last attempt. She traced the air above the good line instead of touching it.

“I like this turn.”

“So do I,” Leda said. “It’s the one I have to learn to do again.”

Istre leaned over, looked, and said the uneven corner could be hidden with a small worked patch if Leda wanted to save the cover. Leda said she wanted the corner visible until she could see why it had happened. The older woman nodded, accepting a reason instead of demanding obedience to her own remedy.

They shared the bread before Istre left. Tesset had brought enough for two, but the bindery keeper supplied cheese and a pot of something pickled after announcing that she did not run an eating house. Istre ate with the cloth folded on her lap. She told them about a man in Gannet House who wore through the same elbow every month and denied leaning on anything. Leda offered to sew a warning onto the sleeve. Istre said it would have to be on the inside, where he might eventually look for an explanation.

When the mending was done, Istre lifted the long cloth and shook it out. Its white surface caught the last light through the shop window. Tesset helped fold it. They had to begin again because she put her hands at unequal distances from the edge. Istre showed her the places without making the correction sound like a judgment of everything else she could do.

At the door, she asked Leda whether the next cover delivery could be brought to the side entrance. The front rooms were occupied with business, and people kept stacking parcels where someone had to step over them to carry a tray.

“I’ll tell the boy,” Leda said.

“Tell him the side entrance has a bell. He likes to stand outside and believe himself ignored.”

They promised. Istre took the cloth beneath one arm and went toward the upper market.

Tesset and Leda walked home slowly. Leda had kept the imperfect cover wrapped against her body. At Tesset’s room, she set it on the table before taking off her coat. The little dog above them barked, ran across the floor, and fell silent when someone opened a cupboard.

“It knows where the food is,” Leda said.

“So do I.”

“You brought it.”

“And shared it with your friends. You’ll have to make me something else.”

Leda kissed her with one hand behind her neck, leaving a faint cool trace where the fingers were still damp from washing. Then she went downstairs to ask the umbrella repairer whether the pot could be warmed on her fire. Tesset lit the lamp and moved the books away from the space where they would eat.

Later, when the covers were safely wrapped and the supper dishes had been taken away, Leda leaned against her and asked about the new trade. Tesset told her what the good customers brought, and what some of them expected in return. Leda listened without asking Tesset to decide the whole matter before bed. She had paste in her hair at the back. Tesset found it while undoing a pin and began to laugh.

“I washed,” Leda protested.

“Most of you.”

“Then leave the rest until morning.”

Tesset did.

The Gannet letter arrived while Tesset was checking the second day’s Blackglass freight.

Its bearer had been instructed to deliver it to Lyra’s own hand. He said this three times, although nobody had offered to take it from him. Tesset pointed him toward the shed stair and went back to the casks. She had three disputed weights and a boy who insisted a boat’s missing mark had floated off in the rain.

Lyra came down before she had finished. Vaska followed, her burned sleeve replaced by a plain dark one that did not match the other. The change pleased Tesset more than the good coat had; it looked like a person continuing to wear something through work, rather than a uniform arriving with the work already done.

“Do you have the current common passage terms?” Lyra asked.

Tesset gave her the public copy. Lyra read a line, compared it with the letter, and laughed without amusement.

“He acknowledges the cut,” Vaska said.

“He acknowledges that we stand in it. That’s less expensive.”

Tesset remained at her table. She could have gone outside to settle the missing mark. Instead she waited while Lyra placed the letter where Vaska could read the lower paragraph.

Daro Venn offered to pay ordinary passage for Gannet cargo. He would not provide a list of all boats working under his protection, permit inspection of unrelated goods, or accept Lyra’s right to forbid his dealings with another house. The route through Blackglass had served more than one owner before the Prince occupied it. Gannet would recognize reasonable tolls and no superior command. Daro invited Lyra to discuss the matter at his table, with supper afterward if the conversation permitted them an appetite.

Tesset remembered Istre’s white cloth opening in the light.

“He is offering you freight,” she said.

“He is offering to choose which part of the agreement applies to him.”

“It is his company.”

Lyra looked at her. “And that is my cut.”

Vaska took the letter, folded it along its original creases, and asked whether she should answer. Lyra went to the shelf where the route drawings were kept. She brought down the one showing the water east of Cinder Basin and spread it on the table beyond Tesset’s account sheets.

“First I want to know every place his boats can enter without passing us,” she said. “And which doors serve his stores after dark.”

“That’s more than a reply.”

“Yes.”

The bearer waited by the stair. Lyra told him she had received Daro’s letter and would send her answer. She paid him for the return journey because he had been kept through the dinner hour. He took the money, thanked her, and went.

Tesset gathered the public terms back into her case. Vaska had put one hand on the map, holding it flat while Lyra found the smaller route beneath the bridge. The casks outside still needed weighing. Tesset picked up her board and went to do that work.

Part Three The Names She Keeps

Chapter 13 Where the knives are

The knife fell out when the man took off his hat.

It was a little flat thing, scarcely longer than Lyra’s forefinger, with a hole through the handle for a cord. It landed on the table between the bowls of soup. Vaska caught it before it slid into hers.

“That’s a poor place,” she said.

The man looked from her to Lyra. He had prepared for suspicion, perhaps even a search, but not for the knife to announce itself while he was being polite.

“I wasn’t bringing it for either of you.”

“Then sit down,” Lyra said. “You’ll tell us who it’s for.”

The room above Harrow Slip had acquired a second table since the taking of Blackglass Cut. The first was for work. This one was supposed to be for eating, and Lyra disliked having to reach around the stranger’s weapon for her bread. She moved the knife beside his empty bowl. Vaska moved it back.

The man sat. His name was Nelm. He handled boats for Gannet House and wore a good coat over a shirt that had been darned at the neck. His shoes made a hard wooden noise whenever he shifted them beneath the chair. He had asked to see Vaska alone. Vaska had brought him upstairs without allowing him to finish the request.

“I’ve been collecting for Daro,” he said. “Landing charges. Not the other work.”

“Then you’ve come to the wrong house,” Lyra said. “I don’t pay him.”

“No. Some of your customers do.”

She dipped the bread in her soup. Nelm watched her hand. His eyes moved from the bowl to the darkened window, where the room’s lamplight did not quite explain the depth of her reflection. He stopped looking there.

“Which customers?”

“The little boats from the dyers’ reach. The pair carrying untaxed indigo. Three night loads of lamp oil that came through Blackglass before you took it. They paid him to keep other men from asking what was aboard.”

“And now they’re paying me to bring it through a place I hold.”

“Yes.”

“That must be expensive.”

Nelm swallowed. “He says he’ll return their money if you’ll let him continue collecting at the outer turn.”

“Did he send you to offer that?”

“No.”

Vaska put the flat knife through the rind of a sour orange. She cut a narrow strip free, tasted it, and made a face without ceasing to eat.

“Tell her why you came,” she said.

Nelm took a folded sheet from his coat. It was one of Gannet’s collection instructions, altered in several places with a finer pen. He laid it on the eating table with the same reluctance with which a person might put down a tooth he had lost.

“He’s letting me keep what I collect above the old charge.”

“Then you should be happy.”

“I was. Until he told the boatmen I was taking it without permission.”

Lyra read the different hands. She could not tell whose finer pen had made the additions. Nelm supplied a second sheet, a message calling him to the house after dark. It promised that if he came alone his explanation would be heard. The phrase had been underlined.

“The knife is for the man on the stair,” Nelm said. “He puts a hand on your shoulder before he lets you up. If I have to get past him.”

“After you’ve explained?”

Nelm’s mouth tightened. “I don’t think they want the explanation.”

Lyra had heard enough accounts of dangerous summons to know that fear made even an honest man arrange his facts. She asked to see the sums he had collected. He told her there were no written sums. She asked where he kept the money. He looked toward the window again.

Vaska pushed the orange away. “There it is.”

“I kept some,” he said. “He told me I could.”

“How much?”

He told them. It was more than a man could steal by misunderstanding three instructions.

Lyra finished her soup. She let him sit with the figure while she put the bowl aside.

“I won’t ask Daro to forgive you,” she said. “And I won’t buy your story by itself. Show me something I can use.”

Nelm wiped his palms on his thighs. “I know which landing takes his money out.”

“Money can leave through a pocket.”

“I know when the house is supplied. Which boat. Who checks it. Where the watergate’s braced.”

Vaska stopped turning the knife.

Lyra looked at the second sheet. The underlined promise had left a groove in the paper. Nelm had folded it so often that the groove was beginning to split.

“You’ll show us,” she said. “From outside. If it’s true, you get passage away and enough to begin somewhere. No work owed afterward.”

“I want it written.”

“So do I.”

“And Daro doesn’t get told where.”

“That depends on whether you try to sell the same visit twice.”

He nodded too quickly. She made him slow down and hear the amount. It was enough to leave with. It was not enough to take a whole Gannet collection with him as well.

When Vaska took him downstairs, Lyra carried the bowls to the washstand. The soup had left a dark ring around Nelm’s. She washed it twice before putting it with the others.

The outer turn at Blackglass smelled of tar even after the burned planks had been removed. Lyra liked the smell. It told her where she was before she rounded the wall.

Three small boats waited beneath the shuttered windows. Their masters had brought cargo, money, and objections. Vaska’s people kept the path clear while Lyra walked the landing with them. One master had a sheet from Daro promising that his old arrangement remained in force. Another had a broken nose from a man who had collected under that arrangement and then asked for more. The third wanted to know whether she could carry a mixed load without opening every package in front of Lyra’s guards.

“What mixture?” Lyra asked.

“Thread and things people put inside thread.”

“Coin?”

“Sometimes.”

“Powder?”

“No.”

“People?”

The woman looked offended. “Where would I put them? It’s a little boat.”

Lyra allowed her to land sealed packages subject to a fixed inspection of the outside. She kept the right to refuse a load before it entered the Cut. The woman accepted that until Lyra added that any other protection payment made on the same passage had to be reported.

“I already paid Gannet.”

“Then show me how much. You won’t pay twice for this journey.”

“And the next?”

“The next is mine.”

The woman stood with her hands on her hips. “The channel is yours. My boat isn’t.”

“I haven’t asked for your boat.”

“You’ve asked where I spend my money.”

Lyra looked at the closed tar store, the repaired rail, the men she had paid to sleep in turns beside its doors. The master’s independence had a sound she liked when it was being exercised somewhere else.

“If you want my men to come when someone boards you here,” she said, “I need to know who believes he’s entitled to board you.”

The woman considered that. At last she took out Daro’s paper and laid it on a dry cask. Lyra read it, copied the amount, and gave it back.

She did not send anybody to collect from the woman afterward. She sent two of Vaska’s people to accompany the next boat that came under Gannet protection. They returned near midnight with the boat, its cargo, and a man whose wrist had been tied to his own belt.

He had a familiar voice. He had shouted from one of the Blackglass windows while Lyra’s crew crossed the lower landing, before the prepared vessel crushed the brace and the whole protected position gave way. Now he stood in the tar store trying to keep his wet sleeve from touching his face.

“Whose knife?” Lyra asked, looking at the blade Vaska laid on the cask.

“Mine.”

“Whose instruction?”

“Mine too, if that makes it easier.”

It would have. She could kill him without having to decide how far Daro’s house extended beyond its walls. She could give his employer a body and let the next instruction be careful.

Vaska leaned close enough that the man could smell the orange on her breath.

“He told the boatwoman you were collecting for Daro,” she said. “Then he put half the money down his boot.”

The man shut his eyes briefly.

Lyra had his boot removed. The money was there, wrapped in a piece of fine blue cloth. A second packet held a woman’s hair ornament, broken where its stones had been pried out. The boatwoman said it was hers.

“Give it back,” Lyra said.

Vaska put it in the woman’s hand. The man tried to explain how it had come to him. Nobody interrupted. He made the explanation longer, discovered that length was not helping, and stopped.

Lyra set Nelm’s collection instruction beside his knife. She did not show him the name.

“Daro has a habit of forgetting what he told people,” she said. “You may want him to remember this carefully.”

The man watched her.

“You’ll take him the money you owe him. Tell him you found no place to collect in Blackglass. Tell him the people who come through here will be met by mine.”

“He’ll send me back.”

“Then don’t come.”

She had his wrist untied. Vaska gave him the empty boot and kept the knife. The man left limping, carrying the boot until he reached the dry stair.

Afterward Vaska asked whether she wanted him followed.

“To Gannet, yes. No farther.”

“He’s frightened enough to run.”

“Then we’ll learn where he goes.”

The boatwoman was still turning the broken ornament in her hands. Lyra offered to have its fastening repaired. She shook her head and put it away.

“I want to leave while I still have the water.”

Lyra stepped aside. The cargo went down to the little boat. It carried thread, three pouches of silver tucked inside the thread rolls, and something else the woman had not chosen to name. Lyra watched it enter the dark turn and found herself smiling when the stern light passed beneath her repaired rail.

Belis had taken over the best-lit end of the workshop. He had put his chair so that people coming through the door approached his right side, and tied a strip of red cloth to the tool cabinet to stop himself turning too quickly toward it. Even so, when Lyra came in he looked over the wrong shoulder and had to close his eyes until the room stopped moving.

“Say something before you step,” he said.

“I did.”

“Louder, then. I’m not getting less vain for your convenience.”

She set down the packet she had brought. Inside were two matched dividers, secondhand but sound. Belis opened them with an expression of caution that became pleasure before he could hide it.

“These cost money.”

“They usually do.”

“I didn’t ask you for these.”

“You asked for a pair that would hold a measure.”

“I asked the quartermaster.”

“She asked me.”

He tried the screw. It held. For a time he had nothing to say, which let Lyra hear the small irregular scrape of his thumbnail against the brass. Since the dedication she could pick one such sound out of a room full of people and follow it until the other sounds became difficult to recover. She turned her head toward the open window, giving herself something farther away to attend to.

On the bench lay three uncharged ceramic bodies in their padded cages. Broad bronze seats had been fitted into their mouths. One had a flaw near its shoulder and had been marked for breaking. The others would go below only after the seats had been checked again.

Belis saw her looking.

“That one could hold water all your life,” he said, pointing to the rejected vessel. “It won’t hold what you want.”

“Break it.”

“I’d like the bronze back first.”

She sat on the edge of the bench. He told her about a room he and his partner had looked at for their own work. It had a second entrance from an alley and enough space for a long bench, but the roof belonged to a different owner from the floor. The owner of the roof would not repair it until the owner of the floor stopped repairing boats in the yard.

“Do they know each other?” Lyra asked.

“They’re brothers.”

“Of course they are.”

Belis laughed and then steadied himself against the bench. She waited without reaching for him. He had become very quick at noticing the hands people prepared to put out.

“I can find you somewhere else,” she said.

“We’ve found two places. This is the one we can afford.”

“I can advance the difference.”

“And then what?”

“Then you pay it when you have it.”

“Out of work for you.”

“If you choose.”

He opened and closed the dividers again. “Put that part in the paper.”

She said she would. The request annoyed her, and she liked him enough to let the annoyance remain small.

Esten’s assistant came for the sound vessels before Lyra left. She checked the marks on their seats against the working board and returned one to Belis for a burr no wider than a hair. He took it without argument. Lyra stayed to watch him remove it. The little shaving curled from his tool and fell onto a cloth he had laid to catch the waste.

“You’re not going below,” she said.

“I know.”

“I wasn’t asking you to.”

“I know that too.” He looked at her at last. “I’d still like to have been the one who brought it back.”

She understood that better than the room with the disputed roof. She put a hand on the bench beside his and stayed while he finished the edge.

Nelm showed them Gannet from a boat with empty fish baskets stacked high enough to conceal their coats. He sat in the stern with a pole and kept his good shoes out of the bilge.

The house stood on a narrow quay beyond a bend that made it seem farther from Blackglass than it was. Two bonded stores extended along the same water. Above the enclosed loading court, the dining gallery’s windows had been opened to the evening. Someone was singing in the kitchen, too softly for Vaska to hear. Lyra recognized Istre’s voice without knowing the song.

“Don’t turn your head like that,” Nelm said.

Lyra looked back at him.

“You’re listening. People can see you listening.”

They went under the watergate as close as an empty boat could reasonably pass. Its two leaves were wide enough to admit a handcart from the quay. Behind them an outer brace ran down to a stone socket. A smaller service drain entered beside the socket, covered by a square grille held with ordinary pins.

“They scour the rope yard through that,” Nelm said. “Only at low water. It’s foul.”

“Does it go beneath the court?” Vaska asked.

“No. Into a cleaning pit. A person could get to the outside of the brace from there. You couldn’t come up inside the house.”

Lyra watched the socket until the boat carried it past her. A charged vessel could break that brace if she reached the known face and held the prepared contact against it. The gallery windows looked directly down into the pit.

“Who guards the stores?” she asked.

Nelm answered. They made a second passage on the other side of the channel, where the gallery could be seen between hanging nets. There were people at supper. Daro stood with a cup in his hand, turned toward somebody Lyra could not see. He was talking with the easy pleasure he brought to a full table. A woman laughed, and he stopped speaking to let her finish.

Vaska shifted beside Lyra. “We can reach him from the kitchen stair. Nelm can put someone there before the meal.”

“And the house?”

“His cousin, probably. Or the long man with the scar. The boats would wait a day before deciding.”

Lyra looked at the two stores. Their cargo had already been promised to buyers. Their crews knew where to go when the house sent for them. Killing one generous host would leave all of that waiting for the next man to take his chair.

“I want the gates and the boats,” she said.

“That costs more.”

“Yes.”

Nelm put his pole down. “My passage.”

“You’ll have it before we go in. You won’t be in the house.”

“Or beside it.”

“Or beside it.”

The boat moved into the darker reach beyond the quay. Lyra looked once more toward the kitchen window. Istre had stopped singing. A dish broke somewhere inside, followed by a brisk voice telling somebody to stand still until the pieces were swept.

Back at Harrow, Vaska spread Nelm’s drawing beneath a lamp. Lyra put weights on its corners and called in the people who would have to make it work. She paid the next watch before discussing the attack. The money made a softer sound than the knives when the crew set their weapons on the table to mark the positions they wanted.

Chapter 14 The better room

The second window was the reason Tesset wanted it.

The landlord kept pointing out the new plaster, the cupboard, the place where a person could put a washstand without having to move it to open the door. Tesset listened and looked at the window. It faced the back court, where a woman had trained a vine over the ugly wall of the umbrella repairer’s shed. The vine had lost most of its leaves. The remaining ones were yellow enough to make the court seem warmer than it was.

“It doesn’t look onto the harbor,” the landlord said.

“I’ve noticed.”

“For the same rent, the front room has a view.”

“The front room has a leak.”

“A repaired leak.”

Leda leaned out of the second window and looked down. “Someone’s growing mint.”

“The woman below. You’re not to shake anything on it.”

Tesset went to stand beside her. The sill was broad enough for a bowl and a book. Across the court, the vine’s owner came out carrying a pair of umbrellas and opened both to dry. One was blue. The other had a yellow patch shaped like a bird if the bird had been flattened under a table.

“I like this one,” Leda said quietly.

“So do I.”

The landlord immediately began explaining how another person had expressed an interest. Tesset made him say the rent again and write down which repairs belonged to him. He objected to the window latch until Leda closed it and showed him the way it fell open afterward.

“It has character,” he said.

“It has a broken latch,” Leda said, and laughed so readily that he laughed with her before remembering the expense.

They took the room. Tesset paid the deposit from money she had earned at the independent landing, counting it on the sill because the landlord had not brought a table. After he left she stood looking at the place where the bed would go.

“A wider one,” Leda said.

“I haven’t enough for another bed.”

“I do.”

Tesset turned. Leda had gone back to the window. She was picking a loose thread from the cuff of her work dress, concentrating on it as though the offer had required no more thought than choosing bread.

“For here?”

“I’d hardly buy one for the umbrella shed.”

Tesset went to her. She put a hand against Leda’s waist and waited until Leda looked up.

“How often would you want to be here?”

“Often enough to mind the bed.”

“I mean—”

“I know what you mean.” Leda caught the loose thread and snapped it. “I still want my own room. I’m not ready to have all my things in one place with somebody else deciding whether there’s space for them. But I can leave a comb. And a dress that isn’t something I came from work in.”

Tesset kissed her. The landlord’s footsteps were still audible on the stair below. She kissed her again, more slowly, and Leda put both arms around her neck.

“We could move the small table today,” Tesset said when they parted.

“We could go out as we intended.”

“We could do both.”

Leda leaned her forehead against Tesset’s. “This is how you make a day off disappear.”

They left the table where it was. On the landing Tesset looked back once at the empty room. The second window was still open. She went in to close it and found Leda waiting for her with no attempt to conceal her amusement.

The boat to the quarry carried two men going to measure a wall, a woman with covered plants, Tesset, Leda, and a parcel of new cloth for the cutting house. They sat in the middle where the motion was easiest. Leda had brought a little bag of nuts and kept finding broken shells in the pocket where the good ones should have been.
