Tesset had written before coming. Dorel’s answer offered an ordinary visiting afternoon, subject to Aria’s wish when they arrived. The thought of taking a trip that might end at the door had troubled Tesset when the arrangement was new. Now she had packed bread, cheese, and a cloth to sit on beside the basin. They would have an afternoon even if Aria wanted a different one.

The quarry water was pale under the sky. At the cutting house the trestle stood sound, its boards darkened by use. Nothing had to be hauled today. Tesset enjoyed walking across it without counting hands or looking for the next rope.

Senn met them carrying a little broom. Someone had broken a pot near the door, and he was still finding pieces where there should not have been any.

“She’s awake,” he said. “Avenna’s with her. We haven’t asked about visitors yet.”

“We can wait.”

Leda gave him the cloth parcel. He began to take it with the hand holding the broom, reconsidered, and put the broom down. Tesset saw Leda notice the small ordinary awkwardness and relax.

They waited in the side room with Callista. She had arrived earlier and was sitting beside an unlit stove, wrapped in a shawl despite the mild afternoon. Katerina was cutting an apple for her with a knife too large for the task. Each piece came out a different shape.

“She’s making them complicated so I think I’ve eaten more,” Callista said.

“You’ve eaten two.”

“They were large.”

“They were the size you asked for.”

Callista looked at Leda. “Do you have to argue about the size of everything?”

“Mostly folded sheets.”

“That sounds restful.”

“It isn’t. Someone always insists the paper was wider before we cut it.”

Callista smiled. It changed her face enough that Tesset remembered the woman in better rooms, wearing clothes that nobody had bought for warmth. Katerina laid another apple piece on the plate and refrained from telling her to take it.

The invitation came while they were speaking about shoes. Aria wanted visitors. Dorel asked whether they would agree to a short visit first, with another question if she seemed able to continue. Leda listened carefully when he explained the signals. One held note meant yes. Two separated notes meant no. Silence would end that question without an answer.

Inside, the crystal stood in its supported carriage beneath the broad windows. Its fractures caught the light in lines that made Tesset want to tilt her head, though she had seen them many times. The harvesting valve remained sealed under its dated cover. Avenna had put a cloth over the choice board until Aria wanted it used.

Tesset introduced Leda. Leda stood with her hands clasped too tightly and then let them fall.

“I’ve brought a book,” she said. “It isn’t clever. The pictures are good.”

She looked toward Dorel, uncertain whether she should continue. He waited for the prepared question. Avenna asked whether Aria wanted the book shown.

One note held, thin at first and then steadier.

Leda opened it. It contained pictures of ordinary plants printed with broad dark leaves, the sort sold to people who wanted to recognize something growing in a yard. The mint was wrong. Leda said so before remembering she had meant to be an agreeable visitor.

“The leaves aren’t that shape. We told the man who cut it. He said no one buying a cheap book would know.”

Tesset laughed. Leda turned the page and showed the drawing of a small orange tree. One fruit had been printed partly outside its branch.

“That one escaped,” Callista said from the chair near the door.

A sound came from the crystal. Tesset could not tell whether it was an answer, a response to the voices, or something with no relation to what they had said. Avenna did not assign it a meaning. She waited, then asked whether Aria wanted to see another page.

Two notes, separated by enough space that Leda stopped with her finger beneath the leaf.

She closed the book.

“Would you like it left here?” Avenna asked.

The held note came again.

Leda laid the book on the table. Her face had warmed. She stood back while Avenna uncovered the choice board and offered a short list they had used before. Curtain open. Curtain partly closed. No more choices now. Each option took time. Twice there was no answer. On the third attempt Aria chose the partly closed curtain, and Senn moved it a hand’s width at a time until the light fell below the upper fracture.

Tesset watched the room alter by that small amount. The strong reflection left Callista’s face. One corner remained bright enough for Leda’s book. Avenna asked whether the position was wanted. The reply was held, then stopped abruptly.

They ended the visit without another question. Outside, Leda rubbed her palms together.

“I talked too much.”

“She asked for the book to stay,” Tesset said.

“That wasn’t what I meant.”

“No. I know.”

They took their bread to the basin edge. Callista came out slowly with Katerina and sat on a low bench rather than the cloth. The wall measurers passed with a length of cord between them, arguing about which end had moved. For a while everyone watched them. Then Leda asked Callista about the shoes she had been describing, and Callista began again with such pleasure that the four of them missed the first call for the returning boat.

Istre was at the bindery when they came back into the city. She stood beside the cutting table with three blue-wrapped linen books and a damaged binding she wanted someone to admire before she complained about it.

“I don’t object to use,” she was saying. “Use is why I bought it. I object to a book opening into separate pieces when somebody sneezes.”

The binder told her the cloth had been pulled against the grain. Istre said she had not pulled it. He said somebody had. She agreed that the book had probably not done it to itself.

Leda put her bag down and took the binding. She examined the torn fold, drew a finger along the sewing, then looked at the binder.

“The gathering’s wrong. Here.”

He came closer, saw what she meant, and sighed. Istre’s face softened immediately.

“I’ll leave it. It can come with the next order.”

“You’ll want it before then,” he said.

“I usually do. I’m trying to be kind.”

He laughed and took the book into the back room. Istre turned to Leda and touched her sleeve.

“Have you eaten?”

“On the boat.”

“That can mean anything. I’ve got cakes.”

She had brought them in a square tin lined with paper. Leda accepted one, and Tesset found herself accepting another without having decided whether she was hungry. Istre watched them eat with satisfaction. Her little red thread case lay beside the blue books.

“You’ve found your room?” she asked Tesset.

“A better one in the same house.”

“Good. A familiar stair saves you learning where to break your neck.”

Leda told her about the second window. Istre listened seriously, asked which way the rain came, and recommended a woman who could make a curtain without charging them for all the cloth she cut away. Then she asked whether Tesset still saw Lyra.

“Sometimes.”

“I thought so.”

She closed the cake tin. The click was small, but it separated the conversation from what had come before.

“My sister’s son works in our kitchen. Tovan. He’s grown, with his own wife, and he’s tired of carrying somebody else’s quarrels with the flour. He wants to leave our service and take a job at the upper market.”

“Will Daro let him?” Tesset asked.

“Yes. Daro’s been decent about it. But the quickest way out with his things goes through Blackglass, and the men there are asking everyone whose mark they carry.”

“Lyra’s men.”

“I know whose they are. I don’t know which one can say yes.”

Tesset put down the cake. “I can’t give him passage.”

“I haven’t asked you to. I thought you might know how to ask.”

Leda was looking at the red case. She had gone very still.

“When does he want to leave?” Tesset said.

“Tomorrow. Before anybody decides tomorrow ought to be different.”

“Give me his name and what he’ll be carrying.”

Istre took out a little household list. It contained Tovan’s pots, a rolled mattress, clothes, and a box belonging to his wife. There were no weapons. Tesset copied it and asked whether he would object to the goods being looked at.

“He’ll object to many things. He can do that after.”

“Will you be coming too?” Leda asked.

“I’ve a house to put to bed.”

“You could leave a house to somebody else.”

Istre looked at her, then at Tesset. “Daro’s mother taught me to sew the collars on his shirts. I won’t tell you that’s a reason you should admire. It’s the one I have. Tovan’s wife hasn’t known any of them long enough to owe a shirt.”

She put away the red case and picked up the books. Leda offered to carry them to the corner. Istre accepted one, kept the rest, and instructed Tesset to finish the cake before she began writing anything important on a hungry stomach.

Tesset took the request to Harrow after supper. Leda came as far as the footway, then said she would wait by the water. Her yellow scarf showed against the dark rails long after Tesset had reached the shed.

Lyra was downstairs with a man Tesset did not know. He sat on a cask while a woman in a well-cut green coat checked a paper against the money on his knee. The woman’s boots were wet. She looked at Tesset with frank interest.

“Vaska,” Lyra said. “Tesset.”

“I know the name,” Vaska said.

Tesset did not ask from whom. She handed Lyra the request.

The man on the cask went on counting. Vaska listened to each coin drop into the bag as though she could tell the sum by its sound. Tesset watched Lyra read Tovan’s list. There had been a time when she would have known every paper waiting above them and why the man was being paid. The ignorance made the room larger and less comfortable.

“He can come through,” Lyra said. “At first light. With this load. He tells the watch he’s expected.”

“Can I have that written?”

Lyra looked up. For a moment Tesset thought she would take offense. Instead she went to the work table, wrote a short permission, and added the watch mark beneath her name.

“Is Istre leaving with him?”

“No.”

Lyra’s pen stopped before she put it down. “She still works there.”

“Yes.”

The man on the cask tied his money bag. Vaska stood between him and the door while he put it inside his coat.

“Who asked you to bring this?” Lyra said.

“Istre. She knows Leda.”

“I remember the books.”

She gave Tesset the permission. It was clear, limited, and addressed to the person who could make it work. Tesset read it twice, half expecting a condition on the back. There was none.

“Thank you.”

Lyra leaned against the table. “Have you moved?”

“On the next floor. We have to get the bed up.”

“We?”

Tesset felt her face warm. “There’s a bed.”

Lyra smiled, and for a moment the unfamiliar room fell away. “Ask Berris to look at the stair before you pay a carrier. She’ll tell you whether it wants turning at the first landing.”

“I can arrange a bed.”

“I know. I’m interested in whether you’ll have to sleep on it halfway upstairs.”

Tesset laughed. She did not mean to, and was glad she had. Lyra asked about the window. Tesset told her. They spoke for several minutes about a room Lyra had never seen while Vaska waited without interrupting.

Outside, Leda was sitting on the lower rail. Tesset showed her the paper. Leda read it, folded it along the same crease, and put a hand against Tesset’s knee.

“Good.”

“It’s only one man’s passage.”

“It’s the one he needed.”

They delivered it to Gannet before the blue-tiled passage was shut. Istre came down herself, holding a lamp high enough to see their faces. She read the permission, then took Tesset’s free hand between both of hers.

“I’ll have him ready.”

From above came Daro’s voice calling for somebody to bring another cup. Istre turned toward the stair, listened, and smiled with an old impatience.

“He can find where cups live for once.”

She remained with them at the door until Leda had finished telling her about the bed.

Chapter 15 The last boat to Gannet

Tovan passed through Blackglass with his mattress, his pots, and his wife’s box. Lyra saw the watchman open the box, discover folded underclothes above a layer of kitchen knives, and look up in uncertainty.

“Cooking knives,” Tovan said. “I work in a kitchen.”

The watchman turned toward her. Lyra let him hear the answer from the man before she gave her own.

“He can keep them. Wrap the edges.”

Tovan’s wife did it. She had brought cloth for the purpose and was annoyed that her husband had put it beneath the blades. When the box was closed they carried it between them, with the mattress tied on top. Neither looked toward Gannet as they left.

Nelm went later on a different boat. Vaska watched him aboard, gave the master the agreed fare, and came back with Nelm’s flat knife. He had asked to keep it. She had offered to buy him a better one in the town where he was going. He had decided he would rather have the money.

Lyra put the little knife in a drawer. It was too thin for the work ahead.

Corven came in while she was fastening the drawer. He had already looked at the watergate from the far side of the channel. He laid his own drawing beside Nelm’s and pointed to an upper window Nelm had omitted.

“They can reach the gate from there.”

“Vaska takes the stores first. The window looks onto the nearer roof.”

“Vaska can’t be on the roof and inside both stores.”

“She has people.”

“Does she have enough?”

Lyra called Vaska in. The three of them went through the approach until the numbers became people in places, each of them able to see only a portion of what was happening. They changed the first landing. They added two ladders and removed a man from the street passage because there was no room there for him to use the weapon he carried.

Corven named his terms after they had finished.

“I help you take the watergate, clear the armed defenders, and bring the wounded out. If they lay down their weapons and you have the house, I’m finished. I don’t stay to administer it.”

“You’ve said that before.”

“And you have more places now.”

Lyra looked at the small window on his drawing. “Agreed.”

“If you use the vessel, I need to see where the force goes.”

“You’ll see it before the closure opens.”

Vaska tapped the street end with a fingernail. “I want no one coming out behind me while I’m moving people off that roof.”

“They won’t,” Lyra said.

“Then pay the men holding that end enough to stay there.”

She did. She also paid for a boat waiting with bandages, splints, and a woman who knew what to do with them. Daven refused the boat work when he heard which house they intended to take. He had two deliveries already promised and a family who wanted him to eat with them that night. Lyra disliked the second reason more than the first. She paid another master without arguing with Daven.

The vessel they took below had a green cord around its cage.

Mavet checked the ceramic body in the daylight outside the works, turning it slowly while Esten’s assistant read the marks on the bronze seat. Belis had signed the little wooden board tied to its handle. His handwriting had become more careful since the injury, each letter made while his head remained still.

Lyra carried it through the outer passage. The sound of the fixed apparatus reached her before the excavation platform. It was too low to hear as a note, too steady to mistake for the pumps. It pressed against the place inside her chest where she took her deepest breath.

The smaller receiving chamber had been made safe enough for this work by repeated, limited acts. The temporary braces remained. The ancient center stood beyond the isolation shutters, unchanged by the amount of labor spent learning how little they could touch at once. The Prince’s inner route was still on the other side of guarded stone. She could not forget that while her hands were on the vessel.

Mavet set it into the matching bronze seat. It settled with a small movement that everyone watched. A poorly fitting jar could appear secure until the source was exposed. Lyra ran worked brine around the contact, checked the closure, and let her fingers remain where ceramic met metal.

“Ready to isolate,” Esten’s assistant said.

Lyra repeated the words. Corven stood beside the retrieval line, away from the shutter handle. Nobody had to guess which hand would do what if she ceased answering.

The source branch opened a measured amount.

The first sensation was distance. The chamber seemed to continue behind the place where her eyes said the wall stood. Her throat changed around the next breath. She held the known edge of the working against the vessel’s prepared mouth and felt something settle there that had no relation to the small amount of water inside.

She wanted to stay in that moment. She could have borne it longer now. Her body had learned a pleasure that made the ordinary weight of a room seem thin.

“Isolate,” she said.

The shutter closed. Mavet watched the seat while the assistant secured the mechanical stop. Lyra kept contact until the pressure ceased arriving from the fixed source and remained contained in the vessel. Then she set its closure and withdrew her hands. Only after that did Mavet release the outer isolation face, freeing the closed vessel from the receiving mouth.

The jar looked almost the same. A bead of moisture gathered below the bronze collar, moved halfway down the glaze, and stopped there without falling.

“One,” Corven said.

“One,” she agreed.

There was no second charged vessel hidden among the tools. The ones upstairs were empty or unfit. If this one broke in the boat, they would attack an ordinary gate with ordinary people and accept the result.

They lifted it only after the source had been checked shut. Lyra carried one end of the padded cage and Corven the other. At the second lintel she paused to let a sudden pressure in her ears pass. He waited. He did not offer to take both handles, and she was grateful enough to resent that he had known she would be.

Outside, daylight made her narrow her eyes. Vaska stood on the landing eating a slice of yellow fruit with its skin still on. She offered one to Corven. He refused, so she offered it to Lyra.

Lyra took it. The sourness was bright enough to hold her attention all the way up the steps.

They went to Gannet on the last provision boat before the evening closing.

The boat carried flour, onions, a side of smoked meat, and two women who were supposed to be collecting the empty storage jars. One was Vaska. The other had worked in Gannet’s outer store years earlier and knew how much complaint would be expected if she had to wait. She began complaining before the bow touched the quay.

Lyra came by the service drain in a low skiff with Corven and the charged vessel. The grille beside the watergate had fresh rust around one pin where Nelm had loosened it during his last ordinary job. Corven pulled that pin first, keeping a hand beneath it so it would not strike the boat. The other two came less easily. By the time the grille moved, Lyra could hear Vaska arguing above them about the number of empty jars.

They entered the cleaning pit on their knees. It smelled as Nelm had promised. Old rope fibers clung to the walls, mixed with grit and the greasy remains of whatever else the yard had chosen to wash away. Lyra kept the cage on a small board while Corven brought the skiff beneath the mouth of the drain.

The outer brace stood above the pit’s far edge. A new iron strap had been fitted since Nelm last saw it. Lyra raised the hood of the lamp just enough to examine the strap. It crossed the face she had intended to use and ran into the stone on the right.

Corven looked at her.

“Different?”

“Yes.”

“Can you move it?”

“Not quietly.”

She put her hand against the fixed socket. The newer strap had been fitted to stop the brace twisting. Its lower fastening entered a patch of mortar already breaking away from the old stone. If she pressed in the first direction, the force might carry into that loose patch and throw the whole fastening toward them. She drew the plan again on the pit wall with a wet finger.

Corven waited. Above, a man told Vaska to stop talking while he counted. Vaska said that if he could count she would have no reason to talk.

Lyra moved the prepared shoe to the brace’s other face. It would drive the wood against its own stone bed. The newer strap would hold it in place long enough for the crushing force to split the grain rather than turn it. She could see the receiving face. She could reach it. The people behind the gate would have a gate falling inward, not the unseen wall she had first feared.

She showed Corven the direction. He examined the socket, touched the loose mortar, and nodded.

They worked the brine into the prepared contact. Lyra chalked the limits on the known face, then set the vessel’s mouth against the shoe. Her right arm began to ache before the alignment was complete. She changed her stance, bringing the cage against her hip while Corven took its outer handle.

A shout came from the nearer store. Vaska’s people were on the roof.

Lyra drew the release peg and opened the closure.

The brace did not explode. It shortened with a deep, ugly sound, its fibers pressed together until they had nowhere left to go. The iron strap bent over them. Dust left the socket in a narrow pale cloud. For the span of a breath Lyra felt the confined depth leaning through her hands toward the face she had given it.

Then it was spent.

The bead beneath the collar fell. The jar became a jar again, cold and empty of the thing she had carried from below.

Corven pulled her back as the broken brace dropped into the pit. One end struck the cage and tore away a strip of padding. The ceramic body held. Lyra left it where it lay and climbed toward the gate.

Above her, someone had cut the rope that restrained one leaf. It swung inward. Vaska’s voice filled the court, calling her people through.

The first man Lyra met had a bread knife in one hand and an unloaded crossbow in the other.

He had come from the kitchen side of the court without deciding which weapon he needed. He made the choice when he saw her, threw the crossbow into her path, and came with the knife. Lyra stepped over the stock, caught his wrist against the open gate leaf, and drove her elbow into his throat.

He folded forward. She pushed him down and kept moving.

A net fell from the dining gallery.

It spread across the first of Vaska’s people and caught on the handcart they had shoved through the entrance. Somebody above pulled its weighted edge. The man beneath it went down on his side, dragging the cart against the gate. Behind him, the next two could neither enter nor retreat without trampling him.

Lyra cut the nearest cord. Another strand caught her blade. She put the knife away before she lost it, seized the net with both hands, and lifted enough for the trapped man to turn. The load came through her bad arm. She set her shoulder against the cart and used her whole body instead.

A bolt struck the wood beside her head.

Corven crossed the entrance with his chain unwrapped. He caught the falling edge of the net on two marked links and hauled it toward the gatepost. The wet cords tightened. Lyra got the man clear, pushed him back through the opening, and turned toward the gallery.

The crossbowman above was already reloading. She could hear the small click of the mechanism under the shouting. It pulled her attention upward with such force that she nearly missed the man coming along the wall below him.

Vaska caught that man before he reached her. She hit him across the face with the pommel of her blade and drove him back into the empty handcart. He grabbed her sleeve. She pulled herself closer and cut beneath his arm. When she stepped away, he remained against the cart with both hands over the wound.

“Stair,” she said.

Lyra went for it.

The servants’ stair beside the linen cupboard was narrow enough that a man on the third step could keep anyone below at a distance. He had a long fork used for moving bundled cloth. Its prongs had been sharpened. Behind him a second defender held the rail with one hand and a loaded short-stocked crossbow with the other.

Lyra stopped below their reach. She heard Istre somewhere above, shouting for someone to get away from a window. The voice made the house briefly ordinary, a place where a broken dish might still require a warning.

Corven came beside her with the cracked shell in his palm. He had prepared it in the boat. He spoke once, and the man with the small crossbow opened his mouth on a cry that did not leave it.

The man with the fork glanced back.

Lyra caught the shaft, pulled it down, and stepped onto the first stair while its owner tried to recover the weight. Corven took the rail and went around her, his chain moving close enough to brush her shoulder. The silent man fired. The bolt struck the linen cupboard behind them.

Lyra drove the fork bearer against the wall. He let go and reached for a knife. She struck his hand with the iron end, then his knee, and heard something break. He fell across the stair. She stepped on the rail to get past him while Corven took the empty weapon from the other man.

At the top, the dining room was full of overturned chairs. A servant had tried to carry a tray through the fight and dropped it intact against the far wall; three glasses remained upright in its rim. Daro stood beside the table with a short sword and a man bleeding onto the carpet at his feet.

The upper window behind him gave onto the nearer store roof. Vaska’s people had reached it, but someone inside the second store was still firing into the crossing. One of Lyra’s men lay flat with his hand pressed over his eye. Another crawled toward him, dragging a ladder.

Daro saw her see them.

“The far store,” he said. “You should have sent more.”

“I sent enough.”

He smiled with his mouth shut. He looked younger in the disordered room, his good coat open and his hair falling across his forehead. There was nothing ceremonial about the way he held the sword.

He came at her before she had finished taking the room in.

The first stroke caught her knife near the hilt and nearly tore it from her hand. She gave ground, struck a chair with the back of her knee, and felt the second stroke pass close enough to move her collar. The larger weapon had reach. Daro knew the furniture. He drove her toward the table where she would have to turn or stop.

She let him drive her. At the table edge she put her left hand flat on the wood and vaulted sideways over its corner, using the good arm and the low ceiling to keep the motion small. His sword struck the edge where her hip had been.

The sound entered her as separate things: wood splitting, steel catching, the breath Daro drew before pulling free. She moved on that breath. Her knife went through his coat at the shoulder and cut along the flesh beneath. He twisted away before she could drive it deeper.

Corven reached the door behind her.

Daro stepped back. The men at the window had seen him give ground. One lowered his weapon. Another kept it raised until Vaska appeared on the store roof with a crossbow pointed through the opening.

“Put it down,” she called. “The store’s taken.”

The man looked at Daro.

“Is it?” Daro asked.

Vaska named the person who had been commanding there and the wound that had stopped him. Daro’s expression changed. He looked at the sword in his own hand, then past Lyra to the stair where Corven stood.

“Enough,” he said.

The man at the window lowered his weapon. Daro put the sword on the table and stepped away from it.

They brought the wounded down before they counted the living who could walk.

Corven carried the man with the broken knee. Vaska’s people lowered the one whose eye had been struck on a door taken from the linen cupboard. Lyra held the door at the lower end while it came down the stair. The injured man kept trying to lift his head to see whether the eye remained. She told him the healer would look at it, and made him stop touching the wound.

Three of Gannet’s armed men had died. One lay beside the handcart where Vaska had left him. Another had fallen from the store roof. The third was the man Daro had been standing over, killed before Lyra reached the dining room. There were more wounds than deaths on her own side. The knowledge pleased her so sharply that she had to make herself listen when the healer asked for another clean cloth.

Daro came down with his sleeve tied over the cut at his shoulder. Behind him were the people who had stayed upstairs: six surviving fighters, Istre, and ten other retainers whose hands were empty. One of the cooks had taken off his apron and folded it beneath his arm. A woman from the linen room carried a small sack of personal things. She set it down when a guard told her to, then looked at him until he let her pick it up again.

Istre saw Lyra beside the injured man. Her gaze moved to the torn cuff she had mended at their supper.

“I did that better than you’ve kept it,” she said.

Lyra looked down. One of the two stitches remained. The other had broken when she caught the net.

“I haven’t had a quiet evening.”

“Neither have we.”

Istre went to Daro and retied the cloth at his shoulder. He stood still while she worked. When she had finished, he put his unhurt hand over hers for a moment.

Corven came back from the boat.

“The wounded are aboard. I’ve put one of theirs beside ours. He won’t walk tonight.”

“Take them to the surgeon we agreed.”

“I will.”

He looked around the court: weapons in a pile, Vaska at the street passage, Daro standing with empty hands. His chain was wrapped. The eel’s vessel remained in its padded box by the watergate, undamaged.

“The house is yours,” he said.

“For tonight.”

He studied her face. “My work here is done.”

She could have offered more money. She could have asked him to remain until she understood what Daro intended to say. Instead she told him where the second payment waited and that the wounded man’s wages would continue until the surgeon had judged his sight.

Corven picked up the eel’s vessel and went to the boat. He did not leave secretly or in anger. Lyra watched him settle between the injured men, give the master the destination, and push the boat clear with his foot.

Daro waited until its stern had passed the gate.

“There is a way to finish this without another body,” he said.

Lyra turned back to him.

He stood beneath the open gallery with a folded paper in his unhurt hand. Above them, the three glasses still waited on the fallen tray, upright against the wall.

Chapter 16 Those who gave up their arms

Daro’s paper was a berth agreement.

He unfolded it on the kitchen table because the dining room was being cleared for the wounded who had not yet been moved. The agreement gave him places aboard a coast trader sailing the next afternoon. A separate hire covered the small boat that would take his household from Gannet’s quay to the trader. Both masters had been paid an advance.

“I made the arrangement when you took Blackglass,” he said. “You may take that as a compliment if you like. I would prefer you took it as evidence that I understand the position.”

Lyra read the names of the masters. She knew the harbor man. The other was a trader who had worked the southern coast long enough to be written into more than one dispute over cargo. He was real, his vessel was real, and there was nothing in the terms that required a miraculous departure.

“Where would you go?”

“A house I have leased near the next port. Small enough that I can hear the cook complain from every room. We can live there while I decide what trade remains to a man who’s lost his stores.”

“Which stores?”

“These. Both of them. The boats attached to them. The berth rights I can transfer without another person’s assent. The disputed ones I’ll release, and you can make your own arrangement with whoever contests them.”

“And the collections?”

He took another sheet from inside the first. “The routes, the outstanding calls, the people still paying. All of it. I cease collecting here. I write to the people carrying my mark and tell them to bring their business to you or find some other protection. I keep enough money to feed the people leaving with me. You can have it counted.”

Lyra turned the second sheet over. There was no condition on the back.

Vaska stood beside the kitchen door. She had washed her hands but not her face. A small line of someone else’s blood had dried below her ear, bright against the green coat she had wanted for a more amusing evening.

“You’d leave the men?” she asked Daro.

“The ones who choose to go with me. Without arms. The ones who want to stay can make their own way, if she permits it.”

“They won’t carry your mark.”

“No.”

“They won’t collect your money after you leave.”

“No.”

Daro sat carefully. His shoulder had begun to stiffen. He looked at the folded cloth beneath his hand before resting on it.

“I am offering to lose,” he said. “People do it. You needn’t make me invent another word.”

Lyra kept reading. The kitchen smelled of duck fat, damp ash, and a pot somebody had taken off the fire too late. On the wall above Daro’s chair hung a row of spoons, each one different. She remembered him at supper discussing the kind of orange peel that would not turn bitter in the sauce. His ease had irritated her then because he could afford to remain interested in supper while she thought about the terms being offered.

Now he was making terms quickly and well. He had brought something that could be checked. He had given her the stores before asking what she would do with him.

“Send for the harbor master,” she said to Vaska. “The one on this hire. Let him come without a weapon. I want to know where the boat is.”

Vaska took the paper and sent a runner. Daro did not try to prevent it. He asked for water, drank, and asked whether his wounded man would be seen by the same surgeon as hers.

“He will.”

“Thank you.”

She disliked being thanked while she still held the knife that had cut him. She put the knife on the table where he could see it and asked for the store keys.

He had already put them in a small bowl beside the door.

They checked the stores while Daro waited under guard.

The nearer one held bolts of imported cloth, jars of dye, and three chests whose owners would prefer not to explain how they had reached the quay. The far store held oil, rope, and a sealed room of goods pledged against loans. A pair of independent marks had been cut into that room’s door. Lyra would have to deal with the people whose claims those marks represented. Killing Daro would not make the goods hers to sell twice.

Vaska’s men had secured the roofs. The street passage was held at both ends. Nobody was coming to relieve the house. Two neighbors had sent to ask whether the fighting was over and whether they could light their shop lamps without being taken for signals. Lyra sent word that they could. The lamps came on while she stood at the watergate, their ordinary little flames reflected in the channel.

The harbor master arrived in the hired boat. He was an older man with a clean scarf wound around his neck and the offended expression of somebody whose paid engagement had been made dangerous without consulting him.

“I was hired to carry people,” he said. “Their possessions with them. No stores, no arms.”

“You can take all eighteen?”

“With two trips to the outer berth. The trader has places. I was aboard this morning.”

“Could you leave now?”

“If the trader takes them early. She will. They’ve paid.”

Lyra looked at his boat. Its benches had been cleared. Blankets lay in a bundle at the bow. There were spare cups tied together by their handles, brought by a man who expected passengers rather than a fight. He had left a dry space for their bags.

“The way out is open?” she asked.

“It was when I came through it.”

“Will anyone stop him at the harbor chain?”

“Not on that passage paper. If you want to send someone to ask, send them. I’d like to finish before the wind changes.”

She sent a runner. The answer came back within the hour. The departure was permitted. No other house claimed the passengers. Daro’s paid agreement covered the fees, and the coast trader had room for them.

Vaska stood beside Lyra while the runner folded the reply.

“We can have them gone before dawn,” she said.

Lyra watched a kitchen worker bring out the first bags. He had packed them badly, tying two together so their handles strained against one another. Istre took the cord from him, untied it, and made a better knot. She did not look toward Lyra while she worked.

“They’ll want food,” Vaska said. “There’s enough in the kitchen.”

“Let them eat.”

Vaska went to give the instruction. Lyra remained by the watergate. The broken brace lay in the pit where the vessel had crushed it. Her people had fitted a temporary bar across the leaves. By tomorrow a carpenter could make something better. By tomorrow the stores would open under her mark and the boats would carry on with different instructions.

The house could leave. The hired boat waited against its fenders. She could sign the permission and go home with the keys.

Daro came out into the court with a bowl in his good hand. He spoke to one of the younger retainers, listened to her answer, and changed the place where he stood so she did not have to look past a dead man’s blood on the paving. The others began arranging themselves around him without being told. He had lost the weapons and the stores. He still knew where each person would go when he lifted his head.

Lyra had wanted more than the gates. She had known that when Vaska offered her a knife on the kitchen stair. Seeing the household ready to leave together made the want exact.

She went back into the kitchen.

Istre had set food on the work table. There was cold duck, bread, and the white beans that had been kept from the earlier meal. She offered Lyra a plate as if the question were whether a guest had eaten, then seemed to remember whose guest she was.

“There’s enough,” she said.

Lyra took the plate.

Istre cut a piece of bread and put it beside the duck. Her little red case sat near the salt bowl. She had been sewing a torn bag while she waited for permission to leave.

“Tovan got through?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Good. His wife wanted to take a stool. I told her the stool would be there when every promise made by a landlord had worn out, but she was afraid it would slow them down.”

“Did she take it?”

“No. It’s upstairs. Perhaps someone here will want it.”

Lyra ate a mouthful of beans. They had been cooked with something sweet in the liquid, an ordinary pleasure that made her aware of how hungry she was. She had not tasted food since the fruit on the landing.

Istre threaded a needle. Her hands were steady. When the thread would not pass through, she cut its end and tried again instead of wetting it with her tongue.

“Daro asked me to tell you we have no other money hidden in the house. Some of us have our own savings. He said you should ask us separately so he doesn’t seem to be taking a share.”

“I’ll have someone check.”

“You can check mine now.”

“I don’t need your savings.”

Istre looked up. “Then I’m glad of that.”

She resumed the seam. Lyra watched the needle pass through the bag. Two stitches, a small turn of the wrist, the thread drawn flat. It was the same motion Istre had made at her cuff on the dining gallery. She could remember the older woman’s hand taking her wrist without asking, the brief annoyance of being attended to, then the pleasure of having the repair done so neatly.

“Would you go with him?” Lyra asked.

“Tonight, yes. Afterward I’d see whether my sister had room. I don’t know the port he’s going to. I expect people need shirts there too.”

“You could leave the house.”

“I’ll have left it by the time we get into the boat.”

Lyra put down her fork.

Istre’s needle stopped. Outside, someone called Daro’s name and got an answer at once.

“What is it?” Istre asked.

Lyra looked at the red case, at the salt bowl, at the open door through which a person could walk into the court and from there to the waiting boat. She knew the route. She knew who held it.

“I haven’t decided your departure,” she said.

“The men said we could pack.”

“You can pack.”

Istre put the bag down. Her expression did not become frightened all at once. It became attentive, then very still.

“I can stay with my sister here. If you don’t want the household going together.”

“No.”

“I’m asking for myself.”

“I heard you.”

The words lay between them. Lyra could have changed them. Istre waited long enough to give her the opportunity.

Then the older woman put the needle back in its case, closed the case, and picked up the bag. She walked out without taking the plate from the table.

Lyra finished the mouthful already in her mouth. She did not touch the rest.

Vaska came when she called.

“Keep the boat outside the gate,” Lyra said. “Pay the master for the hire. Tell him there’ll be no passengers.”

Vaska looked toward the court. “You’re keeping Daro?”

“I’m ending his house.”

“You have the stores.”

“I want the people who would make it again gone.”

Vaska held her gaze. She did not smile or pretend to misunderstand.

“Which people?”

“All eighteen.”

The noise in the kitchen seemed to recede. A drop fell from a hanging cloth into a bowl. Lyra heard it touch the rim before it reached the water.

“Eleven of them didn’t fight,” Vaska said.

“I know.”

“One of my men won’t do this.”

“Put him at the outer door.”

“And the others?”

“Ask them. Then bring me the ones who will.”

Vaska looked down at her own hands. The knuckles were bruised where the man by the handcart had caught her. She closed each fist once.

“You’ll have to be there,” she said.

“I will.”

She left. Lyra remained in the kitchen while the instructions went out. She could hear enough to know that Vaska was saying what had been ordered. No euphemism reached the men chosen for it. One voice rose, stopped, and moved away toward the street passage. The others checked their weapons.

The harbor master began to argue when he was told. He stopped when Lyra came to the gate herself.

“I’ve been paid to take them,” he said.

“You’ll be paid for waiting as well.”

“That isn’t what I’m saying.”

“I know what you’re saying.”

He looked past her toward the bags. Istre stood among them with the red case in her hand. Daro had seen the master look. He was coming across the court.

Lyra told the master to cast off. He did it slowly. One of Vaska’s people pushed the boat clear, and the oars took the man’s argument out into the channel where it could no longer delay anything.

Daro stopped beside her.

“What have you changed?”

“Your household doesn’t leave.”

“We have an agreement ready to sign.”

“I won’t sign it.”

He looked at the guards moving from the stores. They had brought short bows as well as knives. One carried a coil of cord.

Daro’s face lost the expression he had worn through the negotiations.

“Lyra. You can put me in a room. You can take every coin I haven’t shown you yet and discover that I’ve shown you all of it. You can have them swear before anyone you choose. They don’t have to go together. Let them go separately.”

She said nothing.

“Look at them.”

She did. The cook held his folded apron. The woman from the linen room had her sack against her shin. The six men who had surrendered their weapons were watching Vaska’s bows. One of them began to lift his hands, then held them at shoulder height so there could be no doubt about what they contained.

Daro stepped nearer. “Istre fed my mother when she couldn’t feed herself. She knows where the sheets are. That is the dangerous knowledge you mean to end?”

Lyra looked toward Istre. The older woman stood straight, her bag set down beside her feet.

“I know who she is,” Lyra said.

Daro struck her with his empty hand.

The blow caught the side of her face. It was clumsy because of his wounded shoulder, and hard because he had put the whole of himself behind it. She caught his wrist before he could strike again. Vaska moved, but Lyra shook her head.

Daro tried to pull away. She held him long enough to make him look at her.

Then she drove her knife beneath his ribs.

He made a short sound, more surprise than breath. His free hand caught the edge of her coat. She withdrew the blade and let him fall. Istre came forward at once, taking two steps before Vaska’s first bowman fired.

The bolt struck one of the disarmed fighters. He fell against the man beside him. A woman cried out and ran toward the street passage. The guard there brought her down before she reached the blue tiles.

After that the court would not stay arranged.

The cook threw his folded apron at a bowman’s face and tried to get behind the handcart. One of the men who had raised his empty hands went to his knees. Another dragged the wounded fighter away from the firing line, leaving a dark streak on the stones. Vaska gave short instructions and moved her people apart so they would not strike one another.

Lyra went after Istre.

The older woman had knelt beside Daro. She pressed her hand against the wound and told him to look at her. He was trying to speak. Blood filled the space where the words should have been.

Istre looked up when Lyra stopped beside her.

“You can still stop,” she said.

Lyra took her by the upper arm. Istre’s red case fell and opened on the paving. Needles slid from the cloth inside. One caught the light beside Daro’s hand.

“Stand.”

“No.”

Lyra pulled her up. The same strong fingers that had mended her cuff caught her wrist now. Istre did not reach for the fallen needles or the knife. She tried to make Lyra look at Daro.

Lyra put the blade against the place beneath Istre’s jaw and cut.

The woman’s hand tightened once. Lyra held her while her weight changed, then lowered her beside the bag she had packed for the boat. Her own sleeve was wet. The two small cuff stitches had disappeared beneath the blood.

Across the court, Vaska caught the last man trying to climb the servants’ stair. He had no weapon. He turned at the top of the first flight and held the rail between them until she cut his fingers from their grip and pushed him down. Two of her people finished the killing at the foot of the stair.

Lyra turned when someone called her name. The woman from the linen room lay beside the handcart, moving one foot against the stone. She had been missed when the others fell. Lyra crossed the court, knelt, and ended it.

Then she stood waiting for another movement.

There was none that would carry a person out of the house.

They counted eighteen.

Vaska made the count again because one body lay partly beneath another. She called the number to Lyra without raising her voice. A bowman was being sick beside the drain. The man who had refused the work stood at the outer door with his back to the court. He had kept anybody else from coming in.

“Take the weapons away,” Lyra said.

There were none to take from the dead. The weapons remained in the pile where the fighters had laid them after Daro’s surrender. Vaska had the pile removed.

The hired boat had gone beyond the bend. The channel was clear. The neighbors’ lamps were still burning, though two shutters had closed.

Lyra washed at the kitchen basin. The water turned dark. She changed it and washed again. A line of blood remained beneath one nail, and she used the point of a clean kitchen knife to lift it free. The little blade did the delicate work well.

Vaska came in with the store keys.

“The men want to know whether they’re staying.”

“Half tonight. Change them at dawn. No drinking in the stores.”

“Some will leave after their pay.”

“Pay them.”

“You’ll need others.”

“I know.”

Vaska set down the keys. “I want the standing share we discussed put on the next contract. This house, the Cut, and the work that keeps them.”

“You’ll get it if you hold them.”

“We held this one before the extra work.”

Lyra looked at her. Vaska’s face was pale under the dried blood. She did not lower her eyes.

“The share,” Lyra said. “From tomorrow. Your men’s wages separate.”

“Write it.”

She wrote it at Daro’s kitchen table. Her hand was steady enough. Vaska read the promise, folded it, and put it inside the green coat.

Outside, the first bodies were being carried beneath the gallery. Lyra ordered clean coverings brought from the linen room. The new blue-wrapped household books lay on its shelf. Istre had put them there after coming back from the bindery.

Lyra opened one. On the first page, in a neat familiar hand, were the names of the people to whom sheets had been issued and the days they were to be changed. Other names appeared in the older books: suppliers, borrowers, people who could be sent for when something broke. She had the books taken to her office with Daro’s route papers.

The red thread case remained in the court. She picked it up herself. One needle was missing. She looked around the stones until she found it, pushed it back through the small piece of cloth, and closed the case.

A guard asked whether it should be kept with the house property.

“No. With her things.”

He took it. Lyra walked through the blue-tiled passage into the street. The air was cool enough that the wet sleeve felt almost dry. At the corner a woman stood with a basket of unsold bread, watching the Gannet gate.

“Is it finished?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Can I go home?”

Lyra stepped out of her way. The woman passed quickly, keeping the basket between them until she had gone by.

At Harrow, Lyra found the eating table cleared and a covered bowl waiting. She uncovered it, smelled the food, and put the lid back. The second table held the papers for the next day’s cargo. She sat there, laid the Gannet keys within reach, and began with the first boat that would need somewhere to land.

Chapter 17 The wrong copy

The bed would not go around the stair until they took its legs off.

Tesset had paid two carriers. They stood on the landing holding opposite ends while the umbrella repairer explained, for the third time, that the turn had been built when people expected less from a bed. Leda knelt beneath it with a borrowed wrench and asked everyone to stop shifting the weight onto her fingers.

“I told you Berris would know,” one carrier said.

“You told me the legs didn’t come off.”

“I hadn’t seen the screws.”

“Then stop explaining them now.”

The first leg came free. The others followed more easily. They carried the frame through on its side, striking only one patch of new plaster, and set it in the better room beneath the second window.

Tesset paid the carriers before looking at the mark on the wall. It was small. Leda stood beside her with the detached legs tucked under one arm like a bundle of kindling.

“We can put something there.”

“A picture?”

“A very narrow picture of an accident.”

Tesset laughed. They spent the next half hour fitting the legs back on and discovering that the floor was less level than the landlord had promised. Leda folded a piece of spoiled printing beneath one foot. It bore half a notice about proper drainage. When the bed stopped rocking, she sat on it and held out a hand.

Tesset took it. They lay down without unfolding the cover. The room smelled of fresh plaster and the wood polish the seller had used to make the bed appear less old. From the court came the sound of umbrellas being opened one after another.

For a little while Tesset forgot the knock she was expecting from work. She rested her cheek against Leda’s shoulder and felt Leda’s fingers move through her hair. There was enough room that neither of them had to hold the edge.

Then the knock came.

It was a subscriber’s runner with three notices and a folded copy marked for Tesset’s attention. The first concerned a boat that had arrived late. The second asked for an alteration in a cargo weight. The third announced that Gannet’s two stores and attached boats would trade under Lyra’s mark from the following morning.

The folded copy described the transfer as voluntary.

Tesset read it twice. She had heard that the house had been taken. The first account came from a man who knew only that the watergate had broken and the fighting had stopped. The second said Daro had surrendered. She had been carrying a bed when the second arrived and had been grateful there was nothing she needed to do about it immediately.

“What’s wrong?” Leda asked.

“The copy. It says by agreement, but there’s no signed agreement with it.”

“Ask for the right one.”

“I will.”

She wrote a short request and gave it to the runner. He hesitated at the stair.

“There’s another thing. They’ve asked at the bindery for someone who knows the Gannet household.”

Leda rose from the bed.

“Who asked?”

“One of the women carrying their things. She said the housekeeper bought books there.”

Tesset put the notices down.

“What things?” she asked.

The runner looked at her, then at Leda, and became very young in a way he had not been when discussing freight.

“The things they’re returning,” he said.

They went to Gannet together.

The blue-tiled passage had been washed. Water remained along one edge, trapped where a tile had sunk. A guard stood beside the door with a list he appeared to have been told to use without being taught how to pronounce half the names on it.

Leda gave Istre’s name.

He moved his finger down the sheet. Tesset could see the point at which he found it. He asked who she was to the dead woman, and Leda made a small sound that was not an answer.

“She worked with her,” Tesset said.

“Family get the things first.”

“Then we’ll wait for the family. Where are they?”

He looked back at the sheet as though they might be written on the other side. A woman came from the courtyard and took it from him.

“Her sister’s been sent for. You can come in.”

The bodies lay beneath the gallery. Each had been covered. Some coverings were household sheets, some lengths of new cloth taken from the store. Beside one lay a bag with its handles neatly tied together. The red thread case rested on top.

Leda stopped walking.

Tesset had seen Istre’s hands two nights earlier, closing the cake tin, lifting the lamp, taking the passage permission for Tovan. Her mind kept supplying those movements while the covered shape remained still.

“Was she hurt in the fighting?” she asked.

The woman who had brought them in looked toward the guard.

“She was here when they gave up,” she said.

Leda knelt beside the bag. She did not open it. She picked up the red case, felt its familiar shape, and set it down again with both hands.

“I need to see her.”

The woman folded back the cloth far enough. Tesset looked because Leda was looking. Istre’s face had been washed. A different piece of linen had been placed around her throat. It had begun to darken at one edge.

Leda touched the hair above her ear.

“She hated this part loose,” she said. “It came into her sewing.”

She smoothed it back. The hair moved. Nothing else did.

Tesset crouched beside her and put a hand against her back. Leda leaned forward until her forehead almost touched Istre’s. She stayed there for so long that the woman turned away to give them room.

Across the court, two men were taking down the old house sign. It caught on its upper fastening. One swore, put his foot against the wall, and pulled harder. Tesset wanted to tell them to stop. The wish had no useful shape beyond the sound of wood coming loose while Leda knelt.

Istre’s sister arrived before they left. She was shorter, with the same line between her brows, and brought a shawl that had belonged to their mother. Leda gave her the red case. The sister opened it and counted the needles before realizing what she was doing.

“There should be another,” she said.

“There’s one in the fold,” Leda told her.

She found it. Her face crumpled, and Leda held her while she cried.

Tesset went to the watergate. The broken brace remained visible below it. A harbor boat lay farther along the quay, its master arguing about payment for a journey he had not made. She recognized the clean scarf around his neck from the landing office. She had once helped him recover a fee paid to the wrong collector.

He recognized her too.

“I had places for them,” he said before she asked. “Two trips. The trader was waiting.”

“Why didn’t you take them?”

He looked toward the gallery. “You had better ask the woman who gave the order.”

“Lyra?”

He nodded. His anger had gone beyond the usefulness of raised voices. He took the passenger agreement from his coat and held it out.

“She had it checked. The chain, the berth, the whole journey. There was nothing to stop it.”

Tesset read the paper. Daro’s offered withdrawal named both stores, the routes, the household’s departure. The copy on her table at home had kept the property and left the people out.

She returned the paper.

“Will you say that when the claims are examined?”

“Yes.” He put it away carefully. “Write down where to find me.”

Lyra was at the new Gannet office when Tesset returned after taking Leda home. She had moved a desk to the window overlooking the channel. Daro’s maps remained on the wall. A bowl of keys stood beside a cup of cold tea.

“I sent for the signed agreement,” Tesset said.

“There isn’t one.”

“The copy says voluntary transfer.”

“He offered the stores and routes.”

“With passage for the household.”

Lyra looked up from the paper she had been reading. The shadows beneath the desk seemed too deep for the afternoon light. Tesset kept her attention on Lyra’s face.

“The property was his to offer.”

“And the rest of the terms?”

“I didn’t accept them.”

“Then this isn’t the right copy.”

“It describes the stores I hold.”

“It says how you came to hold them.”

Lyra put down her pen. “You can record the control without endorsing what happened.”

“Yes. I can record that you took them. The subscribers need to know who can open the doors. They also need the claims against the goods. I can do that.”

“And if you write seizure?”

“The pledged cargo stays disputed until the owners are heard.”

“It stays disputed anyway.”

“Then you don’t need me to write voluntary.”

For a moment neither spoke. Below, a boatman called for the office key. Somebody answered that the key was upstairs. Tesset looked at the bowl and recognized how easy it would be to give the man what he needed, then sit down and help make the papers usable. She had liked doing that with Lyra. The pleasure had survived more than she wanted it to.

“Were they armed?” she asked.

Lyra leaned back. “Some had fought.”

“When you killed them.”

“No.”

“Istre?”

“No.”

“Did you know she had asked me for Tovan’s passage?”

“You told me.”

Tesset gripped the folded copy until one corner bent into her palm. She had come to prevent a false entry. She had wanted that small piece of work to occupy the place where another question waited.

“Did you give the order?”

“Yes.”

Lyra did not look away. Tesset wished she would. She wished for something she could refuse without having to go on seeing the same woman who had laughed about the bed, carried Jorin from a boat, and remembered which window she wanted.

“She asked to leave separately,” Lyra said.

Tesset went cold. “I didn’t ask you that.”

“I know.”

“Why are you telling me?”

Lyra’s mouth moved once before she answered. “Because you’ll hear it.”

Tesset set the copy on the desk. Her hand was shaking badly enough that she had to flatten it against the wood before she could release the paper.

“Write that it was seized. Send the owners’ claims with it. Someone at the office will take the entry.”

“You won’t?”

“I won’t put my name to this copy.”

“I can get another clerk.”

“Yes.”

The answer seemed to strike Lyra harder than the refusal. She looked at the paper, then at Tesset’s hand where it rested beside the bowl of keys.

“Will you still come to Harrow?”

Tesset took her hand away.

“Not because you send for me.”

“I’d like you to come for supper.”

“I don’t have another answer now.”

The boatman called again from below. Lyra reached for the right key without looking at the bowl. Tesset knew she had found it by touch. She waited while Lyra called someone to carry it down, then picked up the unsigned copy.

“I need this for the objection,” she said.

Lyra let her take it.

At the top of the servants’ stair Tesset stopped. The linen cupboard door was gone, its shelves exposed. Three blue paper wrappers remained on the upper shelf. She could see Leda’s careful folds along their opened edges.

She went back to the office.

“The books from the bindery haven’t been paid for.”

“Send the charge.”

“I’ll send it to whoever is paying Gannet’s bills.”

“That’s me.”

“I know.”

She left before Lyra could give her the money from a drawer.

Leda had taken the cover off the new bed and folded it on a chair. She was sitting on the floor with a bundle of spoiled sheets around her. Her hands moved through the familiar work of aligning their edges, though there was nothing to be made from them tonight.

Tesset sat beside her.

“She ordered it,” she said.

Leda nodded once.

“I told her I wouldn’t enter the transfer as voluntary.”

“Good.”

The word was flat. Tesset did not tell her the rest of the argument. She watched Leda take a sheet from the top of the pile, discover that it had already been sorted, and put it down again.

“I want the bad book back,” Leda said.

“What?”

“The one Istre brought for repair. The gathering was wrong. I told her I’d fix it.”

“It’s at the bindery.”

“I know. I want to fix it.”

“We can get it tomorrow.”

Leda pressed her lips together. “I don’t want her sister to think we sent it back like that.”

Tesset put an arm around her. Leda leaned against her and began to cry, angrily at first, trying to wipe her face before each new breath. Tesset held the hand that kept going up to do it. After a while Leda stopped resisting the mess and let herself be held.

Outside, the umbrella repairer closed his shed. The last umbrella had not dried. He swore at the evening damp and left it open beneath the little roof. The sound was so ordinary that Tesset could have hated him for being able to make it.

Later she brought water, washed Leda’s face with a warm cloth, and went to buy food from the woman at the corner. She bought too much. They ate some of it sitting on the bed, with the plates balanced on the folded cover. Leda asked whether Tovan had reached his new place. Tesset said she would find out in the morning.

“Find out,” Leda said. “Don’t tell me he must have.”

“I will.”

They put away the plates. Tesset opened the second window a little so the room would cool before sleep. The mint below gave off a faint smell where the evening mist had touched it.

In the morning, Tovan came himself to collect the repaired book for Istre’s sister. He brought his wife’s thanks for the passage and stood awkwardly beside the new bed while Leda found the wrapping. He had brought the stool after all. Someone at Gannet had sent it out before the gate closed.

Leda gave him the book and asked him to carry it flat. He promised. When he left, she stood at the window until his footsteps had gone through the court.

Tesset went to the landing office. She entered Gannet’s stores under disputed control, attached the objection to the false copy, and put the harbor master’s name where the subscribers could find it.

Before noon another clerk arrived with a private schedule bearing Lyra’s mark. It assigned the first boats to the captured stores. The entries had been made in a different hand, neat and entirely legible.

Chapter 18 Staying on the water

Corven brought back the little brass lamp he had borrowed for the Gannet approach.

He had cleaned the soot from its glass. The bent wick holder still leaned to one side, and he set it down with that side facing Lyra so she would not discover it by burning her fingers later. His chain bag rested against his leg. The eel’s vessel stood on the floor inside its padded box.

“I thought you might keep it,” she said.

“I have a lamp.”

“The invitation wasn’t about the lamp.”

“I know.”

She had asked him to come to Harrow before she sent the next proposal. Now she wished she had put it in writing and given him the chance to refuse from somewhere she could not see his face. The room was too bright. She went to the second shutter and moved it until the light fell across the table instead of her chair.

Corven watched without remarking on it. He unfastened the box around the eel’s vessel and checked the water. She fetched the saltwater jug before he asked. He looked at the stopper, smelled the water, and accepted it.

“You still check,” she said.

“Yes.”

He changed a little of the water, careful of the small animal turning against the glass. Its eyeless head found the strip of food he lowered beside it. Lyra sat opposite and watched the ordinary care of a creature whose existence had so often announced that something dangerous was about to happen.

“The Prince will come for the source,” she said. “He can’t leave it divided indefinitely.”

“No.”

“I want you on the receiving side when he does. A defined job. No Gannet streets, no collections. The old works and the people getting out of them.”

Corven put the stopper back.

“No.”

She had prepared a better payment than the last one. She had prepared a share of the machinery work after the fighting, a place he could use without accepting a watch, a promise that he could refuse any assignment beyond the written limits. None of it seemed small until he refused before hearing it.

“You can name the conditions.”

“I’ve named one.”

“Don’t do that.”

His expression changed, a little. “Then don’t ask me to make a price for the thing I am refusing.”

Lyra put both hands on the table. The grain felt raised beneath her fingers. She could hear somebody filing metal in the shed below and the little clicks of the file at the end of each stroke. For a moment the sound became so distinct that she had to make herself attend to Corven again.

“You knew what Gannet was when you agreed to the gate.”

“Yes. I knew what I was doing when I cut the man on the stair too.”

“I haven’t accused you.”

“I haven’t asked you to pretend my hands were somewhere else.”

He sat back. The room held the two of them in a familiar arrangement, opposite sides of a table, equipment placed where it could be reached. She remembered the old yard, his razor, the length of rope he had once kept correcting until she threw it at him. His mouth had looked much as it did now, almost amused and unwilling to make the work easier by saying it was finished before it was.

“Your work ended when they laid down their arms,” she said.

“It did. You paid it. I’m refusing the next one.”

She looked at the chain bag. It was closed. He had not come ready to contest the house, and she had no reason to keep him in it except that he was going.

“Where?” she asked.

“I’ve work on two coastal boats. Inspections, if the masters will hear what I tell them. After that I haven’t decided.”

“Someone reliable?”

“Reliable enough to pay before asking for the second boat.”

She almost smiled. He had arranged his own departure without making it an argument for her to win.

“The money’s below,” she said. “The last of Gannet and the damaged coat.”

“The coat was in the hire.”

“You’ll have to replace it.”

“I know what coats do.”

This time she did smile, briefly. She took the pay packet from the drawer and pushed it across. He counted it, returned the extra she had included for the coat, and tied the rest inside his bag.

At the door he stopped to lift the eel’s box more securely. Lyra moved as if to help, then waited. He had it.

“Will you come to supper?” she asked.

He looked at her for a long moment.

“Not now.”

She nodded. He went down the stair with his own equipment and his wages. Nobody stopped him at the footway. Vaska, who had been waiting below, stood aside to let the box pass without striking the rail.

Lyra watched him until he turned beyond the repaired north cheek. Then she took the extra coins off the table and put them back where they had come from.

Serafine chose a room used for card games in the afternoon, before the house began to fill. Green cloth covered the table. A little brass dish held the counters left from the previous night. Odel picked one up, found a tooth mark in its edge, and put it down without comment.

Lyra came with the map of the positions she held. Vaska stayed outside. There were guards elsewhere in the building, enough that Lyra did not mistake the private meeting for a room she could take.

Serafine wore her porcelain half-mask with a dress the color of old wine. The uncovered part of her face looked rested. Lyra had slept badly for three nights and disliked her for it before the woman spoke.

“I have four requests to protect businesses from you,” Serafine said. “Two from people who were paying Daro to protect them from someone else. I’d like to know how much protection I am being asked to sell.”

“Ask where their goods land.”

“I did. They gave longer answers.”

Lyra spread the map. Odel held one corner flat with the damaged counter. The green cloth made the pale lines difficult to see, and Serafine moved a lamp despite the daylight.

“Harrow is mine. Its title isn’t part of this discussion. Blackglass Cut and the stores at its three windows are held by my people. Gannet’s two bonded stores, house quay, and attached boats will work under my mark. Claims on pledged cargo can be heard without anyone using them to put an armed watch through my doors.”

“Your doors,” Odel said. “Not the lane.”

“The lane stays open.”

“And the route between Harrow and the lower harbor?”

“Paid passage as agreed.”

“You’ve sent armed people through twice outside the hours.”

“Once was a wounded man going to a surgeon.”

“The other wasn’t.”

Lyra knew which other one Odel meant. A pair of Vaska’s watchers had followed a Gannet messenger through the Ragman’s passage without waiting for leave. They had found nothing useful. Their mistake had become part of the fee she would have to pay today.

“They won’t do it again without asking.”

Odel rested both hands on the table. “If they do, they’ll be sent back. If they refuse, we won’t send to you to discover whether you think the refusal is reasonable.”

Serafine watched Lyra over the lamp. The porcelain face made stillness easy for her. Lyra had to choose what to do with her own.

“Agreed,” she said.

They went on to the outer works. Lyra marked the inspected entrance beneath the harbor wall, the upper guard gallery taken from the Prince, the excavation platform, and the smaller receiving branch where her crew could now work. Beyond it she drew a line.

“His inner access remains here. The central manifold isn’t wholly in my possession. I’m not asking either of you to supply men for it.”

“You’re asking us not to supply him,” Serafine said.

“Yes.”

“That would once have sounded ambitious.”

“It still is.”

Serafine looked at her uncovered hand, turning a ring a fraction. “I can close my passages to an armed attempt on the positions named here. I cannot promise that every person who trades with my houses will refuse him a lamp or a meal.”

“I’m not asking about meals.”

“No. You have made the distinction between a meal and a household difficult lately.”

Odel glanced toward her. Lyra kept her hand on the map.

“The Cinder mechanism remains with the neighborhood,” she said. “Their watch, their keys. I don’t collect at its footway. Nothing here gives me your courts or the Court’s warehouses. I want the places I can hold to be treated as places I hold.”

Serafine let the ring settle. “And Gannet’s people?”

“The surviving claims can be brought to the office.”

“I asked about the people.”

Lyra looked up. “You know what happened.”

“I want you to know that the next house which signs something with me will not be left waiting for a boat while you decide whether it should continue existing.”

The room was quiet. From below came the sound of furniture being moved for the evening. Somebody tried a stringed instrument and stopped after one note.

“If your protection includes sending men through Blackglass to collect for a rival, it will be tested,” Lyra said.

“If yours includes entering my houses to kill the people I have agreed to protect, it will be tested too.”

Odel moved the lamp away from the edge of the paper. “Good. Now we know where the argument is. Put it there, on the line. The rest can be made usable.”

It took two hours. They disagreed over a landing step which belonged to a warehouse by day and a drinking room after dark. They settled the path by which a disputed cargo could be removed without passing through Gannet’s inner court. Odel refused a permanent night exemption and offered shorter working windows that could be renewed for a fixed charge. Serafine accepted direct dealings with Lyra’s boat masters, then required a person at each of the new offices who could answer for a missing load when Lyra was elsewhere.

Serafine’s clerk had left a space for public confirmation beneath the schedule.

“Strike that,” Serafine said. “These are our arrangements. The subscribers can keep their objections.”

Lyra made him attach the list of pledged cargo to the schedule before he ruled through the empty space. Odel watched until the line reached the edge of the paper.

At the end, Serafine signed the private schedule of passage and noninterference. Odel signed only the terms she had authority to make. Lyra signed beside them with a pen that dragged slightly on the green cloth beneath the paper.

Serafine offered wine. Odel accepted. Lyra did too.

The wine was good. She had expected it to taste of the conversation and was annoyed by how much she enjoyed it. Serafine noticed and smiled with the part of her face the mask left free.

“You may take the bottle,” she said.

“I can buy wine.”

“Then buy this one. My man will charge you.”

Lyra laughed. The tension in the room eased by an amount too small to alter any of the signatures. She paid for the bottle and carried it out herself.

Feska met her aboard the Winter Pear with a length of rope in her hands and no interest in the name announced from the boat below.

“Wait until that’s fast,” she said.

Lyra waited on the hanging ladder while a sailor secured the rope above her. The ship moved differently from the little harbor boats. Its weight seemed to begin farther away and arrive later, a slow lift that made the ladder press against the hull before carrying it clear. She climbed with her left hand taking most of the pull.

The Winter Pear lay at an outer berth with cargo nets spread along the deck. She was a seagoing trader, broad enough that Harrow’s whole working platform would have occupied only part of her waist. A carpenter was repairing a section of rail. Two sailors were arguing over a damaged jar of preserved fruit, each insisting the other had opened it first.

Feska turned when Lyra was aboard. She was a thick-waisted woman with one sleeve rolled higher than the other and a narrow scar running through her right eyebrow. Her boots had been resoled recently. Lyra noticed the quality of the work and then noticed Feska noticing her boots.

“I don’t take a passenger’s name for payment,” the master said. “You can put the first half in coin and the rest against a claim somebody outside your own office will honor.”

“I’ve come about cargo.”

“Then the cargo can have a name too. It still pays.”

Lyra had come because Daro’s route papers included contracts with ships she had never had enough stock to fill. Some of those masters would refuse her. Feska had asked to see the goods, the claims attached to them, and the person who intended to deliver. It was a useful degree of suspicion.

They went below to a cabin with a table bolted to the floor. Feska showed her where cloth could be carried dry and where oil would have to go if Lyra intended to send both. She named the ports at which she traded without stopping to explain any that Lyra already knew.

“Zakov?” Lyra asked when the northern name came.

“When the season and the rates suit. East along the Amaranthine, through the Thepyrgan passage, then across the Dolmis. Nobody gives me a shorter sea because the passenger has become impatient.”

“Do you have people there?”

“I have people who want the cargo I bring. Most years that’s better.”

Lyra opened the list of goods she could supply. Feska rejected two lots because ownership was disputed, accepted a smaller amount of cloth, and offered a poor rate for the oil. Lyra worked the rate upward. Feska made her pay for the time the ship would spend waiting if the boats came late.

Above them, a heavy load struck the deck. Feska stopped speaking and listened. Someone called that it was sound. She waited for the second confirmation before turning back to Lyra.

“You carry guarded passengers?” Lyra asked.

“Depends on what needs guarding.”

“Someone with enemies.”

“That includes half the men who offer to buy my better cabin.”

“Someone whose friends might try to take him off.”

Feska sat back. “Then I want to know which water, which friends, and who receives him at the other end. A hired man with a sword doesn’t make a destination. If you’re asking me to take a danger away from your quay and keep it indefinitely, no.”

“And if there’s a receiving arrangement?”

“Bring it. I’ll consider the actual person. The ship’s master keeps command aboard. Your escort doesn’t make orders to my crew.”

Lyra nodded. She had not offered a name, and Feska did not ask for one yet. The question could wait until it concerned a person Lyra could put aboard.

They signed for the cloth first. Feska’s sailing was not fixed to a particular tide; repairs and the filling of the hold would keep the Winter Pear in port for several more days, and further waiting could be bought at a rate she named without apology. Lyra wrote down the rate. It was high enough to make keeping somebody alive aboard a ship a business decision even before the guards were paid.

On deck, the sailors had solved the fruit dispute by offering the damaged jar around. Feska took a piece and gave one to Lyra. The fruit was sweet, soft, and soaked in a spirit strong enough to catch at the back of her throat.

“That’s going against someone’s wages,” Feska said.

“Whose?”

“I’ll know after supper. They get more truthful when they’re fed.”

Lyra laughed. She stood beside the mast while Feska returned to the work, enjoying the large ship’s slow motion and the fact that the master had not once asked whether she deserved the goods she could deliver. The disputed lots had been refused. The sound cloth would go. There was room in the hold, if she could fill it.

Three vessels waited in the workshop at the end of the week.

One had been kept from the earlier sound batch. Two were newly finished. Their bronze-lined bodies had been examined in daylight, their seats matched separately to the receiving branch, their closures worked until nobody needed to force a peg. Belis had checked each with the new dividers. Rima had made wooden carrying grips that spread the weight across the cage instead of putting it into one narrow handle.

Lyra tried the grips. They fitted her hands well enough that she asked whether Rima had measured them.

“She watched you carry the last one,” Belis said. “She said you keep pretending that right wrist belongs to somebody else.”

“She could have told me.”

“She told me. I find that’s enough trouble for an afternoon.”
