Rima came through the door carrying the third grip and told him that if he was going to quote her he could at least make her sound less patient. Lyra had not heard her laugh before. It was deep and unguarded, and Belis smiled toward her with the easy expectation of hearing it again.

They stayed for food. The workshop table was too small for everyone, so Esten sat on an overturned bucket and Mavet ate standing by the open door. Lyra brought the wine she had bought from Serafine. Rima found cups that did not match and made Belis move the measuring cloth before somebody put a greasy plate on it.

For most of the meal they talked about the workshop with the disputed roof. Belis and Rima had chosen the other room. Its rent was higher, but it had one owner and a window that could be opened without dropping plaster on the bench. Lyra’s advance had been written as she promised: a sum to be repaid from their work generally, with no requirement that the work be hers. Belis had made her read that line aloud before signing.

Mavet asked how much roof there was. Rima told her. Mavet said she would look at the wall bearing the beam before they moved the larger tools. Belis began to say they could not pay for another inspection, and Mavet held up her cup.

“You can feed me. I haven’t said I’m taking the roof apart.”

The meal lasted until the light left the yard. Lyra enjoyed listening to them disagree about where the long bench should go. She poured more wine for Esten, then noticed he had been drinking very little and left the bottle where he could reach it. Tomorrow he would be at the source shutter. Nobody there needed her to decide how much steadiness he could spare.

They charged the vessels over two working periods, with a full inspection between the second and third.

At the smaller receiving branch, the first cage settled cleanly into its seat. Lyra put one knotted cord on its handle before they began. The second would have two knots. The third, three. The cords marked the order of charging. Mavet had cut matching shallow grooves into the wooden crossrails of the cages: one on the first, two on the second, three on the third. Those marks were for hands in poor light as much as for eyes. They had practiced distinguishing them with their faces turned away.

The source remained fixed beyond the shutters. It had not become friendlier because they had learned a way to take something from it. Water in the low channels lay at a depth Lyra could see, while the pressure beyond the old face continued to make her teeth ache.

Iven Sarr came for the comparison work on an ordinary day’s hire. The Zakov broker arrived from his room near the glass market with clean boots, his own knife, and a paper explaining which later alterations he believed had been made to the branch seats. He put the paper down and made clear which parts were guesses.

“You could leave that sentence out,” Lyra said.

“Then someone would discover it while holding the vessel.”

She kept the sentence. Mavet examined the shoulder he had marked, compared it with the vessel’s collar, and found no need to alter the fit. Iven watched her work. When she disagreed with his explanation of one groove, he crouched to see what she meant instead of defending the drawing.

The first fill was uneventful until the end. Lyra felt the source’s depth settle into the prepared container, called for the source shutter, and kept contact while Esten secured it. They waited for the motion at the receiving seat to stop, then Lyra closed the vessel while it remained seated. Only after its closure was secured did Mavet release the outer isolation face to free it for lifting.

The pressure remained where she had put it. She could feel it through the sound material, restrained and particular. When she lifted her hand, the deep sensation in her chest receded enough for her to hear Mavet asking for the carrying board.

They took the first vessel to the dry upper guardroom and fastened its cage into a separate cradle. Vaska put two people on the door, with instructions about fire, careless hands, and whom to admit.

The second fill took longer. The closure resisted on its first turn. They isolated the source, left the vessel seated, and found a small fiber caught where the peg should enter. Esten’s assistant removed it with a pair of fine tongs while Lyra kept the face steady. Nobody struck the peg to make it fit. Afterward they began the sequence again from its first check.

When it was charged and secured beside the first, Lyra sat on the guardroom floor with her back against the stone. She was not short of breath. That was part of the difficulty. Her body wanted the source again while her right hand had begun to tremble from holding the same position. She could have mistaken one kind of endurance for every kind if Esten had not put a cup within reach and asked her to lift it.

Water shook over the rim.

“Tomorrow,” he said.

She looked at the wet floor, then put the cup down. “Tomorrow.”

They did the third after the rest. Lyra slept in the outer room rather than going back to Harrow and found that she could sleep more deeply with so much stone around her. In the morning the desire to remain there was strong enough that she stood at the stair longer than she needed, listening to the ordinary people above it move through ordinary air.

Then she went down to work.

The third vessel took the charge. The source shutter closed and Esten pinned it. Mavet checked the receiving seat while the pressure settled. Lyra completed the vessel’s closure in its seat and waited through the agreed count. Only then did Mavet release the outer isolation face, and Lyra withdrew both hands.

The vessel with three knots joined the other two.

There were three charged vessels. The Gannet jar had been spent. The Blackglass vessel had been spent. The fixed apparatus remained below, with its branch isolated and the Prince’s inner approach still beyond the line they could hold.

They moved the three charged cages to Harrow on a covered lighter, each secured in a separate cradle, with a guard beside the load and another at the bow. In the locked lower workroom they fastened the cradles to a low bench away from the window. Vaska assigned a watch inside and another at the door. Nobody could reach them from the contested inner approach without coming through the city and the slip first.

Vaska brought the last report while Lyra was checking the lower workroom door at Harrow.

The Prince had moved men into the inner maintenance gallery. Two boats had delivered salt and fresh timbers to the landing behind it. A fitter who worked for both sides said they were preparing to recover the central manifold and strip the receiving branch of its newer fittings. He had been paid for saying so and would be paid again only if the report proved useful.

Lyra asked what he had seen himself. Vaska separated the boats from the rumor about the fittings. The men and timber were certain. The rest came through another worker.

“Enough to prepare,” Lyra said.

“We’re prepared.”

“Enough to finish preparing.”

They took a boat back to the outer works and walked the positions together. Vaska had people at the upper guard gallery and the approach from Blackglass, with relief waiting where the ordinary boat traffic could continue. The Cinder watch kept its own gate and would report strangers without taking orders from her. Gannet’s boats could bring supplies through the acquired quay. Harrow held spare ropes, tools, and the business that would have to keep paying while she was below.

Lyra stopped at the branch passage and laid a hand against the stone. Beyond it, the old works carried a pressure she could not yet direct everywhere she wanted. Beyond that, somewhere within the Prince’s held route, men were putting fresh timber into place.

Vaska waited beside her. “You could keep what we’ve got and make him fight for every doorway.”

“Yes.”

“It’s a good amount.”

Lyra looked toward the stairs that led back to the boats. The three vessels waited at Harrow in their separate cradles. She had won the room with the second table, the dry stores, the men who used her name to open a gate. She intended to keep them without asking the Prince which part he would leave her.

“He won’t have the branch,” she said. “Or the center.”

Vaska nodded once and asked which people were to take the first watch.

Lyra named them. She gave the retreat instruction separately, so nobody could mistake the defense of a position for permission to abandon a crew. They returned to Harrow after the watches had changed. In the locked lower workroom Lyra passed her fingers over the grooves in order, then checked the knotted cords and the fastening on each cage.

One groove. Two. Three.

She shut the door, put the key inside her coat, and went to meet the people who would carry them.

Part Four The Salt Prince

Chapter 19 His father’s coat

Vaska brought the money in a flour sack. She put it on Lyra’s supper table between the bread and the fish, where the damp bottom left a pale ring.

“He’d like an answer before midnight.”

Lyra opened the mouth of the sack. A stack of small bars lay under the loose coins. She took one out, turned it to find the assay mark, and put it back.

“He’s paying you badly.”

“That’s to listen. The rest comes when you don’t.”

There was a cut in the side of Vaska’s new coat. The cloth had been brushed so carefully that Lyra noticed the torn place before the blood on her hand. She reached across the table, took that hand, and turned it palm upward. Vaska let her look.

“Whose?”

“Mine. Mostly. His messenger had a friend who disliked being left outside.”

“How much did the friend dislike it?”

“He’ll have to learn to write with the other hand.”

Lyra fetched the little basin from beneath the stair. By the time she returned, Vaska had cut herself bread. She held the crust in her teeth while Lyra washed the wound, then moved it to the sound hand.

The cut was shallow. A narrow piece of something dark remained in it. Lyra held Vaska’s wrist still and took the splinter out with the tip of the little knife she used for such work.

“Horn,” she said.

“His handle broke.”

“Keep it dry tonight.”

Vaska looked past her to the open door. Rain rattled against the yard paving and ran from the shed roof in a continuous fringe.

“I’ll ask.”

Lyra bound the hand. She had done this for people whose names she no longer knew, in borrowed kitchens and rooms where a bowl had to be emptied before it could be used. Here there were three clean cloths on the shelf. She could take one without asking. That small convenience still pleased her.

“Eat,” she said.

Vaska pulled the chair nearer. The fish had gone cool enough for Lyra to enjoy it. She served them both and left the sack where it was.

“What does he want?”

“You in the outer works when he enters from the Salt Ward. Your people waiting for an attack on the slip. Mine taking the long way back.”

“And you?”

“A warehouse. Two boats. Passage under his colors wherever he can make them useful. My choice of the people who go with me.”

“He can’t make those colors useful here much longer.”

“He doesn’t think he’s finished.”

Vaska worked a bone free from the fish with her good hand. Lyra watched the wounded one resting beside the plate. The bandage was already marked where Vaska had closed her fingers too tightly.

“Would you take it?” Lyra asked.

“From somebody else.”

The answer annoyed her before it reassured her. Vaska saw both changes and went on eating.

“I know what his warehouse is. I used one in Zakov. The roof was sound, the rent was small, and when the owner decided my brother had offended him, the door stayed locked with our winter cargo inside. He was very sorry. We could use his clerk to make the complaint.”

“Your brother?”

“He took a woman home who’d been told to stay at a party.” Vaska put down the fish bone. “She’d had enough of the party.”

“Was it the Prince’s house?”

“One of the houses that made themselves useful to his father. I was younger. I thought being good at the work meant someone would want us to keep doing it.”

Lyra passed her the bread.

Below the room, a cart came through the yard. The men guiding it called to one another, their voices made impatient by the rain. A wheel caught against the threshold. Someone laughed, then stopped laughing when he had to lift his corner.

“What do you want from me?” Lyra asked.

Vaska wiped her mouth. She had waited until the question could be asked without the Prince’s offer between them.

“The room over the rope store. In my name. My men paid from the Blackglass cargo before you find another use for the money. And a share that doesn’t disappear when you decide I have been insufficiently grateful.”

“You have a share.”

“For keeping a route open. You’re asking me to help finish a war.”

“You came here with his money.”

“I could have come with his men.”

Lyra laid her knife beside the plate. Vaska did not move. Rain struck the window hard enough to shake the little metal catch.

For a moment Lyra wanted to make the room unbearable. The knowledge of how to begin was there, below the ordinary thought: the door, the narrow interval beneath the sill, the breath held too long in the person opposite. She had no prepared vessel within reach. There was only the room’s damp air and her own altered attention, finding places that would close.

Vaska took another piece of bread.

“I’m eating your fish,” she said. “I’d like to finish it.”

Lyra looked at her, then let out a breath. The catch stopped rattling when the gust passed.

“The room,” she said. “The pay comes first. A larger part of the Blackglass work, provided you hold it.”

“My part of the work is keeping other people from taking it.”

“Then you should find the condition easy.”

Vaska smiled. It was the first smile of the evening that Lyra trusted.

They argued over the extent of the share while the fish disappeared. Lyra would not give away the landing itself. Vaska did not want to own a wall that had to be repaired every winter; she wanted the money passing it. They settled on less than Vaska had asked and more than Lyra meant to offer. The terms would be written before they went below.

“Before?” Lyra said when Vaska made that last condition plain.

“You might die.”

“So might you.”

“Then my sister will have something more useful than people telling her I was brave.”

Lyra pulled a clean sheet toward her. She wrote the sister’s name exactly as Vaska spelled it, asked where the woman lived, and added the direction beneath. Vaska read the whole page with one finger moving slowly beside the lines.

They signed. Lyra folded her own copy into the business case. Vaska put hers inside the dry lining of her coat.

“How does he expect the answer?” Lyra asked.

“A lamp at the rope store. Red glass, toward the water.”

“He has a man watching.”

“Two. The second thinks he is watching the first.”

Lyra rose and went to the window. The public footway shone beneath the lamps. Beyond it, the lower harbor was a darkness pricked by moving lights. A boatmaster somewhere had brought his vessel too close to another and was explaining, at length, why the fault belonged ashore.

The Prince wanted his way back into the old works. He had offered Vaska enough to make the approach seem cleared, and he would bring more than the messenger had admitted. Lyra could keep him out of one passage tonight. He would return by another, or buy a man with a different sister, or wait until three days of rain had made everybody careless.

She turned from the window.

“Find the red glass,” she said. “Let him see it.”

The three vessels stood in their padded cages on a bench in the lower workroom. The woman on watch had placed her stool where she could see the door without leaning against the bench. She was eating an apple with salt. Lyra waited for her to swallow before asking whether anyone had come in.

“Esten. He wanted the drawing. I gave him the copy you left out.”

“Anyone else?”

“A man asking to see the person who pays for rope. I sent him upstairs.”

“He found me.”

The woman held out the apple. Lyra shook her head, then changed her mind and took a little piece.

Mavet had scratched one, two, and three shallow grooves into the wooden cages to be counted with wet fingers in the dark. The ceramic showed through at the corners. In daylight the jars looked disappointingly ordinary, like something a householder might use to keep oil away from mice. The bronze mouths gave them away only to someone who knew how much work had gone into fitting them.

Lyra put a hand on the first cage. The pressure gathered in a row of small round hollows beneath her palm, travelling past her wrist long after the wooden edge had ended. She took her hand away. The cage had not moved.

Three. They had tried to fill a fourth. Its seat had begun singing, a sound too fine for Mavet to hear until the little pool beneath it moved. They had isolated the branch and left it empty. Revet was making another collar. It would not be ready tonight.

Esten came in carrying the rolled drawing under his coat. His hair had flattened against his head. Water ran from his nose as he put the paper on the table.

“I said one at the outer brace,” Esten said.

“One at the outer brace. The others stay closed until I give the order.”

He spread the drawing and held its corners down with four pieces of iron. Beyond the measured inner wall, Esten had left the paper blank.

“He knows his way through that side better than we do,” Esten said.

“He knows where he expects us to be.”

“That isn’t the same as knowing he will come alone.”

“I don’t expect him alone.”

She called the woman from the bench and asked her to fetch Vaska, Mavet, and the two boat crews. Esten continued looking at the drawing. He had a way of rubbing a particular place on his wrist when he disliked a plan. Lyra had first noticed it after Dannet died.

“You can stop at the lifting point,” she said. “Set the purchase, show the crew, and come back.”

“Who reads the movement when the shaft starts taking a different load?”

“I’ll find someone.”

“At this hour?”

She did not answer. Esten smoothed a wet corner of the paper.

“I want the empty passage kept empty. No men waiting there because it’s convenient. No spare sacks. If we have to come out fast, I want the way I inspected.”

“You’ll have it.”

“Say it to them when they come.”

Mavet arrived first. She brought a small sack of wedges and the blunt hammer she used for sounding stone. Vaska came behind her with three people Lyra knew and a fourth who wore the expression of a man expecting to be sent away.

“He knows the inner stair,” Vaska said before Lyra asked. “Used to carry fuel down it.”

“For whom?”

“For whoever could persuade him to come back up without his wages.”

The man took off his wet cap. “Two turns. Then the door. It opens toward you at the bottom, and the upper hinge has dropped.”

Esten pushed the sketch toward him. He looked at it, turned it halfway round, and pointed with a thick, split nail.

“That stair. Not this one.”

“What’s between?” Lyra asked.

“A passage. Three places to pass a barrel if one comes the other way. There’s a drain at the first. Don’t stand on its cover.”

“Coss,” he said when she asked his name. She told him what the work paid and where he would wait if he could not go farther. He put his cap back on and listened.

Lyra checked the cords securing her codex and the little pot at her waist, then put her coat on over them.

They worked through the approach once. Vaska would take the open side toward the Salt Ward and make the retreat look hurried. Her people would then hold the stair mouths from above. Daven’s crew would carry the cages to the outer landing; another small boat would remain there after the Brindle moved clear. None of the lighter masters had agreed to lose his vessel against a submerged wall.

Mavet would go only as far as the old receiving bay. Esten would set the purchase and haul line where they had tested them. If the line parted, they were to pull the iron drop into its lower seat and leave the chamber. Nobody was to reach through a closing slot to save a jar.

Lyra made them repeat that part.

When they had finished, Vaska looked at the blank space on the drawing.

“What if he comes through there?”

“Then we see him when he comes. That’s all I know.”

Vaska nodded. She preferred that answer to the ones Lyra had heard people give before Gannet.

The rope store’s red lamp was visible from the yard gate. Lyra watched its light draw a short broken line across the water. She could not see the men who were meant to be watching it.

Eda waited beside the gate with a wrapped packet beneath her coat.

“Sera says she will have witnesses at the Sluice House,” she said. “For the public mechanism. Her guards won’t go farther.”

“I asked them to keep it working.”

“She wanted that said again.”

Lyra took the packet. It held the agreed passage writing and a short note about the night watch. No endorsement of the work below. She put the papers inside the dry case at her waist.

“Tell her I heard.”

Eda did not leave.

“What?” Lyra asked.

“They’ve been bringing people out of the Salt Ward. The ones who can walk. A woman asked whether she should go to Marda’s.”

“Was she Worn?”

“She was frightened. I didn’t ask her to open her mouth.”

Lyra looked at the girl. Eda’s face had gone red with the effort of holding her gaze.

“Send anyone who needs a dry room,” Lyra said. “Tell Marda the extra food comes from us.”

“I’ll tell her.”

Eda went. Lyra remained at the gate, listening to the rain. She had almost asked why Eda was angry with her. The question would have been ridiculous. She could have asked which part of the anger had arrived today, but she did not want that conversation before going below.

At the water steps, Daven was making a man remove a knife from his belt. The man protested until the master showed him where the point had been working against the side of the carrying cage.

“Hang it somewhere else,” Daven said. “I don’t care which part of you it cuts.”

They lowered the cages separately. Lyra went aboard last. Daven gave her a place by the forward thwart, where she could keep one hand on the cage with the three grooves. The other two lay between the boots of men who had been told not to move them for comfort.

The Brindle’s repaired bench was dark now, scarcely distinguishable from the older wood. Tarn’s blood had long since been scrubbed away. Lyra rested her foot against the timber as the boat moved out.

The city smelled of rain on hot brick, frying oil, and the sour mud stirred beneath the landings. An upstairs window opened over them. A woman tipped water into the canal and drew the shutter closed before anyone could complain. At a doorway farther along, a boy protected a smoking lamp with both hands while his father struggled to turn a key.

Lyra wanted the journey to continue for another street. It was a foolish wish. She was ready, and she had paid for readiness until scarcely anything remained to buy. Still, the boat beneath her and the ordinary wet city on either side offered a few minutes in which she did not have to choose anything.

Daven brought them alongside the outer landing. The moment ended with a rope thrown upward and the scrape of the first cage against a padded board.

Vaska stood above the steps. She had changed into a dark short coat. The injured hand was wrapped in oiled cloth over Lyra’s bandage.

“He’s coming,” she said.

“How do you know?”

“The watchers left when the light went on. One of mine followed. They opened the inner yard.”

“How many?”

“More than he paid me to admit.”

Lyra climbed. Her right arm pulled sharply when she reached for the high ring, and she changed hands without looking down at it. Above, the old passage breathed cold air through the broken stones.

They carried the cages inside.

The first shot struck the lamp over Vaska’s shoulder.

Glass flew into the wet wall. The flame went out so quickly that Lyra saw the man behind the weapon only as a dark movement where the passage narrowed. Vaska threw herself sideways and brought Lyra down with her. A second bolt passed above them and struck the carrying board.

“Leave it,” Lyra said when one of the men reached back toward the lamp.

He flattened himself against the wall. Coss was already behind the first pier, a knife in one hand and his cap still on his head. Lyra heard boots on the stair above: Vaska’s people running to take the higher mouth before the Prince’s men could fill it.

A little salt drifted out of the darkness. It settled on the wet floor and did not melt.

“You have brought everything,” the Prince said.

His voice reached them from farther away than the crossbow. Lyra could not see him. She knew the easy carrying tone, the pleasure of letting other people strain to hear.

Vaska lifted two fingers toward her men. They waited.

“You have a generous employee,” the Prince continued. “I offered her a place of her own. She thought you should have the opportunity to improve the offer.”

“She did,” Lyra said.

“And have you?”

“She’s been paid.”

Something moved beyond the broken lamp. A man stepped backward into the faint light coming from the upper stair. His face was turned away from Lyra. He was looking at the Prince.

“Then I hope she remains satisfied. I find satisfaction much less expensive after the first few difficulties.”

The man in the light tried to move aside. There was no room. A hand appeared against his shoulder, bare and pale, and he was lifted clear of the narrow place with a gentleness that made the movement worse. He struck the wall hard enough for Lyra to hear his teeth meet.

The Prince came forward.

His father’s skin hung from his shoulders, darkened by the wet. The preserved face had been folded under one edge of the collar; a closed eyelid showed beside the clasp. Salt ran in thin seams down the Prince’s throat. His own mouth was smiling, but the smile did not keep pace with the change beneath it. One side of his jaw moved before the other.

He looked past Lyra at the cages.

“You shouldn’t have carried those so close together,” he said.

Lyra caught Vaska’s sleeve.

“Back. Take them back.”

The Prince put his hand against the wall. White spread across the stone, found the iron brace at the narrowing, and flowered over its heads. The sound was low, almost a hum. Esten shouted from behind them. Lyra could not make out the words.

She seized the nearest cage and pushed it toward Coss.

“The receiving bay. Go.”

The brace bent inward. A crack opened in the old lime beside it. The Prince’s smile settled at last, neat and courteous, while the men nearest him began scrambling for the places his touch had not reached.

Lyra followed the last cage through the turn. Stone burst behind her, struck her back, and drove her onto her hands.

She got up before the next piece fell. The earthen pot knocked against her hip. She put a hand beneath it, felt the intact bottom through its padding, and followed the others.

Chapter 20 The receiving room

Coss had the first cage on the uppermost of the three broad steps below the sealed pale face. He was trying to drag it by one side, which made it turn against the stone. Lyra caught the opposite handle. Together they lifted it clear.

“Where’s the other?” she asked.

He pointed. The cage with three grooves had gone ahead. The second stood at the foot of the steps beside a man who was pressing both hands against his neck.

Blood ran between his fingers. Vaska knelt beside him. She looked up at Lyra and shook her head once, angrily, as though the answer had been offered to the wrong question.

“Bring the jar,” Lyra said.

“Give me a moment.”

The man’s boots moved against the floor. Vaska stayed until they stopped. Then she took the cage and came after them with blood up both forearms.

From the top they turned into the side passage and out into the square receiving bay. A bronze rail ran at waist height along two walls. Off to one side, three narrower steps led down to the small cell where Lyra had made her dedication. Their lamps lit the cleared recesses, the old stone floor and the ceiling above the rail. Below the far wall, bronze-rimmed inspection plates remained dark; behind one crystal, a bent pointer rested against the last notch of its scale.

Across the bay from their entrance, behind the old receiving seat, the iron security drop stood raised over the passage to the deeper gallery. Esten’s new purchase hung above it from the two sound points in the roof. They had meant to close that route after the Prince entered the bay. Now a crooked white crust spread across its lower guide. A freshly cut groove crossed the sill beneath it, carrying brine from the gallery the Prince still held. White threads grew from that wet line into the joints of the stone.

Mavet struck it with her hammer. A chip flew away and another took its place.

“He’s caught the foot,” she said. “It won’t come down.”

Lyra put the first cage beside the rail. “Where does it bear?”

Mavet pointed beneath the guide. A short modern brace held the guide true against an older socket. They had fitted it themselves. Lyra remembered arguing about the cost of the second bolt.

“Take everyone away from that side.”

Esten looked at the vessel. “You’ll lose the guide.”

“Can you set the drop without it?”

“If I have time to fit a shoe under the other end.”

“Then get the shoe.”

He went. Mavet marked the stone below the brace with chalk. Lyra knelt beside her, finding the change in the iron’s grain where the smith had drawn it too quickly. Mavet had shown her the flaw weeks before. It had been sound enough for the ordinary load.

Behind them, Vaska’s people came through the passage at a run. One fell into the chamber and was pulled clear by his belt. The crossbow bolt in his shoulder caught against the doorpost. He screamed when somebody broke the shaft to move him farther in.

“He’s at the turn,” Vaska said.

“Hold him there.”

“With what?”

Lyra looked at her. Vaska swore and went back.

The little carrying frame was lashed beneath the first cage. Lyra freed it, set its two feet against the stone, and pressed the contact face along Mavet’s chalk mark. Mavet laid the worked-brine cloth along the contact before Lyra pressed it home. Her fingers remembered the fitting. She had practiced with the empty weight until she hated the sound of it settling onto its pad.

Mavet caught her wrist before she opened the vessel.

“Your knee.”

Lyra moved it out of the line beneath the brace. There was no room to be embarrassed. She seated the bronze mouth, worked the closure loose, and felt the first pressure arrive through her palm.

The chamber grew narrow without changing shape.

For an instant she could no longer imagine the room outside the point she touched. The weak place in the iron became close, nearer than the skin on her own hand. She could hear Vaska fighting at the turn, the scrape and clash, a man’s hoarse cry. The sound came from a world with more room in it.

Lyra opened the vessel.

The brace folded around its flaw. It made a small, ugly noise, less impressive than the breaking of a chair. The new bolt she had grudged paying for tore sideways out of its socket. White salt burst from the guide in a powder that struck her face.

She kept her palm against the frame until the pressure had passed through. Then the vessel was empty. The weight of an ordinary jar came onto her wrist. Against her hip, something small scraped inside the pot; the sound reached her from far beneath the floor.

Mavet pulled her backward by the collar as the guide fell. It struck the floor where Lyra’s knee had been.

Esten and his assistant hauled the broken iron clear. The security drop began to move. A hand’s breadth, then more. Esten shouted for the shoe, and Coss slid it under the unsupported end with a pole. The drop settled onto it crookedly but held.

The growing white crust slowed at the seam. Beneath the crooked iron, a thin wet line still crossed the sill.

“One gone,” Lyra said. Nobody answered. She repeated it until Vaska looked back and nodded.

The Prince appeared in the passage they had entered by, with a man in front of him.

The man was one of Vaska’s. He had lost his knife and was walking backward, pushed by the bare hand at his chest. His heels caught on the threshold. He tried to move sideways. The Prince kept him upright with a casual pressure that Lyra could see was breaking something inside him.

“Your friends are very determined,” the Prince said.

Vaska threw a knife. The man at the Prince’s hand convulsed and went down. The knife had passed above his shoulder. It struck the cloak, hung there for a breath, and dropped to the floor without bringing blood.

The Prince looked at the place it had touched.

“My father preferred threats made in writing.”

Vaska drew another blade.

“He outlived several of the people who obliged him,” the Prince added.

Lyra moved along the bronze rail, drawing his attention away from the two full vessels Coss had put in the recess beside the steps to the small cell. Between that recess and the crystal-lined well stood the old seat they had used to brace the carrying frame. It was only wide enough for a man to stand against.

“You came here for something you couldn’t reach in Zakov,” she said.

The Prince stepped into the chamber.

The lamp nearest him thinned to a blue point. Its glass clouded. Lyra watched the dark seam at his throat open and close with a motion that was not quite breathing.

“You learned where to put your hand,” he said. “Don’t mistake that for understanding the building.”

“I understand what you paid to get inside.”

“Money embarrasses you less than it used to.”

He looked at the empty cage on the floor. His gaze moved to the two full ones. Lyra could almost see him count.

“And you spend so freely.”

She pushed away from the rail as he reached for her.

His hand caught the shoulder of her coat. The cloth stiffened against her skin. She turned with the pull instead of resisting it, drove the little repair knife through the cloth, and cut herself out of the sleeve. The remaining coat tore behind her. Cold caught her bare upper arm, so sharp it felt like heat.

She stumbled against the receiving seat. The Prince came after her without hurrying.

Vaska hit him from the side. Her blade went beneath the cloak, up toward the ribs. It should have stopped him. The Prince folded around the steel, and for a moment the skin of his face loosened, slipping in small folds toward his chin. Then he turned his head toward her.

Vaska tried to pull the blade free.

“Leave it!” Lyra shouted.

The Prince caught Vaska’s wounded hand. Salt flowered across the oiled wrapping. She made a sound through her teeth and drove her knee upward. He did not let go.

Lyra snatched the empty cage from the floor and struck his wrist with its iron-bound corner. Once. Again. The wood broke. His grip opened far enough for Vaska to fall backward.

The knife remained in him.

He took its handle in his other hand and drew it out slowly, examining the dark wetness along the blade. A line opened beside his mouth. Behind it Lyra saw a depth that her eyes could not hold. She looked away, furious with herself for looking away.

The Prince threw the blade. It struck the bronze rail beside Esten’s head. The man dropped flat, dragging his assistant with him.

Coss moved for the second cage.

Lyra saw the Prince turn toward him and understood too late that Coss was doing exactly what the Prince wanted. He lifted the cage to carry it away. The Prince caught the bronze rail and tore a length out of the wall.

It swept across the recess.

The cage broke against the stone. Ceramic split inside the padding with a sound like teeth cracking. Coss went down beneath the rail. Something invisible seized the little space around the shattered jar.

His cry stopped in the middle.

Lyra felt the escaped depth find her face. It pressed behind her eyes, worked at her ears, made the room lean downward. Mavet flattened herself against the wall outside the spill. Esten pulled his assistant toward the opposite corner.

“Stay out of it!” Lyra said.

Coss’s cap lay by her foot. She could see his hand on the far side of the broken cage, fingers opening and closing against the stone. His boot had turned at an angle no boot should keep while someone was still wearing it.

The Prince stepped toward the third vessel.

Lyra went through the spill.

It took her breath before she could choose to hold it. The floor rose against her knees. She moved in the small remembered sequence of her descent: hand, foot, the next fixed thing, no struggle against the whole weight at once. Pain filled her injured arm. Beneath it came the deeper steadiness, immense and patient, and she hated how much relief it gave her.

She caught Coss under the shoulders. His eyes were open. She pulled him toward her by his shirt, let the torn rail take part of his weight, and dragged him out of the narrowing cold.

Vaska seized his other arm from beyond the edge. Together they slid him across the floor. His damaged boot caught on the broken cage. Lyra cut the strap holding it, left the boot behind, and pushed him farther away.

She tried to rise. The pressure found the space beneath her ribs.

For an instant there was no room to move in any direction. She remembered a room at the University with sun on the high windows, Gyllan’s hand moving a bowl out of the place where she would knock it over. She had been angry with him for seeing the mistake before she made it. The memory arrived without comfort. It was simply another place where she had once been able to breathe.

Then the spill exhausted itself. The cold gave way enough for her to draw air, and she was on her feet with the Prince between her and the last jar.

He had watched her cross.

“You have gone rather farther than I expected,” he said.

She struck him with the broken length of the cage.

It was a poor weapon. It glanced from the cloak and jarred her wrist. She struck again, aiming for the bare face. He caught the frame, and the wood went white in his hand.

Lyra let go. She was smiling. She had not meant to smile, and the sight of his changed expression made it wider.

“Move,” she said.

He hit her across the mouth.

She came to herself on one knee with blood running over her tongue. The chamber had become very quiet. That frightened her until she realized the noise was behind a thick ringing in her ears.

Vaska was at the third cage. Mavet stood beside her with the narrow fitting board, its chalked contact edge wrapped in the prepared brine cloth. Esten had climbed halfway onto the receiving seat, trying to get the upper chain through its old bronze eye. His assistant held his legs. The Prince had turned from Lyra to pull the broken rail free of its last fixing.

They had seconds.

Lyra spat blood onto the floor. She put her left hand against the seat’s cold edge and drew herself up.

“Leave the upper one,” she told Esten.

He did not hear. She caught his sleeve and pointed to the lower eye, the one they had tested under load. He nodded and dropped down.

The Prince swung the rail toward them. Vaska ducked. The bronze struck the seat, throwing a spark from a modern iron shoe. Its end caught between the upright and the wall.

Mavet threw her whole weight onto it.

“Now!” she shouted.

Esten pulled the chain round the trapped rail. His assistant drove the fastening pin through. The Prince pulled back and found, for the first time, that something he had touched would not come with him.

He released it at once. Lyra had been counting on anger. He gave her judgment instead.

He took a single step backward toward the entrance.

Vaska cut the line they had rigged over the doorway. The spare timber pad dropped behind him, striking the floor hard enough to crack. It would not hold him for long. It made him turn.

Lyra took the third vessel from its cage.

The weight hurt. She brought it against the prepared seat, mouth to the bronze receiver, bracing her injured hand against the fixed frame, and felt the mismatch before she could see it. A fragment of salt lay across the face. She could not close the joint.

The Prince looked back.

She scraped the salt free with her thumbnail. The nail split. She felt the small precise pain through everything else, and then the bronze seated.

He reached her as she opened the closure.

His hand closed around her throat.

The first pressure went through her own chest. She had set the angle badly. For one awful moment she was the thing nearest the weak face, and the working took that as sufficient direction. Her spine struck the upright. The Prince’s fingers tightened, each with its separate cold.

Lyra could not speak. She could still turn her left wrist.

She turned it.

The receiver found the short space between its old uprights. Pressure arrived there with the completeness of a shut door. The Prince’s hand loosened at her throat. His body drew inward beneath the cloak.

For a moment he looked smaller, merely a thin man in clothing made for someone broader. Then the skin at his jaw split.

Lyra saw the room through the gap.

That was impossible. The wall behind him was stone. What she saw was movement in a darkness laid over the stone, folds passing one another without room to pass. Her eyes tried to make a body of it. She refused them the time.

She held the receiver.

The Prince’s father’s skin tightened around him. The clasp tore. A preserved cheek turned outward, the closed eye opening only because the seam beside it had broken. Salt and dark water ran from the lower edge. His own face remained above it, lucid and furious.

“You’ll break it,” he said.

Lyra did not know whether he meant the vessel, the seat, or himself. She could feel all three giving.

“Esten.”

Her voice came out wrong, low enough that she felt it in her teeth. Esten threw the loose chain round the Prince’s arms and the lower upright. His assistant pulled from the other side. Vaska drove the pin home with the butt of her knife.

The Prince tried to move his foot. The stone beneath it cracked.

Lyra tightened her grip on the receiver, feeling the held depth narrow toward its end. Soon only the chains would hold him.

“The drop,” she said.

Mavet ran to the haul line. The shoe scraped. Iron descended behind the seat toward the brine groove.

It stopped short.

Mavet struck the wedge. Nothing happened. She struck again. Lyra felt the Prince begin to straighten.

“Move your hand,” Esten shouted.

His assistant caught Mavet’s sleeve and dragged her back. Esten kicked the shoe free. The drop fell the last hand’s breadth and seated with a blow that shook dust from the ceiling.

The iron bit through the groove. White growth broke along the sill; the threads reaching from it ceased to spread. The Prince still strained against the pressure, and the chain shook in its eye.

The vessel emptied into the receiver. She stayed with her palm against it, feeling ordinary bronze grow cold beneath her skin.

The Prince hung against the seat.

His head was lowered. The torn cloak had gathered around his body, preserving its shape where his own skin had failed. A little salt fell from his mouth when he breathed.

Vaska put her knife beneath his jaw.

“Are we finished?” she asked.

He raised his eyes to her. The old amusement had not quite gone.

“With that knife? I should hate to keep you.”

Vaska looked at Lyra.

Lyra took her hand off the receiver. The skin of her palm stayed against it for a fraction longer than the hand itself. She saw the raw place and felt very little.

“Bind what moves,” she said. “Leave him breathing.”

They took the chamber one person at a time.

Vaska’s people cleared the upper passage. Two of the Prince’s men were still fighting at the stair mouth; one put down his weapon when he saw who was held below. The other tried to run through the empty inspection route and met the guards at its far end. His shout came back to Lyra, cut short by the door being closed.

She knelt beside Coss. His face had gone a color that made her want to look at the wound instead. She made herself speak to him first.

“We are going to move you.”

“My foot.”

“I know.”

“It isn’t where it should be.”

“No.”

He began to cry. He was embarrassed by it and tried to hide his face against the stone. Lyra held the back of his head while Mavet put the folded pad beneath him.

“Look at me,” she said. “Coss. Look here.”

He looked. She told him what they would do to lift him, where he would feel it, who would hold the damaged leg. She did not offer him the lie that it would be quick. When they raised him, he screamed so loudly that the Prince lifted his head.

“One of yours?” he asked.

Lyra ignored him. She walked beside Coss until the men had him through the turn and she could no longer reach his hand.

Esten wanted to inspect the central manifold before anyone went home. He stood with one shoulder lower than the other, exhausted enough to sway when he stopped speaking.

“Tomorrow,” Lyra said.

“If there’s a leak behind that drop—”

“We close the outer door and post a watch. You come back after sleeping.”

He started to argue. His assistant took his tool bag from him and went toward the steps. After a moment he followed.

Mavet remained, marking the ends of the crack in the receiving seat.

“No more charges until this is braced,” she said.

Lyra put the empty jar back into its cage. She could hear the water beyond the wall with an accuracy that made the chamber seem crowded. It moved in places their drawing left blank. Some of the movement was higher than the harbor. Some was far below any depth she could account for.

She wanted to follow it.

Instead she fetched the lamp and held it while Mavet finished marking the crack. When they had done, they closed the branch cover and put a physical lock through its ordinary iron eye.

The Prince watched.

“My father would have liked you,” he said.

“He seems to have liked being kept warm.”

She had not meant the answer to be funny. Vaska laughed once from the doorway, a startled, ugly sound. The Prince’s gaze moved to her and back.

“I have given him a very long voyage.”

Lyra crouched in front of him. Up close, the face was worse. He was still making it with the parts available to him, correcting the little slips around his mouth as they occurred. His eyes followed her without confusion.

“There is another one,” she said. “For you.”

“You imagine I will leave?”

“You will be carried out of this room. Then we can discuss how much of the journey you want to spend lying down.”

His mouth moved. She waited for the answer. It did not come.

She stood and called the men with the carrying board.

Outside, the rain had stopped. The stone landing shone under a sky beginning to lose its stars. Lyra stepped onto it with the taste of blood still in her mouth and found that she could hear the boats before she could see them.

The Salt Ward storekeepers would not hand over their keys to Vaska.

They had gathered in the yard above the inner approach, three men and a woman with their coats put on over whatever they had been sleeping in. Behind them, the store doors were closed. A watchman stood beside the largest with his spear upright and both hands resting on it. He looked at Lyra coming up the steps, then at the blood on Vaska’s sleeves.

“The Prince will want the morning issue,” the woman said.

Lyra stopped on the last step. The effort of climbing had made her mouth begin bleeding again. She swallowed before answering.

“There won’t be one under his order.”

“I was told to open at first light.”

“You were told before this.”

The woman looked past her. The carrying party was coming out of the lower passage. They had kept the Prince’s bindings out of sight beneath the cloak, but nobody could mistake the way the men held the board level or the guard walking close beside his head.

One of the storekeepers made a small sound. The watchman lowered his spear halfway, realized he was pointing it toward Lyra, and brought it up again.

“Put it against the wall,” she told him.

He did.

The Prince lifted his head as the men passed. His gaze found the woman with the keys. She reached toward him with one hand, stopped, and folded it into the other.

“The morning issue,” he said.

His voice was quieter than it had been below. The woman leaned forward to hear. Lyra stepped between them.

“Take him to the customs room.”

The carrying men continued. The Prince’s eyes remained on the woman until the turn hid him.

She began to cry, very quietly. Lyra did not know whether it was fear for him or for herself. She waited until the woman wiped her face with a sleeve.

“Who opens the food store?” Lyra asked.

One of the men held up a key.

“Open it. The people in the lodging rooms will eat before anyone moves goods.”

“We need the signed issue.”

“You’ll have my name.”

“The accounts are kept inside.”

“Then we won’t have far to carry it.”

The man went to the door. His hands shook enough that he could not put the key into the lock. Vaska took it, opened the door, and gave it back without comment.

Lyra walked through the stores with the woman. Grain, lamp oil, iron, cloth. Goods she had fought to acquire a cartload at a time lay in ordered rows behind doors she had once been admitted through only when somebody expected her. The smell of the place had not changed: dry meal, lamp smoke, the sweetness of spoiled fruit near the back wall.

A small room off the main store held the workers waiting for the morning issue. Some stood when the light reached them. One remained seated with an empty bowl pressed between her knees. Heth was near the door, carrying his cloth bundle.

He looked at Lyra’s ruined coat, her hands, the place beside her where the Prince’s keeper had stopped.

“Is he dead?” he asked.

“No.”

“Are we working?”

“Not this morning.”

He nodded. It was an answer he could use. He went to the seated woman and spoke to her until she raised her head.

The keeper stood against the wall. “What should I do with them?”

Lyra looked at the people in the room. She saw Tarn’s old reaching movement in a man by the window and the beginning of a different one in the woman with the bowl. She had taken the rooms. Now everyone inside them was waiting to discover what that meant.

“Food first,” she said. “Send for the attendants from the shore house. Anyone who is hurt gets seen.”

“And afterward?”

“I’ll tell you.”

The keeper nodded quickly. Lyra disliked the speed of it, then found herself grateful when the woman went to do exactly what she had said.

At the yard gate, an early carrier waited with his empty cart. He had come to collect meal for a kitchen and could see enough of the night on the people around him to understand that the usual explanation would not serve.

“Where do I ask now?” he said.

Vaska pointed to Lyra.

He came toward her, cap in hand. Lyra made herself stand straight while he described the order. She remembered the kitchen. They had paid late twice and served good fish when the cook was left alone to buy it.

“The same quantity,” she said. “Pay at Harrow when it’s delivered.”

He bowed and hurried back to his cart.

The yard began to move around her. A door opened. Somebody fetched a measure. The keeper asked which of the men should accompany the load, and this time she asked Lyra directly.

Chapter 21 The ship east

On the ninth morning the Prince asked for a mirror.

The woman tending him brought the request to Lyra at Harrow. She had come for fresh linen and the balance of her pay, and plainly considered the mirror the least important of the three things.

“Can he stand?” Lyra asked.

“He stood yesterday. Then he sat down before I could find out whether he’d meant to.”

“Let him have one.”

“Glass or polished metal?”

“Whichever costs less to replace.”

The woman gave her a look. “The glass is easier to keep clean.”

Lyra paid for glass.

She had the coins counted onto the table because her right arm was still stiff in the mornings. The raw palm of the other hand had closed under Gyllan’s old practice and a surgeon’s bad-tempered supervision. It hurt more now that it looked better. She had tried to conceal that fact from the surgeon until he made her hold an ordinary cup.

Across the yard, Coss sat in a borrowed chair with his leg stretched on a padded board. The surgeon had saved the foot. No one had promised how well it would carry him. Coss was teaching a child to whistle through a reed, with a lack of success both seemed to enjoy.

Vaska came through the gate while Lyra was watching them. She had the same dark coat, repaired, and a new knife with a narrow hilt her damaged hand could close around. There were pale patches above the bandage where the salt had reached her wrist.

“Feska’s brought the boat round,” she said. “She wants you to look before she says yes.”

“She said yes yesterday.”

“Yesterday she thought he could use a cabin.”

Lyra put away the money box and took her coat. A porter held the door for her. He had begun doing that after the fight, and did it now with such anxious attention that she thanked him more carefully than the service deserved.

Outside, someone had painted a little wave on the stone beside the gate. Dark blue, hastily done. Beneath it a knife pointed upward. The paint was still wet enough for rain to draw a pale line down from the hilt.

Lyra stopped.

“Who?” she asked.

Vaska glanced at it. “Could be a customer.”

“That narrows it.”

“Do you want it scrubbed off?”

Lyra looked at the knife. Its handle had been painted too short for a useful grip.

“Let the rain have it.”

She walked on. At the corner, a man who had been arguing over the price of a basket stopped in the middle of his sentence. The seller kept his hand on the basket until Lyra had passed, then named the same price again.

The Winter Pear lay at a berth with enough water beneath her to leave on the next suitable tide. Her hull looked broad after the harbor lighters. The men working her rigging moved above Lyra with the unhurried concentration of people whose mistakes would happen a long way from shore.

Feska met them at the gangway. She wore no weapon Lyra could see. A silver clasp held her collar closed, and a smear of tar marked the cuff below it.

“Come and look at where you want me to put him,” she said.

The room was forward of the main cargo space, reached by a narrow stair. Its timber lining had been taken back to expose the knees. Two men were fixing removable screens so a guard could watch without sharing the compartment. The door opened outward. Lyra tested its movement and looked at the space beneath the sill.

“No salt in the stores beside it,” she said.

“I’ve moved the cured fish. My crew will have something to say about the inconvenience.”

“They can say it to me.”

“They’ll say it to the cook. The cook will say it to me. You’ll be ashore.”

Feska put a foot on the lowest stair and studied the room. “I can carry him. I can carry the men you’ve hired to watch him. I won’t promise you a voyage with nothing happening, and I won’t put a stranger in charge of my ship because you have paid him to look fierce.”

“You command the ship.”

“Then your men obey me when I tell them where they can stand.”

“If you don’t tell them to leave him unwatched.”

“If I need that room empty, you’ll want them out of it.”

They looked at one another. Feska was not trying to be brave. She wanted an agreement that would still mean something when a wave came over the rail.

Lyra ran her fingers along the exposed timber. Sound wood. Freshly inspected. A door that could be blocked from outside without imprisoning the guards behind it. She would have wanted more iron, but more iron would give the Prince more places to make his salt fast. The answer to every alteration seemed to include something she disliked.

“The watchers can move to the upper hatch if you judge the lower position unsafe,” she said. “Someone keeps sight of him. No landing before Zakov except to save the vessel or the lives aboard. If that happens, the escorts keep him with them.”

“And if he makes himself impossible to carry?”

“Bring back witnesses to what happened.”

Feska considered that answer for longer than Lyra liked. Then she climbed the stair.

Above, the air tasted of tar and the onions being cut outside the cook’s narrow room. Lyra put a hand against the mast while the master showed Vaska where the watch could sleep.

There would be two escorts, sea boats belonging to houses that had lost money to the Prince and now preferred an expensive certainty. They would take their own masters, their own provisions, and enough armed men to make a convenient landing difficult. The Winter Pear would trade on the way, because Feska refused to waste a voyage merely to make Lyra feel that the world had been arranged around one passenger.

“East along the Amaranthine,” she said. “Through the Astroegro Straits with Thepyrgan clearance, then into the Dolmis and north to Zakov. If that wind turns against us, we shorten sail and eat the extra provisions. Buy those now.”

“Your receiver there?”

“Still has a warehouse. Still has reasons to keep doing business with me. The Prince has a landing and a house he hasn’t sold, whatever stories his servants tell about exile. They’ll find room for him.”

“They might send him back.”

“They might. They won’t send him back on my ship.”

Vaska came to the rail beside them. “The receiving master knows whose boats are escorting?”

“He knows their flags and what his own next three voyages are worth. He’ll give you the acknowledgment you asked for. I want mine paid before we cast off.”

Lyra agreed. It would leave less money than she had intended for the new roof. The roof could be covered for another season. The Prince could not.

Serafine received her in a room whose flowers had been changed that morning. Lyra could smell the cut stems in the water beneath the sweeter scent. The woman serving wine set Lyra’s cup on a little square of felt, hesitated, and moved her own sleeve away from it.

The cup had begun to mist.

“Leave us,” Serafine said, without looking at the servant.

Lyra waited until the door closed. The porcelain half-mask looked warmer than Serafine’s exposed cheek. One of its painted flowers had been repaired with a line so fine Lyra could see it only when the woman turned.

“Your boats are ready?” Serafine asked.

“The ships. Yes.”

“Then you have won something. Try not to make the victory more expensive than the war.”

She moved a paper across the table. The names on it belonged to houses prepared to refuse the Prince berth, hired crews, and storage if he returned. Some names had come willingly. Others had been written after Serafine made personal calls.

“The Ragman’s people?” Lyra asked.

“You can ask Odel yourself. She would be insulted if I answered for her.”

“She was insulted before I arrived.”

Serafine smiled. “Then you have been spared the work.”

Lyra read the paper. Each house had pledged only the services it controlled, with penalties if another signatory broke the arrangement. Serafine had left no space for Lyra to countersign above them.

She put the paper down.

“Your messengers have been using the passage behind Gannet without paying.”

“My messengers helped make your passage possible.”

“They can remind the gatekeeper when they pay.”

Serafine’s smile faded without becoming displeasure. She studied Lyra as if a useful new detail had appeared in a familiar piece of work.

“You will want people to bring you news.”

“I’m charging for the carts behind them.”

“There is a difference.”

“Tell them.”

Serafine lifted her cup. “I will.”

They spoke of the ships, the retained Salt Ward stock, the ordinary tradesmen who should be allowed to collect goods they could prove they had supplied. Lyra conceded one disputed consignment because she needed the merchant’s barges. She refused a second because Serafine wanted it too easily.

When they were done, the older woman rose and came to the window. Below, a garden path had been swept clear of fallen petals. A gardener was putting some back around the roots of a shrub.

“You could have been comfortable,” Serafine said.

Lyra joined her. “I am finding out what I like.”

Serafine glanced at the bandage under her glove, then at the shape the cold had left on the wine cup.

“So are we.”

Lyra took the signed paper and went. The servant opened the door before she reached it.

The mirror had improved the Prince’s appearance.

He sat in the old customs room under watch, his cloak arranged over a fresh linen lining. Somebody had sewn its torn edge. The stitch was visible, hurried in one place where the material had resisted the needle. His own face had been made symmetrical again. Lyra could see how much attention he devoted to keeping it so.

He looked at her split lip.

“You should let someone do that properly.”

“It is being done.”

“Then perhaps someone else.”

She put Feska’s terms on the table. He did not reach for them.

“You leave tomorrow if the water serves. The Winter Pear. Two escorts. You may take personal goods that the search clears and two unaltered attendants who choose to go.”

“Unaltered.”

“No Worn.”

“You have become particular about company.”

“You won’t be rebuilding your labor house from the ship.”

The Prince’s gaze stayed on her. Lyra could hear a small dry movement beneath his collar. It stopped when he brought one hand to the seam.

“And what will become of them?”

“They are no longer yours,” Lyra said. “Read the terms.”

He laughed softly. The sound made one of the guards shift outside the door.

“You are quite right. I have spent too much time explaining things to people who meant to keep them.”

Lyra left the paper where he could reach it. He read at last. The old watchful precision returned to his eyes. He asked about the escort masters, the port receiving arrangement, the goods already taken from his stores. He wanted one small bronze chest kept closed. Lyra said it would be opened. He said the contents might disappoint her. She said she could bear that.

“My father was returned to Zakov after his first failure,” he said when he had finished. “He made the voyage in better accommodation.”

“Did he come back here?”

“He found something else to do.”

Lyra watched the seam at his throat. He had not offered her a promise. She would have distrusted one.

“Keep looking,” she said.

For the first time his expression became plain anger, without courtesy laid over it. His hand tightened on the arm of the chair. Salt pushed through the new linen. The guard at the door put his own hand on his weapon.

Lyra remained seated.

The Prince made himself loosen his grip. One finger uncurled more slowly than the others. He saw that she had noticed and looked away toward the mirror.

“I will have my own clothes.”

“The search will clear them.”

“You make everything sound like a kindness performed by an idiot.”

“I’m tired.”

He looked back at her. Then, unexpectedly, he smiled.

“Yes,” he said. “That part improves very little.”

They brought him to the ship shortly before dawn.

The two attendants walked behind the carrying chair. One was a thin man with a shaving case; the other carried a bundle of clean linen and had asked twice what happened if she changed her mind in Zakov. Feska had written the fare home into the agreement. Lyra paid it in advance. The woman still looked doubtful, but she came aboard of her own accord.

Vaska’s men had cleared the gangway. A few people watched from the adjoining landing. They had risen early for work or had not gone to bed; news of the departure passed among them in murmurs that stopped when the chair was turned toward the ship.

The Prince insisted on walking the gangway.

Lyra allowed it. Two watchers went ahead and two behind. He rose slowly, gathered the cloak, and took the first step without assistance. The patched skin dragged over the board. A little pale dust fell where it touched.

Halfway across, he stopped and looked back at Silkstrand.

Lyra stood at the shore end. Behind her, the roofs were becoming distinct from the sky. Someone had begun lighting a kitchen fire. The smoke rose straight for a few feet before the breeze bent it toward the water.

“An expensive place,” he said.

“You should have left sooner.”

“Most people should.”

He continued. Feska received him at the rail with the same grave attention she gave the ordinary cargo. When the party had gone below, she came back to Lyra and held out her hand for the final acknowledgment.

Lyra took it from the inner case, signed, and watched Feska check that the ink was dry before folding it. Vaska passed the escort master’s copy to the waiting boat. Everything that could be done from shore had been done.

The first line came free.

Lyra stayed while the Winter Pear moved off the quay. She watched the escort boats take their places, the master’s figures working at the rail, the slow change in the angle of the hull. No face appeared at the forward opening. The Prince had been taken below and remained there.

Vaska came to stand beside her.

“He’ll hate the onions,” she said.

“What onions?”

“The cook uses them in everything. I asked.”

Lyra laughed. It hurt the split in her lip. She put a finger against it and laughed again, quietly, while the three vessels found their way toward open water.

When she could no longer distinguish the men on the nearest deck, she turned back to the city.

Heth waited beside the customs steps. His hands rested on a cloth bundle. One joint in his left thumb opened with a small click when he lifted it to greet her.

“Now,” he said.

Lyra stopped in front of him. The sunlight had reached one side of his face. He moved a little until the wall shaded it.

“Come inside,” she told him.

He gathered the bundle and followed her up the steps.

Chapter 22 Those who remain

Tesset first heard about the removal from a woman who wanted to know whether she should wash the spare blankets.

They were standing in the rope loft. Hessa sat at the far window with her basket, watching a gull on the opposite roof. Jast had begun to put weight through his healed leg for a few careful steps. The attendant had moved the stool so he could rest before the turn by the door. Tesset had come to arrange the next month’s pay.

“Wash them for what?” she asked.

“The boat. The man who came yesterday said they would need bedding that belonged to no one here.”

Tesset put down her bag.

The attendant went to the shelf and brought a folded notice. It had been written in an unfamiliar hand. The instructions were plain: people in the listed rooms were to be ready for transfer when called; caregivers should report the things each person required; personal possessions could travel if one attendant could carry them. The departure date was left open.

At the bottom was Lyra’s name.

Tesset read it again. The paper made no claim that the people were dangerous. It did not ask whether they wanted to leave.

“Did you speak to her?”

“I spoke to the man who came.”

“Who was he?”

The woman described him. Tesset had seen him at the Blackglass landing, taking messages for Vaska. He had a way of smiling before answering that made every question seem a little late.

Jast reached the stool and sat with an exhalation that was almost a word. The attendant went to him. She straightened the cloth beneath his knee, then waited while he found a comfortable position by himself.

“I won’t tell them the bedding is ready until somebody tells me who will change it,” she said to Tesset over his head. “They can be angry if they like.”

“I’ll find out.”

“Don’t say that unless you will.”

Tesset took the notice with her.

Sera already had one.

Hers lay on the table beneath a cup whose tea had gone untouched. There were other papers beside it, none of them open. Sera was looking at the window when Tesset entered.

“How long have you known?” Tesset asked.

“Since yesterday evening.”

“Why didn’t you send for me?”

“I went to see her. You’d finished work.”

Tesset sat without taking off her coat. The back of her neck was damp from walking quickly. She disliked having arrived out of breath when Sera had already had hours to arrange her face.

“And?”

“She means it.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It’s the answer I got.”

Sera pushed another sheet toward her. It listed the places expected to send people. The rope loft. Marda’s shore house. Three surviving Salt Ward lodging rooms. A warehouse where the Prince had kept the Worn between shifts. Ada’s address.

Tesset stopped there.

“No.”

“I said that.”

“He lives with her.”

“I said that too.”

She looked up. Sera’s mouth had tightened, and Tesset realized that the older woman had not arranged her face so much as exhausted the available expressions.

“The Court is closing its doors to them,” Sera said. “The warehouse houses have agreed not to employ or house them. Odel says the Ragman’s ways won’t be used to move them secretly between rooms. Nobody wants the Prince to have something waiting when he comes back.”

“Then they can stop paying him. They can stop buying his work.”

“They find this easier.”

Tesset looked at Ada’s address again. She could see the yard, the high door that blew closed, the strip of shade Jorin followed beside the wall. She had spent an afternoon arranging someone to sit with him while Ada washed other people’s sheets. It had taken four visits to find a woman who understood when to leave the cup alone.

“What are we doing?” she asked.

Sera rubbed her forehead. “Keeping the care paid while we argue. Making certain nobody takes the Prince’s old keepers as the only people available to accompany them. Asking which households will stand behind an exception.”

“Ada will.”

“I know.”

“Marda will.”

“Marda has.”

For a moment Tesset wanted to push the papers off the table. The desire was so sudden and childish that she gripped the edge instead.

“This isn’t an order she can give us.”

“She has given it to the men at the doors.”

Sera got up. Her skirt caught on the chair; she jerked it free more roughly than necessary.

“Come with me,” she said. “Bring the addresses.”

Lyra received them downstairs at Harrow because the upper room was crowded with drawings. Two men waited in the yard with a broken cart axle. A woman stood beside them holding a basket against her hip. When Lyra came out, all three began speaking at once.

She let the woman finish first. The woman’s husband had been unloading goods when a driver backed over the cart tongue. She wanted it repaired before someone decided the damaged thing proved the driver had not been paid.

Lyra called Orrel. “Find the axle we took out last month. It should fit well enough to get them home.”

The woman thanked her with such relief that Tesset looked away.

Inside, the table had been cleared for them. Lyra sat at one end. Her lip was split at the corner, the swelling mostly gone. The fingers that had held the vessel rested on the table with a thin new shine to the skin. Tesset remembered the excitement of seeing those hands do something difficult well. She had once been proud of understanding what Lyra would need before being asked.

Sera put the notices in front of her.

“You cannot turn a household into a labor holding because its occupant was once forced to work for him.”

“I haven’t.”

“You’ve put them on the same boat.”

Lyra looked at the page. “They have the same destination.”

Tesset leaned forward. “Jorin has a home.”

“I know where he lives.”

“Then take his name off.”

For a few seconds no one spoke. Outside, the broken cart was being lifted. The woman called a warning about a loose board, and Orrel answered that he could see it.

Lyra drew the list nearer.

“The city under the sand draws them already. Heth knows the way. There are people there who understand what they are.”

“Ada understands Jorin,” Tesset said.

“Ada cannot stop someone using him again.”

“Can you stop someone using any of us?”

Sera put a hand flat on the table, not touching either woman. “We have a supported room, paid care, and a person prepared to be responsible for him. Those were the things you demanded before Aria could leave.”

Lyra’s expression changed. Tesset wished Sera had left Aria out of it, then hated herself for still considering how to make a truth less painful for Lyra to hear.

“Aria is not Worn.”

“Jorin is not one of the Prince’s stores,” Tesset said.

“I brought him out.”

“Yes. You did.”

Lyra looked at her. Tesset held the look. She would not give away the rescue to make this easier to describe. She had watched Ada see him again. That had happened.

“You came back,” Tesset said. “You kept visiting. She trusted you to come through the gate.”

Lyra’s hand closed on the list. The paper creased beneath her fingers.

“I’m sending attendants. Food. The things they need. Anyone who belongs with them can go at my expense.”

“She belongs in her own house,” Tesset said.

“She can keep the house.”

The answer was so wrong that Tesset could not find another one immediately. Lyra saw the silence and went on.

“The Prince is gone. His ships are gone. His keepers cannot walk back into the Salt Ward and find their people waiting. I’m not leaving that work half done.”

Sera took the crumpled page back. She smoothed it carefully before speaking.

“These are not the same task.”

“They are to him.”

“He no longer gives the orders here.”

Lyra rose. “No.”

Tesset felt Sera become still beside her.

The quiet lasted long enough for the cart to be lowered onto its replacement axle outside. The woman laughed, a short surprised burst of delight at finding that it would stand.

Lyra sat again.

“Give me the requirements,” she said. “Names of attendants willing to go. What the people need for the voyage. I’ll pay the amounts you can justify.”

“We won’t write that they’ve agreed,” Sera said.

“I haven’t asked you to.”

Tesset looked at the face she knew, at the fatigue beneath the eyes and the healed mark along the cheek. She had been waiting for something that would make Lyra seem unfamiliar enough to stop this hurting. It did not arrive.

“Ada will come to you,” she said.

“I’ll go to her.”

“Don’t bring the men.”

Lyra looked toward the yard. “I wasn’t going to.”

Marda met Tesset at the shore-house door with her apron still on. Flour marked one cheek. Her changed skin made the pale dust look like something that had worked its way outward.

“She won’t hear it?” Marda asked.

Tesset shook her head.

“Then I’ll ask again.”

“Sera will come with you.”

“I didn’t ask for company.” Marda stopped, looked at Tesset, and wiped her cheek with the heel of her hand. “Sorry. Come in. I’ve been saying the same thing all morning.”

The house smelled of warm bread and wet wool. A child was lying on his stomach under a table, drawing a boat in spilled flour. Jorad stepped around him with a bucket. He nodded to Tesset without stopping.

In the cool room, Tarn had put his rope across the empty chair beside him. Tesset moved it carefully before sitting. The shortened ends of his two injured fingers rested against his thigh. His other hand went toward the rope, stopped when he saw where she had put it, and came back.

“We can pack that,” Marda said from the doorway. “If we have to.”

Tesset waited.

“He likes the softer one. The stiff pieces catch his skin. They won’t know.”

“We can tell them.”

“Yes. And then they can listen to whichever person speaks most confidently.”

Tesset had no answer. She took out the little book in which she kept the care arrangements. Marda came to stand beside her and described the rope, the bowl, the places Tarn could sit without reaching toward work that was not there. Tesset wrote what she said in ordinary words. No one needed to turn it into a diagnosis to remember it.

Jorad brought tea. He set one cup beside Marda and another beside Tesset, then stood looking at Tarn.

“They’re not taking us?” he asked.

“No,” Tesset said.

“People in the lane say all the changed.”

“The notice says Worn. Callista is staying too.”

He nodded, with a relief he seemed ashamed of a moment later. Tesset was grateful he had asked. She could at least give that answer clearly.

Marda sat beside Tarn. He moved the rope a little nearer her knee.

“Maeron would have said something beautiful,” she said.

Jorad looked toward the kitchen. “Then we would still have had to pack.”

Marda let out a breath that might have become a laugh on another day. She took up her tea and found it already cool enough to drink.

Leda was mending a cover when Tesset came home. She had spread the work across the wider bed and was counting holes with the needle tip. A new seam lay crooked along one edge. Tesset sat in the only uncovered space.

“Did you eat?” Leda asked.

“I don’t remember.”

“That generally means you didn’t.”

“I might have.”

Leda put down the needle. She fetched the little pot from the shelf and showed Tesset what remained inside. Beans, thickened around a piece of onion. The smell made Tesset hungry so abruptly that she nearly cried.

“Yes,” she said. “Please.”

Leda heated them while Tesset took off her wet shoes. The heel of one stocking had rubbed through. She looked at the small hole for a long time, then pulled the stocking off and left it where it fell.

When the food was ready, Leda sat beside her and asked what had happened. Tesset told her. She got through the notices and the meeting, then stopped at the thought of Marda explaining the rope.

Leda did not try to make her finish.

“Will Ada go?” she asked at last.

“I think so.”

“Of course she will.”

Tesset set the bowl down. “I keep thinking I should be able to say it differently. To make Lyra hear the part she hasn’t heard.”

“Hasn’t she?”

Tesset looked at her. Leda’s voice had been gentle. That made the question harder.

“I don’t know what to do with knowing she has.”

Leda drew the damaged cover out from between them and put it on the chair. Tesset leaned against her. For a while they sat with the cold bowl on the table and the unfinished seam out of sight.

Later, Leda got up to close the shutter. Tesset caught her hand before she reached it.

“Stay tonight.”

“I meant to.”

“I know. I wanted to ask.”

Leda came back. They moved the sewing into its basket and shook the loose threads from the sheet. Tesset found the needle where it had caught in the blanket, put it safely through a scrap, and set it beside the lamp.

The next morning she would have to decide which attendant could leave for months, whose mother needed the pay sent home, how much cloth a person could carry without giving up the hand needed to guide someone else.

Tonight, when Leda asked whether the lamp could go out, she said yes.

Chapter 23 The last washing

At their second meeting, Heth remembered the road better than he remembered the name of the person who had given him his pot.

He showed Lyra the pot in the customs room. It was small, copper-handled, with a dent near the base. Somebody had fitted a pierced strainer inside its mouth. He took the strainer out, put it back, and shook his head.

“A woman,” he said. “I think.”

Lyra waited.

“It wasn’t always a woman when I remembered.”

“You don’t have to tell me.”

“No. I wanted to.”

She moved the water jug within his reach. He poured with both hands round the handle. His thumb clicked twice. The sound had stopped troubling her, which seemed to trouble the guard outside more than the sound itself.

“You know where to take them,” she said.

“I know where I am going.”

“And you can bring the others.”

“If they stay with me.”

“Some won’t understand the instruction.”

“Then people will have to help them. The road doesn’t do that part.”
