He drank. A little water escaped at one side of his mouth. He caught it with the cloth wrapped round the pot and looked down at the cloth as if surprised to find it there.

“You sailed south before?” Lyra asked.

“Across the Amaranthine. To the Ashaani coast. There are roads while you still need to ask about roads. Wells. Men who want money for standing beside the wells.”

“After that?”

Heth turned the pot a quarter turn on the table.

“After that I know.”

Lyra had paid for maps that ended in blank space. She had once thought the blank meant there was nothing useful to put there. Heth’s certainty was different from a navigator declining to admit ignorance. He disliked the thing he knew. It sat inside him like an injury that predicted the weather.

“Could an ordinary guide find it?”

“You could pay him. He might find a city. You wouldn’t have paid enough for the right one.”

“And Ada? The attendants?”

“They follow. People have followed before.”

“Can they leave?”

He looked at her. The water in the pot had stopped moving.

“I left.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“It’s what I know.”

Lyra sat back. Heth took the strainer out again and fitted it more carefully into the mouth. He did not offer to improve his answer.

She respected him for it. She wished he had given her something else to tell the women who would come asking.

“What will you need?” she said.

He had thought about that. Shade on the deck. Water within reach. Places where people could sit without being stepped over. Attendants who knew when hunger or fear was making somebody strike. Coin for the road in small pieces, because the larger pieces encouraged a second demand. Shoes that did not depend on the person wearing them to notice when they rubbed.

He spoke longest about the shoes. Lyra wrote it down.

“And for you?”

“My pot.”

“You have your pot.”

“The man outside wanted to search it.”

“He can search it in front of you. You get it back.”

Heth considered that, then nodded. “And no one is tied to another person on the road.”

Lyra looked up from the page.

“You know some of them will wander.”

“Then hold a hand. Use a cart. If one falls with a rope on him, the next keeps walking. I’ve seen what that does.”

“Separate restraints if they are needed.”

“Where an attendant can reach the knot.”

She added it. There were things she had already arranged that looked poorer under this exact, uncomfortable attention. She did not resent him for finding them. He was doing the work she needed him to do.

When they finished, Heth put the pot back in its cloth. The guard searched it as agreed, awkwardly, making an unnecessary show of looking beneath the strainer. Heth watched until it was returned.

At the door he turned back.

“The people in houses,” he said. “They won’t thank you for the shade.”

“I haven’t asked them to.”

He nodded once and went out. Lyra looked at the page until the ink was dry enough to close it.

The Sedge had carried pilgrims before. Its master said this as though it resolved the question of the passengers.

Lyra went below and found that it resolved very little.

The vessel had broad berths and a useful space beneath the forward awning. The door into the lower sleeping room was too narrow for two attendants to pass together. A barrel had been lashed where someone guiding a frightened person would have to turn. The master said they had always put the barrel there.

“Move it,” Lyra said.

“It holds drinking water.”

“They will still need water when they get past it.”

The master called two crewmen. They shifted the barrel while Lyra went along the berths, testing the distance between them with her own shoulders. She had brought the woman from the rope loft. The attendant looked at the arrangements in silence, then asked where Hessa’s basket would go at night.

“Beside her,” the master said.

“Where?”

He pointed. The woman put her foot in the place he had indicated.

“That’s where I stand to help her.”

Lyra told a carpenter to bring a board and make the shelf wider. The master watched the extra work accumulate with a displeasure he had been paid to endure.

They would not all fit on the Sedge. The last inspection of the Salt Ward had found people in rooms omitted from the Prince’s working lists. Some had not been used for labor in years. Others had been moved so often that two rooms claimed the same person and neither could say where he slept now. Lyra refused to send the boats until the count meant bodies aboard rather than marks on a page.

A second vessel was hired for the remainder, traveling under the same arrangements and landing with the Sedge. The crews argued about escort space and who would go ashore to find carts. Lyra made them settle those questions while they could still point at the things involved.

The rope-loft attendant remained beside her when the master went to fetch the carpenter.

“If you’re paying for all this,” she said, “you could pay for us to stay.”

Lyra looked toward the berth they had just altered.

“Would you go with Hessa?”

“If I can get home afterward.”

“Your fare home will be held separately. You can name the person to keep it.”

“That’s a good arrangement.”

“Then name someone.”

The woman gave her sister’s name. Lyra wrote it down. Neither pretended the earlier question had been answered.

When the carpenter came, the attendant showed him how high the shelf needed to be. Lyra left them working and went back onto the deck. The wind had shifted. She could smell the laundry yards beyond the waterfront, hot cloth and lye, the familiar morning labor of people making other people’s things usable.

She went to Ada’s before she could find another task to put ahead of it.

Ada had washed the spare blue shirt.

It hung across the yard with its sleeves turned outward to dry. Lyra remembered Jorin wearing it during an earlier visit, the cloth loose over shoulders that no longer moved in quite the way Ada expected. One cuff had been mended with a darker strip. She looked at that cuff as Ada opened the gate.

“Come in,” Ada said.

There was no surprise in her face. She had put two cups on the table beneath the strip of awning. A folded piece of paper lay between them. Lyra recognized the notice before she could read it.

Jorin was in the hamper room. She could see the side of his head through the open upper door. He was turning a basket handle, finding the same loose place with each movement.

“He’s had a good morning,” Ada said. “I wanted you to see one.”

Lyra took off her gloves.

“I have seen them.”

“Then you’ll know when it stops being one.”

Ada poured the water. Her hands were reddened from the washing. One nail had split close to the quick and been covered with a narrow piece of cloth. Lyra nearly reached for it, then kept her hands round her own cup.

They sat with the paper between them.

“The woman comes twice a week,” Ada said. “The neighbors know how to reach me. He isn’t left in the yard alone. The lower door fastens from this side, but I don’t use it unless someone is bringing a cart through.”

“I know how you care for him.”

“Then tell me what’s wrong with it.”

Lyra looked at the open door. Jorin had stopped moving the handle. He was listening to Ada’s voice, or to the little tapping of a loose shutter above them. It was hard to know which, and Lyra had learned not to prefer one answer aloud.

“Nothing is wrong with the room,” she said.

“With me, then.”

“No.”

“With him?”

Lyra put down the cup.

“Ada.”

“I have had people say my name like that since they brought the paper. As though it were the thing I kept getting wrong.”

She spoke evenly. Lyra had expected anger and would have preferred it to the care Ada was taking with each word.

“The Prince is on his way to Zakov,” Lyra said. “His local houses are being broken up. The people who kept the Worn are losing the rooms, the boats, the places they sent them.”

“Good.”

“I can’t leave a way for the same thing to begin again.”

Ada looked into the hamper room. “He isn’t a way.”

“Someone else would see him as one.”

“You see him as one.”

Lyra’s fingers tightened on the cup. The cold in her palm reached the water. She could see a little change along its surface.

Ada saw it too. She took the cup from Lyra and set it farther away, without hurry.

“I won’t argue with the water,” she said. “I am talking to you.”

Lyra let her hands lie open on her knees.

Jorin moved in the room. The basket struck the doorpost. Ada rose at once, then stopped herself when he found the opening and came into the yard without assistance. He followed the shade toward the bench, where his cup usually stood.

Ada filled it and moved aside. Jorin drank. He looked at Lyra over the rim with an attention that made her chest hurt.

She remembered the Silt’s deck breaking, Corven hauling her back, the six people they had been unable to reach. She had carried the memory home and returned to this yard because here, at least, somebody had come out. Jorin set down the cup and turned the handle until it faced the wall.

“We can take that,” Lyra said. She heard how useless the sentence was after it had left her mouth.

Ada looked at the cup, then at her.

“You could leave it where it is.”

The yard became very quiet. A drip fell from the blue shirt and struck the stones between them. Lyra could hear each one. She wished she could not.

She had room to change the order. No one was forcing her to keep this name on the list. She could pay the attendant, place a watcher at the lane, tell the other houses that Jorin remained under her protection. She knew what each part would cost. She could already hear the next request, and the next, the difficult little exceptions widening until every door she had closed stood open on a different plea.

She did not want to bargain for the city again one room at a time.

“He goes,” she said.

Ada sat down slowly. She looked older for a moment, though nothing in her face had changed except the work she had stopped doing with it.

“And if I bar the door?”

Lyra looked toward Jorin. He was working the basket handle again.

“I won’t make you watch that,” she said.

“That sounds like something you can arrange afterward.”

Lyra had no answer she was willing to put into the yard.

Ada picked up the notice and folded it along its existing crease. Her hands were steady. She slipped it beneath the little pot beside the wall so it would not blow away.

“I will go with him.”

“I can pay the passage.”

“You will.”

“And someone to keep the house.”

Ada looked at her. “Nessa next door has the other key. You can pay her what she asks to keep the roof sound and the drain clear. Don’t send somebody here to be grateful for the room.”

“I won’t.”

“Put that in the arrangement. I want to know which person I’m supposed to trouble if the promise changes.”

Lyra nodded. She had expected to be refused the chance to help with anything. Being given exact work hurt in a way she had not prepared for.

Ada stood and took down the blue shirt. It was not fully dry. She carried it into the house and came back with a plain cloth bag.

“How long?” she asked.

“Not tomorrow. We are still preparing the second ship.”

“Give me the day when you know it. Early enough to finish what I’ve been paid to wash.”

“You can send the work elsewhere.”

“I know how to send work elsewhere, Lyra.”

Lyra looked down at her gloves. She had put them beside the basin. One had fallen into a little spilled water. She picked it up and wrung the fingers carefully, an action she could complete without being told it was unnecessary.

Jorin brought the basket toward her.

He stopped before reaching the table and held it at the angle used to pass a load. Lyra got up. Ada watched but did not intervene.

Lyra took the handle. It shifted in the loose fastening. Jorin released it and waited, his hands still out.

She sat with the basket on her knees. The loose end could be tucked through the sound weave once and held until a proper repair was made. She found the opening with her smallest finger and passed it through. The motion was familiar. Her injured hand did not prevent it.

When she gave the basket back, Jorin took it without looking at her. He turned toward his bench and sat down.

Ada lifted the empty cups from the table.

“You can go now,” she said.

Vaska was waiting at Harrow with a disagreement she had postponed long enough to dislike postponing it.

“Two men won’t take the quay watch,” she said. “They’ll guard a store. They’ll fight another house. They won’t haul people out of kitchens.”

Lyra hung the wet glove near the door.

“Who?”

Vaska named them. Neither name surprised her. One of the men had carried Jast from the Brindle after the rescue.

“Pay what they’re owed. They can finish with the store watch if they want the days.”

“You aren’t going to make an example?”

Lyra turned. “Did they take the assignment and then refuse it?”

“No.”

“Then find people who will do it.”

Vaska watched her for a moment. “There are enough.”

“I know.”

They went upstairs. The room smelled of dry wood and the onions Sova had sent with the meal. Lyra took off her coat and stood at the basin, washing her hands longer than the dirt required. Vaska sat on the edge of the chair and waited until she had finished.

“What about Callista?” she asked.

“What about her?”

“One of the new men put her name down. Katerina nearly took his eyes out.”

“He has no business adding names.”

“He saw her come from the old care rooms.”

Lyra dried her hands. “She isn’t Worn. Nor is Marda. Nor am I. He can learn the distinction before he is paid for another morning.”

“I’ll tell him.”

“Bring the list. I want to see every addition.”

Vaska rose. She left the food covered. Lyra ate alone, standing by the window because the chair was too near the hearth. Below, the yard was being cleared for the next day’s cargo. Coss had gone inside. The child he had been teaching had learned to make one wavering note on the reed and was practicing it without mercy.

Lyra opened the window.

“Give it a rest,” she called.

The child looked up, startled, and hid the reed behind his back. She saw the fear arrive before he recognized her expression.

“Only while people are eating,” she added.

He nodded. He went out through the gate without playing again.

Lyra stood at the open window until Vaska returned with the names.

Chapter 24 A place aboard

The first person brought to the Sedge arrived before the attendants.

Tesset found him sitting on the quay beside two guards who had been told to wait. He had a cloth bag on his knees and no shoes. When a cart passed, his feet moved as though following steps it had made for him.

“Who brought him?” she asked.

“We did,” one guard said.

“From where?”

He gave the Salt Ward address. Tesset found it on the list. The room keeper was meant to have come too, to tell the receiving attendant what the man required.

“She wasn’t there,” the guard said. “We were told to clear the room.”

“Fetch her.”

“It’s nearly an hour back.”

“Then you should start.”

The guard looked toward Vaska, who stood by the gangway. Vaska heard the question and jerked her head toward the street. He went, leaving his companion beside the seated man.

Tesset took off her own scarf and put it beneath his bare feet. The stones were cold. She did not know whether the cold bothered him. A split along one heel had opened, and she knew what dirt would do to that.

“We’ll find shoes,” she told him.

He looked at her mouth. His lips moved once without making a word.

People began arriving from the shore houses, some on foot, others on low carts padded with folded cloth. Those who had cared for them carried bundles, bowls, the particular things that had acquired a use no one could explain quickly to a stranger. Tesset watched a man argue that three chipped cups could be replaced with sound ones aboard. The woman holding them said they were going. The man looked to Tesset for support and found none.

The Sedge’s master wanted everyone below before the cargo traffic thickened. The attendants wanted each person received by somebody who knew where to put them. The master pointed out that water did not wait for good intentions. Tesset told him he had accepted the sailing time after inspecting the arrangements.

“I didn’t inspect all these arguments,” he said.

“They were here.”

He went to speak to Vaska. Tesset returned to her table, where the ink bottle had begun to collect grit around its rim.

Hessa came with the rope-loft attendant and stopped at the gangway.

She held her basket in both hands. The woman stood a little ahead, showing her the space between the rails. Hessa looked down at the movement beneath the boards and drew back.

“Wait,” the attendant told the guard behind them.

He waited for three breaths, then put a hand on Hessa’s shoulder.

She struck him with the basket.

The guard caught her wrist. The movement pulled her forward. She made a harsh, rising sound and tried to sit down in the middle of the gangway.

“Let go,” the attendant said.

“She’ll go over.”

“Let me take her.”

Tesset put down the pen and went to them. She caught the empty side of the basket so it would stop dragging Hessa’s arms across her body. The attendant knelt on the planks. The guard loosened his grip reluctantly.

Hessa sat. She would not look at the woman or at the vessel. The basket lay across her knees with its newly mended edge crushed inward.

Behind them, the people waiting began moving to find more room. A cart wheel bumped the edge of the landing. Someone swore. The master called down from the deck that they could not block the gangway all morning.

Vaska came over.

“How long?” she asked.

“I don’t know,” the attendant said.

“Can she walk?”

“Yes. She doesn’t want to.”

Vaska looked at the woman, then at Tesset. There was no satisfaction in her face. She had an assignment and a crowded quay.

“Bring the chair,” she told the guards.

The attendant’s hand tightened on Hessa’s arm. Tesset felt the wish to put herself in the way, to make her own body the thing the guards had to decide what to do with. She could imagine the little disturbance clearly: the hesitation, the argument, Vaska taking her aside, the chair being brought anyway.

She stayed where she could hold the basket.

They lifted Hessa into the chair. She fought them with a strength that made one guard stumble, and he swore at her before he could stop himself. The attendant told him to move his hand away from her throat. He did. A broad cloth was fastened across Hessa’s hips. Another held the chair’s loose support in place.

Her basket fell. Tesset caught it before it went beneath the planks.

They carried Hessa aboard with the attendant walking beside her. Tesset followed as far as the deck. She put the basket where Hessa could see it and waited until the woman had the cloth unfastened.

Hessa took the basket. One of her fingers had caught in the broken weave. The attendant worked it free and covered the sharp place with a fold of clean cloth.

“Thank you,” she said to Tesset.

Tesset could not answer. She went back down the gangway and washed the little cut on her own hand in a bucket meant for cleaning the table.

Tarn’s party took longer to come through the gate than to board.

Marda had packed the rope in a separate bag and then taken it out again because he kept reaching for it. Jorad carried his other things. The man who had first brought him from the lighter came too, though Daven said he could stay only until his own boat was needed.

Tarn stopped when he saw the Brindle tied farther along the quay.

His sound hand lifted. His fingers closed around the air.

Daven looked at him, then at Marda. “Shall I move her?”

“No,” Marda said. “Let him look.”

They stood while ordinary traffic passed behind them. Tarn made the old loading movement once more. Then his hand came down onto the soft rope Marda held across her palms.

“This one,” she said. “We’re taking this one.”

He followed when she moved toward the gangway. At its foot she stopped and pressed the rope bag into the attendant’s hands.

“The rough one is for washing while the other dries. Don’t let anyone use either for tying things. He knows when it’s gone.”

The attendant nodded. Marda repeated the instruction in fewer words, then laughed angrily at herself.

“You’ll remember. You’ve been listening.”

Tesset stayed beside her until Tarn was aboard. He did not look back. Marda kept one hand raised for some time after there was no one positioned to see it.

Jorad took her elbow. She shook him off, then took his arm herself.

“I have bread rising,” she told Tesset.

“Go. I’ll come when they’re away.”

“Don’t come to tell me it went smoothly.”

“I won’t.”

Marda went with Jorad. Daven remained a moment longer, watching the place where Tarn had stood. Then a man called from the Brindle, and he returned to the boat whose bench he had once cut apart to free a hand.

Tesset went back to the table. She wrote Tarn’s name beside the attendant assigned to him. The ink was still wet when the next party arrived.

Jast would not sit on the cart.

The attendant who brought him said he had been refusing it since the rope loft. They had walked slowly, resting whenever the healed leg grew tired. He leaned now on a broad stick with a cloth wrapped round its head. His free hand was pressed against the old shoulder seam of his coat, where the pale excavation dust had once lodged.

“There are no steps until the gangway,” Tesset told the woman.

“He can manage steps. It’s waiting that hurts.”

Jast turned toward the water. The nearer ship lifted gently against its lines. He watched the movement, then took another step before the attendant was ready.

She caught up with him. Tesset went ahead to clear the path. Someone had set a basket of small provisions where it would be convenient for the next carry. She moved it beneath the table and returned in time to see Jast at the foot of the gangway.

Heth stood at its upper end.

For a while neither moved. The attendant waited with one hand near Jast’s elbow. Tesset could hear the loose tapping of a rope end against the Sedge’s side.

Jast said something.

The word was too rough for Tesset to make out. Heth answered in the same low voice he had used with the people from the Salt Ward. Jast lifted his stick and pointed it toward the ship. His hand shook with the effort.

“You want to go?” the attendant asked.

He stepped onto the first plank.

Tesset stood aside. Jast went up, placing the stick, following it with the sound leg, then bringing the other through. He stopped halfway and rested with his face turned toward the harbor. Heth came down only far enough to keep other people from passing him.

At the top, Jast reached for the rail and held it. The attendant put his bag down and waited while he looked outward.

“That part seems easier for him,” she said when she came back for the bedding.

“Did you understand what he asked?”

“No. I know what he did.”

Tesset gave her the folded cloths. The woman was traveling for the agreed first term, with another attendant promised to meet the company at the coast. Her nephew had married during the basin recovery. She had missed the family meal and now would miss the first visit to the new house. She had told Tesset about it while naming the person who should receive her pay.

“Will you take a message?” she asked.

“Of course.”

The woman took a little packet from her sleeve. A pair of wooden spoons, wrapped together. “Tell them these are the good ones. I don’t want them put aside for guests and never used.”

Tesset wrote the address on a clean strip and tied it to the packet. The woman read it, corrected the lane number, and shouldered the bedding.

When she reached the deck, Jast had found the shaded end of the rail. He made room for her without being asked. She put down the bedding and stood beside him, pointing out the ship’s water barrel, then the place where he could sit when he was tired.

Tesset put the spoons safely in her bag. They would be delivered that afternoon, while she still remembered how the woman had wanted the message spoken.

Ada carried one bag. Jorin carried the basket.

They came through the yard gate together, with Leda beside them and the neighbor who would keep the house walking a little behind. The neighbor had brought an extra bundle despite Ada’s protests. Tesset saw it and knew there would be no useful argument about the carrying allowance.

Jorin stopped at the edge of the quay. The air coming from the sea touched his face. His shoulders lowered in a way Tesset had never seen in the washing yard.

Ada noticed too. She said nothing. She gave him room to stand, keeping her hand near his sleeve without holding it.

Leda put Ada’s bag beneath the table and came to Tesset. There was a stain on her thumb from work she must have finished early to be here.

“We brought the cup,” she said.

“Good.”

“Ada wanted me to tell you where it is. Wrapped in the shirt, near the top.”

Tesset nodded. Leda looked at the cut on her hand and touched the unhurt part with one finger.

“Later,” Tesset said.

“I’m staying.”

Ada came to the table. She had put the house key on a cord and worn it beneath her clothes. Tesset saw the outline when she bent to retrieve the bag.

“The other key?”

“Nessa has it.”

“The maintenance pay is set aside. She has the first part.”

“She told me. Twice. She thinks if she explains the drain often enough I won’t imagine it stopped.”

Ada smiled. Tesset smiled back, and for a moment they were only two women sharing the mild irritation of somebody else’s kindness.

Then the master called for the next group.

Ada took her bag. “Will you visit her?”

“Nessa?”

“The house. Sometimes.”

“Yes.”

“She’ll keep it properly. I know she will.”

“I’ll go.”

Ada looked toward Jorin. Heth had joined him at the rail and was speaking too softly for Tesset to hear. Jorin was watching the copper handle of the pot in Heth’s bundle. Heth lifted it a little, then lowered it. Jorin moved with him toward the gangway.

Ada caught up. She put her bag over her shoulder and offered Jorin her free hand. He did not take it. She left it within reach while they walked.

At the first slope he stopped. Tesset felt her whole body prepare for another struggle. Heth waited on the higher board, his pot held where it would not knock against the rail.

Jorin shifted the basket to his other hand and continued.

Ada went beside him. Halfway up, she looked back. Nessa was crying without trying to hide it. Leda raised a hand. Tesset found herself doing the same.

Ada nodded to them and turned toward the deck.

Lyra came after the last cart had been unloaded.

She wore a plain dark coat with the sleeve cut to move more easily at the shoulder. Vaska met her at the gate and spoke for a few moments. Lyra listened, looked toward the ships, then came to Tesset’s table.

“Anyone missing?”

“The final count is being checked aboard.”

“The room keeper?”

“Found. She was washing his clothes. Nobody had told her the collection would be before dawn.”

Lyra closed her eyes briefly. “Who gave that instruction?”

“The man fetching him says he was told to clear the room.”

“His name.”

Tesset gave it. Lyra passed it to Vaska, who went to find the man.

Lyra looked at her hand. “You cut yourself.”

“It’s washed.”

“Let me see.”

Tesset moved it beneath the table.

Lyra stopped. The pause was small enough that someone walking past would have missed it. Then she put her own hands in her coat pockets.

“Have you eaten?”

“Leda brought something.”

“Good.”

The word remained between them. Tesset turned to the page the master had sent down for checking. She compared the names against the shore list and marked the places where an attendant had changed ships to remain with someone familiar.

Lyra waited until she had finished.

“Ada aboard?”

“Yes.”

“Would she want me to come up?”

Tesset looked toward the Sedge. Ada stood beneath the forward awning, helping Jorin put the basket in a place where it would not slide. Heth had gone to speak to the master. The rope-loft attendant was sitting beside Hessa, drinking water while the guards moved out of her way.

“You can ask her,” Tesset said.

Lyra followed her gaze. For a moment Tesset thought she would go. She had a way of entering difficult rooms with her chin set, prepared to bear whatever was said there as long as entering remained her decision.

This time she stayed ashore.

The ships waited another hour.

The delay came from a sail fitting, then from the second master’s refusal to leave without the spare casks he had been promised. Tesset sat on the closed money box while Leda unwrapped the food. There was bread, a small piece of cheese, and a pear already cut because Leda had remembered there would be no clean knife.

Tesset ate most of it before she realized she had been given the larger share.

“You haven’t had enough,” she said.

“I ate at the house.”

“Did you?”

Leda showed her the other crust wrapped beneath the cloth. Tesset laughed, a tired sound that surprised her.

“I’m sorry.”

“For eating?”

“For inspecting.”

“You’re off duty for the length of this bread.”

They ate with their shoulders touching. Beyond them, the porter carrying the last water cask slipped and caught himself against the rail. The people nearest him shouted contradictory advice. He listened to none of it and recovered his footing.

Lyra stood at the farther end of the quay, talking to the Sedge’s master. He was pointing toward the open water. She looked where he pointed and nodded. From this distance the conversation resembled any of the hundreds that had kept the landings working.

The second vessel cast off first. Its people watched the shore through the spaces between the awnings. Tesset could not distinguish every face. She knew the names because she had checked them herself. That knowledge seemed inadequate now and would be necessary later, and she kept the paper beneath her hand.

The Sedge followed.

Ada was still at the forward rail. Jorin stood a little behind her with the basket against his chest. When the ship turned, she moved so he could keep the shore in view if he wanted it. He looked past it toward the farther water.

Tesset raised her hand again. Ada raised hers. They kept them up until the vessel’s angle hid the forward rail.

The quay began to empty. A carter waiting with ordinary goods asked when he could use the cleared space. Vaska told him to wait for the final boat. He asked how long that would be. She gave him a look, then a time.

Tesset put the signed sailing record in its case. She kept the shore list separate, with the statements from the people who had opposed the transfer. Sera would want both. The distinction had to survive the first clerk who found it convenient to fasten everything together.

Lyra came back while she was tying the case shut.

“The ships will stay together,” she said.

“I heard the masters agree.”

“Heth has the shore money. The attendants have their separate pay. Nobody has to ask him for their return fares.”

“I know. I entered them.”

Lyra looked at the closed case. “You did this well.”

Tesset pulled the knot tight. Her cut hand stung.

“I wish you hadn’t given us the work.”

Lyra said nothing. Leda stood beside Tesset, holding the empty food cloth. The silence lasted until somebody rolled a cart between them and all three had to move.

“Come to Harrow this evening,” Lyra said when the cart had passed. “Both of you. There’s food.”

Tesset took the case from the table.

“Not tonight.”

She went with Leda to deliver the papers. At the turn beyond the warehouse they stopped to let a boy carry a tray of hot bread past them. The smell followed them up the street, and Leda bought two rolls for Marda before they went on.

Part Five Her own name

Chapter 25 The houses reopen

The man who had stolen the silk arrived to complain about its handling.

He came through Gannet’s blue-tiled passage on the first dry afternoon of the week, wearing gloves too fine for the weather. Lyra watched him from the gallery while Vaska’s clerk took his name. He had rehearsed his indignation carefully. He let it appear only after the clerk asked which of the two stores held his goods, as though the existence of a second store were a further inconvenience inflicted upon him.

“Bring him up,” Lyra said.

Vaska leaned beside the gallery rail, eating something from a paper twist. Her hand had healed enough to close round a knife again. Salt had left pale patches that neither the surgeon’s ointment nor her irritation could remove.

“He says the cloth was packed badly.”

“It was.”

“You’ve inspected it?”

“I watched it being opened.”

“Then I should give him one of these before we begin.”

“What are they?”

“Fruit.”

Lyra took one. Sourness struck the tender place at the corner of her mouth so hard that she closed her eyes. Vaska laughed without hiding it.

“You could have warned me.”

“You could have looked at my face.”

The broker came up the servants’ stair. A new door covered the linen cupboard beside it. Berris had made the door from sound boards recovered elsewhere in the house, and one of its panels was a different color. Lyra preferred it to the costly proposal for replacing the entire cupboard. She had begun to discover how many people offered beauty when they thought the buyer had stopped counting.

The broker saw the sour fruit and relaxed a little. Lyra let him.

He laid his claim on the table. Six bolts of dark silk, creased by damp packing and worth less than when received. He wanted the difference paid in coin and the handling charge returned. He spoke courteously. He had a precise way of keeping his fingers together when he pointed at a sum, and a little scar beside his mouth where an old shave had gone wrong.

“Show me the damaged part,” Lyra said.

“It is downstairs.”

“I know. Show me.”

They went down together. At the store, she made the keeper open the crate. The broker stepped back from the smell of wet wrapping. Vaska stayed in the doorway, offering the paper twist to the woman on watch. The woman took one piece and immediately regretted it.

Lyra lifted the upper bolt. Beneath the first fold, the silk had been cut and joined to a length of much cheaper cloth. The work had been done well. A purchaser examining the outer turn could have paid before the shorter piece was discovered.

The broker’s face remained agreeable for one beat too long.

“Who has opened that?”

“Our keeper. Your packer. The woman who sewed the false lengths in.”

“I don’t know what you mean.”

“I paid her this morning. She had not been paid by you.”

“That woman has been dismissed.”

“Yes. She mentioned it.”

Lyra laid the silk on the crate rim. The broker looked toward Vaska, who had put the fruit away. Two carriers passed behind her with an ordinary load. Neither looked into the room.

“You are accusing me on the word of a dismissed worker.”

“And the cloth. And the six lengths your man sold yesterday at the east market. My buyer left the join marks on them.”

His eyes went from Lyra to the entrance, where Vaska and the keeper stood, then down to his own name on the crate. He took off his gloves.

“What do you want?”

“Your packing account. The other consignments. The names of people who received goods before the owners were told they had been damaged here.”

“This is a dispute over cloth.”

“It was, when you came through the gate.”

He tried a different expression. It almost worked; the appeal to a reasonable person was familiar enough to catch at her attention.

“I have suppliers. People depend on the work. If this becomes public—”

“Then you should write clearly.”

She gave him a stool and a sheet. The keeper brought an ink bottle. For a while he sat with the pen above the page, looking at the new door, the painted marks on the crates, the little black square of daylight visible through a ventilator. Lyra did not tell him to hurry.

When he began, his hand was steady.

By the fourth week after the Sedge sailed, the Salt Ward stores were working six days out of seven. On the seventh, goods arrived whose owners preferred not to discuss the day.

The boats paid. The workshops paid. So did the rooms above the smaller landings, once the broken shutters had been mended and the Prince’s last keepers understood that their old rents bought them nothing. Lyra had refused three offers to take the whole concern off her hands in return for an income elsewhere. The third had been very large. She had kept the paper because she enjoyed reading it.

The stolen silk would be returned to its actual owners. The broker would pay the cost of making the fraud visible to Lyra and concealed from the people who might hang him for it. His list gave her two new routes, a buyer with an appetite for disputed cargo, and a customs examiner who accepted presents through his sister’s flour shop. The old Gallery files were elsewhere. These names had cost her a seamstress’s wages.

Vaska checked two of the broker’s names before Lyra used either. One was false. He had put down a rival’s clerk, a man who was away burying his mother when the claimed delivery took place. Lyra sent for the broker again.

This time they met in the room over the west store, where the windows gave him no view of the gate.

He admitted the invention after she laid the funeral payment beside his account. He began explaining the rivalry. Lyra let him finish, then turned his writing hand palm downward on the table.

Vaska put a small knife through the empty space between his fingers. The point stood in the wood. He jerked his hand back and hit the chair behind him.

“The next list costs you something you use,” Lyra said.

He stared at the blade.

“You can verify them.”

“I did.”

When he left, Vaska pulled the knife free and rubbed the mark with her thumb.

“Berris will charge you.”

“Put it against the silk.”

Vaska grinned. She took the revised list down herself. By evening the customs examiner had received an invitation to dine, and the owners of the six bolts had been told their property was safe. One sent a bottle. Another sent a lower offer for the next season’s handling, assuming gratitude might be contagious.

Lyra refused the lower rate.

The money came in unevenly, in purses, bars, promises secured against things people could not move without her permission. Some days the work exhausted her more than the fighting had. In the receiving chamber she had known the face that would take the pressure. A ruined lot of pepper could be blamed on seven people, all of them honest about one portion of it. She learned which disagreements needed her presence and which improved if she stayed away.

She also learned how quiet a room became when someone thought she might have heard a joke.

At first she turned toward the silence. Now she sometimes finished what she was doing before looking up. The delay told her more.

The receiver had not been repaired by wanting it.

Mavet showed her the crack again at the end of that week. Their lamps had been moved to places where the new timbers would not cast shadows across the work. The broken bronze rail lay against the far wall, tagged with a strip of cloth so no one would take it to be melted with ordinary scrap.

Lyra crouched beside the old seat. One pale line crossed another beneath the upright. The marks Mavet had made after the fight were still visible. The new crack had not grown; the stone around it had begun shedding grains when the upper bearing was touched.

“We could use the smaller branch,” Lyra said.

Mavet pressed her thumbnail into the shedding line. “It bears through here. You put the Prince against it; he tore out the rail. I need the new frame on sound stone before anyone touches that control.”

Lyra stood, brushing pale grains from her knees.

Esten’s assistant was lying on a board beneath the temporary brace, looking along a line stretched between two pins. Esten waited with a wedge in his hand. He did not strike until she told him. The sound ran through the chamber and came back to Lyra from a depth the others did not hear.

She wanted to answer it with her palm against the stone. That was the part she had not learned to explain. At Harrow she could become irritated by a drawer sticking or a servant moving the water jug. Below, the old pressure made every small difficulty withdraw to the edges. It remained even with the shutters shut: the knowledge of more depth than the harbor ought to contain, steady as another body’s weight beside hers.

She kept her hand off the seat.

“How long?”

“Long enough to put the new frame on sound material,” Mavet said. “The larger face stays closed. We inspect the smaller branch when this has taken its ordinary load. Then an empty vessel. Then we talk about what you want.”

“All three are gone.”

“I counted them.”

“So did everyone watching.”

Mavet straightened from her tools. “Then let them watch you pay for a repair.”

Lyra looked toward the isolated source. Ordinary water lay in the groove beside the floor. Upstairs, the sound cage stood beside iron salvaged from the two broken ones. The fourth jar, never charged, still lacked its new collar.

She asked Mavet what would arrive first. The answer was stone, if the carrier could get a clear run to the upper yard. Lyra went up to arrange it.

Above, the air felt thin and busy. She could hear the separate feet of people crossing the guardroom, and a mouse worrying at something inside a wall. She put a finger against her ear. It did nothing.

Vaska noticed when she came out.

“Bad?”

“Loud.”

“Everyone’s trying very hard to be quiet.”

“That’s part of it.”

They stood in the doorway while the next crew went below. Vaska had taken off the oiled glove because the surgeon said the skin needed air. The burned places looked fragile in daylight. Lyra thought of her hand inside the Prince’s grip and felt a sudden fury as sharp as if he were still within reach.

“Does it hurt?”

“Yes. If you ask three more times, it will be the hand I hit you with.”

Lyra touched the sound part of Vaska’s wrist, briefly. Vaska let her. Then she took the stone delivery instruction and went to find a carrier.

Belis and Rima’s new shop smelled of ash wood, warm glue, and the lunch Rima had forgotten on the sill.

The window did open without shedding plaster. Belis demonstrated with a little flourish, then sat down before his balance could dispute the achievement. His work chair had a wide base and a rail at the height his right hand found naturally. Rima had made the rail herself and refused to explain why it was better than the one he had described.

“It isn’t better,” he told Lyra. “It is different in a way that makes argument expensive.”

“It doesn’t tip,” Rima said from the bench.

“That is one of the differences.”

Lyra had brought orders for ordinary carrying handles, wedges, and the wooden cages needed when the source could be used again. Rima took the orders first and the sweet cakes second. She opened both with equal suspicion.

“These are undersized.”

“The cakes?”

“The handles. Who measured?”

Lyra looked at the sheet. Rima pointed. One dimension had been copied from the smaller trial cage.

“Keep the cakes,” Lyra said. “I’ll have the drawing brought.”

“You can bring it. The people you send stand there agreeing with me before I know what’s wrong.”

“I do that too.”

“You argue first. It’s more restful.”

They ate the cakes at the clear end of the bench. Belis could turn toward the window now without the room moving every time, but he still watched a fixed point when rising. His left side stayed deaf. He put Lyra in the chair where he could hear her and made Rima repeat a story about a customer who wanted six identical knobs, none exactly like the others.

Coss had come for a crutch grip and was sitting in the doorway, his damaged foot on a little folded pad. He listened with a solemn face until Belis asked what he was smiling at.

“My sister wants something like that.”

“Knobs?”

“Six things that agree with her but don’t look the same.”

Rima laughed so hard she put down her cake. Coss looked pleased and immediately shy about it. Lyra watched him reach for his stick when the light moved off the sill. The surgeon had saved the foot. It remained swollen, the ankle thick beneath its wrap, and no one had given him a day on which he would walk as before.

She had offered him paid work at the Salt Ward gate when he could sit comfortably for a whole watch. He had asked for time to decide. Today she did not ask again.

Instead she held the pad while he adjusted his leg. He thanked her and resumed the argument about knobs. Belis took the customer’s description from Rima and began drawing six offensive possibilities on a scrap.

For an hour, Lyra let herself remain.

Istre’s sister waited at Gannet on the morning the customs examiner came to dine.

Lyra learned she was there from the new keeper of the front room. The woman had brought no petition about property, no claim for wages, no request that could be laid beside another and answered by a sum. She wanted to speak to the person who had ordered the household killed.

The keeper reported this from the doorway. He was careful not to make the words his own.

“How long has she waited?” Lyra asked.

“Since the gate opened.”

It was almost noon.

“Does she have somewhere to sit?”

“Yes.”

“Water?”

“I asked. She didn’t want any.”

Lyra looked at the figures on her desk. They concerned the pepper, now separated into three lots. One could be sold, one dried, one had become something no honest cook would accept. The examiner waiting upstairs would certify the spoiled portion at a price they had not yet agreed. Beyond the door, someone was laying the lunch table.

“What does she say she wants from me?”

“That’s all she said.”

Lyra could remember Istre’s voice more easily than her sister’s face. A line of song in a warm gallery. The little impatience over a badly cut seam. She remembered the dead woman standing without a weapon and asking to go separately. Nothing the sister might say would supply a fact missing from that room.

“Tell her I won’t receive her.”

The keeper waited.

“Today?”

“No appointment. If she has a claim for the house’s unpaid work, it can be entered.”

He nodded once and went. Lyra stayed at the desk until she heard his steps reach the court. She did not go to the window.

Vaska came in carrying two folded napkins and a bottle.

“The examiner has brought his nephew.”

“Was the nephew invited?”

“He’s invited to everything. Apparently it makes the uncle look honest.”

“Put him at the far end.”

“Beside me?”

“If you can bear it.”

Vaska put the bottle down. She had heard enough in the front room to know who was waiting. Her eyes moved once to Lyra’s face, then away.

“Your coat’s caught at the back,” she said.

Lyra stood. Vaska pulled the folded hem free from beneath the belt and smoothed it. The small service felt more intimate than their arguments over money. Lyra looked at the hand doing it, at the light scars from the salt, and almost said something she had no time to complete.

The lunch bell sounded below.

They went upstairs together. By the time the first dish was served, the front keeper reported that the woman had left.

Chapter 26 A letter from the coast

The letter reached Tesset beneath an invoice for damaged onions.

A ship’s factor had taken both from the master, tied them together to save carrying two bundles, and sent them to the independent landing because Tesset’s name appeared on one and the onions on neither. By the time she worked the knot loose, the man waiting for his freight acknowledgment had explained three times that the onions had been damaged before they entered his boat.

“I believe you,” she said.

“You’ve written that they were damaged in carriage.”

“Sea carriage. Look at the next line.”

He looked. His finger moved slowly beneath the words. Tesset waited, resisting the temptation to read it aloud before he had finished for himself.

“Oh,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Could you put that nearer the beginning?”

She smiled and moved the page so he could sign. Outside, a carter shouted at someone blocking the landing. It was the same shout she had heard all morning, with a new name inserted whenever another obstacle arrived. The office smelled of wet coats, hot ink, and the fried food Sera had forbidden anyone to leave beneath the records.

Tesset saw Ada’s hand on the smaller packet only after the man went.

The writing leaned more heavily on its downstrokes than she remembered. There were two folded sheets. A port mark from the Ashaani coast crossed the corner of the outer one. The ship carrying it back had taken more than a fortnight after the letter was written; Ada and Jorin had been gone from Silkstrand nearly seven weeks.

She put down her pen.

For a moment she did not open it. The packet was something Ada had held after passing beyond the place where Tesset could go to her. There might be ordinary news inside. There might be a sentence that made the office impossible to remain in. She held the fold between finger and thumb and listened to someone in the next room asking why there were two charges for one length of rope.

Then she opened it.

Ada began with the house.

Was the drain clear? Had Nessa found the little shelf beneath the back eave where the wet-weather cloths were kept? Tesset was to tell her that the white sheet with the narrow repair belonged to the woman at the corner, not to Ada. It had been left behind because Ada had expected to finish it on the last morning, and then people kept coming through the gate.

After that came the sea.

We are ashore. Both ships arrived, though we lost sight of the other for a day and the master said that happened at sea as if saying it would improve the day. Heth told me to look at the attendants rather than the horizon. They were still finding things to disagree about, which he said meant we had not reached the part to be frightened of.

Jorin was ill the first days. So was I. I did not know you could become angry with water for moving, but I did. He likes the place near the bow when the sun is not on it. He stands longer there than in the yard. I am telling you because it happened. Please don’t give it to someone who wants to say he needed taking away.

His cup is sound. The blue shirt has been washed twice and comes dry hard with salt even after fresh water. Hessa’s woman has mended the basket again. Jast walked ashore with only an arm to lean on. He looked back at the ship until it was out of sight behind the warehouse. Tarn has his rope. The men unloading tried to take it for a loose box and were sorry afterward.

We have rooms for six nights while they find carts. There is a woman here who makes bread in a pan with onions through it. I would ask her how, but I haven’t enough of her words. Heth knows what she is asking for the rooms, and sometimes he knows how to ask for less. He has not promised a short road. I am grateful to him for that.

I have not decided what I will write about the rest. This letter is to say where we are. Tell Leda the seam held. Tell Nessa I have the key.

The second sheet listed things the shore factor had supplied and things the attendants still needed. There was a small complaint about the thickness of the new soles, a larger one about a cart ordered without anyone measuring the people expected to sit in it, and a line at the end in a different, less careful hand.

He has just put a stone in the basket. I have taken it out twice. I will leave it there now.

Tesset sat with the letter open until Sera came to the doorway.

“Bad news?”

“They reached the coast.”

Sera stopped. She came over slowly, as though a quicker movement might make Tesset say something else.

“All of them?”

“The two ships. The people she names are well enough for the road. That’s what she says.”

“May I?”

Tesset separated the supply sheet from the personal one. “This part.”

Sera took it without reaching for the other. She read beside the window, her lips tightening at the cart dimensions.

“We paid for the larger ones.”

“We paid the factor to find larger ones.”

“Yes. We did.”

Sera took out a clean sheet and began a reply about the money. Tesset put Ada’s first page back inside its fold. The next customer came in holding a broken seal between two fingers. He looked at their faces and apologized before asking about it.

Tesset made room on the desk.

Nessa had kept the drain clear.

She insisted on showing Tesset before allowing the letter to be read. It was a matter of professional pride by now. She lifted the narrow cover with a hook, pointed out the clean channel, and explained why the leaves collected there even though the tree responsible grew two houses away.

“The owner says the wind is not his.”

“He may be right.”

“The branches are his. I would like him to explain the difference to the leaves.”

Tesset laughed. Nessa lowered the cover and brushed her hands against her apron. She had come with her own key and a little bag of ash for the damp place beside the back wall. Ada’s yard smelled of unused stones warming in the afternoon. The awning had been taken down and stored dry. Without it, Tesset could see the old nail marks where Ada had moved the hooks over successive summers.

The hamper room stood open. It held empty hampers, a folded mat, and the small bench Jorin had used. Nessa had left the bench in the shade as Ada instructed. Tesset nearly moved it farther from the door before remembering there was no one to clear a path for.

They sat at the yard table. Nessa fetched cups from her own house. Ada’s cupboards remained closed.

Tesset read the first sheet aloud, stopping when Nessa asked about the crossing. She had no more information than the page. Nessa understood after the second question and listened to the rest with both hands round her cup.

“She asks about a sheet,” Tesset said.

“I returned it. The woman said the wash was late. I told her she could write to the sea.”

Tesset smiled. Nessa did not. After a moment the older woman’s mouth moved with a little pride she had tried not to show.

“I wasn’t polite.”

“I can hear that.”

“She paid what was owing.”

They spoke about the roof, the maintenance purse, the things in the front room that needed airing rather than replacing. Nessa had been paid on the agreed days. Nobody had arrived to ask whether a vacant house could be put to better use. She seemed almost disappointed not to have that further battle prepared.

“I’ll write that,” Tesset said.

“Write that the place is hers.”

“It is.”

“Then use those words.”

Tesset looked toward the gate. She could imagine Ada coming through it with the washing in both arms, turning sideways to keep the clean cloth from the post. The image had the clarity of something she could do nothing with.

“I will.”

When she rose to leave, Nessa took the letter from the table and passed it back with care. “There was a pear in the hamper room. Behind the basket shelf. I threw it out.”

“All right.”

“I thought she might ask.”

Tesset put the letter inside her coat. “I’ll remember.”

Leda’s bindery had acquired an apprentice who believed every silence was an invitation to improve the work.

Tesset found him proposing a new order for the drying sheets. Leda listened until he reached the part about saving time. Then she lifted the uppermost sheet, showed him the damp patch it had taken from the one beneath, and asked him where the saved time should be kept while she made the page again.

He went red. She softened immediately.

“Put those six separately. Use the old frame. And ask before you move things you haven’t seen dry.”

He nodded, relieved to have something exact to do. When he had gone, Leda put down her brush and leaned both hands against the bench.

“I used to do that.”

“You still do.”

“Not with drying sheets.”

“No.”

Leda looked up and saw the letter. Her expression changed before Tesset said Ada’s name.

They read it in the little room where the glue was kept. It was warm, and the single stool creaked under them when Leda pulled Tesset onto the edge beside her. Leda read slowly, going back over the line about the seam, then over the part about Jorin standing at the bow.

“She’s right to tell us,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I wish she hadn’t needed to say what we mustn’t make of it.”

Tesset leaned against her. Leda smelled of paper, starch, and the little sharpness of the paste. One of her hands rested at Tesset’s waist while the other held the page. They stayed like that until the apprentice knocked and asked whether anyone knew where the old frame was.

“Where I put it before you improved the room,” Leda called.

His steps withdrew. She laughed softly and pressed her face into Tesset’s hair.

“I can leave early. Will you wait?”

Tesset said yes. She helped wrap a finished order for Avenna: plain covers, a wide margin, paper heavy enough that the same page could be turned many times without tearing at the fold. The singing teacher had included payment and a note saying Aria preferred the larger letters on the choice sheets but not the dark border. Dorel would collect the parcel when he next came into the city.

Leda put the new sheets at the top. “There. No border.”

It pleased Tesset beyond the size of the task. A preference had traveled from the quarry and been obeyed in a small room over a noisy shop. No one had needed to gather a committee round it.

Outside, they bought little cakes filled with crushed nuts. Leda took the largest and offered Tesset the one with the broken corner, claiming it would be cruel to reject it for an accident of birth. Tesset ate half before Leda confessed that the intact one had more filling.

They went toward the harbor because the light was still good and neither wanted to climb the stairs yet.

Lyra saw them outside the landing office.

She was speaking to a carrier beside a cart of timber. Her coat had been made to fit properly at last, its right sleeve loose enough for her shoulder. Two of Vaska’s people stood farther down the lane. They did not approach when Lyra came over, but Tesset could feel them notice.

“Sera says there’s a letter.”

“From the coast.”

“They arrived?”

“Yes. Both ships.”

Lyra drew a breath. The relief was plain enough that Tesset felt her own anger shift, searching for a place to settle without pretending the relief was false.

“Jorin?”

“He was ill at sea. Ada says he’s better ashore.”

“And the others?”

“She names Hessa, Jast, Tarn. They’re there. The carts hadn’t been settled when she wrote.”

“I’ll have the factor answer for that.”

“Sera has written.”

“Good.”

Lyra looked from Tesset to Leda. Leda had finished her cake and folded the paper into a narrow square. She held it between her hands without looking away.

“Can I read it?” Lyra asked.

Tesset’s fingers closed around the packet inside her coat.

“It’s addressed to me.”

“I know.”

“I’ve told you where they are.”

The carrier called Lyra’s name from the timber cart. She held up a hand without turning. Tesset remembered when that gesture would have seemed a gift: the work kept waiting while Lyra attended to her. Now the waiting man looked frightened that he had interrupted.

Lyra saw the movement of Tesset’s eyes. She glanced back, told the carrier she would come, and lowered her hand.

“All right,” she said.

Leda spoke for the first time. “Nessa’s keeping the house.”

“I said she could.”

Tesset felt Leda become still beside her.

Lyra closed her mouth. For a moment she looked exhausted, and then angry with the exhaustion. “I mean the money is being paid.”

“It is,” Tesset said. “We went there.”

“Was anything needed?”

“No.”

The answer left Lyra without a task to take from the encounter. She looked at the letter’s edge where it showed beneath Tesset’s coat, then at the two of them together.

“I’m glad it came.”

“So am I.”

They parted there. Tesset did not look back until they reached the corner. Lyra had gone to the cart. She stood beside the driver, one hand against a timber, listening while he pointed out where the load had shifted. The men down the lane moved when she moved.

Leda took Tesset’s hand.

“Come home,” she said.

The wider bed still rocked if the paper beneath its foot was displaced.

Tesset had meant to replace the folded drainage notice with a proper wedge. Tonight she found Leda kneeling beside it with a knife and a piece of scrap wood, determined to finish the improvement before the room’s other defects developed opinions.

“The landlord should do that,” Tesset said.

“He can have the knife when I’m done.”

Tesset put the kettle on. She opened the second window and called down for permission to cut a little mint. The woman in the court told her to take plenty and not thank her until the roots stopped appearing in every other pot. Tesset brought back enough to make the room smell of green leaves beneath the hot water.

They ate at the small table, with Ada’s letter placed safely on the sill. Leda described the apprentice’s entire first week. By the end Tesset was laughing too hard to defend him. He had labeled the paste pot with the wrong contents, improved the sheet order, and told a customer that her late husband’s account book would be more useful with fewer pages.

“He meant the blank ones,” Leda said.

“That doesn’t improve it.”

“I know. I had to go into the yard.”

They laughed until the kettle began making a noise that sounded accusatory. Tesset took it off the heat. Leda wiped her eyes and reached for the last piece of bread.

After supper, Tesset wrote to Ada. She described the drain because Ada had asked, the white sheet because Nessa had insisted, the roof and the pear and the fact that no one had been put into the house. She wrote that she and Leda had read the letter together. She did not write that things were getting better. The words would have needed a destination of their own.

Leda sat beside her, repairing the edge of her yellow scarf. When Tesset paused, she put the needle down.

“Tell her I was pleased about the seam.”

“I have.”

“And ask her to write even if she doesn’t have good news.”

Tesset added it. She left room at the bottom, and Leda wrote a few lines herself, shielding them with her hand until she had finished. Tesset pretended to be offended. Leda kissed her ear and told her she could read them later, if Ada chose to show her.

The reply would go to the coastal factor, with a second copy of the supply questions in Sera’s hand. Whether anyone could carry it inland was something no one in Silkstrand could promise. Tesset addressed it carefully anyway.

When the ink was dry, she set the folded letter on the shelf by the door.

Leda took her scarf from the chair and hung it beside the comb she kept here. She still had another room, another key, mornings she preferred to begin alone. Tonight she turned the lamp down and came back to the bed.

The new wedge held.

Chapter 27 The room below the city

On the twenty-first day inland, Ada washed Jorin’s blue shirt in water she had already used twice.

There was dust in the seams, dust in the folded cuffs, a fine pale line where his back had rested against the cart. She worked the cloth between her hands and watched the last clean water become the color of the road. Once, in Silkstrand, she would have put it aside for floors. Here she tipped it into the little basin reserved for washing feet and wrung the shirt until her wrists ached.

Jorin sat beneath the cart with his basket between his knees.

The stone he had put in it at the coast was still there. Ada had learned to leave it unless they needed to make the basket lighter for him. He took it out at some halts and rubbed it with his thumb, then placed it against the side where the handle’s loose fastening had been tucked into the weave. She did not know what arrangement pleased him. She knew when she had spoiled it.

A fly settled at the corner of his eye. She moved to brush it away. He turned his head before her fingers reached him, and she stopped.

“All right,” she said.

He looked past her toward the shade beyond the cart.

They had left the coast after the six nights became nine. The larger carts arrived, but two animals did not, and the man responsible found a new brother to consult whenever someone asked what the payment had purchased. Heth finally sat in front of his door with the copper-handled pot in his lap. He did not threaten him. He talked about the money, repeating the man’s own promises in a voice that did not tire. By noon the animals came.

The first inland roads had been full of ordinary travelers. There were grain carts, women carrying covered jars, a wedding party whose musicians spent the hottest hour asleep beneath a bridge. Ada had watched them from the shade and envied the simplicity of a destination where somebody would recognize the tune.

Then the houses grew farther apart. The wells acquired walls, keepers, prices. At the last large settlement the local drivers said they would go only as far as a broken watchtower. They knew the tower. They knew the ground before it. What lay beyond was not improved by promises from a woman who had crossed the sea to find it.

Ada had wanted to argue. Heth told her to pay for the distance they would travel.

“You can buy less road than you need,” he said. “You cannot buy a man knowing it.”

They reached the tower before sunset. It had lost its upper half long ago. Fig roots held stones apart at the base, though there was no fruit on the small trees. The drivers unloaded the water they had agreed to carry onward, counted their pay, and went back with two of the hired animals. The carts and the remaining beasts belonged to the passage arrangement. Now the attendants would drive them.

Ada watched the departing men until the road dipped below the rising ground. She was angry with them for leaving. They had done exactly what they promised.

Heth stood beside the tower, looking at a blank stretch of pale earth.

“Tomorrow,” he said.

The broken axle cost them the cool part of the morning.

No one had been hurt. The wheel settled crookedly in a rut, Hessa began to cry out, and her attendant climbed into the cart before anyone could try to haul her down by the arms. They put wedges beneath the bed, moved the water casks to another cart, and made a shade with two cloaks while the damaged wood was bound.

Ada held the shade’s edge. Her arm shook. A guard took it from her without asking and tied it to a pole. She was grateful and irritated by the gratitude. On the quay she had hated his coat, the way its wearers looked toward Vaska before they answered a person. On the road he had learned which knots the attendants needed to reach and which voice would make Hessa fight.

He was still one of the men paid to bring them here. He was also holding a pole because her arm could not.

Jast got down from the second cart to make room for a cask. He put his feet carefully on the earth and waited for the attendant’s arm before taking the next step. His healed leg bent stiffly. When the cart was ready to move again, he stayed beside it, watching the ground.

“Do you want to walk?” the woman asked.

He looked toward the road. She offered her arm. He took it.

They walked a short distance before he stopped. The cart waited. His attendant held her sleeve steady while he gripped it and climbed back aboard.

Heth came back from the rise. He had left his pot beneath Ada’s cloth and seemed surprised to find it where he had put it.

“My brother had one,” he said.

“You told me a woman gave you that.”

“Did I?”

“Yes.”

He lifted it, testing its weight. A joint in his wrist made a sharp little crack.

“It was someone.”

Ada folded the cloth around the handle. “You know where we go.”

His eyes met hers. For a moment he seemed almost offended.

“Yes.”

She believed him. That frightened her more than uncertainty would have.

They moved while the sun was still low enough to put shadows beside the wheels. The visible track ended at a dry channel. Heth led them across it and up the opposite bank, where there were stones under the dust and the remains of a broad paving no driver had mentioned. They could not have mistaken the rise for another place. Ada looked back and saw the tower at the angle she expected. The distance had been crossed by feet and wheels. Still, without Heth she would have turned along the channel and gone on looking for a road.

He chose openings between collapsed walls. Some were wide enough for two carts abreast; others had to be cleared with their own hands. The men lifted stones while the attendants kept people in the shade. They stopped to drink, stopped to settle a frightened passenger, stopped because a harness strap had rubbed through its padding. The city did not rise to meet them. They worked their way toward it through the whole long day.

At dusk Ada saw the upper terraces.

They stood beyond the lower ruins, broad steps laid against the sky. Wild figs grew from walls and filled courtyards with a green too dark for the ground they had crossed. A well rope creaked somewhere. The sound brought her to a halt before the sight of water did.

Jorin lifted his head.

Heth stopped at the first standing gate. Nothing closed the opening. A shallow line had been cut across the stone beneath it, worn almost smooth by feet.

“This is Zahk’Kozahn,” he said.

Ada put one hand against the cart.

Hessa’s attendant asked where they could sleep.

Heth looked at her, then through the gate. “I know where to begin asking.”

They counted everyone in a court with a fig tree growing through its broken fountain.

The count took longer than the arrival. A man who had traveled on the second ship had gone toward the well without telling anyone. Two attendants searched the adjoining rooms before he came back carrying a wet cloth. He had remembered a use for it that no one had thought to offer him. The guard began to scold him, then stopped when he saw that the cloth had been folded into four exact squares.

Ada filled Jorin’s cup. The water was clear, cool, and tasted of stone. She drank first, waited through her own ridiculous hesitation, and filled it again for him. He took it with both hands. Most of the water reached his mouth.

A woman came through the court carrying a bundle of bedding.

She was older than Ada, or had lived in a way that made the comparison useless. Her left forefinger would not straighten. She wore an ordinary patched dress, and she was angry because someone had told her to bring four mattresses when the long room would hold only three without blocking its door.

“Who counted?” she asked.

Heth pointed toward the waiting attendants.

“Then count people lying down. Standing is a cheap way of promising space.”

Ada almost laughed. The woman gave her a sharp look, saw that she was near tears, and softened without becoming kind.

“You can bring that man through here. There is a place out of the passage.”

“What does it cost?” Ada asked.

“Tonight? Move the bench.”

Ada moved it with the guard’s help. The woman spread two folded quilts on the cleared floor and showed her where the roof let in rain. There had been no rain for a long time, she said, but people who took her rooms always preferred to learn about the hole after arranging their possessions beneath it.

Jorin sat where the quilts met the wall. He put the basket beside his foot. Ada stayed with him until his shoulders lowered, then went to help unload the others.

Hessa would not release her basket long enough for the blanket to be put beneath her. The attendant waited. Tarn followed the soft rope from the cart to a low chair and kept one end under his sound hand. Jast chose a place where he could see the open court. These were decisions Ada understood, small enough to survive the words others put over them.

Beyond the court, the city continued in ways she could not arrange.

In one open house a table was laid for more people than the room could hold. Food stood in silver dishes beneath cloths that no hand moved. A man sat at the head with his eyes fixed on the empty chairs. From another doorway came laughter, several voices taking it up and stopping together, then beginning again. Ada saw a woman in a robe so heavily sewn with bright stones that two people carried its hem. The woman’s bare feet bled where she had walked; she held another jewel in her mouth and would not let it fall.

No one had tied her. Ada could see both wrists.

She turned back to the court before the woman looked at her.

Heth was paying for grain from the road purse. The seller counted the small coins twice and accepted them. That ordinary exchange steadied Ada more than the clear water had. The money still bought food. The food could be cooked. Someone had to wash the pot.

She took out her own spoon and went to help.

Jorin did not sleep that night.

He lay down when she spread the blanket, rose when the lamps were lowered, and stood facing the passage until she brought the light back. Ada tried sitting beside him. She tried leaving a little room. She offered water and took the cup away when he pushed it so hard it struck the wall.

He had bad nights at home. She knew that. Knowing it did not give her the hamper room, the yard, the particular silence after the washing was done. Here every sound had to be learned before it could be ignored. Footsteps passed in rooms she had not seen. Once she heard a whisper close to the other side of the wall, though the room beyond had no roof and had been empty when she looked.

Near morning Jorin began walking toward the passage.

Ada followed. He stopped at the threshold, put both palms against the stone, and pressed. His skin looked gray in the lamplight. She spoke his name. He turned his head a little, but his hands remained against the wall.

Heth came out of the long room with his pot.

“He can hear her,” he said.

Ada’s throat tightened. “Who?”

“The one below.”

“Is she calling him?”

“I don’t know what he hears.”

“Then don’t tell me what it is.”

Heth looked at Jorin’s hands. “I hear her.”

Ada tucked Jorin’s cup, still holding a little water, into the fold of her bag and held the lamp higher. There was no path visible beyond the passage except the court and the lower street. She had brought Jorin across the sea to a place she had been told understood him. She wanted someone who could say why he was pressing his hands against a wall without filling the silence with guesses.

“Will he stop if we move him?”

“Perhaps.”

“Is there a quieter room?”

“Yes.”

“Show me.”

Heth did not move at once. His face, with its slack places and small hard edges, had become difficult to read.

“The rooms are near where she is.”

“Who is she?”

“Malachai.”

Ada looked at him. She had heard the name from people who believed saying it explained what had been done to Jorin. It had never helped her get him to drink or sleep. Now Heth spoke it as a person in another room, someone he had met and did not enjoy meeting again.

“Do I have to speak to her?”

“You can ask about the room first.”

Jorin took his hands off the wall and turned toward them. Heth went slowly, waiting at each opening. Ada walked beside Jorin with the lamp. He carried nothing. She had left the basket on the blanket because he would not take it, and felt its absence like a missed fastening.

They crossed the lower court and went under the first terrace of the ziggurat.

The stairs were worn in the middle and broken at one edge.

Ada counted them for the return. At the second landing, a little niche held a bowl of clean water and a rag folded beside it. She thought of stopping there. Jorin had begun to move more easily, his attention fixed on something beyond the lamp’s reach. Heth stayed close enough to catch his sleeve without holding it.

Below the landing the air cooled. Stone closed over them in a vault. The lamps in the side passages had been tended recently, their wicks trimmed, their bowls filled. Someone lived with this work. The thought was both comforting and unbearable.

They came to a chapel.

Ada saw the wings before she understood the woman.

They were gathered behind her in a shape too large for the body between them, folded and held by chains whose pale metal gave back the light differently from iron. Ada had seen aetherium only once, on an object brought through a guarded street. She had remembered the way people lowered their voices near it. Here it crossed the wings in repeated heavy lengths, fastened into stone older than any building she had washed cloth for.

Black iron held the arms outward. Hooks took the strain at the wrists and through the dark fittings driven into the wall. The woman’s feet did not rest on the floor.

Her mouth had been sewn shut with silver wire.

Ada stopped so abruptly that the lamp flame leaned forward.

The eyes were open. They found her without surprise, without the plea Ada’s own body had already begun making on the bound woman’s behalf. A wound lay in the throat below the stitched mouth. When the voice came, that was where it moved.

“You have brought him a long way.”

It was quiet, dry enough to catch in Ada’s ears, and almost gentle. The softness did not change the effort of looking at the silver drawn through the lips.

“Who did this to you?” Ada asked.

“People who had cause to bind me.”

Heth stood at the wall. His pot clicked against the buckle of his belt. He steadied it with his hand.

The woman looked at Jorin.

“He is tired. He has been unable to put down what is drawing him.”

“Can you stop it?”

“Yes.”

Ada heard how quickly she had asked. She had not asked the price, or whether this was Malachai, or how much the answer included. She held the lamp until the handle hurt her fingers.

“You are Malachai.”

“Yes.”

“And what is on him?”

“Is mine.”

The wound in the throat darkened at the edge when she spoke. Ada looked at it, at the silver wire, then back at the eyes.

“You could have stopped it before.”

“Yes.”

“Before they put him on that ship. Before he came home unable to say what he wanted.”

“I did not put him on the ship.”

“I know who did.”

Malachai waited. The answer had not defended her, and Ada hated that she had begun the conversation wanting something as small as an excuse to reject.

Jorin had stopped beside a low stone bench. He touched its edge with one hand. Ada moved the lamp there and helped him sit only when he leaned toward her. His weight came briefly against her shoulder, familiar and ordinary enough that she nearly sobbed.

“I want him to sleep,” she said. “I want a room where he can lie down without people stepping over him. I want somebody to tell me what might help before I spend another night doing the wrong thing carefully.”

“There is a room above this level. Two sleeping places. The wall between it and the passage is sound.”

“Who has it?”

“No one tonight.”

“And tomorrow?”

“It can remain yours while you use it.”

“What do you want for it?”

“The room is empty.”

Ada looked at Heth. He did not nod or shake his head. His gaze stayed on Malachai with an attention she had seen him give the road.

“An empty room isn’t an answer to that.”

“No,” Malachai said. “You may take it without making a promise to me.”

The relief came anyway. Ada could feel it, shamefully quick, entering through the thought of a wall thick enough to keep another person’s footsteps out of Jorin’s sleep.

“And the thing drawing him?”

“Below this chamber is a Well. He can drink its ichor, and the curse I put on him will end.”

“Make him himself again?”

“The curse can be removed.”

Ada heard the smaller answer. She made herself keep it smaller.

“What else goes?”

“A memory. The Silent Ones take it for each drink.”

“Which memory?”

“You do not choose it.”

“His memory of me?”

“That could be taken.”

“His memory of why he came?”

“Yes.”
