Ada put a hand over Jorin’s on the bench. He withdrew it. She let him.

“And then you leave him alone.”

“No. I will still know him. He may hear me. I can still offer him something he wants.”

Heth closed his eyes. Ada looked at the pot against his knees, the pierced strainer inside it, the cloth he could not always place in his own history.

“Did you drink?” she asked him.

“Yes.”

“And it helped?”

“For a while. I slept.” Heth looked down at his pot. “I’d forgotten what that felt like.”

Malachai let him finish.

Ada stood. Her knees hurt from the stairs. She was tired enough to want the next instruction from anyone who sounded certain.

“Show me,” she said to Heth. “The Well. I want to see what you’re asking him to do.”

“I have not asked him,” Malachai said.

Ada looked back at her.

“No,” she said. “You haven’t.”

The stair below the chapel was narrow enough that they went one at a time.

Jorin followed Heth. Ada came last, keeping the lamp above her shoulder so the light reached their feet. Her own shadow stretched ahead and broke across the steps. She counted until a turn made the numbers useless and began again.

At the bottom, children waited beside an empty pit.

That was how she first saw them. Small bodies in clean, colorless garments, heads turned together toward the stair. Then the lamp reached their eyes. Silver wire lay where the eyes should have been, worked in close bright crossings. None spoke. The nearest lifted one hand, and Jorin stopped before Ada could touch him.

The rim of the pit was polished by use. She could see down to dry stone, a little dust, a crack too narrow for her smallest finger. There was no water. No black liquid. Nothing to put in the cup she had carried without thinking from the room above.

Heth had set his pot down without its cloth. He took a step toward the rim, stopped, and bent to wrap the handle again. His fingers could not settle on the knot.

“There’s nothing,” Ada said.

“For you.”

She lowered the lamp. The bottom remained plain.

“What do you see?”

“Black. Thick.”

“What does it smell like?”

“Honey. Copper.” He swallowed. “Put your hand back.”

She had reached over the rim without noticing. She withdrew it and looked at the cup. It was Jorin’s cup, carried from the quilts when she filled it before their descent. A little ordinary water remained at the bottom.

“I could bring it to him.”

“No.”

“Heth—”

“You have no curse to reach it with. You would fill the cup with dust.”

The Silent One nearest Jorin moved. It did not threaten. It stood where a person offering help might stand, within reach of the man who could not name what he wanted. Ada went beside him. His face had changed in its attention, but she could not read consent in that any more than she could read it in his following the pot up the gangway.

She turned the cup in her hands. Its handle had a shallow mark worn by Jorin’s thumb before he ever became Worn. She had thought of that mark as something time could not take because it was in clay instead of a mind.

“What if he doesn’t understand?” she asked.

Heth looked at Jorin, then at the waiting figure.

“I don’t know what he understands.”

The same true answer. It had been true at the washing yard. It had traveled over water and roads with her. Here it stood beside a way to alter everything.

Ada took one step back.

“Not now.”

The Silent One lowered its hand. No door closed. Jorin remained at the rim until she offered the ordinary water in his own cup. He drank some. She moved toward the stair and waited. After a long time he followed.

Heth stayed until they had reached the first turn, then came after them with his pot held close to his chest.

Malachai was still where they had left her.

Ada felt foolish for expecting anything else. The chains crossed the wings in the same places; the silver held the mouth shut. At the low bench Jorin stopped to touch the stone again.

“We aren’t doing it tonight,” Ada said.

“You may wait.”

“How long?”

“You may come back when you choose.”

“And he stays like this while I choose.”

“The curse remains until it is removed.”

Ada looked at the bound hands. She had spent so much of the journey answering practical questions that this exact account almost felt merciful. No one told her the road had already cured him because he could stand at a ship’s bow. No one said that care ought to be enough, or that her love had failed because it was not.

There was a way. She would wake beside him tomorrow knowing it was only a stair away.

“The room,” she said.

Heth showed her.

It lay on the level above the chapel, off a side passage where the air moved through a shaft. Two low bed places had been built into the wall. A shelf stood high enough that Jorin could not knock the lamp over when he turned. The door closed without a bar being put across it from outside.

Ada tested the latch three times.

The woman with the bent forefinger brought bedding after Heth fetched her. She inspected the shelf, said it needed washing, and put the blankets on the sound bed rather than pretending both were equally good. The second had a cracked edge beneath its mattress. Ada asked for a board. The woman went to find one.

Nobody asked her to kneel or put her name into a book.

She carried their things down from the court in two journeys. Jorin followed the basket on the second. When she set it beside the bed, he took out the coastal stone, rubbed it with his thumb, and put it back. He sat with his feet on the floor. After a while he lay down without being shown.

Ada hung the blue shirt where the shaft’s draft would reach it. She washed the cup. Heth stood in the doorway, the cloth round his pot coming loose.

“Will the others find rooms?” she asked.

“Some already have. The woman is moving the benches.”

“And the attendants who want to leave?”

“They can ask me when they are ready. I know the road that brought me out before.”

“The same road?”

“I know that road.”

She nodded. It was no more than he had promised in Silkstrand. The return fares existed somewhere beyond the desert, kept separately by people who could not bring the sea nearer. For tonight she had no strength to demand an answer he did not have.

“Will you sleep?”

Heth looked at his pot. “I will sit for a while.”

She offered him the chair. He shook his head and went back up the passage.

Ada closed the door halfway. Jorin lay on his side facing the wall. He was not asleep yet, but his hands were still. She sat on the second bed and took the house key from beneath her clothes.

It had rubbed a small sore place against her breast during the walk. She threaded its cord around the sound bedpost, within reach. Nessa had the other key. The drain was being kept clear. There was a room somewhere with sunlight arriving at the hour she knew, and a bench whose shade changed through the day.

Here the wall was cool against her shoulder.

From below, very faintly, a voice continued. She could not make out its words through the stone. She knew now where she would have to go to hear them.

Jorin moved. Ada put the cup on the low ledge beside him and waited until he had found it before lowering the lamp.

Chapter 28 Northern harbor

The attendant came back with a complaint about the linen.

She stood in Harrow’s yard beside a sea chest, wearing a coat Lyra did not recognize and the same expression with which she had once requested a mirror for the Prince. More than three months had passed since the Winter Pear left. The first long rains had spent themselves. A new season’s freight prices were being argued beneath every dry awning in the harbor.

“They charged the damaged pieces to me,” the woman said.

“Who did?”

“The house receiving him. They said I should have known how to wash out the salt.”

“Did you?”

“No. Neither did they.”

Lyra came down the last two steps. For a moment she could think of nothing except the distance the woman had crossed. Her shoes were worn at both heels. One hand had a dark patch where cold had split the skin and healing had begun badly. The chest smelled of ship wood, old clothes, and a spice Lyra could not name.

“He arrived.”

“Yes.”

“In Zakov.”

The attendant looked at her. “I haven’t come from somewhere more convenient.”

Lyra laughed. The sound escaped before she could judge whether the woman would welcome it. After a moment the attendant smiled too, grudgingly.

“Come inside,” Lyra said. “We’ll find the linen account.”

“I have it.”

“Of course you have.”

Vaska met them at the workroom door. She had heard enough from the stair to send for the escort master before asking anything. The attendant recognized her and looked at the pale marks on her hand.

“Still trouble?”

“In damp weather.”

“Keep the cloth off when you can.”

“Everyone tells me that. Nobody arranges the weather.”

They sat. Sova had sent hot food that morning, and Lyra ordered another bowl before remembering the attendant had not been asked whether she was hungry. The woman said she was, very, and began eating while Lyra read the charges.

The receiver’s house had taken delivery of two passengers in personal service to the Prince. The man with the shaving case had chosen to remain. The woman had chosen the paid passage home. Her name appeared beside both decisions in her own hand. A later line deducted the replacement of three lengths of linen and a basin spoiled by salt.

“That wasn’t in your hire,” Lyra said.

“I told them.”

“Did they withhold the rest?”

“No. Your master insisted they settle it before I went aboard the escort. She said if the house intended to rob its servants, it should first make them its servants.”

“Feska?”

“Yes.”

Lyra put the paper down. The woman continued eating with the concentration of somebody who had been promised enough meals and enjoyed too few of them.

“I can pay the difference here,” Lyra said.

“I’d like it written against the receiving account. I don’t want them telling the next woman that I agreed.”

Vaska smiled into her cup. Lyra took a clean sheet.

The escort master had been shaved at sea by someone whose skills improved whenever the vessel stopped moving.

A little strip beneath one ear had been left longer than the rest. He touched it while waiting for Lyra to open the packet, then caught himself and put both hands on the table. His ship lay at a berth belonging to the house that had paid for her part in the escort. He had reported there first. Lyra was his second visit, after the harbor office and before he went home.

She liked that he had not rearranged those priorities for her.

He brought three kinds of evidence. The receiving master’s acknowledgment, witnessed on the northern quay. A separate port entry naming the Winter Pear, the two escorts, and the passenger delivered under guard. His own account of accompanying the Prince from the ship to the house, with the names of two crew who had done the same and could be found aboard before their pay was settled.

The papers did not look alike. The port sheet had been folded while damp; one corner carried a smear from a seal pressed too soon. The receiving acknowledgment was tidy, its margins wide enough to flatter the person signing. Feska’s copy of the voyage account had been written in a small hand that wasted neither paper nor affection.

Lyra read all three before speaking.

“Tell me from the straits.”

The master leaned back. His shoulders loosened a little. He had expected her to begin with the guarded cabin.

“They made us wait for clearance. Six days. We had the proper papers for the ship and another argument for the passenger. Feska went ashore twice. The second time she brought a witness who could count the cost of keeping us there.”

“The Prince tried to leave?”

“He asked to speak to someone. Feska said he could speak to anyone who came within sight of the hatch. He did not find that an attractive way to renew a friendship.”

Vaska sat on the sill, one foot against the wall. Lyra had asked her to listen because she knew the names of some of the northern houses. She let the master continue.

They had crossed the Amaranthine eastward under weather that favored them for a week and punished them for the next. One escort had lost a spar. The Winter Pear put in to trade and repair while both escorts remained close enough that no shore boat reached the guarded compartment unnoticed. At the Astroegro Straits the Thepyrgan officers inspected the manifests, counted the armed men, and made a point of asking Feska whose orders governed her deck.

“She told them her own,” the master said. “I think she enjoyed that part.”

Lyra smiled.

Beyond the straits lay the Dolmis, colder water and a different traffic. The voyage north took longer than the paper distance suggested. The master described two days under shortened sail, the watch relieved in pairs because a man alone could lose the line in spray, the Prince’s attendant climbing up for dry cloth while the guards argued about whether a closed hatch was still sufficient sight of the prisoner.

“It was sufficient if they stayed under it,” the attendant said from her chair. “I said so then.”

“Feska made two go down.”

“And then they wanted me to make them stop being sick.”

The master gave her a sideways look, almost fond. “You were very clear about the limits of your employment.”

“I learned.”

Lyra let them talk. The details had weight because they had inconvenienced people who were not trying to impress her. They described the Prince asking for warm water and then refusing it when it arrived in the wrong vessel, the guards changing places so no one grew too accustomed to his voice, the new salt seam appearing along the patched cloak during a rough night. He had frightened them. He had also required clean linen, room to sit upright, and assistance getting down the stair when his knee would not hold.

“At the northern port?” Lyra asked.

“The receiver met us with a cart and six men. Two of them knew him.”

“How could you tell?”

“They forgot to look important when they saw him come out.”

Vaska laughed once.

“The house was his?”

“His family’s, by what the receiver said. The entrance had an old salt mark above it. He recognized the man holding the door and spoke to him in a way I wouldn’t wish to hear from someone who remembered me.”

“What did he say?”

“The man had grown old in a comfortable place. He asked whether the comfort had done anything useful for his judgment.”

The attendant stopped eating. “Then he asked for the upper room. The one facing away from the quay.”

“He walked into it?”

“With help at the stair. I saw him put his hand on the upper rail. He turned before the door closed. He was alive, speaking, and angry that we were still there.”

“Who stayed with him?”

“His man. The house’s people. Their guard, after the receiver signed. We remained at the landing another three days while the cargo was entered. He sent for a physician on the second.”

The master pushed the separate port entry toward her. Lyra read the date against the receiving acknowledgment. They agreed. The smaller seal at the foot belonged to the examiner who had no part in taking custody of the Prince and every interest in recording three ships’ fees correctly.

“Did you see him again?”

“On the third day. At an upstairs window. I went to the house for the last receipt. He asked whether I intended to charge him for remaining in sight of his own sea.”

“What did you say?”

“That my pay ended when my master chose to sail.”

Lyra looked up at him. “And he accepted that?”

“He looked at me for a long time. Then he said he hoped I enjoyed being paid promptly while it lasted.”

The room became quiet. Outside, a barrow went over the uneven stones with a noise like rain starting.

“He’s there,” Vaska said.

“Yes,” Lyra answered.

She felt the meaning enter her body more slowly than the facts. The Prince was not waiting beyond a door in the Salt Ward. His old keepers had no hidden landing where she had neglected to look. He had crossed the sea and entered a house in Zakov, under the eyes of people whose work and money did not belong to her. Whatever he might do there, it would begin there.

She put Feska’s account beside the port entry and asked the master what remained owing.

Feska had added a note beneath the final cargo totals.

She would trade farther north before turning south. The Winter Pear would not be held waiting for a reply. One escort had other work along the coast; the master now at Harrow had taken the attendant home on his own return passage. If Lyra wanted another winter berth, she should send goods that could be sold without an argument about their previous owner.

At the bottom, in a darker ink, Feska had written that the Prince’s sea chest contained no miracle that improved the voyage. She recommended fewer guarded passengers and more oil.

Lyra read that part to Vaska after the visitors left. Vaska took the note and examined the handwriting as though a second look might reveal a better offer.

“She likes you.”

“She likes paid space.”

“That’s a useful kind of liking.”

The attendant had gone with the linen difference restored, a written objection attached to the receiving account, and enough food wrapped for the walk to the room she rented before leaving. Lyra had offered a place at Harrow until she found other work. The woman said she had slept beside someone else’s difficult body for months and wished to spend three nights hearing only herself complain.

Lyra had let her go.

Now the room felt unusually large. Vaska sat opposite her with the northern papers spread between them. The names meant more to her than they did to Lyra. She touched one with a finger whose new skin had begun to darken at the edge.

“My brother bought a boat through that office.”

“Did it float?”

“For longer than the promises.”

“Is he still there?”

“No. He found work farther down the coast.”

“You could write.”

“I do.”

Lyra looked at her. Vaska raised an eyebrow.

“I had a life before your landing.”

“I know.”

“You keep being surprised by it.”

Lyra smiled. “Only the parts you tell well.”

Vaska folded Feska’s note and put it back. She would send news of the Prince through her own correspondents, not as a warning written in Lyra’s name but because people she cared about lived near the places he could reach. Lyra felt a brief wish to see those letters before they went. She recognized the wish and left the papers where they were.

“What will you do tonight?” Vaska asked.

“I thought I might stop waiting for this.”

“You’ll find something else.”

“Probably.”

“Then do it after supper. I’ve ordered food from a woman who claims to know how my mother cooked. I want a witness when I accuse her of inventing the woman.”

Lyra laughed. She agreed to come.

Before supper she went to the repaired branch.

The work had taken most of the interval between the ships’ departure and the escort’s return. New stone bore against sound old material; the smaller receiving seat no longer relied on the cracked upright. Beneath a lifted bronze guard, the shutter gearing stood bare, its old teeth meshing with a new pinion Esten had filed until the handle ceased to kick. Mavet had repeated the load test after a wet week. The assistant could now call the shutter positions from the scrape and click without looking at Esten’s hands.

The main ancient face remained sealed. No one had opened it to celebrate victory.

There was one charged vessel in the upper guardroom now, filled after the empty trials and left in a sound cage with its own watch. The other finished containers remained empty. Lyra had wanted two ready before the escort news came. Mavet had found a flaw in the second vessel’s collar and refused the next fill. Revet was being paid to correct it.

Lyra did not need the jar tonight. She left it behind its locked door and went down with a lamp.

At the branch she put her hand on the outer wall, away from the working faces. The pressure was distant through the isolated stone, but she could feel the direction of it. Her breathing slowed. Cold gathered beneath her palm and along the narrow scar where the Prince’s hand had closed on her throat.

For a little while she stayed without asking the place to do anything.

She had once come here to learn what another man wanted. Then she had come to take what he held. Now the measured spaces were hers to open, though she still needed people who could tell her when a brace was unsafe. Under her palm, something shifted with the slow suppleness she had felt through the cage. She left her hand there.

She lowered the lamp and sat on the dry step.

The darkness reached farther into the chamber than the lamp’s light should have allowed. She could hear ordinary water beyond the drop and, beneath it, the deeper movement that had never belonged to the tide. Her body settled toward it. The ache in her right shoulder eased when she held the arm in a particular way. She had learned which posture helped and which merely delayed the pain.

Above, someone opened the guardroom door. Two sets of feet, then the softer sound of a cup put on a bench. She knew Vaska’s walk before the voice reached the stair.

“Are you coming?”

“Yes.”

“There will be nothing left to criticize if it gets cold.”

Lyra stood. The first breath of the upper passage felt too warm. She waited until the sensation passed, picked up the lamp, and went toward the voice.

At the guardroom, Vaska looked at her face and said nothing about it. She took the lamp while Lyra locked the lower door. Beyond them, the harbor’s evening lights had begun to appear in windows she could see from ground she held.

They went out together. The northern papers stayed in the business case at Harrow, where tomorrow’s clerk could find the paid amounts, the arrival date, and the witnesses who had seen the Prince walk into his own house.

Chapter 29 The evening tide

Lyra had never bought a coat because she wanted to be looked at in it.

She had bought warmth, pockets, room for a knife, cloth dark enough to survive being put on the floor. Once she had bought a color because she disliked the person who told her it would not suit her. That was the nearest she had come.

The new coat lay across her bed, blue so dark it changed when she turned the lamp. Its lining was soft without being thick. The right shoulder had been cut to give her room, then altered twice because she could feel the seam when she reached forward. The tailor had eventually made her lift a bucket, saying he could not fit a quarrel he had not been allowed to see.

She put it on and stood before the polished metal on the wall.

Her face remained her own. The healed mark beside her mouth pulled when she smiled too widely. The scar at her throat showed above the open collar, narrow at one side, broader where the Prince’s fingers had tightened. She had considered fastening the coat high enough to cover it and found she disliked that more than being seen.

Below, someone dropped a tray.

Vaska’s voice followed, asking whether the tray had offended anyone before it was sentenced. Lyra smiled and reached for the small pin that held her hair. Her fingers were cold. They had been cold all afternoon despite the warm water she had washed in. The sensation no longer meant she would soon become ill. It meant she needed to make the pin secure before her hands grew impatient.

There was a knock.

“Come in.”

Vaska stopped inside the door and looked at the coat.

“Well,” she said.

“That’s all?”

“I am deciding how expensive the rest of the sentence should be.”

She wore the coat bought after the Prince’s offer, repaired so well that the cut would not show unless a person knew where to look. There was a narrow green scarf at her throat. Her hair had been combed back with water, and she had chosen earrings that caught the lamplight when she moved. Lyra had seen her soaked, bleeding, asleep at a table, and filthy enough to frighten a landlord. The care she had taken tonight made Lyra unexpectedly pleased.

“You look good,” Lyra said.

Vaska’s smile came slowly. “So do you.”

Neither improved the compliment with a joke.

They went downstairs.

The new workroom had been finished before the north cheek was beautiful.

Mavet had insisted on that order. Sound stone mattered more than a surface that looked expensive from the water. Lyra had paid for the bearing work, the roof, the drainage away from the neighbors’ channel, and the broad window that gave the long bench enough daylight. The old rough outer face remained visible in two places. She had asked whether it should be dressed smooth and Mavet had said she could buy a second roof with what people would charge for admiring the first.

Tonight the benches had been covered and the tools moved into the locked store. Sova’s pots stood on a separate table where nobody could put a sleeve through the food while reaching for wine. The chairs did not match. One had been brought specifically for Belis, with a wide base and a place where he could put his hand before rising. He inspected it, thanked Berris, and moved it a little so his right ear faced the room.

“I can hear the door,” he told Rima.

“You can hear the food.”

“That too.”

Rima had put on a dress Lyra had never seen. She looked younger until she bent to examine the new bench, whereupon her expression became so professionally suspicious that Lyra recognized her again. Berris joined her. Within a minute they were arguing over a brace neither believed should have been allowed to reach the painting stage.

Daven arrived carrying an empty jug.

“A present?” Lyra asked.

“A reminder. You have two of mine.”

“Are you staying?”

“If there’s food.”

“There is.”

“Then it can be a present until I leave.”

He put it on the table and went to find Orrel. The two men stood beneath the new window, discussing the harbor in the tone of people who believed it would work better if the water listened to them. Coss came more slowly, using his stick with care on the threshold. He refused the arm offered by a young carrier, then accepted it when the carrier said the floor was still wet from the dropped tray.

Nobody applauded his arrival. Lyra was grateful for that. He took a chair where he could stretch his leg and began asking the nearest woman whether she knew the boy who had learned to whistle through a reed. Apparently the boy had become accomplished enough to be a public difficulty.

The room filled.

Some had fought for Lyra. Some had refused a particular fight and come because she continued paying for the work they did accept. A few had no trade with violence beyond needing their shutters to remain attached when other people argued. She knew who had been invited because she liked them and who because omitting them would cost money. The distinction was less tidy at the table than on the list.

She had not asked Tesset again. She had considered it, held the pen, and put the unfinished note away. At the quarry, Dorel had answered an earlier request for a visit with an offer of a morning next month, if Aria still wanted it when the day came. Lyra had accepted the condition.

Esten sent his assistant in his place. He had an early shift and had begun refusing late dinners on the reasonable ground that Lyra preferred him awake beneath heavy things. The assistant delivered this explanation with such an accurate imitation of his voice that Mavet nearly spilled her drink.

For a time the evening belonged to the roof.

People looked up at its sound beams. They approved the window, criticized the step, admired the new door and asked whether the old one had been sold. Sova stopped serving long enough to inspect the little stove, then told Lyra the flue would draw better if the stack were higher.

“It was built to the measurement you sent.”

“I’ve seen it now.”

Lyra laughed. “Eat something before you make it taller.”

Sova took a plate and sat at the end of the table. Someone else served the next pot.

Serafine came when the lamps had begun to reflect in the window.

She brought a bottle and no apology for missing the inspection of the roof. Her porcelain half-mask had a new flower painted along the temple, dark enough that Lyra first took it for a shadow. Odel came beside her with a plain cloak and the expression of someone who had already heard three explanations for the price of the wine.

“Whose bottle?” Lyra asked.

“Mine,” Serafine said. “Until you open it.”

“Then I’ll open it quickly.”

“Wise. Its value has been rising during the walk.”

Odel handed her cloak to the woman near the door. “Only in the telling.”

Lyra made room for them at the middle of the table. Several conversations diminished as they sat. Daven did not stop his account of a mast repaired with the wrong timber, though he grew more precise about who had supplied it. Serafine listened and asked one question that showed she knew the supplier’s other business. Daven answered cautiously and then, finding that she was not offended, resumed his indignation.

Odel ate with obvious appetite. She admired the fish, asked Sova who had found it at that price, and received the answer that people who wanted a cook’s suppliers should first pay the cook for revealing them. Odel laughed and offered to buy the information after supper.

Lyra watched the table.

At Daro’s gallery she had once sat among people who could give her a wider piece of the city. Here Serafine moved her glass to make room for a plate passed by one of Lyra’s carriers. Odel leaned across the table to speak to a woman who could open a gate Lyra now held. Neither had become less powerful by eating under her roof. Their presence made the roof larger in the minds of everyone who would describe the evening tomorrow.

Lyra enjoyed that as frankly as she enjoyed the fish.

Serafine noticed. Her uncovered eye found Lyra’s across the dishes, and the corner of her mouth lifted. There was no reproach in it. That almost made it more intimate.

After the first plates had been cleared, three musicians came in from the yard. They were local, hired for the evening, and had spent the afternoon explaining to one another why each instrument had been promised the driest corner. One played a little pipe with a voice almost as sharp as Coss’s reed pupil. Coss immediately accused him of teaching the boy.

The musician denied it, then asked whether the boy’s family could pay.

The room laughed. Lyra laughed with them. Her voice carried beneath the other sounds, lower than she meant it to, and a man at the end of the table turned before he knew who had spoken. She saw recognition arrive. He smiled too late and looked down.

She took another drink. The wine tasted dark and sweet, a little warmer than she preferred. The cup misted where her fingers rested. Serafine watched it without moving hers away.

“Do you dislike the heat now?” she asked quietly.

“Sometimes.”

“And the rest?”

Lyra looked at the room. “I am learning.”

Serafine considered her answer, then lifted the bottle and poured for both of them.

They spoke about the eastern landing in the yard after the music grew loud enough to make ordinary conversation difficult.

A trading house wanted an assured night route from its new steps to Blackglass. It had asked the Court for passage and been told that the relevant gate was no longer a favor Serafine could dispense. Now the proposal lay in Lyra’s hands. The house would pay well. It also wanted its own armed watch to accompany every cargo inside her held passages.

“No,” Lyra said.

Serafine looked toward Odel. Odel had chosen the dry side of the yard wall and was unfastening a glove with her teeth.

“You haven’t heard the number.”

“I’ve heard what the men want to carry.”

“They’re concerned about theft.”

“So am I. Their men can meet ours at the outer gate. Goods are checked there. Inside, my watch takes them through.”

“They will say you want to know what they trade.”

“I do.”

Serafine smiled, slowly. “You used to dislike being asked.”

“I still do.”

From inside came the scrape of chairs as people made space for a dance. Lyra looked through the open window. Rima had taken Belis’s hand, not to make him stand but to turn him toward her while she moved with the music. He laughed at something she said. Coss tapped his stick against the chair leg until the woman beside him made him keep a quieter beat.

Odel finished with the glove.

“My part of the passage is still mine,” she said. “If this brings another six carts through each night, the present allowance won’t cover them.”

“Then they pay you for six.”

“They wanted to pay you once.”

“They can learn two numbers.”

“Good. I dislike being included in a generosity I haven’t been asked to finance.”

They settled nothing in full. Serafine would take the terms back. Odel would inspect the requested hours. Lyra would offer a guarded route that did not import another house’s weapons into her rooms. There would be arguments, fees, a boundary somebody tested because the first refusal often mattered less than the cost of repeating it.

A laugh rose from the workroom. Lyra turned toward it, wanting to get back before the music ended. Serafine saw the movement and touched her arm briefly.

“Enjoy your roof,” she said.

“I paid for it.”

“Yes. You did.”

The older woman went inside with Odel. Lyra remained in the yard for a moment, looking at the lit window and the ordinary steam rising where Sova’s emptied pots waited to be washed. Her own window above the shed was dark. She had left a book open on the bed, a borrowed account of a northern harbor she meant to compare with the escort master’s description. Nothing required her to read it tonight.

She went back to the music.

Vaska asked her to dance after most people had decided she would not.

It was a quick harbor measure, made for a floor with less room than enthusiasm. Lyra said she did not know it. Vaska said half the room did not know it and the other half was lying. She took Lyra’s hand on the sound side, avoiding the strained arm with an ease that made the consideration invisible to anyone not looking for it.

Lyra missed the first turn. Daven called advice from his chair. She told him to stand up and demonstrate. He immediately became occupied with his jug.

On the second turn she found the step. Her coat moved around her legs, the lining cool against her thighs. Vaska’s hand was warm except where the scar had taken the skin. They passed close enough to the table that Lyra caught the scent of orange peel from a plate and the smoke of the lamp beside it.

For the length of the tune she did not listen for anything beyond the room.

When the music stopped, Vaska kept her hand until they had cleared the chairs. Lyra was smiling, breath quickened by the movement, her right shoulder beginning to complain. Vaska saw it and led her to the empty place at the window.

“There,” she said. “You can survive something without hiring guards for it.”

“The evening isn’t finished.”

“No. But the musician wants paying before he discovers a longer tune.”

Lyra paid him and asked for another. He looked at the coins, looked at the remaining dancers, and found an entire memory of music he had not previously mentioned.

People stayed later than the first boats of the morning would make wise. Berris told a story about a customer who wanted a staircase with no rise. Sova defended the fish supplier until Odel offered enough money to make confidentiality expensive. Serafine left before midnight, her bottle empty, and paused to say goodbye to Belis because he had supplied the grips on a carrying chest she used. He seemed astonished, then pleased, then began explaining a flaw in the first design until Rima squeezed his shoulder.

“Tomorrow,” she said.

“He knows his work,” Serafine replied.

“Yes,” Rima said. “He likes people to survive hearing about it.”

Lyra watched them smile at one another. It was an ordinary pleasant exchange, no less real because the Court’s guards waited in the lane.

When the last guests went, the room smelled of spilled wine and warm bodies. Coss’s chair had left a mark where his stick caught the floor. The musicians carried their own stools. Daven found both his jugs and offered to take an extra pot before Sova noticed.

“She will notice,” Lyra said.

“She notices everything except the lid she gave me last time.”

He went laughing. The gatekeeper let him through and returned to the watch without asking whether the evening had been a success.

Vaska had her own room over the rope store now.

She took the green scarf from her throat as they crossed the yard, folded it once, and put it inside her coat. The watchman nodded to her from the far gate. She answered by name. Lyra did not know when she had learned that his wife was ill, but she asked, listened to the reply, and told him whom to send if the next watch needed changing.

At the foot of her stair she turned.

“Are you going below?”

“Not tonight.”

“Good.”

“Why?”

“Because you bought the coat, and I would like it to remain a coat for at least a week.”

Lyra smiled. Vaska touched the cuff, feeling its cloth between finger and thumb.

“Worth it,” she said.

Then she went up to her own door, taking her own key from the pocket. Lyra waited only long enough to hear the latch lift. She crossed back to Harrow’s shed.

Upstairs, the room had cooled. She took off the coat and hung it where the lining could dry. Her shoulder ached in the familiar way. She rubbed it with the heel of her left hand, sat on the bed, and removed one boot, then the other.

The folded invitation she had not sent to Tesset lay beneath the book. She saw the edge of it and put the book on the shelf without opening the paper. Tomorrow she could decide whether to keep it. Tonight she was tired, and the music continued faintly in her feet.

She opened the window a little. The evening tide had turned. Water moved beneath the landing in long, dark pulls, touching the repaired stone and going on.

Lyra lay down with the cool air on her face. Below, two people were washing the last of the dishes, arguing over whose turn it had been to carry water. She knew their voices. She let the argument continue until she slept.

Chapter 30 Her own name

The water beneath Harrow was clear enough that morning for Lyra to see the new stone.

Her codex had already spent an hour in the cold salt water beneath the landing. She checked the cuts before wrapping it and changed the crab’s water with salt water drawn from the harbor. It waited under its shell until she dropped in a shred of fish.

She went down beside the south anchorage with a rope round her waist and no intention of staying until someone worried. Orrel had been given the other end. He objected that a woman with all these landings ought to be able to pay someone else to become cold before breakfast. She told him she wanted to see the work. He said that was what Mavet always said before presenting a larger account.

Below the surface, their voices disappeared.

Lyra put a hand against the anchorage and followed its face down. The new blocks met the old in an irregular line. Small creatures had begun to occupy the spaces the masons left, claiming the repair more promptly than any owner. She watched one retreat beneath a shell when her shadow crossed it.

Her lungs held easily. That pleasure remained strange enough to feel new each time. The cold entered her without finding the same places it once had. Beyond the landing, the current moved against the rope, drawing a direction through the water. She felt its ordinary pull and the harbor’s greater weight around it. Neither required her to be anything but the body choosing to remain there.

She stayed long enough to find the lowest joint sound. Then she pushed away from the stone and came up.

Orrel was ready to haul.

“I haven’t signaled.”

“You were considering it very quietly.”

She laughed and caught the lower rung. The climb hurt her right shoulder. It always seemed slightly unfair that coming out could cost more than staying under. Orrel took her left wrist when she reached the top and helped her onto the wet boards without pretending he had only steadied the rope.

Berris had left a rough cloth on the bench. Lyra wrapped herself in it and looked out over the harbor. The first cargo boats had begun moving. A man in the nearest one saw her and raised his hand. She answered. Farther away, two crews who had been arguing over a berth grew quieter when they noticed where she stood.

“Breakfast,” Orrel said.

She went inside before the cloth could become as wet as she was.

Petra Fen arrived while Lyra was eating.

She brought a broken lock, wrapped in a clean handkerchief. The front keeper asked whether she had an appointment. She said she had eight boats due on the evening tide, a partner who had sold their handling rights twice, and a lock that had stopped keeping either buyer out. She had been told Lyra could decide which of those things mattered first.

Lyra heard the account from the stair. She told the keeper to bring her up.

Petra was broad across the shoulders, with a stain at one cuff that repeated when she turned it back. Pitch, Lyra thought, old enough that washing had merely made it shine. She looked at the food and then away quickly. Lyra pushed the bread toward the empty place. Petra sat, unwrapped the lock, and began without eating.

“The east pitch steps. Two covered bays. We handle the smaller riverboats when the main yards won’t take them between larger loads.”

“I know the steps.”

“My partner had a private arrangement with one of the Prince’s collectors. I learned about it when the collector came to continue being paid.”

“How long has he been coming?”

“Since before the ships sailed.”

“And you waited.”

“I thought it might become cheaper when the man he worked for was gone.”

“Did it?”

“No. He had more time.”

Lyra broke a piece of bread and put it on the woman’s side of the table. Petra looked at it, then ate as though admitting hunger might spoil the precision of her complaint.

The steps lay inside the Salt Ward district Lyra held, but the store lease belonged to Petra and her partner. Lyra collected at the maintained outer route and left ordinary disputes to the people whose names were on the property. Some had mistaken that for reluctance to hear about anything smaller than a war. Others had used it to keep charging under old arrangements until someone strong enough asked.

Petra’s partner had taken an advance from a second house against the same evening traffic. He had then gone upriver with the money. The first collector had put men at the store. The second claimant had threatened to bring its own. Neither wanted a dispute over the goods once the eight boats were unloaded. Both preferred to possess the doors first.

“Who sent you here?” Lyra asked.

“I went to the Court. Their woman said the approach is yours.”

“The approach is.”

“Then I asked whether Serafine would hear me. She said I would spend the morning asking someone who would spend the afternoon asking you.”

Lyra smiled. Petra did not. She had not offered the account as a compliment.

“What do you want?”

“The men off my steps. The boats through tonight. After that I’ll find my partner and discover which of us owes the other an explanation.”

“Have you paid the collector?”

“Enough to make him come back. Not enough to make him pleased about it.”

Petra brought out the receipts. They were folded in order and had been kept dry. Lyra read them while the woman finished the bread. The first bore an old company mark. The later ones used the same stamp with the identifying edge deliberately broken away.

She sent for Vaska.

While they waited, Petra looked at the window and the clean shelf beside it. Lyra could see her measuring the room against whatever stories had brought her to Harrow. There were no trophies. The business case stood under the table. A cup had left a ring on the sill. The new coat hung beside an older one whose repaired shoulder had finally stopped being worth another repair.

“The woman downstairs said you’re busy.”

“I am.”

“I can pay for the watch tonight.”

“You can pay for more than tonight.”

Petra’s gaze returned to her face.

Lyra laid the receipts down. “Your steps become a regular receiving point under my protection. My watch controls the working entrance. Your crews keep the handling work. You receive the stated share after wages and maintenance. The boats are assigned through my office.”

“I own half the lease.”

“You keep it.”

“And if I want to take work elsewhere?”

“Your own hands can go where you please. Cargo contracted through the steps comes through the route we agree.”

“For how long?”

Lyra named the term. It was longer than Petra expected. The woman’s fingers closed around the broken lock.

“My partner is the one who took the advances.”

“Yes.”

“You’re offering to take his place.”

“I am offering to keep either claimant from taking yours.”

Petra looked down at the lock. One of its iron cheeks had bent outward when someone forced it. She pressed the bent piece with her thumb, trying a movement that could not undo the break.

Vaska arrived and listened to the account without sitting. She knew the collector. He had once employed two of the men who now watched Blackglass. She asked where his people stood, how many had weapons, and whether Petra’s own workers were still inside.

“Four outside. Two in the upper room. My crews went home when the lock came off.”

“Good,” Vaska said.

Petra looked up sharply.

“They weren’t good for leaving. They were good for keeping their fingers attached until someone could arrange a better afternoon.”

Lyra asked for a clean sheet.

The writing took less time than the argument over it. Petra wanted crew wages paid before the handling share. Lyra agreed. She wanted her niece, who kept the ordinary accounts, retained in the work. Lyra agreed if the woman could do it. Petra wanted the receiving rights to pass with her own lease if she sold. Lyra refused that part.

“You approve the person.”

“Yes.”

“And if we disagree?”

“You can sell what you own. You can’t sell my watch with it.”

Petra sat for a while without speaking. Lyra let her. Through the window came the sound of a cargo being lowered onto the new quay boards, each call answered before the next movement began.

At last the woman took the pen.

She did not thank Lyra. She read the terms again after signing, as though the ink might have altered them. Then she put her copy inside the same dry wrapping as the receipts.

“Will you come?” she asked Vaska.

Lyra stood. “We both will.”

The collector wore one of the Prince’s old badges with the salt mark scraped away.

He had done the scraping carefully. From a distance it looked like a plain piece of silver, enough to suggest an office without committing its wearer to a name. He came down from the pitch store’s upper room when Vaska called him, taking his time until he saw Lyra beside the broken door.

Then he became brisk.

“There has been a misunderstanding.”

Petra stood at Lyra’s left. At her right, Vaska’s people had spread far enough apart that a single rush would not reach them together. The ordinary lane remained open. A carter drove past slowly, looking, and was told by his own passenger to keep his eyes on the animal.

“Who paid you?” Lyra asked.

“The partner. A private arrangement.”

“Which part bought these men?”

“Security.”

“For whom?”

He glanced at Petra. She looked back with the deliberate stillness of a person who had spent too many mornings being interrupted by him.

The collector began explaining the advances. Lyra let him name the second house, the amounts, the reasons no one could be expected to surrender a good position merely because the original employer had sailed away. He spoke more confidently as he continued. A reasonable settlement seemed possible. His men watched his face and took their cue from it.

Lyra held out her hand.

“The badge.”

He stopped speaking.

“It represents no claim against you.”

“Then give it to me.”

He removed it slowly. The fastening caught in his coat. For a moment he struggled with the pin while everyone watched. When it came free, he placed the badge in her palm with a faint movement of annoyance he seemed unable to prevent.

She turned it over. The old stamped number remained on the back.

“You have continued collecting with this.”

“People knew me.”

“They do.”

She gave the badge to Vaska. “Bring the money down.”

The collector’s face changed. “The money has been paid under agreement.”

“Bring it down.”

One of the men at the stair moved his hand toward his knife.

Vaska was nearer than he thought. She caught his wrist, stepped inside the turn, and drove him against the doorpost. His head struck wood. The knife fell. He tried to pull free, and she twisted until his knees gave. The sound he made brought the other three men forward half a pace.

Lyra looked at them.

They stopped.

“Leave his hand useful,” she said.

Vaska eased the pressure by just enough. Blood ran from the man’s eyebrow onto the threshold. He stayed kneeling, breathing through his mouth. The collector looked at him, then up the stair.

“I will fetch the box.”

“One of mine goes with you.”

He made no further objection.

Petra had gone pale. She watched the injured man with a tightness around her mouth, but when Lyra glanced toward her she said nothing. She had asked for the steps. The steps were being returned to a working order with different people at the entrance.

The box contained less than the receipts promised. Lyra asked where the rest was. The collector began to name expenses.

“Those are yours,” she said.

“Some of this belongs to the other house.”

“They can come to me.”

He heard what she had offered and what she had taken. The disputed payment had become her argument to conduct. He could leave without the box or remain beside his kneeling man until she found another use for the afternoon.

He left.

Vaska released the man at the post. He rose unsteadily and kept his injured arm against his chest. No one offered his knife back. The six went down the lane together, their steps quickening after the turn.

Petra opened the store.

They found the work almost ready for the boats.

The floors had been cleared, the empty jars stacked on pads, the scales hung where the light could reach them. Petra’s crews had left only after doing what they could to protect the ordinary labor waiting behind the quarrel. She went through the bays touching things that had been moved, putting a hook back on its nail, lifting a sack onto the higher shelf where a damp patch would not reach it.

Her niece came before the first crew. She had ink on the side of her hand and an expression that became less agreeable with each new detail. Petra showed her the signed arrangement. The woman read it standing beside the scale.

“You should have sent for me.”

“I sent yesterday.”

“To my room. I was here, being told the lock belonged to the man who broke it.”

Petra closed her eyes briefly. Lyra saw how the afternoon would continue after she left: the explanation, the anger at the explanation, the work interrupting both because the boats would not wait for a family to finish speaking honestly.

“Read it before you make the entries,” Lyra told the niece.

“I intended to.”

Vaska turned away to hide a smile. Lyra almost smiled too. She had liked the resistance in the woman until she reached the line granting Lyra approval over a replacement and looked up.

“You could refuse anyone.”

“Yes.”

Petra put a hand on her niece’s arm. The younger woman looked at it, then at the broken door. She folded the agreement and went to fetch the old books.

Lyra watched her go.

Outside, Vaska was placing the watch. Two people at the working gate, one at the water steps, another free to move between them. Their food and pay came through Blackglass before any share was taken. The second claimant would be told where to bring its evidence. No one would bring armed men through the store because a man upriver had sold a promise twice.

Petra asked which mark to put on the evening receipt.

“Your store’s,” Lyra said. “And mine for the passage.”

“Whose name underneath?”

“Lyra.”

Petra waited, expecting a house or title to follow.

Lyra took the pen from the desk and wrote it on the blank receipt. The letters were clear. There was room beneath them, but she added nothing.

She returned to Harrow while the lamps were being lit.

The day’s other work had continued. A door needed a new hinge. A customer had overpaid and would be told before he found a reason to call it theft. Mavet had sent word that the second collar could be inspected in the morning; until then, the upper guardroom held one charged vessel and the rest remained empty. The paid watch had changed. The public footway was crowded with people whose business did not belong to her.

Lyra washed the pitch from her hands. It came away slowly, leaving a dark line beside one nail. She scrubbed it with a brush until the skin around it grew sore, then stopped. Downstairs, the keeper was asking whether the woman who had come about Gannet’s dead should be admitted if she returned tomorrow.

“No,” Lyra said.

He took the answer and went.

She stood at the basin for another moment, listening to the water settle in the bowl. Then she dried her hands, took the older coat from its hook, and went down to the landing.

Vaska was there with news that the first two pitch boats had arrived. Petra’s crews were unloading them. The second house had sent a message instead of men.

“What does it say?” Lyra asked.

“That its claim deserves an audience.”

“Tomorrow. Before the collar inspection.”

“I’ll tell them.”

“Eat first.”

“I have.”

Lyra looked at the paper twist in her hand.

“Something that isn’t fruit.”

Vaska offered her a piece. Lyra took it despite knowing exactly how sour it would be. They stood beside the repaired anchorage while she let the taste work through her mouth.

Across the water, lamps marked the approaches to the places she held. Harrow. Blackglass. Gannet’s quay. The Salt Ward steps, where ordinary people were doing ordinary work beneath her watches. Beyond them lay other lights, other houses, the Court’s boats and the Ragman’s ways, a city she had neither bought nor been given.

A lighter waited off the landing with its oars shipped. Its master raised a lamp when he saw her. He had a load accepted for the new workroom and wanted to know whether there was room to bring it in before the tide turned harder against him.

Lyra looked along the quay. One berth was occupied, the other clear. Orrel had the winch ready. A carrier stood with the first rope coiled over his forearm, waiting for her decision.

She stepped to the bell fixed beneath the new roof.

The sound carried across the short reach of water. The lighter’s oars went out. Its bow turned toward Harrow, and Lyra stayed at the edge while the people on her landing moved to receive it.
